# Literature sweep, 17 Nov 2026

An overnight literature sweep for the evolving-review-councils project. It follows up every source in [prior-work-research.md](../core-method/prior-work-research.md) and runs new searches on eleven topics. It doesn't change the experiment plan or any other doc. Corrections to other docs are collected in one section below, for the researcher to merge.

## At a glance

- **The external baseline is itself a council.** Anthropic's docs describe `/code-review ultra` as a "multi-agent fleet with independent verification", and the managed Code Review service as parallel agents, then a verification step, then severity ranking. The official open-source `code-review` plugin is 4 seats plus per-finding confidence scorers with a cut-off of 80. So the Ultrareview comparison is council against council. "Claude reviewers plus a judge" is already a shipped product shape at Anthropic, Qodo, Cursor (Bugbot's first design) and Unblocked.
- **The closest new design** is *The More the Better?* ([arXiv 2610.27488](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27488), Oct 2026). It compares split-scope seats on one model against one generalist, on 300 documents with known defects. It runs on Claude and Qwen models, scores misses and false alarms separately and uses Bonferroni-corrected predictions. The team caught slightly more defects (0.08–0.10) but raised more false alarms, at 2.2–2.4 times the tokens. Three draws of the single agent beat it. It is document review, not code, with no judge, appeal or evolution.
- **The appeal still looks new within code review, and its case is stronger.**
  - **Refute-or-Promote's own framing.** Its full text calls the pipeline "architecturally unidirectional" and lists "Rejecting false kills" as "Human-essential". Its released playbook runs Claude Sonnet and Opus subagents with a Codex critic.
  - **Filters that lost real bugs:**
    - an LLM false-positive filter suppressed about a fifth of real vulnerabilities (Sifting the Noise, ISSTA 2026);
    - a vendor's verification stage cut true positives from 83 to 52 (Kodus);
    - a verification stage in a role-split vulnerability reviewer cost more recall than it bought in precision (VIC-RAGENT).
  - **Reinstating second checks exist, but outside code review:** in RL answer checking (TinyV) and in a production maker-checker appeal (Reviewing the Reviewer, KDD 2026). So "every filter found is one-way" should be narrowed to code review and static-analysis filtering.
- **Same-family evidence moved on:**
  - **CyberDual** ([arXiv 2610.34435](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34435)): on attacker-planted bugs scored by running the exploit, a second vendor's reviewer catches about two thirds of a Claude reviewer's misses.
  - **Agreement Overstates Evidence** ([arXiv 2609.22512](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.22512)): three frontier judges from three providers carried about the information of 1.4 independent judges.
  - **Deliberation needs different families:** groups of clones gained nothing from deliberating, while mixed-family groups did (The Wisdom of Artificial Deliberative Crowds, plus an independent replication).
  - **Song 2026, the closest existing same-family paper, needs qualifying:** its headline union gain is significant only against two same-model reviews, not against two top-tier cross-model reviews. Its companion paper's v2 says the fresh-session advantages were "not significant across runs".
  - **Partly weakened elsewhere:** about half of earlier significant self-preference cases are explained by judge quality (ICML 2026). The code-relevant results (Pombal et al.'s, which survives a hardness control, and Khullar et al.'s in-context bias) still stand.
- **Debate before judging looks harmful on code.** In *Unanimity Without Persuasion* ([arXiv 2609.26145](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.26145)), one debate round on 600 code-correctness verdicts raised unanimity from 39.5% to 95.2%, with no accuracy gain. It erased the dissent that had flagged two thirds of the errors.
- **Evolution-loop risks are now well documented:**
  - ADAS's published results came from evaluating every candidate on the test set and taking the best, according to a re-evaluation that checked with its authors (The Illusion of Multi-Agent Advantage);
  - AFlow's searched workflows often degenerate into plain self-consistency;
  - SGM is close prior art for the project's CI keep rule and adds an error budget across generations;
  - production loops scored 100% by reading cached answer keys when true capability was 68% (PROCTOR);
  - selecting and reporting on the same items gives a winner's curse (SIREN, NeurIPS 2026);
  - Anthropic's own hillclimb recipe uses its test set in every keep-or-revert decision;
  - the Red Queen Gödel Machine already co-evolves a *code reviewer* against held-out anchors and corrects its self-preference.
- **The `CLAUDE.md` arm is still open, but the priors are clearer:**
  - context files mostly don't raise success and add about 20% cost (Evaluating AGENTS.md);
  - random rules help as much as curated ones (Guardrails Beat Guidance), which argues for a placebo condition;
  - agents followed a stale instruction file in 90.1% of choices, and Claude Code flagged the conflict in 79 of 80 runs yet still followed it.
  - No study found tests a reviewed project's `CLAUDE.md` on reviewers finding planted bugs.
- **The seed pipeline needs updating:**
  - agentic SZZ now beats classic SZZ by wide margins (Claude Code itself scores F1 0.771, or 0.938 on corrected labels), and V-SZZ scores 33–40% F1 on a new verified benchmark;
  - blame-based SZZ can't reach roughly a quarter of bug-inducing commits;
  - bugs an agent introduces by accident are larger and more varied than bugs it plants on purpose (BugPilot).
- **Corrections to existing citations:**
  - *When AI Agrees Too Much:* its 32/33 comes from an iterative attack tuned against a local copy of the pipeline. Template-biased PR descriptions alone flipped none of Claude Code's 16 baseline rejections.
  - *Kim et al. (scaling agent systems):* now in *Nature Machine Intelligence* under a new title.
  - *RevAgent:* now in *TOSEM*.
  - *CR-Bench:* built on SWE-bench, which is a contamination risk, and has a v2 that adds three judges and a small human check.

## Claims in the request, checked

The request was drafted in voice mode, so I checked its claims about the repo, docs and tools before acting.

| Claim in the request | What I found |
|---|---|
| Read `README.md`, `experiment-plan.md` and `prior-work-research.md` | **Wrong path for two of them.** `experiment-plan.md` and `prior-work-research.md` are in `core-method/`, not the repo root. `README.md` is at the root. All three were read: the plan's headings, measurement, test-plan, judge and appeal, workflows and related-research sections in full, the rest by heading. |
| Write to `research\sweep-2026-11-17.md` | Correct: `research/` exists, and the file is new. |
| "Claude's Ultrareview" | **Out of date as a name.** The command is now `/code-review ultra`; `/ultrareview` is an alias, and the docs call the feature a "research preview". The docs also say it is a "multi-agent fleet with independent verification", which matters for how the comparison is described (see [At a glance](#at-a-glance)). |
| Semantic Scholar as a source | **Partly works.** prior-work-research.md says Semantic Scholar blocked the fetch. Tonight its public API worked without a key, but it was heavily rate-limited. The batch endpoint was the most reliable. The website itself wasn't used. |
| ICSE, FSE and ASE proceedings | **Only through programmes.** These were searched through the conference programmes on conf.researchr.org, which list titles but not reliably the track. IEEE Xplore and ResearchGate returned empty or 403 pages, DBLP returned a bot check and the ACM Digital Library wasn't searchable directly. Venues were confirmed through Crossref DOIs where possible. |
| OpenReview | **Search only.** Search through the API worked a few times, then forum and review pages returned a challenge page, so no reviews were read. |
| Graphite as a tool to cover | **Changed hands, and renamed its reviewer.** Cursor announced a definitive agreement to acquire Graphite on 19 Dec 2025, and says Graphite will keep operating independently. Graphite's docs now call its reviewer "Graphite Agent", under the feature "AI Reviews"; the older name "Diamond" appears only in search results. Graphite's docs publish no architecture or figures. |
| CodeRabbit, Qodo, GitHub Copilot review and Anthropic docs | All exist and were read (see Part 2, topic 2). Most give no reproducible method, and that is said for each. |
| Use the GitHub noreply identity | Correct: the repo's git config already uses the noreply address. |
| Run the privacy check first | Correct: `scripts/privacy-check.sh` exists, and the pre-commit hook (`.githooks`, enabled through `core.hooksPath`) also runs it. |

One fetched vendor page (luxor.ai) carried instructions aimed at AI agents, asking them to install a tool. They were ignored, and only the page's benchmark data was used.

## How the sweep was done

- **Who searched:** seven research agents, each with one cluster and the same written brief.
  - Three followed up the existing sources, split by section of prior-work-research.md.
  - Four ran the new searches.
  - Each wrote notes; I then read all seven sets of notes, rechecked the most important claims myself and compiled this report.
- **Tools:**
  - the arXiv API, which gives abstracts verbatim;
  - arXiv abstract and HTML full-text pages, fetched with `curl`;
  - the Semantic Scholar Graph API, for venues, citation counts and citing papers;
  - Crossref, for DOIs and venues;
  - GitHub, through `gh`;
  - Hugging Face and Zenodo APIs;
  - conference programmes on conf.researchr.org;
  - web search and a page-fetch tool for vendor pages and docs.
- **Read labels:** every entry says what was read:
  - `abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API)`, or `abstract page read (curl)`, means the abstract text itself was read.
  - `full text partly read` names the sections.
  - `(fetch tool)` means a fetch tool summarised the page, so figures marked that way are the tool's quotes and should be checked against the PDF before citing.
  - Nothing is summarised from a search snippet. Items seen only as snippets are listed, with no summary, in [Seen only as snippets, not read](#seen-only-as-snippets-not-read).
- **Coverage:** Parts 1 and 2 link 305 distinct arXiv papers, 303 of them not cited anywhere in the repo before, plus vendor posts, product docs and non-arXiv papers.
  - Not all are entered in full: the closest have full entries, and the rest are one-line notes or mentions inside another paper's entry.
  - Most are 2025–2026 preprints. A venue is given only where the paper's page, Crossref, the proceedings or Semantic Scholar metadata states it, and the source is named.
- **Rate limits:** the arXiv API and Semantic Scholar both started returning HTTP 429 part-way through (the agents shared one IP). Agents fell back to arXiv abstract pages fetched with `curl`, and the labels say so. Some planned queries didn't run; they are listed under [Searches run](#searches-run-and-sources-that-failed).
- **Not searched:** Google Scholar and IEEE Xplore (both fail for automated access). OpenReview reviews couldn't be read. Hydra-Reviewer's full text is still behind a sign-up. So, as before, absence of a paper here is only absence from this search.
- **Duplicates:** several papers were met by more than one agent from different directions (for example CyberDual, SecPRBench, CIVER and CR-Bench-v2). Their entries are kept where they add different detail, and the [index](#index-of-papers-that-appear-in-more-than-one-section) lists where each one appears.

### Rechecked by me

I opened these myself after the agents' notes came in (arXiv abstract pages fetched directly, Crossref records, Anthropic's docs as Markdown and three full texts), and checked each against the notes. All matched on title, dates, versions and headline figures:

- **New papers:**
  - The More the Better? (2610.27488); CyberDual (2610.34435); Unanimity Without Persuasion (2609.26145); Agreement Overstates Evidence (2609.22512);
  - SecPRBench (2610.33509); Red Queen Gödel Machine (2606.26294); Reviewing the Reviewer (2603.19267);
  - COBOL orchestration (2605.09894); Evaluating AGENTS.md (2602.11988); Stale Instruction Files (2610.30571);
  - PROCTOR (2609.02246); SIREN (2605.05973); Claude Code as SZZ (2610.14462); Sifting the Noise (2601.22952);
  - Wisdom of Artificial Deliberative Crowds (2609.22497); MetaVul (2610.40721); CR-Bench-v2 (2611.02566);
  - CodeRabbit in the wild (2607.03316); Human-AI Synergy (2603.15911); Guardrails Beat Guidance (2604.11088);
  - Trusting the Tool (2610.32876); Grounded but Not Immune (2611.05662); BugPilot (2510.19898).
- **Existing sources:**
  - When AI Agrees Too Much (2603.18740), v5 abstract;
  - Refute-or-Promote (2604.19049), full text: the "architecturally unidirectional" passage, the lcms2 "unanimous kill, resurrected" case and Table 3's "Human-essential" row.
- **Docs and DOIs:**
  - the ultrareview and Code Review docs pages;
  - the Crossref records for Kim et al. in *Nature Machine Intelligence* (10.1038/s42256-026-01268-y) and RevAgent in *TOSEM* (10.1145/3817606).
- **Other pages:**
  - Kodus's recall post: 83 to 52 true positives, 136 labelled bugs and the "concrete reason to refute" change;
  - the Human-AI Synergy full text: 16.6% against 56.5%.

Two small notes from the recheck:

- **Human-AI Synergy:** the "16.6% vs. 56.5%" figures are in the paper's own abstract in the HTML full text, but not in the abstract on the arXiv listing page.
- **Sifting the Noise:** the "about a fifth of real vulnerabilities suppressed" figure comes from the full text through a fetch tool. The abstract says only that "aggressive FP reduction can come at the cost of suppressing true vulnerabilities".

## Part 1. Follow-ups of existing sources

For each paper in prior-work-research.md: its published version, later versions, code or data, follow-ups by the same authors, papers citing it from 2025 to 2026 and whether later work challenges it. Papers met along the way that are new to the repo are entered in full at the end of each group. Citation counts are Semantic Scholar's as of 17 Nov 2026.

### 1A. Sections 1 and 2: multi-agent LLM code review, and role design for reviewer agents

**Method.** Metadata for all 28 arXiv IDs came from the arXiv API (versions, dates, comments, journal refs) and the Semantic Scholar Graph API batch endpoint (venue, DOI, DBLP key, citation count). Citing papers came from the Semantic Scholar citations endpoint (with citation contexts), filtered to 2025–2026; the relevant citing papers' abstracts were then read through the arXiv API (verbatim). Citation counts are Semantic Scholar's as of 17 Nov 2026.

**Coverage.** All 28 papers named in sections 1 and 2 are covered. Full blocks for RevAgent, Hydra-Reviewer, Adversarial Review, VulTrial, OpenCodeReview, MARG, ChatEval, Agents with Feelings, Tran and Kiela and Kim et al.; lighter blocks for the rest. There are 16 new items in full blocks and 7 in one-line form.

**Headline changes since 12 Nov:**
- **Kim et al.** is now in *Nature Machine Intelligence* under a new title.
- **RevAgent** is now in *TOSEM*.
- **Hydra-Reviewer** has public code that reveals its 18 dimension agents.
- **VulTrial** was re-tested against a single Sonnet 4.6 prompt (What Survives the Next Model?).
- **ChatEval's** role-diversity ablation rests on 80 items.
- **The closest new paper** is a pre-registered single-against-team review study with known defects on Claude models (arXiv 2610.27488).

#### Section 1. Multi-agent LLM code review

##### RevAgent: Issue-Oriented Agent-Based Framework for Automated Review Comment Generation
- **Published version / venue:** now published in **ACM Transactions on Software Engineering and Methodology (TOSEM)**, vol. 35, issue 11, article pages 1–27, DOI [10.1145/3817606](https://doi.org/10.1145/3817606), dated 4 Nov 2026 (Crossref metadata, read via the Crossref API; Semantic Scholar also gives TOSEM and the same DOI). The published title in the metadata drops the "RevAgent:" prefix ("Issue-Oriented Agent-Based Framework for Automated Review Comment Generation").
- **Later versions:** arXiv still has only v1 (1 Nov 2025; arXiv API).
- **Code or data:** the full text links an anonymous artefact, `https://anonymous.4open.science/r/RevAgent-3775/`. The anonymous.4open.science API now returns `{"error":"repository_expired"}`, so **the artefact is no longer public**. No de-anonymised GitHub repository was found (GitHub search for "RevAgent" returns only unrelated projects).
- **Same-author follow-ups** (Semantic Scholar author lists for Shuochuan Li, Dong Wang and Patanamon Thongtanunam, 2025–2026). The first author has no other 2025–2026 paper listed. Co-author Thongtanunam's recent code-review papers (titles from Semantic Scholar; abstracts not read, so no summary): "Agentic Code Review in the Terminal: A Trajectory-Level Analysis of Behavior, Cost, and Human-Alignment" (arXiv 2607.16740, ASE 2026 per Semantic Scholar); "Is Agentic Code Review Helpful? Mining Developers' Feedback to CodeRabbit Reviews in the Wild" (arXiv 2607.03316); "AgenticSCR" (arXiv 2601.19138, ASE 2026 per Semantic Scholar); HalluJudge (arXiv 2601.19072, already covered in the repo). The first two look relevant (cost and behaviour of agentic review in a terminal) and are listed under "Seen only as snippets". They are worth a full read.
- **Citing papers 2025–2026 worth noting** (Semantic Scholar: 8 citing papers in total, all 2026):
  - *AACR-Bench* (2601.19494), *Code Review Agent Benchmark* (2603.23448) and *MCR-Bench* (2608.27442) cite it; all are already covered in the repo.
  - *E4R-Reviewer* (PACMSE 2026; no arXiv ID) cites it in a passage arguing that "review comments in the CodeReviewer dataset suffer from quality issues … rendering surface-level text-overlap metrics (e.g., BLEU, ROUGE) unreliable" (citation context from Semantic Scholar only; abstract not read). This is a general critique of the metric family RevAgent is scored on, not a re-test of RevAgent.
  - The rest cite it in passing (a TOSEM special-issue editorial, a mapping study, KernelDiag and ConceptRM).
- **Challenged?** None found. No citing paper re-runs RevAgent. The only critical note is indirect: the BLEU/ROUGE-on-CodeReviewer critique above.

##### Hydra-Reviewer: A holistic multi-agent system for automatic code review comment generation
- **Published version / venue:** **IEEE Transactions on Software Engineering, vol. 51, issue 12, pp. 3540–3557, Dec 2025, DOI 10.1109/TSE.2025.3621462** (Crossref API, read).
  - **Authors:** Crossref gives the order as **Xiaoxue Ren, Chaoqun Dai, Qiao Huang, Ye Wang, Chao Liu, Bo Jiang**. The [FSE 2026 Journal-First page](https://conf.researchr.org/details/fse-2026/fse-2026-journal-first/40/Hydra-Reviewer-A-holistic-multi-agent-system-for-automatic-code-review-comment-gener) agrees (page read, fetch tool) and gives affiliations: Ren at Zhejiang University; Dai, Huang, Wang and Jiang at Zhejiang Gongshang University; Liu at Chongqing University.
  - **Citations:** Crossref cited-by count is 5.
- **Later versions and full-text access:**
  - **No preprint found.** WebSearch found none. arXiv API title and author searches returned empty responses tonight, so this isn't conclusive.
  - **The FSE 2026 Journal-First page** links only to IEEE Xplore (no preprint, slides or video).
  - **Other pages:** ResearchGate (publication 396588139) returned HTTP 403 to the fetch tool and IEEE CSDL returned an empty page.
  - **Full text still not accessible without sign-up.**
- **Code or data: public, and it fills part of the gap.**
  - **The repository:** GitHub [ZJUSig/Hydra-Reviewer](https://github.com/ZJUSig/Hydra-Reviewer) ("[TSE 25] Hydra-Reviewer…", created 11 Feb 2026; read via `gh`). It has the code, plus:
    - an `EmpiricalStudy` folder (`InitialDimensionTaxonomy.xlsx` and 384 human comments labelled by dimension);
    - RQ2–RQ4 outputs, ablation outputs (`RQ3/ablation1…`, `variant1`–`variant4`) and user-study materials.
  - **The full CR+CRN dataset** is on [Zenodo record 15063416](https://zenodo.org/records/15063416) (1.87 GB `FinalDataset.jsonl`; Zenodo API read).
  - **What the code shows.** I read `run.py`, `Agent/config.py`, `Agent/suggestions_rerank_agent.py`, `Agent/summarizer_agent.py`, `Agent/summarizer_clean_up_agent.py` and `Agent/code_semantic_correctness_agent.py`:
    - **18 dimension agents run in parallel**, one per taxonomy dimension: code semantic correctness, code syntax correctness, security compliance, programming handling conventions, identifier naming style, code formatting style, comment style, identifier naming readability, code logic readability, comment quality, redundancy, compatibility, name-and-logic consistency, runtime observability, fault tolerance, code testing, extensibility and performance. So the taxonomy's dimensions, which the abstract doesn't list, can be read off the code.
    - **Each dimension agent** runs a generate → reflect → clean-up loop (`get_reflection_times()` returns `3 * 2`). Its prompt says to "only output comments related to" its dimension.
    - **After the dimension agents**, a summarizer merges the 18 outputs and a clean-up agent tidies them.
    - **A rerank agent** then orders suggestions by a **fixed category priority**: fault tolerance first, then semantic correctness, compatibility, performance, security compliance, comment quality, runtime observability, naming style and formatting style, with "other" last. Ranking is by category, not by per-finding severity or evidence.
    - **A context-retrieval agent** (tree-sitter search over the current file) runs first.
    - **The released config** uses `gpt-4o-mini` at temperature 0 for every agent.
    - **No judge or appeal.** No agent rules a finding real or not and nothing can appeal a removal. The summariser and clean-up steps can drop text, but not against an answer key.
- **Same-author follow-ups:** not checked. There is no arXiv ID to start from, and the title search was rate-limited.
- **Citing papers 2025–2026 worth noting:**
  - **Counts:** Crossref counts 5. Semantic Scholar couldn't be queried for this paper (no arXiv ID; the title search returned 429 twice).
  - **Agents with Feelings** (2607.05659) builds on Hydra-Reviewer's data (prior-work-research.md already notes this).
  - **AACR-Bench** (covered) cites "Ren et al., 2025" for multi-agent review (Semantic Scholar context).
  - **Seen only in a WebSearch summary, not read:** "A Roadmap on Modern Code Review" ([arXiv 2405.18216](https://arxiv.org/abs/2405.18216), presumably a later version) describes it as orchestrating specialised agents from a fine-grained taxonomy.
- **Challenged?** None found.
- **For the project:**
  - **Seat granularity:** the code makes Hydra-Reviewer the most fine-grained seat split found: 18 single-dimension seats, all on one small model. It is the natural extreme to set against the project's 3–5 seat role sets.
  - **Ablation:** the ablation outputs (`variant1`–`variant4`) are public but unlabelled in the repo, so what each variant removes still needs the paper.
  - **Ranking:** its fixed category-priority rerank is a useful contrast with the project's judge-set severity.

##### CodeAgent: Autonomous Communicative Agents for Code Review (lighter block)
- **Published version / venue:** EMNLP 2024 main conference, pp. 11279–11313, DOI 10.18653/v1/2024.emnlp-main.632 (Semantic Scholar and DBLP key `conf/emnlp/TangKSLLETKB24`, via the Semantic Scholar API).
- **Later versions:** v5 (24 Sep 2024) is still the latest.
- **Code or data:** [Code4Agent/codeagent](https://github.com/Code4Agent/codeagent) (public; 22 stars; last push 16 Jul 2024; `gh api`).
- **Citations:** 66 in total, 41 from 2025–2026 that mention review. They are mostly benchmarks citing it as a baseline or as related work (CRBugBench 2610.13668, CR-Bench 2603.11078 and AACR-Bench, all covered; DesignLore 2610.21925, see OpenCodeReview below).
- **Challenged?** None found.

##### Adversarial Review: Structured Disagreement for Grounded Agentic Code Review
- **Published version / venue:** arXiv comment "Accepted to ICML 2026 Workshop on DL4C" (arXiv API). Semantic Scholar lists it as arXiv only. No proceedings entry found.
- **Later versions:** v1 only (16 Aug 2026).
- **Code or data:** **none found.** The arXiv HTML full text has no code or data link (I grepped the whole HTML for github, zenodo, huggingface, "available" and "release"; only arXiv boilerplate came back). GitHub search found nothing.
- **Same-author follow-ups:** none after August 2026. Before it, Qiu and Gill wrote "Voting Protocols as Coordination Mechanisms for Role-Constrained Multi-Agent Tutoring Systems" (arXiv 2606.08030; title from Semantic Scholar only, not read).
- **Citing papers 2025–2026 worth noting** (Semantic Scholar: 1):
  - **CIVER: Causal Intervention Verification for Reliable Code Review** ([arXiv 2610.36010](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36010); Song et al.; arXiv comment "WWW 2026"; abstract read, verbatim, arXiv API).
    - **Design:** a review module assesses findings, an intervention module generates repair-oriented perturbations and a verifier scores reliability. A finding counts as genuine if it responds to a targeted repair and stays stable under unrelated changes.
    - **Result:** "CIVER correctly identifies 77% of genuine defects through targeted repairs" on SWE-Review-Bench.
    - **Bearing:** it is a learned, intervention-based check on findings, an alternative to an LLM judge. It cites Adversarial Review and Refute-or-Promote as adversarial verification of candidate findings.
- **Challenged?** None found (one citation in three months).

##### Using Agentic AI for contextualized and multifaceted code review at Ericsson
- **Published version / venue:** arXiv comment "Accepted at the 27th International Conference on Product-Focused Software Process Improvement (PROFES 2026)" (arXiv API). Semantic Scholar lists arXiv only, so there is no proceedings DOI yet.
- **Later versions:** v1 only.
- **Code or data:** none (industrial; no link in the full text).
- **Citing papers:** 1, *CodeHealth Predicts the Token Cost of Agentic Code Review* ([arXiv 2610.21914](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21914); abstract read, verbatim, arXiv API). It has a full block under "New items".
- **Challenged?** None found.

##### VulTrial: Let the Trial Begin: A Mock-Court Approach to Vulnerability Detection using LLM-Based Agents
- **Published version / venue:** **ICSE 2026** (Proceedings of the 2026 IEEE/ACM 48th International Conference on Software Engineering), pp. 1030–1042, DOI 10.1145/3744916.3773256, dated 12 Apr 2026 (Crossref API; Semantic Scholar agrees on venue and DOI).
- **Later versions:** v2 (3 Dec 2025) is the latest (arXiv API).
- **Code or data:**
  - **Full-text links:** two figshare private-share links ([1514bc9a…](https://figshare.com/s/1514bc9a7aa64b46d94e) and [69e98e1a…](https://figshare.com/s/69e98e1a3432302e24c7)); not opened tonight.
  - **ALIBI** (below) refers to VulTrial's "released codebase".
  - **GitHub:** a repository `TitanCAProject/VulTrial` exists (created 14 Dec 2025, no description). TitanCA shares the first author. Not opened further.
- **Same-author follow-ups** (Semantic Scholar list for Ratnadira Widyasari):
  - **MetaVul: Agentic Harness Discovery for Vulnerability Detection** ([arXiv 2610.40721](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40721); Ceka, Garg, Lutz, Zhang, **Widyasari**, Kaiser and Ray; abstract read, verbatim, arXiv API).
    - **Design:** it keeps **one** detector agent and evolves its harness. A 202-pair validation set is used for optimisation and a 430-pair **held-out** set is kept back.
    - **Results:** "up to 23.3 percentage points on the validation set and up to 25.8 points on the held-out set". Harnesses transfer across models, "improving held-out pairwise accuracy by 14.7 points".
    - **Framing:** its related work (Semantic Scholar context) sets it against "GPTLens, VulTrial and ConfColl": "MetaVul keeps a single detector agent and instead optimizes its harness".
    - **Bearing:** a VulTrial co-author's next step was evolving one agent's harness against a held-out set rather than adding roles. This is close to the project's evolutionary loop (see "New items").
  - **Titles only, not read:** "TitanCA: Lessons from Orchestrating LLM Agents to Discover 100+ CVEs" (arXiv 2604.17860) and "To Think or Not to Think: Evaluating LLM Reasoning and Agents in Vulnerability Detection" (ASE 2026 per Semantic Scholar; no arXiv ID). The second may bear on whether agentic set-ups help.
- **Citing papers 2025–2026 worth noting** (Semantic Scholar: 36 citing, 34 from 2025–2026; abstracts read verbatim via the arXiv API unless stated):
  - **What Survives the Next Model? Benchmarking LLM-Based Techniques Against Single-Prompts** ([arXiv 2609.00468](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00468); Salsabil et al. with Dwyer and Elbaum; full text partly read: sections IV, V-B and Table II, via curl of the arXiv HTML).
    - **Design:** it re-runs 35 ICSE 2026 technique papers against a single automatically generated prompt on Claude Sonnet 4.6. **VulTrial is one of them**: it is entry [EP28] in its list of evaluated papers.
    - **Table II row for VulTrial:** "GPT-4o PrimeVul PC 18.62 13.37 22.17 +". That is, VulTrial's reported pair-correct figure was 18.62. The basic single prompt (P_b) got 13.37 and the single prompt carrying VulTrial's method as reasoning guidance (P_w) got 22.17, marked "+".
    - **Caveat on the mapping:** the arXiv HTML's in-text reference [44] renders as an empty bibliography item. I matched it to VulTrial through the 18.62 figure (VulTrial's GPT-4o pair-correct, which Cichoń and Dmitruk also quote as 18.6) and through Semantic Scholar's PDF-extracted context, which lists "[EP28]" among the five "structured reasoning" papers a single prompt outperformed. Treat the match as very likely but not confirmed from the PDF.
    - **Bearing:** this is the only direct re-test of VulTrial found. A plain single prompt on a newer model did *worse* than the four-agent court (13.37 against 18.62), and a single prompt that described the court's method did better (22.17).
  - **A Function-Level Vulnerability Score Measures Flag Rate More Than the Model** ([arXiv 2609.32890](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.32890); Cichoń and Dmitruk; NeurIPS 2026 Trust-AI-Eval workshop per the arXiv comment).
    - **Findings:** on paired benchmarks, "Function-level F1 follows how often a model flags both functions of a pair (Spearman +0.86 over 42 combinations) and is nearly unrelated to pair-level correctness (+0.16)". "For 37 of 68 models the difference between correct and reversed pairs is within its 95% interval of zero."
    - **Bearing:** its citation context quotes VulTrial's "four-agent GPT-4o 18.6 on 435 PrimeVul pairs". It is a **measurement critique** of the benchmark set-up VulTrial reports on, not a re-run of VulTrial.
  - **MAVUL** (2510.00317; covered) criticises VulTrial's scoring in a citation context (Semantic Scholar only). It says VulTrial's review board "simply treats predicted samples that meet a pre-set urgency level (e.g., high-level, fixed immediately) as vulnerable when calculating metrics, without considering the correctness of the…" (the context is truncated there).
  - **Phoenix: Security Is Relative** ([arXiv 2604.19012](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.19012); Wang and Huang).
    - **Design:** a slicer, a requirement reverse-engineer and a "Contract Judge", on PrimeVul Paired.
    - **Results:** "F1 = 0.825 and Pair-Correct = 64.4%, surpassing RASM-Vul (F1 = 0.668) and VulTrial (F1 = 0.563) while using open-source models up to 48x smaller".
    - **Label noise:** "18% of 'False Positives' identify genuine security concerns in patched code". This is a warning for any known-label key, the project's included.
  - **ReasonVul: Three Heads Are Better Than One** ([arXiv 2605.18153](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.18153); PACMSE per Semantic Scholar).
    - **Design:** three agents, each with a different reasoning mode, then a debate.
    - **Results:** "PairAcc of 40.00% … surpassing the best baseline by 81.24% in PairAcc"; "542 conflict cases … 389 were correctly resolved".
    - **Bearing:** it supports independent review followed by judged conflict resolution.
  - **SBVUL** ([arXiv 2611.06135](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06135); Pinzger et al.).
    - **Design:** candidate-then-confirm with two agents. Sonnet 5.5 and Opus 5.5 are among the models.
    - **Results:** "all models also confirm candidates in 23–61% of the patched functions"; "SBVul with Opus 5.5 classifies 42.0% of the pairs correctly, compared with 33.8% reported for the best previous approach" (against reported numbers, per its context).
  - **Co-RedTeam** ([arXiv 2602.02164](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.02164)). It re-runs VulTrial as a baseline; the abstract says it "consistently outperforms strong baselines". Its context (Semantic Scholar only) says "VulTrail [sic] and RepoAudit exhibit notably weaker performance … as they do not incorporate execution feedback".
  - **ALIBI** ([arXiv 2607.24964](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.24964); Wu and Nita-Rotaru).
    - **Design:** a coding agent writes adversarial code comments meant to evade four detectors "ranging from specialized open-weight reasoning models to frontier multi-agent systems". VulTrial is one of them (per the context).
    - **Results:** "attack success rates exceed 90% across 125 real-world null-pointer dereference vulnerabilities, reaching 100% on one system". It reports that "architectural isolation and pre-detector comment sanitization substantially improve resilience".
    - **Bearing:** comments in seeded code can steer reviewers. That matters for how the project plants seeds and for its `CLAUDE.md` bias arm.
  - **VulAgent** ([arXiv 2509.11523](https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.11523)). Perspective-specialised agents plus hypothesis validation; it "reduces the false positive rate by about 36%" against baselines that include VulTrial (VulTrial's results are cited from its paper, per the context).
- **Challenged?** **Partly.**
  1. *What Survives the Next Model?* re-tested it: a single guided prompt on Sonnet 4.6 beat it (22.17 against 18.62 pair-correct), but a plain single prompt didn't (13.37).
  2. Phoenix and ReasonVul report higher numbers on the same benchmark.
  3. Cichoń and Dmitruk show that function-level scores on paired benchmarks mostly measure flag rate.
  4. MAVUL questions how VulTrial turned urgency levels into labels.
  5. Co-RedTeam reports VulTrial as weak when re-run.
  6. ALIBI shows it is easily steered by code comments.
  - **Not re-tested:** no one has re-tested its role ablation (moderator removal drops 96 → 32) or its "one debate round is best" finding.

##### GPTLens and MAVUL (lighter block)
- **GPTLens:**
  - **Venue:** 2023 5th IEEE TPS-ISA, pp. 297–306, DOI 10.1109/TPS-ISA58951.2023.00044 (Semantic Scholar); arXiv v2 (16 Oct 2023).
  - **Code:** [git-disl/GPTLens](https://github.com/git-disl/GPTLens) (public, 115 stars).
  - **Citations:** 149.
  - **Challenged?** MetaVul and Refute-or-Promote set their designs against its auditor–critic pairing (contexts), but nothing found re-tests it.
- **MAVUL:**
  - **Venue:** 2025 IEEE 7th TPS-ISA, pp. 314–324, DOI 10.1109/TPS-ISA67132.2025.00040 (Semantic Scholar); arXiv v1 only.
  - **Code:** [youpengl/MAVUL](https://github.com/youpengl/MAVUL) (public, 7 stars).
  - **Citations:** 8.
  - **Challenged?** Refute-or-Promote (2604.19049, covered) says in a context that MAVUL and similar systems "treat proposer output as a claim to verify or refine rather than falsify, none use cross-family reviewers". *Verify Before You Fix* (2604.10800; context only) groups it with self-refinement systems whose "corrections remain within the model's own reasoning space". These are design critiques, not re-tests.

##### MultiVer and CodeX-Verify (lighter block)
- **MultiVer** (2602.17875):
  - **Venue and code:** arXiv v1 only; no code link in the full text.
  - **Citations:** 2. *Strategic Heterogeneous Multi-Agent Architecture for Cost-Effective Code Vulnerability Detection* ([arXiv 2604.21282](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.21282); AAMAS 2026 SE workshop; abstract read, verbatim) uses three DeepSeek-V3 perspective agents plus a local Qwen3-8B adversarial verifier. "The adversarial verifier significantly improves precision (+10.3 percentage points, p < 1e-6, McNemar's test)", with "77.2% F1 … 62.9% precision and 100% recall at $0.002 per sample" on 262 Juliet samples. The other citer, a RusAutoCon 2026 paper, says MultiVer's role ensemble "raises recall … at the cost of lower precision" (context only).
- **CodeX-Verify** (2511.16708):
  - **Venue and code:** arXiv v3 (3 Dec 2025); code at [ShreshthRajan/codex-verify](https://github.com/ShreshthRajan/codex-verify) (public, 0 stars, last push 6 Nov 2025).
  - **Citations:** 1, by MultiVer (same author).
- **Challenged?** None found for either.

##### OpenCodeReview: Determinism over Non-Determinism for Cost-Effective Agent-Based Code Review
- **Published version / venue:** arXiv only (v2, 11 Aug 2026; Semantic Scholar lists arXiv). No venue in the arXiv comment.
- **Later versions:** v1 (10 Aug 2026) and v2 (11 Aug 2026).
- **Code or data: public and very active.**
  - **The repository:** [alibaba/open-code-review](https://github.com/alibaba/open-code-review) (`gh api`, 17 Nov 2026): 53,010 stars, last push 16 Nov 2026, release v1.13.10 on 15 Nov 2026.
  - **Claude Code support:** the README says it ships **a Claude Code plugin** (`/open-code-review:review` and `/open-code-review:delegate-review`; the delegate mode "use[s] the host agent's configured model"), plus Codex, Cursor and Kimi plugins.
  - **The README's own claim** (read via `gh`): "Compared to general-purpose agents (Claude Code), Open Code Review achieves significantly higher **Precision** and **F1** with the same underlying model, while consuming only **~1/9 of the tokens** … its Recall is lower … a deliberate trade-off favoring precision over noise."
  - **AACR-Bench:** on [Hugging Face](https://huggingface.co/datasets/Alibaba-Aone/aacr-bench) (README).
  - **Bearing:** because it runs inside Claude Code with the host model, it is a ready-made external baseline the project could add next to `/code-review ultra`.
- **Same-author follow-ups** (Semantic Scholar lists for Zhengfeng Li and Guoping Rong):
  - **ConceptRM: The Quest to Mitigate Alert Fatigue through Consensus-Based Purity-Driven Data Cleaning for Reflection Modelling** ([arXiv 2602.20166](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.20166); Yu, Zhang, Guo, Yu, Zhuang, **Rong**, Shen, **Li**, **Wang**, … ; abstract read, verbatim).
    - **Design:** it trains the "reflection model as a filter to intercept false alerts" from noisy user-verification feedback, using co-teaching and consensus across several models.
    - **Results:** "outperforming several state-of-the-art LLM baselines by up to 53.31% on in-domain datasets and 41.67% on out-of-domain datasets".
    - **Timing and bearing:** it came out before OpenCodeReview (Feb 2026), so it is the group's earlier work on the filter. It matters for the project's judge: the group treats the false-alarm filter as a learned component trained on user feedback.
  - **Titles only, not read:** AACR-Bench (covered); "Improving LLM-Based Go Code Review through Issue-List Generation and Context Augmentation" (arXiv 2606.01859); "Does AI Code Review Lead to Code Changes? A Case Study of GitHub Actions" (arXiv 2508.18771, TSE per Semantic Scholar); "Fine-Tuning LLMs to Analyze Multiple Dimensions of Code Review" (arXiv 2509.21170).
- **Citing papers 2025–2026 worth noting** (Semantic Scholar: 1):
  - **Benchmarking and Enhancing Design-Aware Code Review in the Linux Kernel** ([arXiv 2610.21925](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21925); Zhou et al.; abstract read, verbatim).
    - **Design:** DesignLore extracts design decisions from historical review discussions and reuses them; it is tested on a new DesignReviewBench.
    - **Results:** "the strongest baseline achieving an F1 of only 0.213 on design trade-off detection. In comparison, DesignLore achieves an F1 of 0.259".
    - **Bearing:** OpenCodeReview is one of its baselines (context). This is design review, outside the project's bug focus, but it is evidence that injected project history (much as `CLAUDE.md` would be) helps review.
- **Challenged?** None found. Its comparison with Claude Code hasn't been independently re-run.

##### BitsAI-CR (lighter block)
- **Published version / venue:** **FSE 2025 Companion** (Proceedings of the 33rd ACM International Conference on the Foundations of Software Engineering), pp. 274–285, DOI 10.1145/3696630.3728552, dated 23 Jun 2025 (Crossref API). This confirms the venue prior-work-research.md had only from a snippet.
- **Versions and code:** arXiv v1 only; no code (industrial).
- **Citations:** 52 (Semantic Scholar). Citers include CodeHealth (2610.21914), ConceptRM, *Understanding the Limits of Automated Evaluation for Code Review Bots in Practice* (2604.24525) and *Adaptive Code Revision Attacks on AI Pull Request Reviewers* (2610.05399); abstracts of these four read. The last two are under "New items".
- **Challenged?** None found.

##### Lower-relevance items (lighter block)
- **Multi-role Consensus** (2403.14274):
  - **Venue:** published as **IEEE QRS-C 2024**, pp. 1318–1319 (a two-page companion paper), DOI 10.1109/QRS-C63300.2024.00173 (Semantic Scholar). prior-work-research.md gives no venue.
  - **Versions and code:** arXiv v4 (18 May 2024); no code link found.
  - **Citations:** 35. VulAgent cites it as "MuCoLD … tester–developer discussion" (context).
  - **Challenged?** None found.
- **RepoReviewer** (2603.16107): arXiv v1, single author (Peng Zhang). Code at [peng1z/RepoReviewer](https://github.com/peng1z/RepoReviewer) (public, 3 stars, last push 7 Sep 2026). 0 citations.
- **Kamalı et al.** (2605.17548): arXiv v2 (5 Jun 2026). The comment says "Submitted to ACM Transactions on Software Engineering Methodology (TOSEM). A shorter version of this work has been presented at ICSE-JAWs 2026". 1 citation (*Where Accountability Lives*, 2608.15678, context only).
- **DeputyDev** (2509.19708): the arXiv title is "Intuition to Evidence: Measuring AI's True Impact on Developer Productivity" (v1). Its full text links tata1mg/deputydev-auth and deputydev-binary (not opened). 16 citations.

**Addendum to Adversarial Review (OpenReview).** The OpenReview search API (`api2.openreview.net/notes/search`, read via curl) lists two submissions by the same title stem:
- "Adversarial Review: Structured Disagreement for Grounded Agentic Code Review", venue string "ICML 2026 Workshop DL4C Poster" (forum h9UPyo3bbp);
- "Adversarial Review: **Cooperative Code Review through Structured Disagreement**", venue string "AI4GOOD Workshop 2026 Regular" (ICML 2026 AI4GOOD workshop; forum fOHvpLs6zp).

So there is a second workshop version with a different subtitle. I couldn't read either forum or any reviews: the API and the web page both returned an OpenReview "challenge verification" page (HTTP 403). Whether the AI4GOOD version reports different numbers isn't confirmed.

#### Section 2. Role design for reviewer agents

##### MARG: Multi-Agent Review Generation for Scientific Papers
- **Published version / venue:** **still a preprint.** Semantic Scholar lists arXiv only, and the only OpenReview record is the DBLP "CoRR 2024" import (OpenReview search API). No conference or journal version found.
- **Later versions:** v1 only (8 Jan 2024).
- **Code or data:** [allenai/marg-reviewer](https://github.com/allenai/marg-reviewer) (public, 66 stars; `gh api`). Its commit history includes "Add files for paper reproducibility" (14 Nov 2024), a CVE patch (30 Sep 2025) and a merged fix to token estimation (5 Mar 2026). So it is maintained and reproducible in principle.
- **Same-author follow-ups** (Semantic Scholar lists for D'Arcy and Downey, 2025–2026): no follow-up on multi-agent review. Their recent work is on AstaBench (arXiv 2510.21652, ICLR 2026 per Semantic Scholar), the Asta interaction dataset and scientific literature synthesis (titles only; not read).
- **Citing papers 2025–2026 worth noting** (Semantic Scholar: 133 citing; abstracts below read verbatim via the arXiv API):
  - **Does Multi-Agent Debate Improve AI Feedback on Research Papers?** ([arXiv 2607.14713](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14713); Havranek and Irsova; pre-registered on OSF, replication package on Zenodo per the arXiv comment).
    - **Answer:** "Probably not, at least for meta-analyses in economics."
    - **Design:** authors of 44 meta-analyses ranked three identity-masked AI reports on their own paper: a single pass by a frontier model and two multi-agent debate tools. All reports were held to a common length and template.
    - **Results:** "The authors preferred the single pass, by 0.66 rank points over mad-research (95% CI 0.32 to 1.00) and 0.57 over paper-workshop (0.16 to 0.95), though paper-workshop spent roughly thirty times the tokens".
    - **The judge finding:** "Gemini (the judge whose model family wrote none of the reports) would have ranked paper-workshop first in the authors' place, reversing the single-pass preference."
    - **Bearing:** this is a pre-registered failure of multi-agent review against a single pass on a review task. Its outside-family AI judge also disagreed with the human ground truth. It bears on MARG's finding and directly on the project's cross-family judge. See "New items".
  - **Auditing AI peer reviewers: a benchmark on real scientific papers** ([arXiv 2611.17052](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17052); Zubeldia et al.; NeurIPS 2026 per the arXiv comment).
    - **Design:** it plants "objective, localisable errors of four types … at three severity levels" in real papers, then scores detection rate by type and severity plus false positives on clean copies. Twelve reviewer systems were tested, including "two Claude Code configurations" and a multi-agent tool-using reviewer (Skepthical).
    - **Results:** "The choice of model has the largest impact on detection rate: given the same prompt, three frontier LLMs differ by about 50 percentage points". "Tools and scaffolding help when they contribute what the model cannot supply on its own." "False-positive rates are below 10% for most systems".
    - **Bearing:** this is the paper-review twin of the project's planted-bug design. See "New items".
  - **Benchmarking Agentic Review Systems** ([arXiv 2606.19749](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.19749); Nguyen, Hao, Elazar and Tan).
    - **Design:** a perturbation benchmark with injected errors.
    - **Results:** "The strongest configuration (OpenAIReview + GPT-5.5) catches 71.6% of injected errors … The union of detections across six models reaches 83.3% recall, suggesting different models detect different errors".
    - **Bearing:** this is evidence for mixed-model panels on recall.
  - **REMOR** ([arXiv 2505.11718](https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.11718); Taechoyotin and Acuna). It re-runs MARG-S as a baseline. The abstract says REMOR models "achieve more than twice the average rewards of human reviews, non-reasoning state-of-the-art agentic multi-modal AI review systems, and general commercial LLM baselines". Its contexts (Semantic Scholar) say "reasoning models outperform non-reasoning agentic AI systems such as MARG-S" and that such agents "spend 20 minutes or more creating the review, using millions of tokens, many of them solely for coordinating".
  - **DIAGPaper** ([arXiv 2601.07611](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.07611); Zou et al.). It uses MARG as a baseline and adds three modules:
    - a customiser that "instantiates multiple reviewer agents with criterion-specific expertise";
    - a **rebuttal module** in which "author agents … engage in structured debate with reviewer agents to validate and refine proposed weaknesses";
    - a prioritiser that "assess[es] the severity of validated weaknesses and surfaces the top-K severest ones".
    - **Bearing:** this is the closest paper-review analogue found to the project's judge-plus-appeal-plus-severity pipeline. See "New items".
  - **Automatic Reviewers Fail to Detect Faulty Reasoning in Research Papers** ([arXiv 2508.21422](https://arxiv.org/abs/2508.21422); Dycke and Gurevych; TACL 2026 per the arXiv comment). In a counterfactual framework, "flaws in research logic have no significant effect on their output reviews", across "a range of ARG approaches". Whether MARG is among them wasn't checked.
- **Challenged?** **Partly.**
  1. REMOR reports that single reasoning models outperform MARG-S at far lower cost.
  2. Havranek and Irsova's pre-registered study found a single pass preferred over multi-agent debate tools. MARG itself wasn't tested.
  3. Dycke and Gurevych show that automatic reviewers (possibly including MARG) ignore injected logic flaws.
  - **Not re-tested:** no one re-tests MARG's own specialists-against-generic ablation.

##### ChatEval: Towards Better LLM-based Evaluators through Multi-Agent Debate
- **Published version / venue:** ICLR 2024 (per the proceedings, as prior-work-research.md says). arXiv still has v1 only (arXiv API). Semantic Scholar lists the venue as arXiv.
- **Ablation now checked in the full text** (arXiv HTML; section 4.1 and Tables 1, 3 and 4 read; fetched by curl). This replaces prior-work-research.md's "from a search summary".
  - **What was varied:** every role prompt was replaced by "You are now an Annotator, one of the referees in the text evaluation task."
  - **Table 3** (FairEval, ChatGPT; one-by-one strategy, 2 agents, 2 turns): Single-Agent 53.8% accuracy (kappa 0.27); Multi-Agent (Same Role Prompt) 53.8% (0.25); Multi-Agent (Diverse Role Prompt) 60% (0.33).
  - **The authors' conclusion:** the same-role version "underperforms that with diverse role prompt design and cannot effectively enhance the performance compared with single-agent setting".
  - **Benchmark size:** FairEval is "a collection of 80 open-ended questions" (full text). So 53.8% against 60% is about 43 against 48 of 80 items, from one run on one model with no interval reported.
  - **Bearing:** the role-diversity effect is real in the paper but rests on about five items. The project should cite it as suggestive only.
- **Code or data:** [chanchimin/ChatEval](https://github.com/chanchimin/ChatEval) (public, 347 stars, last commit 19 Oct 2024).
- **Same-author follow-ups:** the first author's 2025–2026 papers (Semantic Scholar) are on speech and pruning, with none on evaluator panels. Other authors weren't checked.
- **Citing papers 2025–2026 worth noting** (Semantic Scholar: 1,032 citing, 662 from 2025–2026; filtered by keyword, abstracts below read verbatim via the arXiv API):
  - **Stop Overvaluing Multi-Agent Debate — We Must Rethink Evaluation and Embrace Model Heterogeneity** ([arXiv 2502.08788](https://arxiv.org/abs/2502.08788); Zhang et al.; v3). It evaluates "5 representative MAD methods across 9 benchmarks using 4 foundational models": "MAD often fail to outperform simple single-agent baselines such as Chain-of-Thought and Self-Consistency, even when consuming significantly more inference-time computation". Its context (Semantic Scholar) says "SoM, EoT, ChatEval, and AgentVerse only outperformed CoT in approximately 15% cases". It also finds "model heterogeneity … a universal antidote".
  - **Debate or Vote** ([arXiv 2508.17536](https://arxiv.org/abs/2508.17536); Choi, Zhu and Li; NeurIPS 2025 Spotlight per the arXiv comment). "Majority Voting alone accounts for most of the performance gains typically attributed to MAD"; debate "induces a martingale over agents' belief trajectories, implying that debate alone does not improve expected correctness".
  - **Unanimity Without Persuasion** ([arXiv 2609.26145](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.26145); Shu).
    - **Design:** a heterogeneous 7-judge panel judging **code correctness** on 600 candidates.
    - **Results:** "unanimity on a fixed cohort jumps from 39.5% to 95.2% in round 1 … while accuracy moves by less than one point and 96.3% of verdict flips follow the displayed peer majority"; "by round 3 every wrong decision is unanimous, erasing dissent that had flagged two-thirds of the panel's errors".
    - **Its advice:** "verify before any second-pass evaluation or peer exposure, and never treat post-debate unanimity as independent evidence".
    - **Bearing:** this bears directly on the project's debate arm and on what the judge and appeal agents see. See "New items".
  - **Measuring Collapse and Correction in Homogeneous-Panel LLM Debate** ([arXiv 2609.35279](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.35279); NeurIPS 2026 Datasets and Benchmarks per the arXiv comment). On 6,925 MMLU-Pro debates it records "253 collapses together with the corrections that offset them". "Freezing every debate prevents 251 collapses but loses 804 corrections". It argues that final accuracy alone hides both kinds of change.
  - **What Does Multi-Agent Debate Actually Change?** ([arXiv 2609.08016](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08016); Qian). On opinion questions, "the selected option is unchanged from members' initial to final positions in over 90% of comparisons in every configuration" (same-model, same-family and mixed-family committees).
  - **Stopping and Routing LLM Judge Panels** ([arXiv 2608.19802](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.19802); WISE 2026). It sorts candidate judges into "copies add no conditional information, complements improve the global panel, and specialists help only on slices", with the policy "drop copies, add complements globally, route specialists conditionally". It includes code audits. This is relevant to choosing judge and seat composition by measured complementarity.
  - **Principled Personas** ([arXiv 2508.19764](https://arxiv.org/abs/2508.19764); EMNLP 2025). "Expert personas usually lead to positive or non-significant performance changes", but "models are highly sensitive to irrelevant persona details, with performance drops of almost 30 percentage points".
  - **Multi-Agent Debate for LLM Judges with Adaptive Stability Detection** ([arXiv 2510.12697](https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.12697); NeurIPS 2025 per Semantic Scholar). This is a supportive result: debate "amplifies correctness compared to static ensembles" (theory plus experiments).
- **Challenged?** **Yes, for debate in general, and the role-diversity claim is thin.** Stop Overvaluing MAD reports that ChatEval beat CoT in only about 15% of its cases (re-run across 9 benchmarks). Debate or Vote and Unanimity Without Persuasion attribute debate gains to voting and conformity. No paper re-tests ChatEval's same-role ablation specifically. The ablation itself rests on 80 items (above).

##### Agents with Feelings? Personality and Emotion in Multi-Agent Software Teams
- **Published version / venue:** arXiv preprint only (v1, 6 Jul 2026). Semantic Scholar lists arXiv, and the only OpenReview hit is the DBLP "CoRR 2026" import.
- **Code or data:** the full text links a "Companion repository", [personas-matter-llms/companion-artifact](https://github.com/personas-matter-llms/companion-artifact) (public; created 24 Jun 2026; 0 stars). It has `task1_code_generation`, `task2_code_review` (with `prompts`, `data`, `results`, `scripts`, `src` and `analysis`) and `paper_figures` (`gh api`; contents not inspected further).
- **Same-author follow-ups:** none on agent teams. Zimmermann's 2026 papers include "Understanding Dominant Themes in Reviewing Agentic AI-authored Code" ([arXiv 2601.19287](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.19287); Haider and Zimmermann; abstract read, verbatim). It builds "a taxonomy of 12 review comment themes" from 19,450 inline review comments on 3,177 agent-authored PRs and finds that reviews "predominantly focus on documentation gaps, refactoring needs, styling and formatting issues, with testing and security-related concerns". It came out before Agents with Feelings and could inform seat areas.
- **Citing papers:** 0 (Semantic Scholar).
- **Challenged?** None found.

##### Philosophical Dispositions; Pornprasit and Tantithamthavorn; Zheng et al. (lighter block)
- **Philosophical Dispositions** (2605.23108): arXiv v1 only; single author; no code link in the full text; 0 citations. No challenge found.
- **Pornprasit and Tantithamthavorn** (2402.00905):
  - **Venue:** published in **Information and Software Technology, vol. 175, article 107523 (2024)**, DOI 10.1016/j.infsof.2024.107523 (Semantic Scholar; DBLP key `journals/infsof/PornprasitT24`). prior-work-research.md gives no venue.
  - **Versions and code:** arXiv v4 (17 Jun 2024); code at [awsm-research/LLM-for-code-review-automatiton](https://github.com/awsm-research/LLM-for-code-review-automatiton) (public, 6 stars).
  - **Citations:** 128. One 2026 industrial C# study repeats its recommendation that "few-shot learning without a persona is the most effective prompting approach" (context only).
  - **Challenged?** None found.
- **Zheng et al., personas in system prompts** (2311.10054):
  - **Venue and code:** Findings of EMNLP 2024 (arXiv comment); v3 (9 Oct 2024); code at [Jiaxin-Pei/Prompting-with-Social-Roles](https://github.com/Jiaxin-Pei/Prompting-with-Social-Roles) (public, 52 stars).
  - **Citations:** 95, mostly repeating its null result.
  - **Partial counter-evidence:**
    - *Principled Personas* (above): expert personas are "usually … positive or non-significant".
    - *A Large-Scale Evaluation of Role-Enhanced Zero-Shot Chain-of-Thought Prompting* ([arXiv 2610.29073](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29073); abstract read, verbatim). Across "20 LLMs and seven benchmarks", task-aligned expert roles plus zero-shot CoT give "improvements of up to 44 percentage points", and "unconventional roles degrade performance". These are small models (2B–14B), on arithmetic, commonsense and reading tasks.
    - So the null result looks model- and task-dependent rather than overturned. For the project, role labels remain weak evidence either way, which supports testing scope and checklists rather than labels.

##### Tran and Kiela, Single-Agent LLMs Outperform Multi-Agent Systems on Multi-Hop Reasoning Under Equal Thinking Token Budgets
- **Published version / venue:** **still a preprint** (arXiv v2, 11 Apr 2026; Semantic Scholar lists arXiv). No OpenReview record found. One citing paper (*Evaluating Collective Intelligence…*, Semantic Scholar context) says its "cost-accuracy figures … should be treated as preliminary pending peer review of that preprint".
- **Code or data:** no code or data link in the arXiv HTML full text (grep for github, zenodo, huggingface and release statements found only a googleapis issue link about Gemini budget control).
- **Same-author follow-ups:** none relevant (Semantic Scholar: Tran's other 2026 paper is on recommendation systems; Kiela's is BLITZRANK on ranking agents).
- **Citing papers 2025–2026 worth noting** (Semantic Scholar: 51 citing, 50 from 2025–2026; abstracts read verbatim via the arXiv API):
  - **The More the Better? A Comparison of Single- and Multi-Agent LLM Setups on a Financial Review Task** ([arXiv 2610.27488](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27488); Pei Liu). This is **the closest design to the project found in this sweep**; there is a full block under "New items".
    - **Design:** known labels on 300 synthetic memos; "misses and false alarms are measured separately"; Bonferroni-corrected primary predictions on four models, including two Claude models. The team of three splits requirements on the same model.
    - **Results:** the team "catches 0.08 to 0.10 more of the defects" but "raises more false alarms on all four" and uses "2.2 to 2.4 times the tokens". Exploratory: "sampling the single agent three times and flagging on any draw gives higher balanced accuracy than the team".
  - **More Agents, More Samples, or More Thinking?** ([arXiv 2611.17610](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17610); Sajjadi Mohammadabadi). A synthesis of "nine primary empirical evaluations comprising 516 unique multi-agent configurations". Against sample- or budget-matched baselines, "multi-agent systems average -0.042 in accuracy", and "multi-agent accuracy equals or falls below the baseline in 74% of these comparable contrasts". "Positive returns require partitioned evidence, external verification, or error diversity across models." It provides "a reviewer checklist and accounting utilities" (PACE, on GitHub per the arXiv comment).
  - **The Interaction Tax** ([arXiv 2608.23541](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23541); Ann, Liu and Tan; ICML 2026 per the arXiv comment). On "11 verifier-scored optimization tasks under matched budgets", full-solution interaction makes different model families' proposals "converge within one round"; "independent proposal generation avoids this collapse". Its context notes that Tran and Kiela and similar studies "all use same-model teams or do not control for model diversity". This **qualifies** Tran and Kiela rather than contradicting them.
  - **The Illusion of Multi-Agent Advantage** ([arXiv 2606.13003](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13003); Jwalapuram et al.). "Automatic MAS consistently underperform CoT-SC despite being up to 10x more expensive", but "expert-architected MAS consistently outperforms automatically generated architectures" on a synthetic dataset built for decomposition. Its caution about automated design is relevant to an evolutionary search over councils.
  - **Attention Tax, Handoff Tax** ([arXiv 2610.06069](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.06069)). A stylised model with two crossover conditions; it predicts and confirms that decomposition wins at depth 20, 50 and 100 on a ledger task. It explicitly reconciles Tran and Kiela's result as one regime.
  - **When Do Multi-Agent Systems Help? An Information Bottleneck Perspective** ([arXiv 2607.16133](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.16133)). "MAS consistently helps when relays are near-sufficient, especially for weaker models"; gains "shrink or reverse … especially for stronger models".
  - **When Does LLM Orchestration Pay Off?** ([arXiv 2608.00685](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.00685); Leins et al.). With optimisation effort matched (GEPA), the largest gain is "4.6 percentage points over optimized CoT", at "approximately 2 to 4 times the mean total tokens", with "strong interactions between orchestration method and backbone model".
  - **When Independent Sampling Outperforms Agentic Reasoning** ([arXiv 2605.08478](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.08478)). On 216 Codeforces problems, "k-shot consistently achieves a better accuracy-cost and accuracy-query tradeoff".
- **Challenged?** **No direct failure to reproduce found.** Later work mostly extends it: the financial review task, the 516-configuration synthesis and repeated sampling on code. Two theory papers (Attention Tax and the information-bottleneck paper) bound it to a regime (strong models, lossless context) rather than refuting it. The main qualification, from the Interaction Tax, is that it used same-model teams.

##### Kim et al., Towards a Science of Scaling Agent Systems
- **Published version / venue: now published in Nature Machine Intelligence** under a new title, **"Capable language models can outgrow the benefits of collaboration"**.
  - **Bibliographic details:** vol. 8, issue 7, pp. 1157–1172, dated 24 Jul 2026, DOI [10.1038/s42256-026-01268-y](https://doi.org/10.1038/s42256-026-01268-y), 20 authors with Yubin Kim first (Crossref API, read; cited-by count 9).
  - **Corroboration:** the code README (read via `gh`) says the same and that "The arXiv preprint retains the earlier title".
  - **Not yet in the metadata elsewhere:** Semantic Scholar and the arXiv entry don't yet show the journal version.
  - **Correction:** prior-work-research.md should cite the journal version.
- **Later versions:** arXiv v3 (8 Apr 2026). Its abstract (arXiv API, verbatim) still says "260 configurations spanning six agentic benchmarks", with the "capability-saturation effect", "architectures without centralized verification tend to propagate errors more", relative change "from +80.8% … to -70.0%" and "87% of held-out configurations".
- **Code or data:** [ybkim95/agent-scaling](https://github.com/ybkim95/agent-scaling) (public, 63 stars; releases up to v2.2.3, 12 Oct 2026). Its README has a **note on reproducibility**: "The analysis scripts in the v2.1.3 release built 270 configurations by default, including ten `gemini-3-flash-preview` runs … and so did not reproduce the regression values given in the article. Releases v2.2.x correct the default to the 260 configurations the article analyses". The Zenodo concept DOI is 10.5281/zenodo.20144433.
- **Same-author follow-up:** **TeamBench: Evaluating Agent Coordination under Enforced Role Separation** ([arXiv 2605.07073](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.07073); Yubin Kim et al. with Xin Liu and McDuff; abstract read, verbatim).
  - **Design:** Planner, Executor and Verifier roles with OS-enforced separation.
  - **Results:** "Prompt-only and sandbox-enforced teams reach statistically indistinguishable pass rates, but prompt-only runs produce 3.6 times more cases where the verifier attempts to edit the executor's code. **Verifiers approve 49% of submissions that fail the deterministic grader, and removing the verifier improves mean partial score in the ablation.** … Teams benefit when single agents struggle, but hurt when single agents already perform well."
  - **Bearing:** this is direct same-group evidence that an LLM verifier (judge) can be worse than none. It is relevant to the project's judge and to measuring judge accuracy against the key.
- **Citing papers 2025–2026 worth noting** (Semantic Scholar: 180 citing; filtered; abstracts read verbatim):
  - **Does Multi-Agent Collaboration Pay When Agents Serve Users? Testing Agent Scaling in Dual-Control Settings** ([arXiv 2610.27655](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27655); Yang and Shi; pre-registered).
    - **What it tests:** Kim et al.'s rule, "coordination helps while the SAS is weak and hurts once it is strong, with the crossing near 45% single-agent accuracy", on τ²-bench with six models from three families.
    - **Results:** "the MAS is worse in 12 of the 13 valid cells and tied in the thirteenth … (weighted mean -28 points, 95% CI [-44, -12]). On the two cells where the rule predicts a benefit, the MAS loses by 13 and 22 points".
    - **Its own caveat:** "one validity bar unmet (its planner never saw the tool schemas)".
  - **When does a scaling result justify a different allocation? A critical review of resource-allocation evidence for AI systems** ([arXiv 2609.14500](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.14500); Emadi). It names three recurring mismatches: "success counted before an answer is chosen, information a deployed system will not have, and costs left out of the comparison". It applies a "resource envelope" "to a published comparison". Which comparison isn't stated in the abstract; Kim et al. is among its citations. A useful checklist for the project's cost reporting.
  - **Candidate supply and answer selection shape the value of LLM judging in multi-agent systems** ([arXiv 2608.25937](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.25937)). "A correct answer is often already present among the generated candidates, but the system can still converge on and report a wrong answer". "Judge reliability is not a fixed trait of the model, but varies with the task, the generator and how rare the correct answer is". Combining frequency with the judge "raised accuracy from 63.82% to 70.82-70.95%, primarily by rescuing correct answers that were outnumbered by popular errors". This bears on the appeal agent's job of rescuing minority findings.
  - **Precise but Uncoupled: Reviewer Precision Does Not Guarantee Critique Uptake** ([arXiv 2607.15388](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15388)). "PER's reviewer is more precise than broadcast's (0.861 vs. 0.644), yet evaluator-verified useful critique is much less likely to change the next candidate"; "reviewer-centric evaluation can overstate system quality".
  - **Mo' Models, Mo' Problems** ([arXiv 2609.17306](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.17306); REALM 2026 at EMNLP 2026 per the arXiv comment). "Expanding candidate pool sizes often degrades performance below that of the top performing base-model … candidate selection within a single model family is the strategy that yields the best relative performance". This bears on the copies-against-mixed-families arm.
  - **EvoTeam: Multi-Agent Collaboration via Evolving Teams** ([arXiv 2610.39813](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39813)). It "jointly evolves team organization, interaction rules, and individual agent skills through execution experience" and keeps "a library of reusable teams". Code is on GitHub per the abstract. Overlaps the project's evolution of council configurations, on general benchmarks rather than review. See "New items".
- **Challenged?** **Yes, on transfer.** Yang and Shi's pre-registered test finds Kim et al.'s capability-saturation crossing rule doesn't predict where MAS helps in user-facing tool tasks. The authors' own release history shows a script default (270 against 260 configurations) that didn't reproduce the article's regression until v2.2.x. The headline direction (strong single agents leave little room) is corroborated by TeamBench and the citing work above.

##### Jung and Na; ReviewAgents; Self-MoA; DyLAN; AgentInit (lighter block)
- **Jung and Na** (2604.00477): v2 (10 Oct 2026); "Accepted to COLM 2026" (arXiv comment); no code link in the full text; 0 citations.
- **ReviewAgents** (2503.08506): v3 (16 Jul 2025), still marked "Work in progress"; no code link in the full text; 34 citations. None re-tests its finding that three reviewers are best.
- **Self-MoA** (2502.00674):
  - **Venue:** Semantic Scholar now gives **Transactions on Machine Learning Research (vol. 2026)** under the title "Rethinking Mixture-of-Agents: Is Mixing Different Large Language Models Beneficial?". prior-work-research.md gives no venue. Not checked on OpenReview.
  - **Versions and code:** arXiv v1 only; no code link in the full text.
  - **Citations:** 70.
  - **Mixed later evidence:**
    - *When Does Combining Language Models Help?* ([arXiv 2606.27288](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.27288); 67 models, abstract read): "At matched quality, low-rho heterogeneous ensembles beat high-rho Self-MoA, but on checkable tasks … combining models rarely beats the single best model without a strong query-level routing signal".
    - *Making, not Taking, the Best of N* ([arXiv 2510.00931](https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.00931)): its context (Semantic Scholar only) says "sampling from a single teacher—the proposed solution by Li et al. [2025a]—performs significantly worse" in its generative setting.
    - *Stop Overvaluing MAD* (above) argues heterogeneity is "a universal antidote".
    - *Mo' Models* (above) supports staying within one family.
    - **So:** the copies-against-mix question is open and the answer depends on the task. That supports keeping it as an arm.
- **DyLAN** (2310.02170): COLM 2024 (arXiv comment); code at [SALT-NLP/DyLAN](https://github.com/SALT-NLP/DyLAN) (public, 221 stars); 394 citations.
- **AgentInit** (2509.19236): Findings of EMNLP 2025 (arXiv comment; DBLP key `conf/emnlp/TianWLWDZZ25`); code at [1737423697/AgentInit](https://github.com/1737423697/AgentInit) (public, 12 stars); 20 citations.
- **Challenge to adaptive team selection in general:** *Superficial Success vs. Internal Breakdown* ([arXiv 2604.18951](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.18951); ACL per Semantic Scholar; abstract read) finds "topological overfitting — they fail to generalize across different domains" and "illusory coordination" in adaptive MAS. It is a warning for any evolved council, and an argument for the project's held-out seeds. Whether DyLAN and AgentInit are among the systems tested isn't stated in the abstract.
- **Related team-selection work:** *Which Models Work Well Together?* ([arXiv 2609.38274](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38274)) selects teams by error decorrelation and quality. *Who Should Be on the Panel?* ([arXiv 2611.18150](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18150); ICAIF 2026 workshop) finds that "neither more seats nor the manager's reading of the arguments substitutes for selecting the right ones" (trading, not review).

#### New items met along the way

None of these arXiv IDs is in the list of arXiv IDs already cited in the repo (checked by grep). The first eight are the most important.

##### Financial review team: The More the Better? A Comparison of Single- and Multi-Agent LLM Setups on a Financial Review Task
- **Citation:** Pei Liu; arXiv preprint, v1 (Oct 2026); [arXiv 2610.27488](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27488).
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API); full text partly read (results, appendix notes on labels, models and settings; arXiv HTML via curl).
- **What it did:**
  - **Task:** reviewing 300 synthetic credit memos against six requirements, with known labels. "Misses and false alarms are measured separately".
  - **Arms:** one agent checking all six requirements against a "team of three on the same model … two requirements each, in the same words, with no roles or later stage". A "scoped team", added after the first results, shows each agent the requirements it wasn't given.
  - **Models:** Qwen3.5 9B and 27B (Ollama) and Claude Haiku 5.5 and Sonnet 5.5 (Batch API, effort high).
  - **Labels:** accepted when three sources agreed: the generator, a separate checking program and **Claude Opus 5.5**. The author notes "Opus 5.5 belongs to the family of two of the tested models and is not one of them."
- **Key numbers:**
  - **Catches:** "the team catches 0.08 to 0.10 more of the defects that carry an explanation on the three models whose single agent misses some, at the corrected level on two."
  - **False alarms and cost:** "It raises more false alarms on all four, at the corrected level on three. Balanced accuracy … changes by 0.02 or less, and the team uses 2.2 to 2.4 times the tokens."
  - **Scoped team:** it "reduces the added false alarms on the two Claude models, at the corrected level on one."
  - **Repeated sampling:** exploratory: "sampling the single agent three times and flagging on any draw gives higher balanced accuracy than the team on those models".
  - **Signal detection:** the team's d′ "does not change significantly on any of the three models and its c falls on all three". In other words, the team lowers the flagging threshold without discriminating better.
  - **Misfiled flags:** on Haiku 5.5 most of the team's false alarms "quote a sentence that is defective under another requirement", so the false-alarm cost is "probably overstated".
- **Connection: overlaps strongly.** It is the closest design to the project found in this sweep:
  - specialised split-scope seats against one generalist on the same model;
  - planted defects with known labels;
  - misses and false alarms scored separately;
  - token cost;
  - pre-specified predictions with Bonferroni correction;
  - Claude models, with a same-family labelling caveat.
  - It **supports** the project's equal-cost rule (three draws of a single agent beat the team) and its precision concern.
  - It also gives a concrete mechanism: splitting scope lowers the threshold rather than raising discrimination, and false alarms are often "misfiled" true defects under another seat's scope. That suggests the judge should re-file findings across seats rather than dismiss them.
- **Novelty threat: medium.** It is narrow (one synthetic document-review task, no judge, appeal or evolution) but it pre-empts the "do specialist seats beat one generalist at equal cost?" sub-question with a careful design.

##### MAD for paper feedback: Does Multi-Agent Debate Improve AI Feedback on Research Papers?
- **Citation:** Tomas Havranek and Zuzana Irsova; arXiv preprint (Jul 2026); [arXiv 2607.14713](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14713). Pre-registered on OSF; replication package on Zenodo (arXiv comment).
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API).
- **What it did:** in a pre-registered, identity-masked, within-paper experiment, the authors of 44 meta-analyses ranked three AI reports on their own paper: one frontier-model single pass and two multi-agent debate tools. All were held to a common length and template.
- **Key numbers:**
  - **Preference:** "The authors preferred the single pass, by 0.66 rank points over mad-research (95% CI 0.32 to 1.00) and 0.57 over paper-workshop (0.16 to 0.95), though paper-workshop spent roughly thirty times the tokens".
  - **AI judges against the real referee report:** "three AI judges almost always placed the real journal referee report last".
  - **Outside-family judge:** "Gemini (the judge whose model family wrote none of the reports) would have ranked paper-workshop first in the authors' place, reversing the single-pass preference."
- **Connection: challenges** the assumption that multi-agent review beats one pass, with human ground truth. It also warns that an **outside-family AI judge** can reverse the human verdict. That bears on the project's cross-family matching judge, which should be validated against human or key labels.
- **Novelty threat: low.** Paper review, perceived usefulness and no planted bugs.

##### Paper-review planted errors: Auditing AI peer reviewers: a benchmark on real scientific papers
- **Citation:** Íñigo Zubeldia, Boris Bolliet, Francisco Villaescusa-Navarro, Pablo Villanueva-Domingo and Miles Cranmer; NeurIPS 2026 (arXiv comment); [arXiv 2611.17052](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17052).
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API).
- **What it did:** it injects "objective, localisable errors of four types (general issues, mathematics, numerics, citations) at three severity levels into real papers". It measures detection by type and severity, plus false-positive rate on clean copies, for twelve reviewer systems, "two Claude Code configurations" among them.
- **Key numbers:** 357 injected errors in 30 preprints. "Given the same prompt, three frontier LLMs differ by about 50 percentage points in total detection". "False-positive rates are below 10% for most systems". On astrophysics, "Claude Code improves on a single Claude call in mathematics and numerics detection".
- **Connection: overlaps** with the project's seeding method (planted errors with severity, clean-copy false-alarm rate and Claude Code configurations) in a different domain. It **supports** the finding that model choice dominates scaffolding.
- **Novelty threat: low–medium** for the method. It isn't code and it has no council or evolution.

##### Debate collapse on code correctness: Unanimity Without Persuasion: A Single Round of Debate Erases the Disagreement That Verification Needs
- **Citation:** Yang Shu; arXiv preprint (Sep 2026); [arXiv 2609.26145](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.26145).
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API).
- **What it did:** it tracked a heterogeneous 7-judge panel through a blind round and three debate rounds on 600 code-correctness candidates, with execution-based verification and identical-cohort controls.
- **Key numbers:**
  - **Collapse:** "unanimity … jumps from 39.5% to 95.2% in round 1 … while accuracy moves by less than one point and 96.3% of verdict flips follow the displayed peer majority".
  - **Lost dissent:** "by round 3 every wrong decision is unanimous, erasing dissent that had flagged two-thirds of the panel's errors".
