  - **Controls:** "real labels without reasoning reproduce 91.5%" of the collapse.
  - **Robustness:** it holds for panel sizes 3–7.
- **Connection: challenges** debate before judging. It **supports** the project's independent review followed by a judge, and suggests the appeal agent should see the pre-debate dissent.
- **Novelty threat: low.**

##### MetaVul: Agentic Harness Discovery for Vulnerability Detection
- **Citation:** Ira Ceka, Arnav Garg, Ryan Lutz, Ting Zhang, Ratnadira Widyasari, Gail Kaiser and Baishakhi Ray; arXiv preprint (arXiv comment "Originally announced September 2026"); [arXiv 2610.40721](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40721).
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API).
- **What it did:** a self-improving loop over a single vulnerability-detection agent's harness. It searches harness history with trajectories, logs and metrics, and returns Pareto-optimal harnesses. It is optimised on 202 validation pairs and tested on 430 held-out pairs.
- **Key numbers:** "up to 23.3 percentage points on the validation set and up to 25.8 points on the held-out set"; transfer to unseen models "improving held-out pairwise accuracy by 14.7 points".
- **Connection: overlaps** with the project's generational evolution, its held-out seeds against gaming and its known-label scoring. It evolves one agent's harness, not a council's composition, and it has no statistical keep-or-revert rule in the abstract.
- **Novelty threat: medium.** It is the nearest "evolve the review harness against labelled code with a held-out set" paper found. The project's distinguishing features are the council structure, the judge and appeal and the CI-gated acceptance rule.

##### DIAGPaper: Diagnosing Valid and Specific Weaknesses in Scientific Papers via Multi-Agent Reasoning
- **Citation:** Zhuoyang Zou, Abolfazl Ansari, Delvin Ce Zhang, Dongwon Lee and Wenpeng Yin; arXiv preprint, v2; [arXiv 2601.07611](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.07611).
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API).
- **What it did:** three modules. A customiser instantiates "reviewer agents with criterion-specific expertise". A rebuttal module has "author agents that engage in structured debate with reviewer agents to validate and refine proposed weaknesses". A prioritiser "assess[es] the severity of validated weaknesses and surfaces the top-K severest ones". It is evaluated on AAAR and ReviewCritique.
- **Key numbers:** none in the abstract beyond "substantially outperforms existing methods" (MARG is among its baselines per a Semantic Scholar context).
- **Connection: overlaps** with the judge, appeal and severity ranking. Here the rebuttal plays the defence against reviewer findings, the inverse of the project's appeal against the judge's dismissals. It reviews papers, not code, and has no planted key.
- **Novelty threat: low–medium.**

##### What Survives the Next Model? Benchmarking LLM-Based Techniques Against Single-Prompts
- **Citation:** Nahian Salsabil, Joy Saha, Simantika Bhattacharjee Dristi, Nicholas Phair, Nusrat Jahan Mozumder, Matthew B. Dwyer and Sebastian Elbaum; arXiv preprint (Sep 2026); [arXiv 2609.00468](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00468). Code: github.com/less-lab-uva/What-Survives-the-Next-Model (per the abstract).
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API); full text partly read (sections IV and V, Table II; arXiv HTML).
- **What it did:** it re-ran 35 ICSE 2026 LLM-technique papers' evaluations against a single auto-generated prompt on Claude Sonnet 4.6, in two forms: a basic prompt (P_b) and one carrying the paper's method as guidance (P_w).
- **Key numbers:** "for between 37% and 63% papers, a newer model with a single prompt natively outperforms the heavily engineered tooling". For VulTrial: original 18.62 pair-correct against 13.37 for P_b and 22.17 for P_w (see the VulTrial block, mapping caveat there).
- **Connection: supports** the project's single-reviewer baselines and argues for re-running baselines on the current model. It is relevant to the threat that a council's gain is a "workaround to temporary model deficits".
- **Novelty threat: none.**

##### TeamBench: Evaluating Agent Coordination under Enforced Role Separation
- **Citation:** Yubin Kim, Chanwoo Park, Taehan Kim et al. (12 authors, with Xin Liu, Hamid Palangi and Daniel McDuff); arXiv preprint (May 2026); [arXiv 2605.07073](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.07073).
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API).
- **What it did:** 851 task templates and 931 seeded instances, with Planner, Executor and Verifier roles separated by OS-level access controls, compared against prompt-only separation, plus a 40-session human study.
- **Key numbers:** "Verifiers approve 49% of submissions that fail the deterministic grader, and removing the verifier improves mean partial score in the ablation"; "prompt-only runs produce 3.6 times more cases where the verifier attempts to edit the executor's code".
- **Connection: challenges** the assumption that an LLM verifier or judge adds value. It supports measuring the judge's own accuracy against the key and keeping a no-judge (pooled) arm.
- **Novelty threat: none** (not review).

##### CodeHealth Predicts the Token Cost of Agentic Code Review: A Pre-Registered Study of 465 Agent-Authored Pull Requests
- **Citation:** Lo Gullstrand Heander, Markus Borg, Emma Söderberg and Christofer Rydenfält; arXiv preprint (Oct 2026); [arXiv 2610.21914](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21914). Data: doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.22127920.
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API).
- **What it did:** a pre-registered observational study. A "frozen five-agent review-and-fix pipeline processed every PR with three different LLMs"; mixed models of log tokens on CodeHealth and PR footprint.
- **Key numbers:** "each CodeHealth point was associated with 3.5% to 6.1% lower token cost"; "Adjusting for the size of the scored files absorbed the association".
- **Connection: loosely related.** Code quality (file size) of the reviewed code drives council token cost, so cost comparisons across seed sets should control for file size.
- **Novelty threat: none.**

##### CRJudgeBench: Can AI Detect Plausible but Invalid Code Reviews?
- **Citation:** Yue Pan, Jiawei Li, Ziyuan Zhang, Xiangxin Zhao and He Ye; arXiv preprint, under review at ICLR 2027 (arXiv comment); [arXiv 2609.37216](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.37216). Dataset on Hugging Face (dcloud347/CRJudgeBenchmark).
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API).
- **What it did:** 1,199 instances from real PRs plus expert-verified perturbations, each a trustworthy or "plausible but untrustworthy" review comment. Sentinel is a trained repository-grounded agentic judge.
- **Key numbers:** "Sentinel achieves 76.60% accuracy, outperforming GLM-5.3 by 6.13 percentage points and its base model by 19.78 points"; "even state-of-the-art general-purpose LLMs struggle to identify untrustworthy comments".
- **Connection: supports and overlaps** with the judge role. It is a ready benchmark for how well a judge rules a finding real or not.
- **Novelty threat: low.**

##### Adaptive Code Revision Attacks on AI Pull Request Reviewers
- **Citation:** Jingzhi Gong, Jie M. Zhang, Gunel Jahangirova and Meng Wang; arXiv preprint (Oct 2026); [arXiv 2610.05399](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.05399).
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API).
- **What it did:** an attacker uses the reviewer's feedback to repair reported problems while keeping a vulnerability. AFCRA-Bench has 159 disclosed vulnerabilities with executable exploits.
- **Key numbers:** "Across five-round interactions with Sonnet 5 and GPT-5.5 reviewers, AFCRA reaches success rates 2.5x and 12.5x those of the strongest evaluated text- or comment-based attack".
- **Connection: loosely related.** It is a seed-design risk: in sequential or iterative review arms, reviewers accept fixes to what they reported and miss surviving bugs.
- **Novelty threat: none.**

##### Understanding the Limits of Automated Evaluation for Code Review Bots in Practice
- **Citation:** Veli Karakaya, Utku Boran Torun, Baykal Mehmet Uçar and Eray Tüzün; EASE 2026 (arXiv comment); [arXiv 2604.24525](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.24525).
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API).
- **What it did:** G-Eval and an LLM-as-judge pipeline were compared with 2,604 developer fixed/wontFix labels at Beko, across Gemini-2.5-pro, GPT-4.1-mini and GPT-5.2.
- **Key numbers:** "Agreement ratios range from approximately 0.44 to 0.62".
- **Connection: supports** the project's choice of planted keys over developer-action labels and LLM-judge usefulness scores.
- **Novelty threat: none.**

##### More Agents, More Samples, or More Thinking? Untangling Coordination, Ensembling, and Test-Time Compute in Multi-Agent LLMs
- **Citation:** Seyed Mahmoud Sajjadi Mohammadabadi; arXiv preprint (Nov 2026); [arXiv 2611.17610](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17610). The PACE checklist and utilities are at github.com/mahmoudsajjadi/pace (arXiv comment).
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API).
- **What it did:** a synthesis of nine primary evaluations (516 multi-agent configurations), with a six-dimension resource vector and a four-category baseline ladder.
- **Key numbers:** against sample- or budget-matched baselines "multi-agent systems average -0.042 in accuracy"; they are at or below baseline "in 74% of these comparable contrasts".
- **Connection: supports** the equal-cost rule. Its checklist could be used to report council cost.
- **Novelty threat: none.**

##### EvoTeam: Multi-Agent Collaboration via Evolving Teams
- **Citation:** Qinuo Cao, Xin He, Dianxuan Fu et al. (10 authors); arXiv preprint, v2 (Oct 2026); [arXiv 2610.39813](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39813). Code: github.com/DEEP-JLU/EvoTeam (per the abstract).
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API).
- **What it did:** it "jointly evolves team organization, interaction rules, and individual agent skills through execution experience", keeping a library of reusable teams. Tested on four benchmarks.
- **Key numbers:** none in the abstract.
- **Connection: overlaps** with evolving council configurations, on general tasks with no review or planted key and no stated acceptance test.
- **Novelty threat: low.**

##### Does Multi-Agent Collaboration Pay When Agents Serve Users? Testing Agent Scaling in Dual-Control Settings
- **Citation:** Ethan Yang and Xiangcheng Shi; arXiv preprint (Oct 2026); [arXiv 2610.27655](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27655).
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API).
- **What it did and key numbers:** see the Kim et al. block. It is a pre-registered test of the capability-saturation rule on τ²-bench: the "MAS is worse in 12 of the 13 valid cells".
- **Connection: challenges** Kim et al.'s rule. It **supports** a single-agent default unless the setting itself shows compute-matched evidence.
- **Novelty threat: none.**

##### AI-to-AI Code Reviews of GitHub Pull Requests
- **Citation:** Niruthiha Selvanayagam and Taher A. Ghaleb; ESEM 2026, Emerging Results track (arXiv comment); [arXiv 2608.21311](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21311).
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API).
- **What it did:** 248,641 AI-authored PRs with AI reviews from the CodAGE dataset, split into cross-product and same-product review.
- **Key numbers:** "For three of four dual-role reviewers, mean comments per PR were 58-65% higher in the same-product group, although effect sizes were small or negligible"; "CodeRabbit labeled 35.0% of its comments on Claude Code-authored PRs as refactor comments, compared with 10.5% on Copilot-authored PRs".
- **Connection: loosely related** to the same-family risk. It is field evidence that reviewer output differs when the reviewer and author are the same product, though confounded by PR traits.
- **Novelty threat: none.**

##### Smaller items (abstract read, verbatim, arXiv API; one line each)
- **CIVER** ([arXiv 2610.36010](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36010); WWW 2026 per the arXiv comment): an intervention-based verifier of review findings that "correctly identifies 77% of genuine defects through targeted repairs" (see Adversarial Review). An alternative to an LLM judge for ruling findings real.
- **Candidate supply and answer selection** ([arXiv 2608.25937](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.25937)): judge reliability "varies with the task, the generator and how rare the correct answer is". Frequency plus judge rescues minority correct answers (63.82% → 70.8%). Relevant to the appeal agent.
- **The Interaction Tax** ([arXiv 2608.23541](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23541); ICML 2026): mixed-family proposals converge within one round when agents read each other's outputs, and independent generation avoids this. Relevant to the debate arm and the mixed-families arm.
- **Is Agentic Code Review Helpful? (CodeRabbit)** ([arXiv 2607.03316](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.03316); Lin, Liang, Thongtanunam and Tantithamthavorn): of 31,073 review/feedback pairs, "36.4% were accepted … 56.3% were rejected", mainly "false positives, redundant, or out of scope".
- **Agentic Code Review in the Terminal** ([arXiv 2607.16740](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.16740); Charoenwet, Tantithamthavorn, Thongtanunam et al.; ASE 2026 per Semantic Scholar): "agentic reviewers achieve higher review precision but incur substantial exploration and validation overhead". The abstract gives no numbers and no tool names; the full text would say which terminal agents were studied (possibly Claude Code; not confirmed).
- **"Go Home Copilot, You're Drunk"** ([arXiv 2607.21997](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21997); Cynthia, Widyasari, Roy, Zhang and Lo): 54,791 agent review comments from five agents, Claude among them. "Incorrect suggestions and intentional design decisions" are the commonest reasons comments go unresolved, and an inline code suggestion is the strongest predictor of resolution.
- **Phoenix / Security Is Relative** ([arXiv 2604.19012](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.19012)): "18% of 'False Positives' identify genuine security concerns in patched code". This is a warning that a known-label key can be wrong in the reviewer's favour. The project's appeal or human audit should sample "false alarms".

### 1B. Sections 3 and 4: scoring against known or planted bugs, and judges, filters and appeals

Scope: every paper named in sections 3 ("Scoring LLM code review against known or planted bugs") and 4 ("Judges, filters and appeals in review pipelines") of `prior-work-research.md`, plus the Kumar et al. date check from "Discrepancies found". Metadata for all papers comes from one arXiv API pull (`id_list=`, verbatim fields) and the Semantic Scholar Graph API (venue, citation counts and citing papers). Citing papers are listed only where I opened at least the abstract.

#### Kumar et al. date check (Discrepancies section)

- **arXiv API record (verbatim):** id `2606.15689v1`, `published` 2026-04-09T06:56:13Z, `updated` 2026-04-09T06:56:13Z. Authors Shivam Pankaj Kumar, Swati Bararia and Kislay Raj. Comment: "7 pages, 4 figures, 13 tables, 13 references". No journal ref and no DOI.
- **arXiv abstract page (curl, raw HTML):** "[Submitted on 9 Apr 2026]", and the submission history reads "[v1] Thu, 9 Apr 2026 06:56:13 UTC (14 KB)".
- **Verdict:** the fetch summary was right. The paper was submitted on 9 April 2026 but carries a June 2026 (2606) number. arXiv assigns the YYMM number when it announces a paper, so the likeliest cause is a paper held in moderation for about two months. That cause is my inference and is not confirmed. Cite it as "arXiv 2606.15689, submitted 9 Apr 2026". The same thing, on a smaller scale, applies to arXiv 2603.19267 (submitted 27 Feb 2026, with a March number).

#### Section 3: scoring LLM code review against known or planted bugs

##### CRBugBench: (When) Should a Software Factory be Lights-off? Benchmarking Code Review Agents on Bug Detection
- **Published version / venue:** none. arXiv preprint only (arXiv API: v1 9 Oct 2026, no comment or journal ref). Semantic Scholar lists no venue.
- **Later versions:** none. v1 is still the only version.
- **Code or data:** public, though only partly. The paper's Data Availability section reads: "We are unable to make the replication package publicly available. We provide an open-source version of the benchmark at https://github.com/malusamayo/CRBugBench" (full text partly read, arXiv HTML via curl, Data Availability section). The repository belongs to Chenyang Yang, the first author (GitHub profile: "PhD student at CMU"). It was created on 8 Oct 2026 and has no licence file. Its README reads: "We rebuilt CRBugBench from descriptions in the paper". It holds "995 Harbor-format code-review tasks ... 500 on the fix track and 495 on the comment track", against the 1,000 open-source tasks in the paper. The 800 proprietary tasks are not released. The default judge panel in the release is "gpt-5.5, claude-opus-4.8, gemini-3.5-flash", while the paper used gemini-3.1-flash. The README says building every task takes "about 650 GB" of disk. There is no leaderboard (page read, GitHub API README).
- **Method details relevant to the project (full text partly read, sections 3.3, 4.1 and 4.2):** a panel of three judges from three families (gpt-5.5, claude-opus-4.8, gemini-3.1-flash) takes a majority vote, "To mitigate judge biases towards same-family models and single-judge instability". The authors "independently annotated 40 samples of agent reviews" and report "almost perfect human-LLM agreement". The kappa values are in formulas I could not extract. The proprietary portion is used as a contamination control, because its repositories are "certainly not part of models' training data". On that portion agents drop "up to 25.5%" in performance. The repositories were collected on 26 June 2026.
- **Same-author follow-ups:** Hao He, the second author, is first author of *AI Writes Faster Than Humans Can Review* ([arXiv 2607.01904](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.01904), v3 10 Nov 2026; abstract read, verbatim, arXiv API). It is a longitudinal study of 802 developers and 196,212 PRs, which found that after an AI mandate "load per reviewer doubled and automated review exceeded human review in coverage within months". It cites CRBugBench. It gives context only and is not a benchmark.
- **Citing papers 2025–2026 worth noting:** Semantic Scholar lists 2 citing papers: 2607.01904 (above) and *Developers as Managers of AI Agents* ([arXiv 2610.33588](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33588); abstract read, verbatim, arXiv API). The second is a shadowing study of 11 developers and has little bearing on the project.
- **Challenged?** None found. The paper is five weeks old.

##### CR-Bench: Evaluating the Real-World Utility of AI Code Review Agents
- **Published version / venue:** none found. arXiv preprint only (arXiv API v1 10 Mar 2026, with no comment). Semantic Scholar gives "arXiv.org". The authors are at Nutanix, Inc. (full text, author block).
- **Later versions:** none (v1 only).
- **Code or data:** a dataset card titled "CR-Bench Dataset" for arXiv 2603.11078 was published on Hugging Face as `rghosh8/cr-bench-verified` on 23 Oct 2026. It is not gated, has 112 downloads and states no licence (HF API and README read). `rghosh8` presumably belongs to the third author, Rajat Ghosh, but that is not confirmed. I found no CR-Evaluator code and no leaderboard.
- **Important detail not in prior-work-research.md (full text partly read, section 4):** "We derived this dataset using SWE-Bench ... as our foundation". There are "two variations – CR-Bench, which is generated from SWE-Bench, and CR-Bench-verified from SWE-Bench-verified". So the 584/174 instances are SWE-bench and SWE-bench Verified bugs traced back to the PRs that introduced them. **This is a contamination risk:** SWE-bench repositories and fixes are widely present in model training data. The point bears on the project's own contamination concern and on any use of CR-Bench numbers as a reference point.
- **Same-author follow-ups:** **CR-Bench-v2: Evaluating the Real-World Utility of AI Code Review Agents**, by the same four authors ([arXiv 2611.02566](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02566); v1 2 Nov 2026, v2 3 Nov 2026 with the comment "revise the prompt template format for better readability"; abstract read, verbatim, arXiv abs page via curl). It keeps the 584/174 instances "derived from SWE-Bench Verified" and adds two metrics, "Known-Defect Detection Rate (KDDR)" and "Usefulness Rate". It evaluates "eight code review agents over four backbones, plus commercially available Gemini Code Assist, using three independent LLM judges". It finds that "Even the strongest systems detect only about one-third of known defects (KDDR ≈ 33%), and Gemini Code Assist detects 8.1%." So v2 replaces v1's single Claude Sonnet 4.5 judge with three judges, which partly answers the "judge isn't checked" difference recorded in prior-work-research.md. Whether the three judges are checked against human labels I did not confirm, since I read only the abstract. It is not in the list of IDs already cited in the repo.
- **Citing papers 2025–2026 worth noting** (Semantic Scholar: 9 citing papers):
  - *CRJudgeBench: Can AI Detect Plausible but Invalid Code Reviews?* ([arXiv 2609.37216](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.37216); abstract read, verbatim, arXiv API). A benchmark of 1,199 instances for judging whether an individual review comment's technical claims are correct. It bears directly on the project's judge; see "New items".
  - *SWE-Review* ([arXiv 2607.06065](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06065); abstract read, verbatim, arXiv API). A reviewer agent accepts or rejects an AI-generated PR and gives feedback in a generate-review-revise loop. It is loosely related.
  - *Improving LLM-Based Go Code Review* ([arXiv 2606.01859](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.01859); abstract read, verbatim, arXiv API). It finds that issue-list review (enumerating all issues) beats primary-issue review, and that merging candidates from different context settings raises coverage. That is a weak analogue of pooling several reviewers.
  - *Code Review is a Conversation* ([arXiv 2607.22095](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22095); ICSME 2026 vision track per the arXiv comment; abstract read). Loosely related.
  - CRBugBench, c-CRAB and Kumar et al. also cite it.
- **Challenged?** Not directly. CRBugBench positions itself against CR-Bench and similar benchmarks but does not dispute their numbers. Its being built on SWE-bench (above) is a validity weakness I found, not one a later paper raised.

##### SWR-Bench: Assessing LLM Performance in Real-World Code Review Comment Generation
- **Published version / venue:** **confirmed FSE 2026.** Crossref for DOI [10.1145/3808144](https://doi.org/10.1145/3808144) gives container "Proceedings of the ACM on Software Engineering", volume 3, issue "FSE", issued 30 Jun 2026. Semantic Scholar gives the DBLP key `journals/pacmse/ZengSHLSWYXYZ26`. This replaces the "snippet only" marking in prior-work-research.md.
- **Later versions:** v1 1 Sep 2025 and v2 5 Jun 2026 (arXiv API). The v2 abstract says the judge "aligns strongly with human judgment (~90 agreement)".
- **Code or data:** public under the MIT licence at [github.com/ZZR0/SWRench](https://github.com/ZZR0/SWRench). Note the misspelling "SWRench" in the repository name. It was created 15 Apr 2026, last pushed 5 Jun 2026 and has 13 stars (GitHub API; README read). Its README says "1,000 real PR instances from 12 popular Python open-source projects", "500 PRs with reviewer-confirmed introduced changes, 500 clean PRs", "(e.g., scikit-learn, sympy, matplotlib, astropy)". The repository includes a `pyszz_v2` directory, so SZZ is used to find the commits that introduced each issue. There is no leaderboard.
  - **Two cautions.** (1) The v2 paper's reference [35], its replication-package link, is titled "SWR-Bench: A Benchmarking Suite for Serverless Cold Start Time Reduction" while pointing at `https://github.com/ZZR0/SWRench`. This is a bibliography error in the paper (full text, reference list). (2) The Hugging Face dataset `ming0531/SWR-Bench` is an unrelated hyperspectral-imaging dataset with the same name (HF dataset card read). Don't confuse the two.
- **Bearing on the project:** the 500 clean PRs give a false-alarm denominator, which CRBugBench and CR-Bench lack. The 12 repositories overlap heavily with SWE-bench's (astropy, sympy, scikit-learn, matplotlib), so the same contamination caveat applies as for CR-Bench. The SZZ-based mining is a precedent for the project's R-SZZ/V-SZZ seeds.
- **Same-author follow-ups:** *From Software History to Executable Regression Tasks: A System for Evaluating Software Agents* by Ruikai Shi, Zhengran Zeng, Rui Xie, Shikun Zhang and Wei Ye ([arXiv 2610.25308](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25308), 18 Oct 2026; abstract read, verbatim, arXiv API). It builds executable regression instances from pairs of commits that introduce and fix a bug, with developer tests that fail on the introducing version and pass on the fixing version: "172 validated instances from 522 candidates across 11 Python projects", with 83 enriched instances evaluated. It adds execution evidence to SWR-Bench-style defect keys, which matters for seed validation (see "New items").
- **Citing papers 2025–2026 worth noting** (Semantic Scholar: 18 citing papers; all those below were read at abstract level, verbatim, arXiv API):
  - *SecPRBench, Can Agentic Code Review Catch Vulnerabilities Before They Are Merged?* ([arXiv 2610.33509](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33509)). 265 real commits that introduced vulnerabilities, with an LLM judge, a public benchmark and a results website. See "New items".
  - *PrivacyPR* ([arXiv 2611.18293](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18293)). It reverts privacy fixes to reintroduce defects. Agents detect "42-51%" of defects with a generic prompt and "88-91%" when asked to check privacy, "while flagging 11-15% of non-fix PRs". This is evidence that a role brief sharply changes what reviewers catch. See "New items".
  - *Adaptive Code Revision Attacks on AI Pull Request Reviewers* (AFCRA; [arXiv 2610.05399](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.05399)). See "When AI Agrees Too Much" below.
  - *DesignReviewBench / DesignLore* ([arXiv 2610.21925](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21925)). Design-aware review in the Linux kernel: the best baseline has F1 0.213, against 0.259 for DesignLore. Loosely related.
  - *A Survey of Code Review Benchmarks and Evaluation Practices* ([arXiv 2602.13377](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.13377)). It covers 99 papers from 2015–2025 and is useful as a citation source for the benchmark landscape.
  - *AI-to-AI Code Reviews of GitHub Pull Requests* ([arXiv 2608.21311](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21311), ESEM 2026 per the arXiv comment). It compares same-product with cross-product AI review in the field, so it bears on same-family review. See "New items".
  - MCR-Bench, CRBugBench, PRWeaver, c-CRAB, CR-Bench and AACR-Bench also cite SWR-Bench.
- **Challenged?** No direct challenge found. AACR-Bench's abstract criticises "noisy, incomplete ground truth derived from raw Pull Request (PR) comments", which is the source of SWR-Bench's key. It reports "a 285% increase in defect coverage" after AI-assisted expert annotation, meaning many real defects in PRs are missing from comment-mined keys. That is an indirect label-completeness critique of SWR-Bench-style keys (abstract read, verbatim, arXiv API), and it matters for scoring "unplanted findings" in the project.

##### MCR-Bench (From Static to Dynamic: Benchmarking Real-World Code Review with MCR-Bench)
- **Published version / venue:** **confirmed ISSTA 2026.** The arXiv comment reads "Accepted at ISSTA 2026". Crossref for DOI [10.1145/3832219](https://doi.org/10.1145/3832219) gives "Proceedings of the ACM on Software Engineering", volume 3, issue "ISSTA", issued 1 Oct 2026.
- **Later versions:** none (v1 27 Aug 2026 only).
- **Code or data:** public under Apache-2.0 at [github.com/DeepSoftwareAnalytics/MCR-bench](https://github.com/DeepSoftwareAnalytics/MCR-bench). It was created 28 Jan 2026 and last pushed 12 May 2026 (GitHub API; README and file tree read). The full dataset sits in `MCR-bench/dataset/{csharp,java,js,py,ts}.jsonl` (about 10–14 MB each), together with an `Appendix.pdf` and per-model judge outputs for claude-haiku-4-5, deepseek-v3.2, gemini-3-flash, glm-4.7, gpt-5.2 and kimi-k2. There is no official leaderboard. A third-party tool (`akbokha/retrace`) says it is "evaluated on MCR-bench", but I did not open it.
- **How the key was built (full text partly read, section 3.2; README):** defect "cards" come from an LLM pipeline: per-round extraction, then cross-round linking run three times, then a consistency check that keeps "only agreed-upon issues". The README's example uses `--model-name gpt-4`. The retained cards are then checked by two human annotators drawn from a pool of six developers, with Cohen's kappa "0.87". A third annotator arbitrates disagreements. **The caveat for the project:** humans verify what the LLM proposed, so a defect the LLM pipeline missed can't enter the key. Recall against MCR-Bench is recall against the LLM's own candidates, which is the same circularity risk the project faces with Claude-planted seeds.
- **Same-author follow-ups:** arXiv author search for Dewu Zheng (page read). There is no follow-up on review. The nearby SWE-agent work (*SWE-Prime*, 2608.27449; *Efficient SWE Agent Benchmarking via Trajectory-Aware Evaluation*, 2609.01603) was seen only as titles and not read.
- **Citing papers 2025–2026 worth noting:** Semantic Scholar lists 1, *Adaptive Code Revision Attacks on AI Pull Request Reviewers* (AFCRA, [arXiv 2610.05399](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.05399); abstract read, verbatim, arXiv API). Its multi-round reviewer–attacker interaction is the adversarial counterpart of MCR-Bench's multi-round setting. See "When AI Agrees Too Much".
- **Challenged?** None found.

##### c-CRAB (Code Review Agent Benchmark)
- **Published version / venue:** none found (arXiv v1 24 Mar 2026, v3 7 Apr 2026, no comment; Semantic Scholar "arXiv.org").
- **Code or data:** public at [github.com/c-CRAB-Benchmark/dataset](https://github.com/c-CRAB-Benchmark/dataset), created 24 Mar 2026 (GitHub API). The repository contains `preprocess_swe_care.py`, so c-CRAB is at least partly built from **SWE-CARE**, the CodeFuse-CR-Bench dataset. That is a lineage link the prior-work doc doesn't record. There is no leaderboard.
- **Citing papers 2025–2026 worth noting** (Semantic Scholar: 9 citing papers): PrivacyPR (2611.18293) and CRBugBench (both above). *BulkPR-Bench* (2608.02685) was seen only as a title.
- **Challenged?** None found.

##### AACR-Bench
- **Published version / venue:** none found (arXiv v3 30 Jan 2026; Semantic Scholar "arXiv.org").
- **Code or data:** public under Apache-2.0 at [github.com/alibaba/aacr-bench](https://github.com/alibaba/aacr-bench) (261 stars; last pushed 16 Nov 2026) and on Hugging Face as `Alibaba-Aone/aacr-bench` and `Alibaba-Aone/aacr-bench-repos` (README read). **A v2 dataset now exists**: v1 has 196 positive PRs with 1,506 comments over 50 projects, and v2 has 151 PRs with 944 comments over 42 projects. v1 also ships "a negative dataset containing 155 PRs and 639 comments". The README's quick-start pairs v2 with OpenCodeReview. A **community leaderboard** exists at `5wxwxwxw5/aacr-bench-leaderboard` (README read, in Chinese). It ranks by F1 with line and semantic matching over all 196 samples, and also reports average time and average tokens per sample. This is the only leaderboard I found among these benchmarks that reports cost. One third-party reviewer (`zhiyuwang720-dev/CodeSage`, 76 stars) claims "#1 on AACR-Bench with 52.8% precision, 23.4% recall, and 32.4% F1" using "parallel reviewers" and per-finding verification. That is a self-claim, seen only in the GitHub search description and not verified.
- **Citing papers 2025–2026 worth noting** (Semantic Scholar: 9 citing papers): *Agentic Code Review in the Terminal* ([arXiv 2607.16740](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.16740); ASE 2026 per Semantic Scholar's venue string "Proceedings of the 41st IEEE/A…", not confirmed; abstract read, arXiv abs page). It is by the HalluJudge group and finds "agentic reviewers achieve higher review precision but incur substantial exploration and validation overhead". CIVER (2610.36010) also cites it; see Refute-or-Promote.
- **Challenged?** None found.

##### Sphinx
- **Published version / venue:** none found (v1 6 Jan 2026 only).
- **Code or data:** the abstract says "The data will be released after review" (verbatim, arXiv API). I found no release on GitHub or Hugging Face (searches for "Sphinx pull request review" and for the dataset gave nothing relevant).
- **Citing papers:** Semantic Scholar lists 4: SWEeper-Bench (2610.14096; abstract read; interactive bug discovery, loosely related), MCR-Bench, Agentic Code Review in the Terminal and MathlibPR (title only).
- **Challenged?** None found.

##### ContextCRBench (Benchmarking LLMs for Fine-Grained Code Review with Enriched Context in Practice)
- **Published version / venue:** **FSE Companion 2026.** Crossref for DOI 10.1145/3803437.3805235 gives the event "FSE Companion '26", issued 5 Jul 2026. The track is not stated in the Crossref record; it is probably Industry, given the ByteDance deployment, but that is not confirmed.
- **Later versions:** v2 30 Dec 2025.
- **Code or data:** the GitHub repository `kinesiatricssxilm14/ContextCRBench` is small (81 KB; README, a download script and a review script; no licence). A Hugging Face dataset `Buotte/ContextCRBench` exists, but its card holds only a licence line, so who published it is not confirmed.
- **Citing papers:** Semantic Scholar lists 10, including Ericsson's agentic review paper (already covered) and DesignReviewBench (above).
- **Challenged?** None found.

##### CodeFuse-CR-Bench (dataset SWE-CARE)
- **Published version / venue:** none found (arXiv v3 23 Oct 2025; Semantic Scholar "arXiv.org").
- **Code or data:** public. [github.com/codefuse-ai/SWE-CARE](https://github.com/codefuse-ai/SWE-CARE) is under Apache-2.0 (GitHub API), and the dataset is on Hugging Face as `inclusionAI/SWE-CARE`. c-CRAB reuses it (above).
- **Citing papers:** Semantic Scholar lists 9. *AgenticSCR* (2601.19138) was seen only as a title. CIVER is covered under Refute-or-Promote.
- **Challenged?** None found.

##### SEVRA-BENCH
- **Published version / venue:** the repository README says "Accepted at FLMSec 2026 (Foundations of Language Model Security), a NeurIPS 2026 workshop" (README read). There is no arXiv comment or journal ref, and Semantic Scholar shows "arXiv.org". Treat it as a workshop paper per the authors' README.
- **Later versions:** v2 6 Jul 2026.
- **Code or data:** code at [github.com/rufimelo99/sevra-bench](https://github.com/rufimelo99/sevra-bench), with no licence file, a project page and a trace explorer. The dataset `RedAI4Code/SEVRA` is gated ("read token required"), and there is a second gated set, `RedAI4Code/SEVRA-plus` (HF API). The README's headline table shows the refusal rate on the "retained challenge split (1,062 PRs)": "Claude Opus 4.7 | 98 | 70" against "Claude Haiku 4.5 | 53 | 44" (RR % | security-reason rate %). It also defines a "False-Decline Rate" on legitimate fixes. That table works as a de facto leaderboard.
- **Citing papers 2025–2026 worth noting** (Semantic Scholar: 5; all opened at abstract level, arXiv abs page via curl):
  - **CyberDual** ([arXiv 2610.34435](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34435)): see "New items". It is highly relevant to cross-vendor review.
  - *"You're Right, Let Me Fix It"* (CAVE-Bench, [arXiv 2609.32616](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.32616)): "false accusations damage correct work in up to 60.06% of runs" across 14 models in Claude Code. It bears on what a fixer does with a wrong judge or appeal verdict.
  - SecPRBench and AFCRA (above).
- **Challenged?** None found.

##### When AI Agrees Too Much: Manipulating Automated Code Review via Bias Injection
- **Published version / venue:** the arXiv comment on v5 reads "To appear at the 49th International Conference on Software Engineering (ICSE 2027)" (arXiv API). Semantic Scholar shows no venue yet. Not yet in proceedings.
- **Later versions (arXiv abs pages for v1, v2 and v3 read via curl):** five versions, and **the title and headline numbers changed substantially**:
  - **v1 (19 Mar 2026):** titled "Measuring and Exploiting Confirmation Bias in LLM-Assisted Security Code Review". It covers four models and "Framing a change as bug-free reduces vulnerability detection rates by 16-93%, with strongly asymmetric effects: false negatives increase sharply while false positive rates change little". Adversarial framing "succeeds in 35% of cases against GitHub Copilot ... and in 88% of cases against Claude Code".
  - **v2 (23 Apr 2026):** titled "Measuring and Exploiting Contextual Bias in LLM-Assisted Security Code Review". 6 LLMs, "17 CVEs across 10 real-world projects", and the iterative refinement attack "achieves 100% success".
  - **v3 (12 Sep 2026):** same title as v2.
  - **v4 (23 Sep 2026) and v5 (27 Oct 2026):** current title. "33 CVEs across 20 real-world projects and two popular ACR pipelines (Claude Code and CodeRabbit)", with "32/33 (97%)".
  - **Cite v5 and the ICSE 2027 venue.** Earlier preprints with different titles and numbers may circulate.
- **Read:** full text partly read (arXiv HTML of v5 via curl; contributions, RQ1–RQ3 summaries). This fixes the missing read status that prior-work-research.md flags.
- **What prior-work-research.md should say instead of "A biased PR description got past review in 32 of 33 cases":**
  - The 32/33 comes only from the **LLM-assisted iterative refinement** attack, which is refined against "a local clone of the review pipeline". The RQ3 summary gives "100% attack success rate (17/17) against Claude Code review pipelines and 94% (15/16) against CodeRabbit".
  - **Template-based biased descriptions failed**: "Of Claude Code's 16 baseline rejections, none is flipped; of CodeRabbit's 13, only one is".
  - **Redacting metadata is an effective defence**: "Redacting metadata before passing the PR to ACR is an effective defense, restoring detection in all affected cases". Redacting the PR description recovered 12/17 for Claude Code but only 4/15 for CodeRabbit, and redacting all commit metadata recovered the rest.
  - The RQ1 exploratory study covers "6 LLMs across four model families (OpenAI, Anthropic, Google, and DeepSeek) under five framing conditions (14,910 queries in total)", on 250 CrossVul CVE/patch pairs.
- **Bearing on the project:** this is direct evidence that a judge or reviewer reading PR text or a `CLAUDE.md`-style framing can be pushed toward "no bug". The v1 finding that false negatives rise "while false positive rates change little" is the asymmetry an appeal stage would target. Redacting metadata is a cheap control arm.
- **Code or data:** the paper says "We provide complete experimental artifacts and a full replication package for all study components (see supplementary material)". I found no public link in the HTML.
- **Same-author follow-ups:** arXiv author search for Georgios Alexopoulos (page read). There are no further papers on code review. Two papers on other topics (*Cross-Ecosystem Vulnerability Analysis for Python Applications*, 2603.18693; a serverless paper) were seen only as titles.
- **Citing papers 2025–2026 worth noting** (Semantic Scholar: 8; each read at abstract level):
  - **AFCRA, Adaptive Code Revision Attacks on AI Pull Request Reviewers** (Gong, Zhang, Jahangirova and Wang; [arXiv 2610.05399](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.05399); arXiv API). The attacker uses the reviewer's own feedback to "repair the reported problem while preserving a vulnerability". Over "five-round interactions with Sonnet 5 and GPT-5.5 reviewers", AFCRA is "2.5x and 12.5x" more successful than the strongest text- or comment-based attack. It is built on AFCRA-Bench (159 disclosed vulnerabilities with executable exploits). **This bears on sequential review and appeal rounds**, because feedback leaks what would pass.
  - **ALIBI** ([arXiv 2607.24964](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.24964); arXiv API). Adversarial source-code comments get past LLM vulnerability detectors with attack success "exceed[ing] 90%" on 125 null-pointer bugs. "architectural isolation and pre-detector comment sanitization substantially improve resilience".
  - **Trusting the Tool: Asymmetric Deference of LLM Vulnerability Auditors to Static-Analysis Evidence** ([arXiv 2610.32876](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32876); arXiv API). "false assurances lowered the detection of real vulnerabilities by up to 19 points, whereas false alarms never significantly lowered accuracy". In a pre-specified test, "Verifying each claim in a separate call and showing the refuted claims raised accuracy under false assurances by 12 points". This is the same asymmetry, and it bears on a judge consuming reviewers' claims. See "New items".
  - **SecPRBench** (2610.33509) and **SEVRA-BENCH**.
  - *Counting the Bandages* ([arXiv 2610.35942](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35942); arXiv abs page). In a small pre-specified experiment, an AI reviewer told the drafter erred "ten times less often than it did ... let a wrong figure through in 22.5% of briefs instead of none". That is evidence that a reviewer's checking effort follows its stated prior. It also cites Kumar et al.
  - *Are Frontier LLMs Ready for Cybersecurity?* (2605.23243) and an automotive human-agent paper (2610.35982) are loosely related.
- **Challenged?** No paper disputes it. AFCRA argues that text-only attacks such as this paper's understate the threat, because revising the code with feedback is stronger. That extends the finding rather than refuting it.

##### PRWeaver
- **Published version / venue:** none found (v1 3 Aug 2026 only).
- **Code or data:** no repository found (GitHub search "PRWeaver" empty). The paper's only GitHub links are to tools it evaluates, such as `alibaba/open-code-review` (full-text link scan).
- **Citing papers:** CyberDual and AFCRA (both above).
- **Challenged?** None found.

##### Jin and Chen, Are LLMs Reliable Code Reviewers? Systematic Overcorrection in Requirement Conformance Judgement
- **Published version / venue:** **published in the journal *Automated Software Engineering*** (Springer), volume 33, issue 3, article 90, issued 26 Jun 2026. Crossref gives DOI [10.1007/s10515-026-00638-5](https://doi.org/10.1007/s10515-026-00638-5). Semantic Scholar's venue string wrongly says "International Conference on Automated Software Engineering"; the DOI shows it is the journal.
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API). Its abstract confirms that "more detailed prompt design, particularly with those requiring explanations and proposed corrections, leads to higher misjudgment rates". It proposes a "Fix-guided Verification Filter" that treats the model's proposed fix as "executable counterfactual evidence".
- **Citing papers 2025–2026 worth noting** (Semantic Scholar: 12):
  - *Prior Audit-Repair Context Shifts LLM Verifier Thresholds Toward Leniency* ([arXiv 2608.16003](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.16003); abstract read, arXiv abs page). A completed audit-and-repair episode already in the checker's context "lowers false alarms in 15 of 15 model x wording combinations, by 2.8 to 11.5 percentage points". This is a threshold shift, not a change in discrimination. It bears directly on a judge or appeal agent that has seen earlier rounds. See "New items".
  - *The Specification as Quality Gate* ([arXiv 2603.25773](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.25773); abstract read). Small same-family experiments (Claude reviewing Claude-generated code) plus a cross-family panel, all on a planted-bug corpus. See "New items".
  - *Cross-Model LLM Code Review: Should you use Claude to review Codex or vice versa?* (2607.21656) is already covered.
  - A measurement-systems-analysis protocol for qualifying LLM code-review evaluators (ASE journal, DOI 10.1007/s10515-026-00701-1) and SlopCodeBench (2603.24755) were seen only as titles.
- **Challenged?** None found.

##### DeepCRCEval
- **Published version / venue:** **FASE 2025**, in the Springer LNCS proceedings. Crossref gives DOI 10.1007/978-3-031-90900-9_3, and the arXiv comment says "Accepted to ... FASE 2025".
- **Code or data:** none found.
- **Citing papers 2025–2026 worth noting** (Semantic Scholar: 22): SWE-PRBench ([arXiv 2603.26130](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.26130); abstract read, arXiv abs page). It has 350 PRs with a human-annotated key and an LLM judge "validated at kappa=0.75". "8 frontier models detect only 15-31% of human-flagged issues", and all 8 "degrade monotonically" as more context is added. That bears on the project's question of whether `CLAUDE.md` context helps (see "New items"). Others seen only as titles: *Balancing usefulness and naturalness: an LLM-based curation pipeline for code review comments* (EMSE, 2607.09524) and *Towards Practical Defect-Focused Automated Code Review* (ICML 2025, 2505.17928).
- **Challenged?** No. Later work extends its argument against similarity scoring.

##### Jiang et al., Beyond Lexical Similarity
- **Published version / venue:** none found. arXiv v1 9 Jan 2025 only; Semantic Scholar "arXiv.org"; Crossref title search found no match.
- **Code or data:** the GradedReviews benchmark was not found on GitHub or Hugging Face.
- **Citing papers:** Semantic Scholar lists 9, none adding much beyond those above.
- **Challenged?** *Understanding the Limits of Automated Evaluation for Code Review Bots in Practice* (EASE 2026 per the arXiv comment; [arXiv 2604.24525](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.24525); abstract read, arXiv abs page) qualifies the LLM-scoring approach. On 2,604 industrial bot comments labelled fixed or wontFix, G-Eval and an LLM judge reach only "Agreement ratios range from approximately 0.44 to 0.62". The authors argue that developer actions are not objective ground truth. That supports scoring against a planted key rather than against developer reactions.

#### Section 4: judges, filters and appeals in review pipelines

##### Refute-or-Promote: An Adversarial Stage-Gated Multi-Agent Review Methodology
- **Published version / venue:** none. arXiv preprint (v1 21 Apr 2026 only). Its comment reads "10 pages, 3 tables. Artifacts: https://github.com/abhinavagarwal07/refute-or-promote (Zenodo DOI: 10.5281/zenodo.19668799)". Semantic Scholar gives "arXiv.org".
- **Read:** full text partly read (arXiv HTML via curl: sections 3, 4.5 and 5). This upgrades the "abstract page read" status in prior-work-research.md.
- **Code or data:** the GitHub repository is under the MIT licence, created 20 Apr 2026, last pushed 23 Apr 2026 and 5 KB in size. It contains LICENSE, a one-line README and **`strat.md`, the orchestration playbook** (read in full, GitHub API). **The playbook is written for Claude Code subagents.** Its rules include "Launch 3 sonnets to find potential candidates", round 1 uses "1 creative sonnet" and "2 adversarial sonnet", and round 2 adds "1 adversarial opus". The final rounds use "1 sonnet + 1 opus (duplicate)". The cross-family "super final round" runs `codex exec -m gpt-5.4 -c model_reasoning_effort=xhigh`. So the "Cross-Model Critic" is OpenAI Codex critiquing a Claude Sonnet/Opus council. That is very close to the project's planned cross-family judge arm, though it uses prompts only and has no benchmark.
- **Verified the "snippet only" claim:** section 4.5 reads "lcms2: unanimous kill, resurrected. The lcms2 CubeSize() integer overflow (CVE-2026-41254) was unanimously killed in Round 4 ('DO NOT FILE' ...) and resurrected only via a creative uplift agent tasked with a different role". Section 5, "False negatives and the unidirectional pipeline", reads: "Refute-or-Promote optimises precision but does not measure recall. The pipeline is architecturally unidirectional: it has structural mechanisms against false positives (kill mandates) but relies on the human orchestrator to rescue true positives incorrectly killed." Table 3 lists "Rejecting false kills, override decisions" as "Human-essential". **This is the clearest published statement of the gap the project's appeal agent fills.** It also shows a recovery triggered by a human but carried out by an agent with a different role brief.
- **Other numbers (verbatim, full text):**
  - Cost: "$250 out-of-pocket on a standard LLM subscription for 36+ outcomes (including 4 CVEs)".
  - Limitations: "Single operator with domain expertise (not independently replicated). No ablation studies isolating individual mechanisms."
  - The introduction says "80+ agents—including dedicated adversarial reviewers—unanimously endorsed" the false OpenSSL oracle, while the abstract says "ten dedicated reviewers". Quote the abstract's figure with this caveat.
  - It also reports a wolfSSL case where "same-family consensus actively propagated a mistake that a fresh-context reviewer caught".
- **Same-author follow-ups:** none. An arXiv author search for "Agarwal, Abhinav" returns only unrelated namesakes (robotics, physics).
- **Citing papers 2025–2026 worth noting** (Semantic Scholar: 2):
  - *CIVER: Causal Intervention Verification for Reliable Code Review* ([arXiv 2610.36010](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36010); comment "WWW 2026"; abstract read, verbatim, arXiv API). Three modules, review, intervention ("repair-oriented perturbations") and verifier, separate genuine defects from false positives by checking whether a finding "respond[s] to targeted repairs while remaining stable under unrelated changes". It "correctly identifies 77% of genuine defects" on SWE-Review-Bench. It is a filter, not an appeal. See "New items".
  - *Enhancing Multi-Agent LLM Output Quality Through Adversarial Critique: a Cross-Domain Evaluation* (IEEE ICNCCom 2026, DOI 10.1109/ICNCCom69117.2026.11736314). Seen only as a title.
- **Challenged?** No outside challenge found. The paper names its own confounders (single operator, no ablations, a protocol that changed during the campaign).

##### Evident: The Hitchhiker's Guide to Program Analysis, Part III: Mostly Harmless LLMs
- **Published version / venue:** none. arXiv v1 13 Jun 2026 and v2 21 Oct 2026, no comment. Semantic Scholar gives "arXiv.org".
- **Read:** full text partly read (arXiv HTML via curl: abstract, introduction, sections 6.1–6.2).
- **Details worth adding (verbatim):**
  - The LLMs were "GPT-5.5, GPT-5.4 mini, Claude Sonnet 4.6, and GLM-5.1", and "we run each method three times per warning and report the majority outcome". With GPT-5.5, Evident "filters out 102 false alarms (63.8%)" and keeps all 40 bugs. Claude Sonnet 4.6 filters 98, with 1 confirmed bug unresolved (and therefore kept).
  - **The baselines include the authors' own earlier system:** "We compare Evident with BugLens (Li et al., 2025a) ... and Codex, a general-purpose coding agent. Both BugLens and Codex leave the final bug decision to" the LLM. The 200 warnings come from "the BugLens dataset".
  - So the 27.5–45.0% miss rate is partly a **self-challenge of BugLens**, the authors' Part II paper (see below).
  - Cost "about $0.50" per case attempt with GPT-5.5, against "$1.20/case" for the Codex baseline. Runtime is "about 10 minutes per case".
- **Code or data:** no repository link found in the HTML. The only GitHub link is to `google/oss-fuzz-gen`, a related tool.
- **Same-author follow-ups and predecessors** (arXiv author search for "Qian, Zhiyun", page read):
  - *The Hitchhiker's Guide to Program Analysis, Part II: Deep Thoughts by LLMs* (BugLens; Haonan Li, Hang Zhang, Kexin Pei and Zhiyun Qian; [arXiv 2504.11711](https://arxiv.org/abs/2504.11711), v3 31 May 2025; abstract read, arXiv abs page). It reports that the LLM filter "improves precision approximately 7-fold (from 0.10 to 0.72)" on Linux kernel taint-style bugs. Part III now shows that this style of LLM-decides filter misses confirmed bugs. It is a useful pair of citations: the same group, same data, opposite emphasis.
  - *SyzHarness* (2609.23889) and *Patch-to-PoC* (2602.07287) were seen only as titles.
- **Citing papers 2025–2026 worth noting** (Semantic Scholar: 1): *Beyond QASecClaw* (below).
- **Challenged?** None found.

##### Replacing Judges with Juries (PoLL)
- **Published version / venue:** none found. Semantic Scholar gives "arXiv.org" only, and Crossref title search found nothing. arXiv v1 29 Apr 2024 and v2 1 May 2024. Treat it as an arXiv preprint.
- **Code or data:** none linked in the arXiv record.
- **Same-author follow-ups:** an arXiv author search for Pat Verga (page read) shows nothing later on judges or juries. The later work is *Command A* (2504.00698) and *FLARE* (2410.11900), both seen only as titles.
- **Citing papers 2025–2026 worth noting** (Semantic Scholar: 431 citations; I screened only the first 100 returned and opened these abstracts on the arXiv abs page via curl):
  - **JuryProbe** ([arXiv 2608.20607](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20607); "Accepted at Transactions on Machine Learning Research (TMLR), 08/2026" per the arXiv comment). It finds that "agreement may reflect shared false-negative blind spots rather than independent evidence". It measures "FN-only correlations 0.402 and 0.368; lifts 3.13x and 18.13x" on FEVER corruptions and routes unanimous accepts to grounded verification. **This is a direct challenge to PoLL's independence assumption**, and it is the false-negative case that matters for a judge's dismissals.
  - **Swapping the Judge Changes the Ranking** ([arXiv 2611.04090](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04090); preregistered). Five judges from three providers on 4,000 Chatbot Arena battles: "median Cohen's kappa = 0.22", and "system rankings did not survive a change of judge". Two-provider replication helped "+0.10 precision", but only by filtering out one poorly aligned judge. "Disagreements with the human vote co-occurred across judges far more often than independence predicts". It bears on the project's score, which depends on the judge: swapping the judge could flip which council wins.
  - **Correlated Failures of AI Approval Monitors** ([arXiv 2610.29178](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29178)). Monitors that share inputs fail together: "a rule that counts sources instead of votes blocked 0.9% of correct payments, against 7.4% for the best counting rule, at equal safety". Its advice is "Diversify the inputs, harden the monitors, count sources, not votes". That matters for councils whose seats read the same diff and the same PR text.
  - **A latent dimension of Condorcet's jury theorem for multiple AI advisers** ([arXiv 2609.14438](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.14438)). Theory: below an adviser accuracy of 0.8, "visible dissent eventually becomes more likely than a correct majority". It is a caution against reading council disagreement as failure.
  - **Stopping and Routing LLM Judge Panels** ([arXiv 2608.19802](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.19802); WISE 2026 per the arXiv comment). It casts panel design as allocation: "drop copies, add complements globally, route specialists conditionally, and stop when validation gain falls below a threshold". The audits include code. It is a close analogue of the project's keep-or-drop rule for seats.
  - *Who Judges Matters* (2609.17857), with a same-family lift of 3.4–8.4 points, is already covered elsewhere in the repo.
- **Challenged?** **Yes, partly.** JuryProbe, *Swapping the Judge* and *Correlated Failures* all find that errors in panels are correlated well beyond independence, which weakens PoLL's premise that a panel of disjoint families cancels bias. None re-runs PoLL's own six datasets, so its specific results stand unreplicated rather than refuted.

##### Appeal paper 1: Multimodal Multi-Agent Empowered Legal Judgment Prediction (JurisMMA), arXiv 2601.12815
- **Citation:** Zhaolu Kang, Junhao Gong, Qingxi Chen et al. (10 authors); ICASSP 2026 per the arXiv comment ("Accepted to the IEEE International Conference on Acoustics, Speech, and Signal Processing (ICASSP) 2026") and Semantic Scholar's venue. v1 19 Jan 2026 to v5 19 Feb 2026. [arXiv 2601.12815](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.12815).
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API). Full text partly read (fetch tool, arXiv HTML; section 2 stages, Table 1 ablations). The fetch tool summarises, so the quotes below are as the tool returned them.
- **What it did:** a six-stage legal pipeline with role agents (Junior Judge, Senior Judge, Assistant, Chief Judge, Legal Counsel, Defendant). Stage III gives a first-instance decision. In Stage IV the Defendant "may accept ... or raise valid objections". In Stage V "the Legal Counsel constructs an appeal brief", and in Stage VI "Second-instance Review" the Chief Judge re-executes stages I and II and "render[s] a final judgment". The dataset JurisMM has "101,544 cases" in its text subset and "83 high-quality multimodal samples".
- **Key numbers:** with GPT-4o, Law Articles accuracy is 0.872 against 0.768 for the best baseline, and Charges accuracy 0.882 against 0.751 (Table 1, via the fetch tool). **There is no ablation of the appeal stages.** The only ablations are "w/o KB" and "w/o MA", and the paper doesn't report how often the second instance changes the first-instance verdict (fetch tool, asked directly).
- **Code or data:** "JurisMM is available at: https://github.com/Zhaolu-K/JurisMMA" (via the fetch tool; I did not open the repository).
- **Connection:** loosely related. The appeal is part of a staged simulation, and the **same Chief Judge role** reviews its own first decision. There is no independent appeal agent and no measurement of the appeal's effect.
- **Novelty threat:** none. It has an appeal stage in name, but nothing in it tests whether appeals recover wrongly dismissed items.
- **Citing papers:** Semantic Scholar lists 23, almost all unrelated (multimodal, legal and memory papers). *Task-Aware LLM Council with Adaptive Decision Pathways* (ICASSP 2026, DOI 10.1109/icassp55912.2026.11462526) was seen only as a title.

##### Appeal paper 2: Reviewing the Reviewer: Graph-Enhanced LLMs for E-commerce Appeal Adjudication, arXiv 2603.19267
- **Citation:** Yuchen Du, Ashley Li and Zixi Huang; KDD 2026 Applied Data Science Track per the arXiv comment ("10 pages, 3 figures, KDD 2026 Applied Data Science Track"). Semantic Scholar shows only "arXiv.org". Submitted 27 Feb 2026 (v1 only). [arXiv 2603.19267](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.19267).
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API). Full text partly read (fetch tool, arXiv HTML; sections 5.1–5.3 and the conclusion). Quotes are as the tool returned them.
- **What it did:** in a hierarchical "Maker-Checker" workflow for seller appeals, "a second-tier reviewer (Checker) then re-examines these rejected cases and may overturn the decision". The system learns from historical Checker corrections of **Maker rejections**, using an Evidence-Action-Factor-Decision (EAFD) graph. It retrieves precedents and can return "Request More Information (RMI)" when "evidence is insufficient". The backbone is Claude Sonnet 4.0 on Amazon Bedrock.
- **Key numbers (abstract, verbatim):** "a standard LLM-only baseline achieves only 70.8% alignment with human experts", "incorporating action modeling with RMI improves alignment to 87.5%" and the full framework reaches "95.8%" offline and "96.3%" in production. Per the fetch tool, the evaluation "spans over 260 production-level appeal cases" with "an 80/20 chronological split", and section 5.3 states "Over four weeks (136 cases), the system achieved 97.1% alignment". **There is an internal inconsistency:** the conclusion says "a 96.3% cumulative alignment rate over 136 production cases". The fetch tool reported that "The system never reinstated a seller that the human Checker kept blocked" (100% precision on reinstatements).
- **Code or data:** the paper has no availability statement, according to the fetch tool.
- **Connection:** overlaps in structure. It is **the closest published analogue of the project's appeal**: a second tier that reviews only the first tier's *rejections* and may reinstate them. The LLM imitates the human Checker rather than acting as an independent appeal agent, though, and the domain is not code. Its RMI outcome, abstaining while asking for evidence, is a design option the project's appeal agent lacks.
- **Novelty threat:** low. A different domain, and it predicts human appeal outcomes rather than testing whether an appeal stage recovers true items.
- **Citing papers:** Semantic Scholar lists 0.

##### One-way filters (lighter blocks)
- **HalluJudge:** **FSE 2026 Industry Track confirmed.** The arXiv comment reads "Accepted at FSE'26: Industry Track, Full-Length, Peer-Reviewed", with DOI 10.1145/3803437.3805236, and Crossref gives "FSE Companion '26". v3 11 Jun 2026.
  - **Same-group follow-up:** *Agentic Code Review in the Terminal* (Charoenwet, Tantithamthavorn, Thongtanunam et al.; [arXiv 2607.16740](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.16740); abstract read). It is a trajectory-level analysis of terminal-based agentic reviewers and finds that agentic reviewers gain precision at the cost of exploration and validation overhead.
  - **Citing papers:** Semantic Scholar lists 8. *Is Agentic Code Review Helpful? Mining Developers' Feedback to CodeRabbit Reviews in the Wild* (2607.03316) was seen only as a title.
  - **Challenged?** Indirectly. *Understanding the Limits of Automated Evaluation for Code Review Bots* (EASE 2026, 2604.24525; abstract read) finds that LLM judges agree with developer labels at only about 0.44–0.62, which is consistent with HalluJudge's 67% production alignment rather than contradicting it.
- **Du et al., Reducing False Positives in Static Bug Detection with LLMs:** **ICSE-SEIP 2026 confirmed.** Crossref for DOI 10.1145/3786583.3786910 gives "ICSE-SEIP '26". v1 only. Semantic Scholar lists 27 citing papers. Opened:
  - *VulnSAGE* ([arXiv 2610.23035](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23035); abstract read). It "rejects 89 of 97 false alarms and retains 85 of 90 real defects" by checking the premises of each LLM claim with program analysis.
  - *Trusting the Tool* (2610.32876; above).
  - **Challenged?** Evident (above) is the strongest counter-evidence to LLM-decides filtering in general.
- **ZeroFalse:** no venue found (v1 only; Semantic Scholar "arXiv.org"). Semantic Scholar lists 13 citing papers. *Does Architecture Matter? Comparing Multi-agent Workflows for Filtering and Prioritizing Static Analysis Findings* (ACM, DOI 10.1145/3843778.3844516) and *Memoir* (2608.09181) were seen only as titles.
- **QASecClaw:** no venue found (v1 only). **Same-author follow-up:** *Beyond QASecClaw: Agent Scaling, Verifier Feedback, and Evidence Reliability in Software Assurance* (S M Abdulla Al Mamun, Akif Islam and Mohd. Ruhul Ameen; [arXiv 2610.36947](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36947), 27 Oct 2026; abstract read, arXiv abs page).
  - It works with Qwen2.5-Coder-7B. "increasing the context count from 1 to 50 increases token use 49-fold without improving unsafe clearance".
  - Raw verifier feedback "raises mean correct model verdicts from 12.7 to 22.0, but also raises incorrect verdicts from one to seven".
  - Models "reject 70-100% of applicable counterexamples".
  - The authors conclude in favour of "deterministic evidence admission".
  - **This is a partial self-challenge of QASecClaw's multi-agent approach**, and its finding that more specialist contexts cost more without helping bears on the project's council-size arm.
- **RepoAudit:** **ICML 2025 per Semantic Scholar** (publicationVenue "International Conference on Machine Learning"). The arXiv record gives no venue, and Crossref title search failed. Semantic Scholar lists 108 citing papers. Opened:
  - *Where AI Vulnerability Discovery Harnesses Go Quiet* ([arXiv 2610.29379](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29379); abstract read). It maps "13 points" where harness steps silently drop vulnerabilities ("a single file that six harnesses dropped before their model saw its code, five of them without notice"). That bears on any filter stage in the project.
  - *Do Coding Agents Catch Their Own Bugs? (They Don't.)* ([arXiv 2611.01181](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01181); abstract read). Foresight-SWE: 212 pairs of PRs where a second PR fixes defects the first introduced. "FRR ranges from only 6.13% to 24.53%". This is a possible seed source; see "New items".

##### Juries, courts and judges of judges (lighter blocks)
- **D3:** **EACL 2026 long paper confirmed.** Crossref for DOI 10.18653/v1/2026.eacl-long.392 gives the 19th EACL, Volume 1. The arXiv journal ref also reads "EACL 2026".
  - **Earlier title confirmed:** the v1 abstract page (v1 dated 7 Oct 2024 in the arXiv API; page read via curl) has the title "Adversarial Multi-Agent Evaluation of Large Language Models through Iterative Debates". v3 (11 Oct 2025) has the current title. This replaces the "snippet only" note.
  - **Citing paper that challenges debate-based judging:** *When Persuasion Overrides Truth in Multi-Agent LLM Debates* ([arXiv 2504.00374](https://arxiv.org/abs/2504.00374); abstract read). With five open models of 3B–14B, "even smaller models can craft persuasive arguments that override truthful answers-often with high confidence". These are small models only.
- **Leveraging LLMs as Meta-Judges:** no venue found (v1 only; Semantic Scholar "arXiv.org"). Semantic Scholar lists 22 citing papers. A survey, *Meta-Judging with Large Language Models: Concepts, Methods, and Challenges* (2601.17312), was seen only as a title.
- **SE-Jury:** **ASE 2025 confirmed**, under a slightly different title. Crossref for DOI 10.1109/ASE63991.2025.00214 gives "SE-Jury: An LLM-as-Ensemble-Judge Metric for Narrowing the Gap with Human Evaluation in SE", by the same nine authors (Xin Zhou ... David Lo), at the "2025 40th IEEE/ACM International Conference on Automated Software Engineering (ASE)". The arXiv v2 title is "An LLM-as-Judge Metric for Bridging the Gap with Human Evaluation in SE Tasks".
- **CodeJudgeBench:** **ACL 2026 long paper confirmed.** Crossref for DOI 10.18653/v1/2026.acl-long.888 gives the "64th Annual Meeting of the Association for Computational Linguistics (Volume 1: Long Papers)". The dataset is public on Hugging Face (`mattymchen/codejudgebench`), and a second set, `mattymchen/codejudgebench-adversarial`, was created 13 Feb 2026 (HF API). I found no paper describing the adversarial set. Citing papers that challenge code judges (abstracts read, arXiv abs page via curl):
  - *Bias in the Loop: Auditing LLM-as-a-Judge for Software Engineering* ([arXiv 2604.16790](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.16790)). It finds that "judge decisions are highly sensitive to prompt biases even when the underlying code snippet is unchanged", with effects "large enough to change task-level conclusions and alter relative model rankings".
  - *Impossible Code, Barely Changed Score* ([arXiv 2611.01572](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01572)). Trained checkpoints "produce invalid Python on 63%-67% of attempts", yet the judge's Pass@32 falls "only about 3 percentage points".
  - *When Is a Multi-Agent Code Judge Actually Grounded?* ([arXiv 2609.30328](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.30328); WiML at NeurIPS 2026 per the arXiv comment). The unmodified MARCH multi-agent verifier "declares both solutions equally good on 78 to 95% of comparisons, reaching 4.4% accuracy where the same model asked directly reaches 43.7%".
  - **Challenged?** Yes, in the sense that later work extends CodeJudgeBench's warning about judge unreliability. Nothing contradicts it.
- **CodeJudge (Tong and Zhang):** **EMNLP 2024 confirmed** (Semantic Scholar DBLP key `conf/emnlp/TongZ24`; arXiv [2410.02184](https://arxiv.org/abs/2410.02184)). Semantic Scholar lists 98 citing papers. *Impossible Code, Barely Changed Score* (above) also cites it.
- **Agent-as-a-Judge:** **ICML 2025 confirmed** (Semantic Scholar DBLP key `conf/icml/Zhuge0AWKXLCKTS25`). This replaces the "snippet only" marking. The arXiv comment links the code (`github.com/metauto-ai/agent-as-a-judge`) and the dataset (`huggingface.co/DEVAI-benchmark`). Semantic Scholar lists 302 citations.
  - **Challenged:** *Grounded but Not Immune: Confirmation Bias in Agent-as-a-judge* ([arXiv 2611.05662](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05662), 3 Nov 2026; abstract read, arXiv abs page). "a single false pre-screening note, attributed to an authority and carrying no evidential bearing on the correct verdict, overturns up to 40% of a tool-using judge's correct verdicts across four judges and five datasets". "For the two Claude judges, the failure is predominantly over-trust (67-75% of harmful flips)", and "Lightweight mitigations blunt but never remove the bias". **This bears directly on the project:** a judge, or an appeal agent, that reads a reviewer's confident note or a judge's dismissal rationale may defer to it even after gathering contrary evidence. See "New items".
  - *Groundability, Not Scale Alone: When Weak Reviewers Can Audit Strong Coding Agents* ([arXiv 2610.01023](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01023); abstract read) finds "Reviewer size is not a consistent predictor of quality" when execution evidence is available.

##### Kumar et al., citing papers
- Semantic Scholar lists 1 citing paper, *Counting the Bandages* (2610.35942; see "When AI Agrees Too Much").

#### New items met along the way

None of these arXiv IDs is in the list of arXiv IDs already cited in the repo (checked by grep). They are ordered roughly by importance to the project.

##### CyberDual: AI Attackers versus AI Code Reviewers in Real Software Maintenance
- **Citation:** Chenyuan Yang, Hwiwon Lee, Jongseong Kim et al. (7 authors); arXiv preprint; submitted 25 Oct 2026; [arXiv 2610.34435](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34435).
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv abs page via curl).
- **What it did:** an arena where attacker agents hide an exploitable bug in real maintenance work on open-source projects and reviewer agents try to find and repair it. "Execution decides each match: we rerun the attacker's proof of concept (PoC) on the code that comes out of review." The windows "can be rebuilt each quarter from the latest upstream history".
- **Key numbers:** "Three agentic reviewers catch 85-91% of 1,088 valid attacks from six attackers". "When they miss, Claude reviewers mostly approve the attack, while GPT-6-Sol mostly repairs the wrong code; **a reviewer from a second vendor catches about two thirds of a Claude reviewer's misses**". "GLM 5.3 in Claude Code already gets an exploit past Sonnet 5 in 17% of its attack slots."
- **Connection:** supports and overlaps. It is direct evidence for cross-family reviewers on planted (attacker-hidden) bugs scored by execution, and its quarterly rebuild is a contamination control like the project's SWE-bench-Live check. It differs from the project in having no judge or appeal stage and no evolving configurations.
- **Novelty threat:** medium. It is the strongest published cross-vendor complementarity number on hidden bugs, and the project should cite it and position its councils against it.

##### Grounded but Not Immune: Confirmation Bias in Agent-as-a-judge
- **Citation:** Drake Benjamin Brown, Khushbu Pahwa, Yubin Ge et al. (8 authors); arXiv preprint ("Preprint. Under Review"); 3 Nov 2026; [arXiv 2611.05662](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05662).
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv abs page via curl).
- **What it did:** a single false "pre-screening note" attributed to an authority is injected into tool-using judges, and the authors classify how the judges' trajectories change.
- **Key numbers:** it "overturns up to 40% of a tool-using judge's correct verdicts across four judges and five datasets". "For the two Claude judges, the failure is predominantly over-trust (67-75% of harmful flips)."
- **Connection:** challenges any design where the appeal agent sees the judge's dismissal rationale, or the judge sees reviewers' confident labels. It argues for blinding the appeal agent to the judge's reasoning, or at least testing that as an arm.
- **Novelty threat:** none. It is a judge-reliability risk, not a competing study.

##### SecPRBench: Can Agentic Code Review Catch Vulnerabilities Before They Are Merged?
- **Citation:** Yihan Yang, Chenyuan Yang, Zijie Zhao et al. (8 authors); arXiv preprint; v1 24 Oct 2026, v2 30 Oct 2026; [arXiv 2610.33509](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33509). The comment gives "Benchmark: https://github.com/secprbench/secprbench Results website: https://secprbench.github.io" (not opened).
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API).
- **What it did:** 265 validated real-world commits that introduced vulnerabilities, from nine systems including Firefox, V8 and OpenSSL, harvested by an agentic pipeline. LLM-as-a-judge credits findings that match the key.
- **Key numbers:** "recall ranges from 32.5% to 78.1%". "in 68% of them [misses], the agent reports on a file that holds the flaw but argues for a different mechanism". "OpenAI's Codex Security lowers recall by 8.3 points, mostly by abstaining, while Anthropic's Defending Code harness raises it by 7.7 points, at a higher cost."
- **Connection:** overlaps with the scoring side (real introducing commits, a judge-matched key and a public results site). The 68% "right file, wrong mechanism" finding bears on how strictly the project's judge matches a finding to a seed.
- **Novelty threat:** low. Single agents, security only.

##### AFCRA: Adaptive Code Revision Attacks on AI Pull Request Reviewers
- **Citation:** Jingzhi Gong, Jie M. Zhang, Gunel Jahangirova and Meng Wang; arXiv preprint; 4 Oct 2026; [arXiv 2610.05399](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.05399).
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API).
- **What it did:** an attacker uses the AI reviewer's feedback to revise code so that the reported problem is fixed while a vulnerability survives. It is built on AFCRA-Bench, 159 disclosed vulnerabilities with executable exploits.
- **Key numbers:** "Across five-round interactions with Sonnet 5 and GPT-5.5 reviewers, AFCRA reaches success rates 2.5x and 12.5x those of the strongest evaluated text- or comment-based attack."
- **Connection:** loosely related to seeds, but bears on sequential review: feedback across rounds leaks what will pass. It is relevant if the project ever lets the bug author respond to reviewers.
- **Novelty threat:** none.

##### Trusting the Tool: Asymmetric Deference of LLM Vulnerability Auditors to Static-Analysis Evidence
- **Citation:** ZhiHong Liu and Shuyao Gao; arXiv preprint ("Submitted to IEEE Access"); 23 Oct 2026; [arXiv 2610.32876](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32876).
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API).
- **What it did:** it audited 916 pairs of vulnerable and patched PrimeVul functions under six evidence conditions, including fabricated assurances and fabricated alarms. It covers 11 model configurations from 4 vendors and 16,572 audits, with an internally registered replication.
- **Key numbers:** "false assurances lowered the detection of real vulnerabilities by up to 19 points, whereas false alarms never significantly lowered accuracy". "Verifying each claim in a separate call and showing the refuted claims raised accuracy under false assurances by 12 points in a pre-specified test (p=0.002)."
- **Connection:** supports the appeal's purpose. A judge that reads "this is fine" claims is pushed toward wrong dismissals far more than toward wrong keeps, which is the same asymmetry as "When AI Agrees Too Much" v1. The claim-by-claim verification arm is a design the project could borrow.
- **Novelty threat:** none.

##### Prior Audit-Repair Context Shifts LLM Verifier Thresholds Toward Leniency
- **Citation:** Parsa Mazaheri and Kasra Mazaheri; arXiv preprint; 17 Aug 2026; [arXiv 2608.16003](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.16003).
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv abs page via curl).
- **What it did:** it measured checker false alarms on traces that humans verified as correct, with and without an earlier audit-and-repair episode in context.
- **Key numbers:** "lowers false alarms in 15 of 15 model x wording combinations, by 2.8 to 11.5 percentage points". "the criterion moves in 15 of 15 combinations and survives correction in 13 while d' survives in none."
- **Connection:** bears on the judge and appeal design. Agents that carry earlier rounds in context shift their threshold rather than their discrimination. A judge reused across rounds, or an appeal agent that saw the judge's work, may be systematically more lenient, which supports fresh contexts per stage.
- **Novelty threat:** none.

##### CRJudgeBench: Can AI Detect Plausible but Invalid Code Reviews?
- **Citation:** Yue Pan, Jiawei Li, Ziyuan Zhang, Xiangxin Zhao and He Ye; arXiv preprint ("Under review at ICLR 2027"); 29 Sep 2026; [arXiv 2609.37216](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.37216). The dataset is at `huggingface.co/datasets/dcloud347/CRJudgeBenchmark` (per the arXiv comment; not opened).
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API).
- **What it did:** 1,199 instances of trustworthy review comments and "plausible but untrustworthy" ones (expert-verified perturbations of real PRs). It trains Sentinel, a repository-grounded agentic judge.
- **Key numbers:** "Sentinel achieves 76.60% accuracy, outperforming GLM-5.3 by 6.13 percentage points and its base model by 19.78 points" on the 359-instance test set.
- **Connection:** overlaps with the project's judge role (deciding whether a finding is real) and gives a public benchmark for calibrating a council judge outside the planted-bug setting.
- **Novelty threat:** low.

##### The Specification as Quality Gate: Three Hypotheses on AI-Assisted Code Review
- **Citation:** Christo Zietsman; arXiv preprint; 26 Mar 2026; [arXiv 2603.25773](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.25773).
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv abs page via curl).
- **What it did:** it argues that AI review without executable specifications is "structurally circular", and runs "three small contrived experiments". "The first two experiments are same-family (Claude reviewing Claude-generated code); the third extends to a cross-family panel of four models from three families. All use a planted bug corpus".
- **Key numbers:** none in the abstract. The author calls them "directional evidence, not a controlled demonstration".
- **Connection:** overlaps with the project's central same-family question (Claude writes, plants and reviews) on planted bugs, but at very small scale.
- **Novelty threat:** low. Small and argumentative, but it should be cited as a prior statement of the circularity worry.

##### JuryProbe: An Empirical Consensus-Risk Diagnostic for Routing Reference-Free Factuality Judge Panels to Grounded Verification
- **Citation:** Tianxin Zhou and Ruixi Lin; TMLR (accepted 08/2026 per the arXiv comment); v2 27 Aug 2026; [arXiv 2608.20607](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20607).
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv abs page via curl).
- **What it did:** it measures false-negative-only correlation among panel judges and routes high-risk unanimous accepts to grounded checking.
- **Key numbers:** "FN-only correlations 0.402 and 0.368; lifts 3.13x and 18.13x".
- **Connection:** challenges PoLL-style independence, and its accept-conditioned grounding is an analogue of escalating unanimous dismissals to an appeal.
- **Novelty threat:** none (factuality, not code).

##### Swapping the Judge Changes the Ranking
- **Citation:** Sangdo Han and Moonsoo Kim; arXiv preprint (preregistered); 3 Nov 2026; [arXiv 2611.04090](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04090).
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv abs page via curl).
