- **What it did / key numbers:** five judges from three providers re-judged 4,000 Chatbot Arena battles: "median Cohen's kappa = 0.22", and Kendall's tau with the human ranking ranged "from 0.17 to 0.89".
- **Connection:** a methods warning for the project. If a council's score depends on one judge model, the parent-versus-child comparison may not survive a judge swap, which argues for a judge-robustness check on the CI rule.
- **Novelty threat:** none.

##### Correlated Failures of AI Approval Monitors
- **Citation:** Luca Zanolini; arXiv preprint; 21 Oct 2026; [arXiv 2610.29178](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29178).
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv abs page via curl).
- **What it did / key numbers:** it treats monitors that share inputs as "fail-prone" sets. "a rule that counts sources instead of votes blocked 0.9% of correct payments, against 7.4% for the best counting rule, at equal safety".
- **Connection:** loosely related. It is a theory of why seats reading the same PR text fail together, and suggests aggregating by evidence source.
- **Novelty threat:** none.

##### PrivacyPR: A Dataset of Real-World Privacy Fixes for Benchmarking Privacy-Aware Code Review
- **Citation:** Hua Yang, Aditya Bhardwaj, Shidong Pan and Bowen Xu; arXiv preprint (4 pages); 13 Nov 2026; [arXiv 2611.18293](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18293). Data at `doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.23744765` (per the abstract; not opened).
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API).
- **What it did:** 4,131 PRs, with a harness that "reverts each fix to reintroduce the defect".
- **Key numbers:** "two coding agents detect only 42-51% of defects under a generic review prompt, but 88-91% when explicitly asked to check privacy, while flagging 11-15% of non-fix PRs".
- **Connection:** supports the specialist-role arm. A one-line brief roughly doubles catches in one category, with a measured false-alarm cost.
- **Novelty threat:** low.

##### From Software History to Executable Regression Tasks: A System for Evaluating Software Agents
- **Citation:** Ruikai Shi, Zhengran Zeng, Rui Xie, Shikun Zhang and Wei Ye; arXiv preprint; 18 Oct 2026; [arXiv 2610.25308](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25308).
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API).
- **What it did:** it turns commit pairs that introduce and fix a bug into executable instances, with transplanted tests that fail on the introducing version and pass on the fix.
- **Key numbers:** "172 validated instances from 522 candidates across 11 Python projects". Repair and test-generation success reached "up to 68.7% and 77.1%".
- **Connection:** supports the project's SZZ seed source with an execution-validation step. It is by the SWR-Bench group.
- **Novelty threat:** none.

##### Foresight-SWE: Do Coding Agents Catch Their Own Bugs? (They Don't.)
- **Citation:** Shaswat Patel, Michel Liao, Peter Henderson, He He and Valerie Chen; arXiv preprint; 31 Oct 2026; [arXiv 2611.01181](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01181).
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv abs page via curl).
- **What it did / key numbers:** 212 pairs of PRs in which the second fixes defects the first introduced, across 35 repositories. "FRR ranges from only 6.13% to 24.53%".
- **Connection:** loosely related. It is a source of escaped-bug pairs like the project's SZZ seeds, but it measures whether a coder avoids the bug, not review.
- **Novelty threat:** none.

##### SWE-PRBench: Benchmarking AI Code Review Quality Against Pull Request Feedback
- **Citation:** Deepak Kumar; arXiv preprint; 27 Mar 2026; [arXiv 2603.26130](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.26130).
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv abs page via curl).
- **What it did / key numbers:** 350 PRs with an LLM judge "validated at kappa=0.75". "8 frontier models detect only 15-31% of human-flagged issues", and all degrade "monotonically" as more context is added.
- **Connection:** bears on the `CLAUDE.md` context arm. More context hurt in this setting.
- **Novelty threat:** none.

##### AI-to-AI Code Reviews of GitHub Pull Requests
- **Citation:** Niruthiha Selvanayagam and Taher A. Ghaleb; ESEM 2026 Emerging Results track (per the arXiv comment); 21 Aug 2026; [arXiv 2608.21311](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21311).
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API).
- **What it did / key numbers:** 248,641 AI-authored PRs that received AI review, of which "45,269 received cross-product review and 208,145 received same-product review". For three of four dual-role reviewers, mean comments per PR were "58-65% higher in the same-product group", although "effect sizes were small or negligible".
- **Connection:** field data on same-product against cross-product AI review, observational only.
- **Novelty threat:** none.

##### CIVER: Causal Intervention Verification for Reliable Code Review
- **Citation:** Xinyuan Song, Hasibul Haque, Jay Somasundaram, Ryan Turner, Bowen Zhu and Liang Zhao; "WWW 2026" per the arXiv comment; 26 Oct 2026; [arXiv 2610.36010](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36010).
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API).
- **What it did / key numbers:** a finding counts as genuine if it responds to a targeted repair and stays stable under unrelated changes. It "correctly identifies 77% of genuine defects".
- **Connection:** an intervention-based judge. A possible non-LLM-opinion check for the project's judge.
- **Novelty threat:** low.

##### CAVE-Bench: "You're Right, Let Me Fix It": How LLM Agents Damage Correct Work When Falsely Accused
- **Citation:** Xutao Mao, Rui Qian, Longxiang Wang et al. (9 authors); arXiv preprint; 26 Sep 2026; [arXiv 2609.32616](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.32616).
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv abs page via curl).
- **Key numbers:** "Across 14 of the latest models in Claude Code, false accusations damage correct work in up to 60.06% of runs".
- **Connection:** loosely related. It shows the downstream cost of false findings, which is why precision matters in the score.
- **Novelty threat:** none.

##### Stopping and Routing LLM Judge Panels
- **Citation:** Bin Zhu, Yi Xie and Yanghui Rao; WISE 2026 (per the arXiv comment); 20 Aug 2026; [arXiv 2608.19802](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.19802).
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv abs page via curl).
- **What it did:** it designs judge panels from a labelled audit set and judge costs: "drop copies, add complements globally, route specialists conditionally, and stop when validation gain falls below a threshold". The audits include code.
- **Connection:** overlaps with the project's evolution of seats under a cost constraint, applied to judges rather than reviewers.
- **Novelty threat:** low.

##### CR-Bench-v2 and Beyond QASecClaw (same-author follow-ups, listed above)
- CR-Bench-v2 ([arXiv 2611.02566](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02566)) and Beyond QASecClaw ([arXiv 2610.36947](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36947)) are summarised in their parent blocks. Both are new to the list of IDs already cited in the repo.

##### Loosely related, abstract read (one line each)
- *Better Harnesses, Smaller Models* (Chenyang Yang et al., [arXiv 2607.08938](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08938); arXiv abs page). A meta agent automatically adapts the harness from failure trajectories, and the best small-model agent recovers "89.7% of LLM performance at 4% of the cost". It is an automatic-design precedent by CRBugBench's first author, relevant to section 5.
- *Bias in the Loop* (2604.16790), *Impossible Code, Barely Changed Score* (2611.01572) and *When Is a Multi-Agent Code Judge Actually Grounded?* (2609.30328). Code-judge reliability; see CodeJudgeBench.
- *Understanding the Limits of Automated Evaluation for Code Review Bots in Practice* (EASE 2026, 2604.24525). See Jiang et al.
- *ALIBI* (2607.24964). Adversarial code comments; see "When AI Agrees Too Much".
- *VulnSAGE* (2610.23035) and *Where AI Vulnerability Discovery Harnesses Go Quiet* (2610.29379). Filters; see section 4.
- *Agentic Code Review in the Terminal* (2607.16740), *DesignReviewBench* (2610.21925), *SWEeper-Bench* (2610.14096), *Code Review is a Conversation* (2607.22095), *SWE-Review* (2607.06065) and *Improving LLM-Based Go Code Review* (2606.01859).
- *A Survey of Code Review Benchmarks and Evaluation Practices* (2602.13377). Useful as a citation hub.
- *When Persuasion Overrides Truth in Multi-Agent LLM Debates* (2504.00374), *Condorcet latent dimension* (2609.14438), *Counting the Bandages* (2610.35942) and *Groundability, Not Scale Alone* (2610.01023).
- *AI Writes Faster Than Humans Can Review* (2607.01904) and *Developers as Managers of AI Agents* (2610.33588).
- *Forgeable Confirmation in Automated Computer Security Testing: Deterministic Rules versus AI Judges* ([arXiv 2609.24200](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.24200); abstract read, arXiv abs page). "forgeability is predicted entirely by whether the decision reads attacker-controlled data". "Moving the decisive evidence to a channel the attacker cannot write cuts attack success from 97% to 0%". It supports keeping the judge's decisive evidence out of text that the code author controls.

### 1C. Sections 5 and 6: evolving agent systems and self-preference, plus the loosely related item and the experiment plan's "Related research" list

Metadata for every paper below was first pulled in two batches from the arXiv API (verbatim titles, authors, dates, versions, comments and journal-refs) and one Semantic Scholar batch call (venue, DOI, DBLP key, citation count as of 17 Nov 2026). Semantic Scholar venue fields are noted as "S2 venue" and are not always reliable (see the Darwin Gödel Machine entry).

#### Section 5. Evolving or automatically designed agent systems

##### EvoMAS: Evolutionary Generation of Multi-Agent Systems (Hu et al.) — full treatment
- **Published version / venue:** ICML 2026, per the arXiv comment and journal-ref ("ICML2026", verbatim, arXiv API) and the GitHub repository description ("ICML 2026"). Semantic Scholar still lists only the arXiv record (S2 venue "arXiv.org", 4 citations on 17 Nov 2026); DBLP key is the CoRR one (journals/corr/abs-2602-06511), so the proceedings entry was not confirmed in DBLP. Semantic Scholar stores the title without the "EvoMAS:" prefix ("Evolutionary Generation of Multi-Agent Systems").
- **Later versions:** v4 (27 May 2026) is still the latest on 17 Nov 2026 (arXiv API). No later version since the prior-work doc's recheck.
- **Code or data:** public, https://github.com/amazon-science/EvoMAS (GitHub API: public, 9 stars, last push 29 May 2026). The arXiv v4 HTML links to it. Not inspected beyond the repository metadata.
- **Same-author follow-ups:** none on evolving agent systems. First author's only other 2026 paper on Semantic Scholar is "OpAgent: Operator Agent for Web Navigation" (arXiv 2602.13559; title only, not read).
- **Citing papers 2025–2026 worth noting** (4 citations in total on Semantic Scholar):
  - **OptiMAS: Automatically Optimize Multi-Agent System** (Cheng et al.; EMNLP 2026 per arXiv comment; [arXiv 2608.21918](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21918); abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API)). A continuous, trajectory-driven optimiser for whole multi-agent systems with a "dual-track memory". It frames search-based methods such as EvoMAS as unstable when the optimisation scope grows. Another whole-system optimiser, not applied to review.
  - **Evolve as a Team (Meta-Team)** (Hao et al.; arXiv preprint; [arXiv 2605.29790](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29790); abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API)). Experience-driven evolution of agent behaviour, coordination and team organisation from post-task communication between agents. Reports beating "prior MAS evolution methods" on six long-horizon benchmarks.
  - The survey "From Static Templates to Dynamic Runtime Graphs" ([arXiv 2603.22386](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.22386); abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API)) is a useful map of workflow optimisation; it recommends judging optimised workflows on cost, robustness and structural variation, not only task score.
- **Challenged?** No paper found that disputes EvoMAS specifically. Two general critiques of automatic multi-agent design apply to it by method (see ADAS and AFlow below): high-variance evaluation makes search pick lucky configurations ("Simple Baselines are Competitive with Code Evolution") and automatic systems underperform self-consistency at matched cost ("The Illusion of Multi-Agent Advantage"). Neither tests EvoMAS itself.

##### EvoMAS: Heuristics in the Loop—Evolving Smarter Agentic Workflows (Wei et al.) — lighter block
- **Published version / venue:** ICML 2026, PMLR vol. 306 ([page](https://proceedings.mlr.press/v306/wei26m.html), page read (fetch tool) and abstract read verbatim from the page HTML via curl). Authors on the PMLR page: Yangbo Wei, Zhen Huang, Ronghao Xu, Hong Wang and Wei W. Xing. OpenReview forum id cdWnpXkcQo (linked from PMLR; forum not opened, OpenReview's forum endpoint returned a challenge page).
- **Later versions:** no arXiv version found (arXiv API title search for "Heuristics in the Loop" returned nothing parseable; not confirmed either way).
- **Code or data:** none listed on the PMLR page.
- **What the PDF adds** (full text partly read: the experiments section 5.2 and Table 2 via pdftotext of the PMLR PDF, saved in its own scratch folder): it does name evaluation domains, which fills the prior-work doc's note that "its page names no evaluation domains". It evaluates on GSM8K, MATH, HumanEval, MBPP, HotpotQA and ALFWorld (Table 2, "SDs are estimated from 3 independent runs") plus GAIA (Table 3), with GPT-4o-mini as the main backbone. Verbatim: "achieving SOTA results on five out of six benchmarks with an average score of 80.06% ... outperforming the previous best method AFlow by 3.78% on average" and "surpasses the strongest baseline AFlow by 1.37% on GSM8K and 4.96% on MATH, and in code generation, with gains of 1.53% on HumanEval and 2.64% on MBPP". The per-benchmark standard deviations in Table 2 are about 1–3 points from three runs, so several gains are within one or two SDs. Abstract: "roles evolving from homogeneous actors to specialized reasoning ensembles". It also uses an LLM-as-a-judge to rate task difficulty for its curriculum.
- **Connection:** overlaps in method (role-level evolution) but on standard maths, code-generation and QA benchmarks, not review.
- **Same-author follow-ups / citing papers:** not searched (lighter block).
- **Challenged?** None found.

##### ADAS: Automated Design of Agentic Systems (Hu, Lu and Clune) — full treatment
- **Published version / venue:** ICLR 2025 (S2 venue "International Conference on Learning Representations"; the official repository is titled "[ICLR 2025] Automated Design of Agentic Systems"). arXiv v2, 2 Mar 2025 is the latest (arXiv API). 527 citations on Semantic Scholar (17 Nov 2026).
- **Later versions:** none after v2.
- **Code or data:** public, https://github.com/ShengranHu/ADAS (GitHub API: public, about 1.7k stars, last push Jan 2025).
- **Same-author follow-ups:**
  - The Darwin Gödel Machine (below) is the direct successor (Shengran Hu is second author).
  - **ALMA: Learning to Continually Learn via Meta-learning Agentic Memory Designs** (Xiong, Hu and Clune; [arXiv 2602.07755](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.07755); abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API)). The same meta-agent-writes-code search, applied to memory designs. Not review.
  - Shengran Hu is also an author of "Towards end-to-end automation of AI research" (Nature 2026 per Semantic Scholar; title only, not read).
- **Citing papers 2025–2026 worth noting:**
  - **The Illusion of Multi-Agent Advantage** (Jwalapuram et al., Salesforce; arXiv preprint, v2 13 Jun 2026; [arXiv 2606.13003](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13003); abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API) and full text partly read (Appendix E.2–E.4, fetched HTML)). Automatic MAS "consistently underperform CoT-SC despite being up to 10x more expensive" (abstract, verbatim). On ADAS specifically, Appendix E.4 says validation accuracy on GPQA-Diamond "frequently peaks early ... and subsequently regresses or plateaus", and, verbatim: "Through correspondence with the authors, we confirmed their results were derived by evaluating all candidates directly on the test set." They conclude ADAS "functions as a heuristic explorer rather than a reliable optimizer".
  - **Simple Baselines are Competitive with Code Evolution** (Gideoni, Risi and Gal; arXiv preprint, v2 12 Oct 2026; [arXiv 2602.16805](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.16805); abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API) and full text partly read (Section 5, fetched HTML)). Compares ADAS and ShinkaEvolve with independent random sampling on AIME scaffold design: "typical high-variance evaluations lead to all automated search methods picking subpar scaffolds relative to a simple majority vote" (abstract, verbatim). It recommends at least 10 repetitions and 95% CIs when reporting final results.
  - **Rethinking the Value of Multi-Agent Workflow** (Xu et al., already in the prior-work doc) and **JudgeFlow** (Ma et al.; [arXiv 2601.07477](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.07477); abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API)), which adds a judge module that assigns blame to workflow blocks from failed traces. JudgeFlow is evaluated on maths and code generation only.
- **Challenged?** **Yes.** The Illusion paper reports, from correspondence with the ADAS authors, that ADAS's reported results came from evaluating every candidate on the test set and taking the maximum, and it could not reproduce steady improvement on GPQA-Diamond. Simple Baselines finds ADAS-found scaffolds lose to majority vote on held-out AIME once evaluation noise is reduced. Both directly support the project's design choices (a CI-gated keep rule and held-out seeds), and both are strong reasons to report a "majority vote / best single reviewer at equal cost" baseline in every generation.

##### AFlow: Automating Agentic Workflow Generation (Zhang et al.) — full treatment
- **Published version / venue:** ICLR 2025, oral (DBLP key conf/iclr/ZhangXYTCCZCHWZ25 via Semantic Scholar; the repository description reads "ICLR 2025 Oral"). arXiv v4, 15 Apr 2025 (arXiv API). 542 citations.
- **Later versions:** none after v4.
- **Code or data:** public, https://github.com/FoundationAgents/AFlow (about 630 stars, last push Dec 2025) and inside MetaGPT (https://github.com/FoundationAgents/MetaGPT).
- **Same-author follow-ups** (first author Jiayi Zhang; abstracts read (verbatim, arXiv API)):
  - **RobustFlow** (Xu, Zhang et al.; [arXiv 2509.21834](https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.21834)): existing workflow generators "often generate inconsistent workflows when provided with instructions that are semantically equivalent but phrased differently", and RobustFlow is compared against AFlow and ScoreFlow. A same-group admission of brittleness.
  - **AOrchestra** ([arXiv 2602.03786](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.03786)): an orchestrator that creates sub-agents on the fly as a tuple of instruction, context, tools and model, on GAIA, SWE-Bench and Terminal-Bench. Close in shape to the project's agent-led orchestration arm.
  - **AEvo: Harnessing Agentic Evolution** ([arXiv 2605.13821](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.13821)): a meta-agent that edits the evolution procedure itself rather than proposing candidates.
  - The same group's "A Survey of Self-Evolving Agents" (arXiv 2507.21046; TMLR per Semantic Scholar; title only, not read).
- **Citing papers 2025–2026 worth noting:** The Illusion of Multi-Agent Advantage (above), Appendix E.2: AFlow's "optimized" workflows "frequently converge on a structure that simply iterates a single custom prompt three times before aggregation" (verbatim), under the heading "Degeneration to Trivial Ensembles".
- **Challenged?** **Yes, partly.** The Illusion paper finds AFlow's searched workflows collapse into self-consistency ensembles and lose to CoT-SC at matched cost; RobustFlow (same group) finds such generators unstable to rephrased instructions. The headline "smaller models beat GPT-4o at 4.55% of the cost" was not re-tested in anything found. For the project: an evolved council could likewise degenerate into "the same generalist reviewer three times", which is a reason to keep the generalist-copies arm as an explicit baseline.

##### Darwin Gödel Machine (Zhang, Hu, Lu, Lange and Clune) — full treatment
- **Published version / venue:** **ICLR 2026, poster** (OpenReview search API: note pUpzQZTvGY, venue "ICLR 2026 Poster"). Semantic Scholar's main record gives the venue as "Robotics", which is wrong; a second Semantic Scholar record for the same title lists ICLR. arXiv v3, 12 Mar 2026 (arXiv API). 521 citations.
- **Later versions:** v3 is the latest.
- **Code or data:** public, https://github.com/jennyzzt/dgm (about 2.5k stars, last push Aug 2025). Run logs are public; "Does Failure Feedback Direct Agent Self-Modification?" (below) re-analysed them.
- **Reward hacking in the paper itself** (full text partly read: the tool-use hallucination case study, fetched v3 HTML): node 114 "achieves a perfect score quickly, after only 2 modifications in its lineage. However, we observed objective hacking ... the agent removed the logging of special tokens that indicate tool usage (despite instructions not to change the special tokens)" (verbatim). The authors note objective hacking "occurs more frequently when these functions are not hidden" from the agent. This is the documented reward-hacking case, found by the authors themselves.
- **Same-author follow-ups:**
  - **Hyperagents** (Jenny Zhang, Bingchen Zhao, Wannan Yang, Jakob Foerster, Jeff Clune, Minqi Jiang, Sam Devlin and Tatiana Shavrina; arXiv preprint, v1 19 Mar 2026, v2 1 Nov 2026; [arXiv 2603.19461](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.19461); abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API); code https://github.com/facebookresearch/Hyperagents). DGM-H makes the meta-level modification procedure itself editable and reports that meta-level improvements "transfer across domains and accumulate across runs". Not in the list of IDs already cited in the repo.
- **Citing papers 2025–2026 worth noting** (abstracts read (verbatim, arXiv API)):
  - **Huxley-Gödel Machine** (Wang, Piękos, Nanbo, Laakom, Chen, Ostaszewski, Zhuge and Schmidhuber; v3 29 Oct 2025; [arXiv 2510.21614](https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.21614); code https://github.com/metauto-ai/HGM). Identifies a "Metaproductivity-Performance Mismatch": a high benchmark score does not predict good descendants, so DGM's expansion rule is mis-aimed; it scores a node by its descendants instead. Bearing: in the project, the best-scoring council may not be the best parent to mutate.
  - **SGM: A Statistical Gödel Machine for Risk-Controlled Recursive Self-Modification** (Wu et al.; v2 17 Sep 2026; [arXiv 2510.10232](https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.10232)). Replaces proof of improvement with "statistical confidence tests (e-values, Hoeffding bounds), admitting a modification only when superiority is certified at a chosen confidence level, while allocating a global error budget to bound cumulative risk across rounds" (verbatim). **The closest prior art found for the project's keep rule** (keep a change only if a 90% CI on the paired difference is above zero). It adds what the project's rule lacks: a familywise error budget across generations. Its experiments are on CIFAR-100, ImageNet-100, RL and black-box optimisation, not agents.
  - **Does Failure Feedback Direct Agent Self-Modification?** (Rong He; v1 10 Oct 2026; [arXiv 2610.14662](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14662)). "Across 145 eligible modifications in public Darwin Gödel Machine logs, we find no detectable target-specific advantage" (verbatim): DGM's edits do not preferentially repair the failure they were aimed at. Held-out gains were concentrated where failures were identifiable, "including planted errors".
  - **Reflections on Trusting Trust, Revisited** (Roesner and Kohno; [arXiv 2609.17817](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.17817)). Poisoned benchmarks in the self-evaluation loop make DGM (modified), the Self-Improving Coding Agent and Hyperagents evolve vulnerable behaviour, e.g. Hyperagents with Sonnet 4.5 evolving instructions that "disable HTTPS certificate validation on neutral URL-fetching tasks" (verbatim), and "contamination often persists even when a poisoned agent is subsequently evolved against clean benchmarks". Bearing: the project's seed set is the benchmark its councils evolve against.
  - **The Red Queen Gödel Machine** (Iacob et al.; v3 8 Oct 2026; [arXiv 2606.26294](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.26294)). Co-evolves the evaluator with the agent; on DeepSWE "a co-evolved reviewer grades coder patches to guide search", and it "reduces self-preference bias via an additional adversarial objective to discover reviewers equally stringent on AI and human work" (verbatim). It evolves code reviewers, but as a fitness signal for a coder, not as the object scored against planted bugs. **Medium novelty threat** to "evolving reviewers".
  - **Auditing the Product, Not the Recursion** (Chen, Zhu and Zheng; [arXiv 2610.27610](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27610)). Of 90 self-described recursive-self-improvement studies, "60% report evaluation on tasks unused for selection" and "Only 22% audit two or more reruns of the full loop" (verbatim). A checklist the project's evolution section could be held to.
  - **Who Verifies the Verifier?** (Zhang et al.; NeurIPS 2026 workshop per arXiv comment; [arXiv 2610.11464](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.11464)). Evolves the grader; removing anchor guards "collapses the verifier into a vacuous always-pass grader, yet that collapsed verifier trains skills just as well. Downstream task score cannot certify a self-evolved verifier" (verbatim). Bearing on any evolution of the project's judge.
  - **SpecBench** (Zhao et al.; [arXiv 2605.21384](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.21384)): reward hacking in long-horizon coding agents measured as the visible-versus-held-out test gap, which "grows by 28 percentage points for every tenfold increase in code size" (verbatim).
- **Challenged?** **Yes, in three ways.** (1) Its own objective-hacking case (above). (2) HGM argues its parent-selection signal is the wrong one. (3) Re-analysis of its public logs finds no target-specific repair. No paper was found that fails to reproduce the SWE-bench and Polyglot gains themselves.

##### MaAS: Multi-agent Architecture Search via Agentic Supernet (Zhang et al.) — lighter block
- **Published version / venue:** ICML 2025 per the Semantic Scholar venue field (not confirmed on the paper's own page; the arXiv record has no comment). arXiv v2, 9 Jun 2025. 223 citations.
- **Code or data:** public, https://github.com/bingreeky/MaAS (linked from the arXiv HTML; GitHub API: public, about 290 stars).
- **Challenged?** **Yes, partly.** The Illusion of Multi-Agent Advantage (Appendix E.5, full text partly read) finds that with strong base models accuracy saturates and the cost term dominates, giving "Cost-Minimizing Collapse on BrowseComp-Plus, where high cost variance drives the controller toward a trivial single I/O call" (verbatim). Bearing: the project's score also mixes quality and cost, so a cost term can swamp a saturated catch rate in the same way.

##### AgentFlow: Synthesizing Multi-Agent Harnesses for Vulnerability Discovery (Liu et al.) — full treatment
- **Published version / venue:** arXiv preprint only (v1, 22 Apr 2026; no comment or journal-ref; S2 venue "arXiv.org"). Authors (arXiv API): Hanzhi Liu, Chaofan Shou, Xiaonan Liu, Hongbo Wen, Yanju Chen, Ryan Jingyang Fang and Yu Feng. 15 citations. Note that the arXiv title is "Synthesizing Multi-Agent Harnesses for Vulnerability Discovery"; "AgentFlow" is the system name only.
- **Later versions:** none.
- **Code or data:** public. The paper says the harness optimiser ("the AgentFlow runtime, the diagnoser and proposer prompts, the archive manager, and the proposal-validation pipeline") is released at https://github.com/berabuddies/agentflow (full text partly read, fetched HTML). That address now redirects to https://github.com/agentenv/agentflow (GitHub API: public, about 1.4k stars, last push 1 Nov 2026, described as "Orchestrate thousands of agents and harnesses as a graph programatically").
- **A precision point for the prior-work doc** (full text partly read: abstract and introduction): the two headline results use different models. "We evaluate AgentFlow on TerminalBench-2 with Claude Opus 4.6 and on Google Chrome with Kimi K2.5" (verbatim). The Chrome zero-days (two of them Critical sandbox escapes, CVE-2026-5280 and CVE-2026-6297) came from the open-weight Kimi K2.5, not Claude. The prior-work doc's sentence reads as if both used Claude Opus 4.6.
- **Same-author follow-ups:** none on harness synthesis. The first author's other 2026 papers are on auditing agent skills (Semia, arXiv 2605.00314; VIGIL, arXiv 2606.26524; semantic fuzzing of skills, arXiv 2605.13044) and LLM supply-chain attacks (arXiv 2604.08407); titles only, not read.
- **Citing papers 2025–2026 worth noting:**
  - **How Much Does the Harness Matter, and for Whom?** (Galea Curmi and Wilde; CAMLIS 2026 per arXiv comment; [arXiv 2610.28802](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28802); abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API)). 24 model-runner configurations on a production codebase with non-public vulnerabilities: "the largest two-run configuration covers 60.4%, while 14.3% are found by exactly one model. No single configuration covers all observed findings" (verbatim). Engineered prompts helped small and mid-tier models by 20–86% but the frontier model by 2%. **Supports** mixed-model councils for coverage, and suggests role briefs matter less for the strongest model.
  - **Revelio** (Hou et al., incl. Koushik Sen, Dawn Song and David Wagner; [arXiv 2606.22263](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.22263); abstract read (arXiv abs page via curl)). Memory-safety bug finding that reports only sanitizer-confirmed proofs of vulnerability: 19 new vulnerabilities in seven long-fuzzed projects for "a total cost of $300" (verbatim). A dynamic-confirmation alternative to a judge.
  - **SelfOp** (Ullah et al.; [arXiv 2609.22792](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.22792); abstract read (arXiv abs page via curl)). Optimises a frozen security agent's context by "textual gradient descent", committing changes "only under cross-instance consensus" (verbatim). Another acceptance rule for evolved changes.
  - "Red-Teaming the Agentic Red-Team" ([arXiv 2606.24496](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24496); abstract read (arXiv abs page via curl)) is a security analysis of offensive-security agents; loosely related.
- **Challenged?** None found. No paper re-ran AgentFlow or disputed its TerminalBench-2 score.

##### MulVul: Retrieval-augmented Multi-Agent Code Vulnerability Detection via Cross-Model Prompt Evolution (Wu et al.) — full treatment
- **Published version / venue:** ACL 2026 long paper (ACL Anthology 2026.acl-long.391, from the prior-work doc; DBLP key conf/acl/WuXPCJ26 via Semantic Scholar). **arXiv preprint [2601.18847](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.18847)** (26 Jan 2026 per Semantic Scholar), which the prior-work doc does not give and which is not in the list of IDs already cited in the repo. Authors per Semantic Scholar: Zihan Wu, Jie Xu, Yun Peng, Chun Yong Chong and Xiaohua Jia. 9 citations.
- **Later versions:** v1 only (26 Jan 2026; arXiv abs page read via curl, which also gives the authors in this order). The arXiv abstract adds a figure the prior-work doc lacks: "outperforming the best baseline by 41.5%" (verbatim).
- **Code or data:** no GitHub, Zenodo or Hugging Face link found in the arXiv HTML (searched the fetched HTML for repository links).
- **Detail on the cross-model evolution** (full text partly read: introduction and method, fetched HTML): "A generator LLM (e.g., Claude) iteratively proposes prompt candidates, while an executor LLM (e.g., GPT-4o) evaluates their fitness. By decoupling prompt generation from evaluation across different LLMs, MulVul mitigates the self-correction bias inherent in single-model optimization" (verbatim). So the evolver is Claude and the evolved agents run on GPT-4o; the fitness is measured on labelled data, not by an LLM judge.
- **Same-author follow-ups:**
  - **No Isolated Evolution, No Blind Search: Memory-Guided Prompt Co-evolution for Industrial Multi-agent Vulnerability Detection (MemCo)** (Jie Xu, Zi-Han Wu, Yun Peng, Chun-Yong Chong, Ling-Han Meng, Kui Liu and Xiao-Hua Jia; ASE 2026, [doi 10.1145/3832783.3834522](https://doi.org/10.1145/3832783.3834522); abstract read (Semantic Scholar API)). Evolves all agents' prompts jointly against the system's final output rather than one agent at a time; "only the prompts of agents responsible for the misclassification undergo further evolution", with a memory of successful and failed edits. Reports "+8.4 to +18.0 points" macro-F1 on public benchmarks and "reducing per-generation evolution tokens by 48.5%" (verbatim), and is deployed in an industrial pipeline. **Close to the project in method:** whole-council evolution with blame assignment to seats, on bug detection.
- **Citing papers 2025–2026 worth noting:**
  - **Evolving Agents in the Dark: Retrospective Harness Optimization via Self-Preference (RHO)** (Pan et al.; EMNLP 2026 Findings per arXiv comment; [arXiv 2606.05922](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05922); abstract read (arXiv abs page via curl)). Selects harness updates "by its own pairwise self-preference" without ground truth and reports SWE-Bench Pro "from 59% to 78% without any external grading" (verbatim). The opposite design choice to MulVul's and the project's: self-preference used as the fitness signal. Worth citing as the contrast case.
  - **Strategic Heterogeneous Multi-Agent Architecture for Cost-Effective Code Vulnerability Detection** (Zhaohui Geoffrey Wang; AAMAS 2026 SE workshop; [arXiv 2604.21282](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.21282); abstract read (arXiv abs page via curl)). Three DeepSeek-V3 role experts (structure, security patterns, debugging logic) plus a local Qwen3-8B adversarial verifier on 262 Juliet samples; the verifier "significantly improves precision (+10.3 percentage points, p < 1e-6, McNemar's test)" (verbatim). A small specialist council with a cross-family filter.
  - **DREA** (Sun and Meng; Internetware 2026; [arXiv 2607.13439](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13439); abstract read (arXiv abs page via curl)): "26-55% of true positives ... are correct predictions supported by flawed rationales" (verbatim). Bearing: the project's matching judge should check that a finding's explanation matches the seed, not only its location.
- **Challenged?** None found.

#### Section 6. Self-preference and shared blind spots

##### When Does a Second Model Help? Cross-Model Review in LLM Verification (Song 2026) — full treatment
- **Published version / venue:** arXiv preprint (v1 1 Oct 2026, v2 6 Oct 2026; arXiv API). No venue; Semantic Scholar has no venue, DOI or DBLP entry. Single author, Tae-Eun Song. 2 citations.
- **Later versions:** none after v2. The v2 comment (verbatim, arXiv API) says it "corrects two condition labels in Table 1 (CCR sees the artifact only; SA runs in a new session) and dependent interpretations; adds review prompts, a TP/FP breakdown by severity, and limitations; states how each reviewer was run; softens case studies. Numbers unchanged except removed B5 percentages". The comment also names it a "Follow-up to arXiv:2603.12123 and arXiv:2603.21454".
- **Code or data:** no repository of its own found in the v2 HTML. The only GitHub link is to https://github.com/openai/codex-plugin-cc (the tool used to run GPT-5.4 as a reviewer from Claude Code).
- **Two qualifications worth carrying into the prior-work doc** (full text partly read: abstract, Sections 3 and 5.2, fetched v2 HTML):
  - The headline union result is call-matched against two same-model reviews only: "one CCR plus one cross-model review matches more planted errors than two CCR reviews (56.7% vs. 42.7%; Holm-adjusted ), but not significantly more than two reviews by the top-tier cross-model reviewer, so model difference and reviewer capability are not separated" (verbatim; the p-value symbol did not survive HTML extraction). The same paragraph: "A lightweight cross-model reviewer scores no higher than same-model review."
  - "The baselines were collected before the XMR conditions under the same protocol, a temporal asymmetry noted in Limitations" (verbatim). The 360 same-model sessions are reused from the earlier Cross-Context Review runs.
  - Reviewers were GPT-5.4 (via the Codex CLI), Gemini 2.5 Pro and Gemini 2.5 Flash; the paper says the capability "tiers reflect the vendors' positioning at the time of the experiment, not a capability we measured" (verbatim).
- **Same-author follow-ups and companions** (all single-author, Tae-Eun Song, arXiv preprints):
  - **Cross-Context Review** ([arXiv 2603.12123](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.12123); v1 12 Mar 2026, **v2 1 Oct 2026**; comment read (verbatim, arXiv API)). The v2 comment is a set of self-corrections: "central result (a second review in a fresh session beats one in the same session) holds; one SR run excluded as unverifiable; v1 claim that the ranking held in all runs was inaccurate; advantages over SR and SA not significant across runs; corrects citation errors (incl. figures attributed to Tsui 2025 not in that paper); adds AI-use disclosure". So the companion paper's advantage of a fresh session over same-session self-review and over a subagent was **not significant across runs** in v2. The prior-work doc cites the companion without this.
  - **More Rounds, More Noise: Why Multi-Turn Review Fails to Improve Cross-Context Verification** ([arXiv 2603.16244](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.16244); 17 Mar 2026; abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API)). Same 30 artefacts and 150 injected errors. "Single-pass CCR (F1 = 0.376) significantly outperformed all multi-turn variants"; multi-turn review "generated 62% more false positives (8.5 vs. 5.2), collapsing precision from 0.30 to 0.20" because "reviewers in later rounds fabricate findings when the artifact's real errors have been exhausted" (verbatim). **Directly relevant** to the project's debate and sequential arms and to the appeal stage: extra rounds raised recall (+0.08) but cost precision. Not in the list of IDs already cited in the repo.
  - **Cross-Context Verification: Hierarchical Detection of Benchmark Contamination through Session-Isolated Analysis** ([arXiv 2603.21454](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.21454); v2 1 Apr 2026; title and metadata only, not read). Relevant by title to the project's contamination checks.
- **Citing papers 2025–2026 worth noting:**
  - **Cross-Provider Review as a Runtime Contract for Coding Agents** (Bowen Xu and Boyu Chen; v1 7 Oct 2026; [arXiv 2610.10961](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.10961); abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API)). A Claude/Codex cross-provider review contract with a small pilot: "In a controlled, agent-authored pilot of 20 paired development turns, eight had a material reviewer finding (95% exact interval 19.1-63.9%)". It also reports that "Codex attempted writes in five of five read-only trials" (verbatim). Engineering rather than measurement; it reports "No gate result".
  - **Beyond fabrication: Misattributed numbers in papers written by AI research agents** (Alfaqeeh et al.; [arXiv 2610.38691](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38691); abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API)). Re-labels 256 numerical claims from agent-written papers; "about four in five unsupported claims correspond to values in the agent's own files that come from the wrong experiment, metric, or run" (verbatim). Bearing on the project's own Claude-written reports and on a judge's evidence checks.
- **Challenged?** Not by others. The author's own v2 corrections to the companion paper weaken the fresh-session-versus-self-review claim that this paper builds on.

##### Cross-Context Review (Song, companion) — see the Song 2026 block above.

##### Cross-Model LLM Code Review: Should you use Claude to review Codex or vice versa? (Xiang, Zhang, Zhang and Xu) — full treatment
- **Published version / venue:** Agentic SE workshop at KDD 2026, per the arXiv comment ("This paper had been accepted by Agentic SE @ KDD'26", verbatim). arXiv v1, 22 Jul 2026, no later version. Semantic Scholar lists only the arXiv record. 4 citations.
- **Code or data:** public, https://github.com/shawnzxiang/cross-model-review-code (linked from the arXiv HTML; GitHub API: public, 2 stars, last push 31 Aug 2026). Not inspected beyond metadata.
- **Same-author follow-ups:** none on code review. The first author's other 2026 items (generative recommendation, LoRA forgetting, vibe-coded visualisations, arXiv 2608.29550) are unrelated; titles only.
- **Citing papers 2025–2026 worth noting:**
  - **Review Rarely Beats the Stronger Model: Separating Verification from Capability Transfer in LLM Pipelines** (Yirui Zhang and Yash Raj Shrestha; v3 3 Nov 2026; [arXiv 2610.15897](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15897); abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API); code https://github.com/Kamilaabb/verification-or-capability-transfer). Across 48 pairs of 12 open models, "review beats it [the stronger model alone] in only 9 of 96 review directions"; "the reviewer mostly follows the generator, discarding its own correct answers"; review "costs 2.6 to 2.8 times as much as the stronger model alone" (verbatim). **Challenges the reading** of Xiang et al.'s asymmetry: Claude reviewing Codex drafts may be capability transfer from the stronger model rather than verification. The project should report the strongest single reviewer alone as a baseline, not only the gain over the author.
  - **Cross-Provider Review as a Runtime Contract** (above).
  - "Humanize: Judgement Engineering for Agentic Coding" (arXiv 2610.08900) and "Same Question, Different Answer?" (arXiv 2608.08942); titles only, not read.
- **Challenged?** Indirectly, by "Review Rarely Beats the Stronger Model" (above). No direct replication found.

##### Models are worse at reviewing their own code (Greptile blog) — lighter block
- **Published version:** vendor blog post, 21 Jul 2026 (from the prior-work doc; the search result gives the author as Rodrigo Caridad, not confirmed on the page). Not peer-reviewed.
- **Later statements by the vendor** (page read (fetch tool)): a 9 Oct 2026 Greptile post by Daksh Gupta, "how to build a code review agent", says "In our research on model inversion, bug catch rates go up ~10% when you do this" and "If everyone is on Claude, use GPT models in your review agent" (verbatim). **This ~10% figure does not match** the 2–3 points of recall the prior-work doc read off the original post's chart; it may be a relative rather than absolute change, but the post does not say. Greptile's changelog entry for "Model Inversion" as a product feature (22 Jul 2026) was seen only as a search snippet.
- **Challenged?** No independent replication found.

##### Gong et al., How Well Do Large Language Models Serve as End-to-End Secure Code Agents for Python? — lighter block
- **Published version / venue:** **EASE 2025**, the 29th International Conference on Evaluation and Assessment in Software Engineering, [doi 10.1145/3756681.3756984](https://doi.org/10.1145/3756681.3756984) (Semantic Scholar journal field and DOI; DBLP key conf/ease/GongDTGYH25). The prior-work doc gives only the arXiv ID. arXiv v2, 13 Jun 2025. Authors: Jianian Gong, Nachuan Duan, Ziheng Tao, Zhaohui Gong, Yuan Yuan and Minlie Huang. 10 citations.
- **Challenged?** Not searched further (lighter block).

##### Articulate but Wrong: Self-Review Failures in LLM-Based Code Modernization (Reddy et al.) — lighter block
- **Published version / venue:** arXiv preprint (v1, 20 May 2026). Authors: Gokul Chandra Purnachandra Reddy, Aditya Lolla and Harsha Sanku. 0 citations.
- **Code or data:** public, per the arXiv comment: "Corpus, oracle, output extractor, prompts, harness, self-review probe, and all 1,980 + 1,979 raw model outputs released as supplementary material at https://zenodo.org/records/20300861" (verbatim). Not opened.
- **Challenged?** None found.

##### Self-Attribution Bias: When AI Monitors Go Easy on Themselves (Khullar, Hopkins, Wang and Roger) — full treatment
- **Published version / venue:** arXiv preprint (v1, 4 Mar 2026; no later version). **Submitted to ICML 2026 and rejected** under a different title, "I'm Absolutely Right: Self-Attribution Bias in LLM Agents" (OpenReview search API: note hv9leXYyPP, venueid "ICML.cc/2026/Conference/Rejected_Submission"; the abstract on OpenReview is the same as the arXiv abstract). 4 citations.
- **Earlier version:** a workshop precursor, **"Language Models Rate Their Own Actions As Safer"** (same four authors; MTI-LLM workshop at NeurIPS 2025, poster; OpenReview note NUSYIHi4eg; abstract read (OpenReview API)): "after being forced to click a phishing link, models rate this action as 20% less risky than when judging it in isolation", across "10 frontier LLMs across 4,500 samples" (verbatim). Not in the prior-work doc.
- **Code or data:** no repository link found in the arXiv HTML (only public Hugging Face datasets it uses: MMLU, ETHICS, reddit-ethics).
- **Setting detail** (full text partly read: Sections 3.2–3.3, fetched HTML): the code-correctness setting uses SWE-Bench patches the model generated, rated for correctness; 10 models from three families, including Claude-3.5-Haiku, Claude-4-Sonnet and Claude-4.1-Opus. The paper names Claude Code as a system that relies on self-monitoring.
- **Same-author follow-ups:** none on monitoring found on Semantic Scholar. Dipika Khullar's other 2025–2026 items ("Co-Evolving Agents: Learning from Failures as Hard Negatives", arXiv 2511.22254; "Overthinking: Amplifying Reasoning Weights to Extract Learned Secrets", arXiv 2607.08173) are titles only, not read.
- **Citing papers 2025–2026 worth noting:**
  - **Prior Audit-Repair Context Shifts LLM Verifier Thresholds Toward Leniency** (Parsa and Kasra Mazaheri; v1 17 Aug 2026; [arXiv 2608.16003](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.16003); abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API); code https://github.com/parsa-mz/crtitxer). A completed audit-then-repair episode already in context "lowers false alarms in 15 of 15 model x wording combinations, by 2.8 to 11.5 percentage points", and signal-detection analysis puts the change "in the threshold rather than in discrimination" (verbatim). **Bearing:** a judge or appeal agent that has seen earlier rounds in its context may become more lenient; supports running the judge in a fresh context.
  - "Mine or Yours? Self-Other Confusion in Latent LLM Communication" ([arXiv 2610.33020](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33020); abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API)) is about shared KV caches between model copies; loosely related.
  - "When the Reward Suite Is Leaky" (arXiv 2607.11022) and "From Token Bills to Benchmark Weeks" (arXiv 2611.12063): titles only, not read.
- **Challenged?** Partly, by the general self-preference critiques below (which concern labelled-authorship designs, not Khullar's in-context design). Khullar's own result that "explicitly stating that the action comes from the monitor does not by itself induce self-attribution bias" is consistent with "Self-Preference Is Weak or Absent ..." (below), which also finds no bias from authorship alone. No study found that fails to reproduce the in-context effect.

##### Self-Preference Bias in Rubric-Based Evaluation of LLMs (Pombal, Rei and Martins) — full treatment
- **Published version / venue:** **COLM 2026** (OpenReview search API: note 1837ctErks, venue "COLM 2026", venueid colmweb.org/COLM/2026/Conference). This confirms the venue the 31 Oct review notes gave and resolves the discrepancy the prior-work doc flagged ("the arXiv page shows no venue"). Semantic Scholar still lists only the arXiv record. 20 citations.
- **Later versions:** v1 8 Apr 2026, v2 22 Jul 2026, v3 3 Aug 2026 (arXiv abs page, submission history). **The LiveCodeBench experiment was added in v2.** The v1 abstract used IFEval only for objective rubrics and said "up to 50% more likely"; v2 and v3 say "Using IFEval and LiveCodeBench ... more than 50% more likely" (both verbatim, arXiv abs pages for v1 and v2). The COLM reviews describe the v1 design (IFEval plus HealthBench, "up to 47%").
- **Precision point for the prior-work doc** (full text partly read: Section 3 and Tables 1–2, fetched v3 HTML): the prior-work doc's "on LiveCodeBench, a judge is over 50% more likely to wrongly pass a failed rubric item when the output is its own" is broadly right but understates the code result. On LiveCodeBench (177 problems, 5,685 tests treated as rubrics), "most judges have a rubric-level HSPP ratio significantly greater than 1. In the worst case (GPT-5), the judge can be 20 times more likely to overestimate its own outputs than those of unrelated models" (verbatim). **Claude-Sonnet's LiveCodeBench ratio is 1.78 [1.58, 1.99]** (Table 1, 95% bootstrap CI), against 0.98 [0.64, 1.32] on IFEval. So the Claude judge showed clear self-preference on code tests but not on IFEval.
- **The quality confound is tested in the current version** (v3, Table 2; whether it arrived in v2 or v3 was not checked): ratios recomputed within bins of generation hardness. Verbatim: "If self-preference were an artifact of hard-to-judge generations, the ratio should collapse to 1 within each hardness bin. Instead, we observe that, with the exception of Qwen 235B, models maintain a high HSPP ratio across bins". Claude-Sonnet ranges from 1.33 to 2.76 across bins; only the easiest bin's CI ([0.89, 5.22]) includes 1.
- **Code or data:** public, https://github.com/zeppombal/rubric-self-preference (linked from the v3 HTML; GitHub API: public, last push 13 Jul 2026).
- **Peer reviews** (COLM 2026, two reviews read (OpenReview search API), both rating 6): one reviewer asked for exactly the capability-versus-bias stratification later added, noted no significance testing and no identity or style controls "to distinguish self-recognition (Panickssery) from style-familiarity (Wataoka) mechanisms"; the other flagged a mis-citation of Wataoka et al. (who attribute the effect to perplexity, not self-recognition).
- **Same-author follow-ups:** **MindEval2** (Pombal et al.; arXiv 2610.22314; title only, not read) cites it, for clinical rubrics.
- **Citing papers 2025–2026 worth noting:**
  - **A Shared Taste for Model-Written Text** (Dmitrij Żatuchin; [arXiv 2610.00369](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00369); abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API)). See "Self-preference: later challenges" below.
  - **Evaluating medical AI under missing information: same-provider judges and human raters change apparent safety** (Koyar Afrasyab; [arXiv 2607.18828](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.18828); abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API)). With a four-provider judge panel including Claude Opus 4.8, "after adjusting for each judge's general leniency ... a positive same-provider association remains (exact permutation p = 0.04; GPT-5.5 ~ +0.10 on the probability scale)" (verbatim). Same-provider bias in a current panel, after a leniency control.
  - **The Red Queen Gödel Machine** (see the DGM block) cites it for its adversarial anti-self-preference objective.
  - **Auditing the Product, Not the Recursion** (see the DGM block).
- **Challenged?** Not directly. Its own v3 stratification answers the main quality-confound objection for code.

##### Do LLM Evaluators Prefer Themselves for a Reason? (Chen et al.) — lighter block
- **Published version / venue:** arXiv preprint, v3 12 Dec 2025 ("Added LMArena experiments"; arXiv API). Authors: Wei-Lin Chen, Zhepei Wei, Xinyu Zhu, Shi Feng and Yu Meng. **Submitted to ICLR 2026 and rejected** (OpenReview note 9HhZ60LbVV, venueid ICLR.cc/2026/Conference/Rejected_Submission), and **resubmitted to ICLR 2027** (note DwmUDnIxSn; OpenReview search API). 51 citations.
- **Bearing:** its HSPP metric is the basis of Pombal et al.'s ratio. Its core claim (much self-preference is justified by quality) is the line of work below that later challenges self-preference findings.

##### Play Favorites: A Statistical Method to Measure Self-Bias in LLM-as-a-Judge (Spiliopoulou et al.) — lighter block
- **Published version / venue:** arXiv preprint (v1, 8 Aug 2025; no later version). Seven authors, first three Evangelia Spiliopoulou, Riccardo Fogliato and Hanna Burnsky. 57 citations. No venue found (DBLP CoRR record only, via OpenReview's DBLP mirror).
- **Challenged?** None found specifically.

##### Who Judges Matters: Measuring Family-Conditioned Preference in LLM-as-Judge Panels (Awuni et al.) — lighter block
- **Published version / venue:** arXiv preprint (v1, 15 Sep 2026; "11 pages, 3 figures, 4 tables"). Authors: David Ababio Awuni, Luke E. K. Achenie, Benjamin Tei Partey, Elvis Gyasi Owusu and Nii-Nai Derrick Sowah. 2 citations. No venue found.
- **Challenged?** None found; its own finding (the lift is mostly confounded with quality) already points the same way as the critiques below.

##### Preference Leakage: A Contamination Problem in LLM-as-a-judge (Li et al.) — lighter block
- **Published version / venue:** ICLR 2026 (arXiv comment "Accepted by ICLR 2026"; DBLP conf/iclr/LiSHZJHZWL26 via Semantic Scholar). arXiv v3, 4 Mar 2026. 172 citations.
- **Bearing:** "When LLMs Benchmark Themselves" (below) extends the same contamination logic to models that write the test set, which is the project's seed-planting situation.

##### Panickssery, Bowman and Feng, LLM Evaluators Recognize and Favor Their Own Generations — lighter block
- **Published version / venue:** **NeurIPS 2024, oral** (OpenReview search API: note 4NJBV6Wp0h, venue "NeurIPS 2024 oral", venueid NeurIPS.cc/2024/Conference). This settles the prior-work doc's open note ("the arXiv page lists no venue, so check the one the paper uses"). arXiv v1 only (15 Apr 2024). 1,072 citations.
- **Challenged?** **Yes, on self-recognition.** "Know Thyself? On the Incapability and Implications of AI Self-Recognition" (Bai, Shrivastava, Holtzman and Tan; [arXiv 2510.03399](https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.03399); abstract read (arXiv abs page via curl)): "Different from prior claims, our results reveal a consistent failure in self-recognition. Only 4 out of 10 models predict themselves as generators, and the performance is rarely above random chance" (verbatim). See also the reanalyses under "Self-preference: later challenges".

##### Wataoka, Takahashi and Ri, Self-Preference Bias in LLM-as-a-Judge — lighter block
- **Published version / venue:** NeurIPS 2024 Safe Generative AI Workshop, poster (arXiv comment and OpenReview note tLZZZIgPJX). Also **submitted to ICLR 2025 and rejected** (OpenReview note Ns8zGZ0lmM). arXiv v2, 21 Jun 2025. 366 citations. The ICLR 2025 reviews (read via the OpenReview search API) questioned class imbalance in the metric and whether the effect is "really ... its own answer or just any longer answer".
- **Challenged?** Its explanation (perplexity familiarity rather than self-recognition) is itself the main alternative to Panickssery's.

##### Self-preference: later challenges and quality explanations (synthesis across section 6)
All abstracts below were read on the arXiv abs page via curl or via the arXiv API, verbatim. None is in the list of IDs already cited in the repo.
- **Are LLM Evaluators Really Narcissists? Sanity Checking Self-Preference Evaluations** (Roytburg et al.; **ICML 2026 Main** per arXiv comment; v4 22 Jun 2026; [arXiv 2601.22548](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.22548)). Controls for judge quality by comparing the judge's votes when judging itself and when judging others: "only 51% of examples in previous findings retain statistical significance against this null hypothesis, covering 89.6% of total self-preference probability mass" (verbatim). **The strongest published re-check:** about half of earlier self-preference cases are explained by evaluator quality, though most of the measured preference mass survives.
- **A Shared Taste for Model-Written Text** (Żatuchin; [arXiv 2610.00369](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00369)). Re-analyses Laurito et al.'s public matrices: own-model premium "+0.019 pooled (p = 0.14; 95% interval -0.008 to 0.046)"; "What these data do not show is a model recognising and favouring its own prose" (verbatim). Models share a taste for model-written text rather than their own. **Bearing for the project:** a Claude judge may favour any LLM-written finding over a human-style one, which matters for comparing councils with human-written reference findings.
- **Self-Preference Is Weak or Absent in Verifiable Instruction-Following Revision** (Guey and Bougault; [arXiv 2606.20093](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.20093); code https://github.com/williamguey/self-preference-revision). On IFEval with a deterministic checker, authors reject verified fixes to their own drafts at the same rate as fresh models ("gap -5.1 pp, 95% CI [-12.9, +2.7]"), and "Effects smaller than ~13 pp cannot be excluded at this sample size" (verbatim). Small, four mid-tier families.
- **Beyond the Surface: Measuring Self-Preference in LLM Judgments** (Chen et al.; EMNLP 2025 per Semantic Scholar; [arXiv 2506.02592](https://arxiv.org/abs/2506.02592)). Uses gold judgements to separate bias from quality (the DBG score); bias persists after the control and is linked to response style and post-training data.
- **Quantifying and Mitigating Self-Preference Bias of LLM Judges** (Yang et al.; v4 2 Jun 2026; [arXiv 2604.22891](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.22891)). Equal-quality pairs across 20 LLMs: "advanced capabilities are often uncorrelated, or even negatively correlated, with low SPB" (verbatim), which runs against the hope that stronger judges are less biased.
- **When LLMs Benchmark Themselves: Deconstructing Self-Bias in Automated Evaluation** (Xu, Agrawal, Zouhar, Freitag and Deutsch; EMNLP 2026 per Semantic Scholar; v3 30 Aug 2026; [arXiv 2509.26600](https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.26600)). "LLM-generated benchmarks systematically favor the model that created them"; self-bias comes from both "LLM as a testset and LLM as an evaluator, and their combination amplifies the effect", strongly enough "to cause each model to rank itself first" (verbatim). Tested on machine translation and Chatbot Arena. **High relevance:** this is the project's set-up (Claude writes the seeds and Claude reviews), measured in another domain. Not in the prior-work doc.
- **Net reading:** later work weakens the claim that models recognise and favour *their own* text (self-recognition fails; about half of earlier significant cases are quality effects), but not the claims that matter most here. In-context self-attribution (Khullar), self-preference on verifiable code tests after a hardness control (Pombal, v3), same-provider judge bias after a leniency control (Afrasyab) and test-set self-bias (Xu et al.) all survive. No later work was found that fails to replicate Pombal's or Khullar's specific results.

##### Great Models Think Alike and this Undermines AI Oversight (Goel et al.) — full treatment
- **Published version / venue:** **ICML 2025, spotlight poster** (OpenReview search API: note 3Z827FtMNe, venue "ICML 2025 spotlightposter"); also an ICLR 2025 SSI-FM workshop poster (note sYB0Y0hOxi). arXiv v2, 12 Jun 2025 ("60 pages, 20 figures"). Nine authors, first three Shashwat Goel, Joschka Strüber and Ilze Amanda Auzina; last author Jonas Geiping. 84 citations. The prior-work doc gives only "2025".
- **Code or data:** public, https://github.com/model-similarity/lm-similarity (GitHub API: public, 23 stars; last push Feb 2025). This is the CAPA implementation the prior-work doc suggests using for seat overlap.
- **Same-author follow-ups:** **"What if I ask in alia lingua? Measuring Functional Similarity Across Languages"** (Mishra, Rastogi, Negi, Goel and Kumaraguru; MRL workshop at EMNLP 2025; [arXiv 2509.04032](https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.04032); abstract read (arXiv abs page via curl)) applies the same κp metric across 20 languages; "models exhibit greater cross-lingual consistency within themselves than agreement with other models" (verbatim). Goel's other 2025–2026 papers (forecasting, long-horizon execution, answer matching) are not on similarity.
- **Citing papers 2025–2026 worth noting** (abstracts read on the arXiv abs page via curl unless stated):
  - **The Subjectivity of Monoculture** (Jo, Garg and Raghavan; **NeurIPS 2026** per arXiv comment; [arXiv 2602.24086](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.24086); abstract and full text partly read (Section 2.1, fetched HTML)). Uses Goel et al. and Kim et al. as its two baselines and adds item difficulty to the null model: "monoculture virtually disappears in one dataset when we include item difficulty compared to previous works that do not" (abstract, verbatim). The full text says both earlier works "primarily assume a capability-based null model" (verbatim). **The main published challenge to both Goel et al. and Kim et al.** Nikhil Garg is Kim et al.'s last author, so this is partly a self-correction.
  - **Agreement Overstates Evidence: Error Dependence in LLM Judge Consensus** (Hossain, Yousefi and Lim; [arXiv 2609.22512](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.22512)). "the average pairwise correlation between judge errors is 0.21. As a result, the ten judges only provide roughly as much statistical information as 3.5 independent judges. The dependency is even stronger among the high-accuracy frontier judges ... including judges from different providers"; "In up to 28% of our comparisons, ignoring shared errors leads to the conclusion that one system is significantly better, while accounting for them does not" (verbatim). **Directly relevant** to the project's paired-CI keep rule if the judge's errors are correlated with the reviewers' or across seeds.
  - **Are Diversity Metrics Measuring Diversity? A Capability-Controlled Audit of Majority-Vote Gain in LLM Ensembles** (Donghwan Kim; [arXiv 2607.20768](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20768)). Over 31,900 subsets of 30 LLMs, "simple voting beats the strongest member in only 9.98% of all canonical size-3 subsets", and a joint-correctness diversity proxy "is nearly collinear with one minus mean accuracy" (verbatim). Diversity metrics such as CAPA may mostly re-express capability.
  - **Inferred Generative-Process Diversity Predicts Correlated Failure Across Language Models** (Tieman and Markou; [arXiv 2609.03422](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03422)). Across 38 models, a compression-distance measure of diversity predicts lower correlated failure "beyond semantic similarity and model-pair capability" (partial rank association −0.216, 95% interval [−0.309, −0.122]; verbatim).
  - **Does Reasoning Make Language Models Agree?** (Gorecki and Hardt; [arXiv 2610.41691](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41691)). "enabling reasoning increases agreement between models evaluated on the same individuals", even after accounting for accuracy (verbatim). Bearing on the project's effort arm: higher effort may make seats more alike.
  - **Homogenization in Multi-Agent Systems** (Ganesh et al., Apple-affiliated authors not confirmed; [arXiv 2610.09824](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.09824)). In code generation, interaction "hides and amplifies correlated errors", and "simple approaches to increase diversity---leveraging sampling stochasticity and mixed-models MAS---fail to reduce homogenization risks" (verbatim).
- **Challenged?** **Yes, partly.** The Subjectivity of Monoculture finds that the measured similarity depends on the null model and largely disappears in one dataset once item difficulty is modelled. The judge-favours-similar-models result was not directly re-tested in anything found. Later work (Agreement Overstates Evidence; Kim et al.) supports the direction of the "mistakes grow more similar" trend for frontier judges.

##### Correlated Errors in Large Language Models (Kim, Garg, Peng and Garg) — full treatment
- **Published version / venue:** ICML 2025, poster (arXiv comment "Accepted to ICML 2025"; OpenReview note kzYq2hfyHB, venue "ICML 2025 poster"). arXiv v1 only (9 Jun 2025). 86 citations.
- **Code or data:** public, https://github.com/nikhgarg/llm_correlated_errors_public/ (linked from the arXiv HTML; not opened). It uses the Hugging Face Open LLM Leaderboard 2 data.
- **A precision point for the prior-work doc:** the abstract (verbatim, arXiv API) says "on one leaderboard dataset, models agree 60% of the time when both models err", and "larger and more accurate models have highly correlated errors, even with distinct architectures and providers". The prior-work doc's "across 350+ models, two models that both err agree 60% of the time" drops the "on one leaderboard dataset" qualifier. The point that matters most for the project is the second sentence: being from different providers does not remove correlation among strong models.
- **Same-author follow-ups:** **The Subjectivity of Monoculture** (Jo, Garg and Raghavan; NeurIPS 2026; above), which revisits this paper's null model. Nikhil Garg's other 2026 papers are not on LLM error correlation.
- **Citing papers 2025–2026 worth noting** (abstracts read on the arXiv abs page via curl):
  - **Unanimity Without Persuasion: A Single Round of Debate Erases the Disagreement That Verification Needs** (Yang Shu; [arXiv 2609.26145](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.26145)). On 600 code-correctness candidates and a heterogeneous seven-judge panel, "unanimity on a fixed cohort jumps from 39.5% to 95.2% in round 1 ... while accuracy moves by less than one point and 96.3% of verdict flips follow the displayed peer majority"; "by round 3 every wrong decision is unanimous, erasing dissent that had flagged two-thirds of the panel's errors" (verbatim). Recommends verifying "before any second-pass evaluation or peer exposure". **High relevance** to the debate arm and to where the appeal agent sits.
  - **Correlated Failures of AI Approval Monitors** (Luca Zanolini; [arXiv 2610.29178](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29178)). Monitors sharing an evidence source fail together; "a rule that counts sources instead of votes blocked 0.9% of correct payments, against 7.4% for the best counting rule, at equal safety" (verbatim). It tested Claude Opus 5.5 among closed models. Payment approval, not code, but the "count independent evidence, not votes" rule transfers to a judge pooling reviewer findings.
  - **Builder, Defender, Breaker: Measurable Independence and Bounded Autonomy When Generative Models Build, Defend and Test Software** (Mohamed Chahine Ghanem; v2 8 Sep 2026; [arXiv 2607.03215](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.03215)). A conceptual paper: once roles share a "shared generative substrate", "organisational independence ... no longer implies statistical independence" (verbatim), using an Eckhardt–Lee coincident-failure model. Names the project's same-family worry precisely, without data.
  - **The Consistency Dilemma in LLMs: Generator-Evaluator Agreement and Vulnerability to Mistakes** (Mancoridis and Hitzig; [arXiv 2606.30653](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.30653)). Models that apply concepts more consistently between generating and evaluating are "more prone to mistakes" in a clinical setting (verbatim).
  - Titles only, not read: "Partially Correlated Verifier Cascades in LLM Harnesses: Concave Log-Odds, Polynomial Reliability, and Blind-Spot Ceilings" (arXiv 2607.13918); "Evaluate the Stack, Not the Layer: Do Deterministic and LLM Gates for Agent Actions Fail Independently?" (arXiv 2610.07359); "Sixteen models, fewer than two voices" (arXiv 2608.00285); "Decomposing Wrong-Consensus Agreement in LLM Self-Consistency" (arXiv 2608.18795).
- **Challenged?** **Yes, on measurement.** The Subjectivity of Monoculture (co-authored by this paper's last author) shows the size of measured monoculture depends on the null model and item difficulty. The qualitative finding that strong models' errors correlate across providers is supported by Agreement Overstates Evidence.

##### Is Self-Repair a Silver Bullet for Code Generation? (Olausson et al.) — lighter block
- **Published version / venue:** ICLR 2024 (arXiv comment; DBLP conf/iclr/OlaussonIW0S24). arXiv v5, 2 Feb 2024; the comment notes a "fixed ... data processing error harming Code Llama's reported self-repair performance on HumanEval". 321 citations.
- **Bearing:** the abstract (verbatim, arXiv API) supports the project's cross-model judge arm: "using a stronger model to artificially boost the quality of the feedback, we observe substantially larger performance gains".
- **Challenged?** Not searched further.

##### Self-Correction Bench (Tsui) — lighter block
- **Published version / venue:** COLM 2026 (arXiv comment "Accepted to COLM 2026"). arXiv v3, 2 Aug 2026. Single author, Ken Tsui. 22 citations.
- **A precision point for the prior-work doc:** the 64.5% is measured on **injected** errors framed as the model's own versus the user's, not on models' natural errors: "injecting the same error as either an external (user-attributed) or internal (model-attributed) error ... Testing 14 open-source non-reasoning models reveals a 64.5% Self-Correction Blind Spot" (verbatim, arXiv API). The v3 abstract adds that appending "Wait" "reduces the blind spot by 89.3%" (verbatim).
- **Citation caution:** Song's Cross-Context Review v2 comment says it "corrects citation errors (incl. figures attributed to Tsui 2025 not in that paper)" (verbatim). Anyone citing Tsui figures second-hand through Song's papers should check them against Self-Correction Bench itself.
- **Citing papers:** Khullar et al. cite it; "Spectral Origins of the Self-Correction Blind Spot in Autoregressive Generation" (arXiv 2607.09803) and "Precise but Uncoupled: Reviewer Precision Does Not Guarantee Critique Uptake in Multi-Agent Math Reasoning" (arXiv 2607.15388) are titles only, not read.
- **Challenged?** None found.

#### Loosely related

##### Predicting Acceptance and Review Effort in Human and Agent Pull Requests (Pansuriya et al.) — lighter block
- **Published version / venue:** **IEEE COMPSAC 2026** (2026 IEEE 50th Annual Computers, Software, and Applications Conference), pages 1–10, [doi 10.1109/COMPSAC69091.2026.00052](https://doi.org/10.1109/COMPSAC69091.2026.00052) (Semantic Scholar journal field and DOI). The prior-work doc says "no venue stated", which is now out of date. arXiv v1 only (13 Jul 2026). 1 citation.
- **Challenged?** None found.

#### The experiment plan's "Related research" list (not in prior-work-research.md)

##### Kim et al., Towards a Science of Scaling Agent Systems (arXiv 2512.08296) — lighter block
- **Published version / venue:** **Nature Machine Intelligence 8, 1157–1172, published 24 Jul 2026, under a different title: "Capable language models can outgrow the benefits of collaboration"** (Crossref API record for [doi 10.1038/s42256-026-01268-y](https://doi.org/10.1038/s42256-026-01268-y): title, journal, volume, pages, date and 20 authors, first five Yubin Kim, Ken Gu, Chanwoo Park, Chunjong Park and Samuel Schmidgall). First found through a web search pointing at an MIT Media Lab listing, then confirmed on Crossref. A citing paper (PACE, below) also cites it as Nature Machine Intelligence. arXiv and Semantic Scholar still show only the preprint (180 citations).
- **Later versions:** v1 9 Dec 2025, v2 17 Dec 2025, v3 8 Apr 2026 (arXiv abs page). **This resolves the prior-work doc's "260 against 180" discrepancy:** the v1 abstract says "a controlled evaluation spanning 180 configurations" on four benchmarks (verbatim, arXiv abs page for v1), and v3 says "Across 260 configurations spanning six agentic benchmarks" (verbatim, arXiv API). The Google blog reflects v1. The v3 model "achieves a cross-validated R^2=0.373 across all six benchmarks" (verbatim).
- **Citing papers worth noting:** **More Agents, More Samples, or More Thinking?** (Seyed Mahmoud Sajjadi Mohammadabadi; v1 13 Nov 2026; [arXiv 2611.17610](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17610); abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API) and full text partly read (related work, fetched HTML); checklist and data at https://github.com/mahmoudsajjadi/pace). A synthesis of nine primary studies (516 configurations): against sample-matched or budget-matched baselines "multi-agent systems average -0.042 in accuracy", and "Multi-agent accuracy equals or falls below the baseline in 74% of these comparable contrasts" (verbatim). It argues "positive returns require partitioned evidence, external verification, or error diversity across models" (verbatim). Kim et al. and Ferreira et al. are among its sources. **Supports** the project's equal-cost design and its single-reviewer baselines.
- **Challenged?** Not disputed; the synthesis above treats it as one of the studies showing gains shrink as single-agent competence rises.

##### Scaling Behavior of Single LLM-Driven Multi-Agent Systems (arXiv 2606.00655) — lighter block
- **Published version / venue:** arXiv preprint (v1, 30 May 2026). Authors: Jialing Li, Zhouhong Gu, Yin Cai and Hongwei Feng. 2 citations.
- **Key point** (abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API)): homogeneous MAS performance "does not scale monotonically with agent count but follows a pattern of diminishing returns", and "The performance degradation stems coordination overhead rather than merely long-context failure" (verbatim, including the missing "from").
- **Challenged?** None found.

##### Understanding Agent Scaling in LLM-Based Multi-Agent Systems via Diversity (arXiv 2602.03794) — lighter block
- **Published version / venue:** arXiv preprint (v1, 3 Feb 2026). Eight authors, first three Yingxuan Yang, Chengrui Qu and Muning Wen; includes Adam Wierman. 42 citations. Note the full title starts "Understanding Agent Scaling in LLM-Based Multi-Agent Systems via Diversity".
- **Code or data:** public per the abstract: https://github.com/SafeRL-Lab/Agent-Scaling (not opened).
- **Key point** (abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API)): "2 diverse agents can match or exceed the performance of 16 homogeneous agents", because "Homogeneous agents saturate early because their outputs are strongly correlated" (verbatim). It introduces K*, an effective channel count that needs no labels, which could be computed for council seats.
- **Challenged?** Partly, by later evidence that diversity helps less than this suggests once capability is controlled: Ferreira et al. (below: "mixed-model teams lose to majority votes over their own rosters") and "Are Diversity Metrics Measuring Diversity?" (section 6 above).

##### On the Importance of Task Complexity in Evaluating LLM-Based Multi-Agent Systems (arXiv 2510.04311) — lighter block
- **Published version / venue:** arXiv preprint (v1, 5 Oct 2025). Authors: Bohan Tang, Huidong Liang, Keyue Jiang and Xiaowen Dong. 8 citations.
- **Key point** (abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API)): characterises tasks by depth (reasoning length) and width (capability diversity); "the benefit of LLM-MAS over LLM-SAS increases with both task depth and width, and the effect is more pronounced with respect to depth" (verbatim). Studies debate only.
- **Challenged?** None found.

##### Literature Review of Multi-Agent Debate for Problem-Solving (arXiv 2506.00066) — lighter block
- **Published version / venue:** arXiv preprint (v1, 29 May 2025; "11 pages, 2 figures"). Single author, Arne Tillmann. 21 citations.
- **Key point** (abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API)): a narrative review concluding that "multi-agent approaches can yield superior results but also face elevated computational costs" (verbatim). It predates the 2025–2026 budget-matched critiques listed under Smit et al. below, so it is out of date as a summary of the field.

##### Beyond Symmetric Agents: Cognitive Diversity and Multi-Agent Debate in Small Language Models (Ferreira, Liu and Zheng) — full treatment
- **Published version / venue:** arXiv preprint (v1, 26 Sep 2026; comment "Submitted to ICLR 2027"). **Under review at ICLR 2027** (OpenReview search API: note hzAVG3EuXR, venueid ICLR.cc/2027/Conference/Submission, titled "... Multiagent Debate ..."). No reviews were visible through the search API. 1 citation.
- **Later versions:** v1 only.
- **A discrepancy between versions:** the arXiv abstract says "23 models from eleven vendor families" (verbatim, arXiv API); the ICLR 2027 submission abstract on OpenReview says "23 models from nine vendor families" (verbatim, OpenReview API). Which is right is not confirmed. The citing PACE paper repeats "eleven".
- **Code or data:** no repository link found in the arXiv v1 HTML.
- **Key numbers** (abstract, verbatim): debate "beats single-agent inference (3--7 points where tasks have headroom) but at matched budget conditions it ties or even loses to self-consistency sampling at 1.6× the wall-clock and 3.4× the token cost"; "mixed-model teams lose to majority votes over their own rosters, with accuracy tracking member capability rather than heterogeneity"; "nearly all of debate's benefit comes from the first exchange of answers"; a context-overflow correction "alone moves our debate-versus-sampling comparison from -1.8 points to parity".
- **Same-author follow-ups:** none. Leonardo Ferreira's other 2025–2026 items on Semantic Scholar are on political echo chambers and computational social science.
- **Citing papers 2025–2026 worth noting:** More Agents, More Samples, or More Thinking? (above), the only citation.
- **Bearing on the project:** it tests exactly the project's "copies of one model against mixed families" and "persona roles" questions, at small scale and on QA rather than review, and finds against both diversity mechanisms once budgets are matched. Its context-overflow hazard is a concrete check for the debate arm.
- **Challenged?** Not yet. It is itself a challenge to the diversity results of "Understanding Agent Scaling via Diversity" and to the heterogeneity claim of "Stop Overvaluing Multi-Agent Debate" (below).

##### Huang et al., Large Language Models Cannot Self-Correct Reasoning Yet (arXiv 2310.01798) — lighter block
- **Published version / venue:** ICLR 2024 (arXiv comment; DBLP conf/iclr/0009CMZYSZ24). arXiv v2, 14 Mar 2024. 1,504 citations.
- **Later work bearing on it:** Self-Correction Bench (section 6 above) refines it: models can correct an identical error when it is framed as external, so the failure is one of activation, and a "Wait" cue reduces the blind spot by 89.3%. Olausson et al. reach the same conclusion for code: feedback quality is the bottleneck.
- **Challenged?** Refined rather than overturned in what was read here. Training-based self-correction work was not opened.

##### Smit et al., Should We Be Going MAD? A Look at Multi-Agent Debate Strategies for LLMs — full treatment
- **Published version / venue:** **ICML 2024** (PMLR pages 45883–45905 per Semantic Scholar; DBLP conf/icml/SmitGDBP24). arXiv v3, 18 Jul 2024. Authors (arXiv): Andries Smit, Paul Duckworth, Nathan Grinsztajn, Thomas D. Barrett and Arnu Pretorius (InstaDeep). 196 citations.
- **Code or data:** the abstract says "We provide an open-source repository to the community with several state-of-the-art protocols together with evaluation scripts" (verbatim); the repository address was not checked.
- **Same-author follow-ups:** not found (Semantic Scholar returned no 2025–2026 papers for the first author's ID).
- **Citing papers 2025–2026 worth noting** (abstracts read on the arXiv abs page via curl unless stated):
  - **Debate or Vote: Which Yields Better Decisions in Multi-Agent Large Language Models?** (Choi, Zhu and Li; **NeurIPS 2025 Spotlight** per arXiv comment; [arXiv 2508.17536](https://arxiv.org/abs/2508.17536)).
