"Majority Voting alone accounts for most of the performance gains typically attributed to MAD", and debate "induces a martingale over agents' belief trajectories, implying that debate alone does not improve expected correctness" (verbatim).
  - **Stop Overvaluing Multi-Agent Debate -- We Must Rethink Evaluation and Embrace Model Heterogeneity** (Zhang et al.; position paper; [arXiv 2502.08788](https://arxiv.org/abs/2502.08788)). Five MAD methods on nine benchmarks "often fail to outperform simple single-agent baselines such as Chain-of-Thought and Self-Consistency", but model heterogeneity is "a universal antidote" (verbatim). Ferreira et al. later contradict the heterogeneity claim.
  - **Unanimity Without Persuasion** (section 6 above): one debate round on 600 code-correctness candidates collapses dissent without improving accuracy.
  - **Too Many Cooks Spoil The Broth: Auditing Multi-Agent LLM Councils For Medical Reasoning** (Mukul Joshi, Pratik Jalan, Akhilesh Magotra and Kshitij Sharad Jadhav; EMNLP 2026 main, pages 49230–49245; [ACL Anthology 2026.emnlp-main.2164](https://aclanthology.org/2026.emnlp-main.2164/); abstract page read (fetch tool), summary only). A five-model "council" of 4–8B open models on 8,402 medical questions: most accuracy was set before debate, "Agreement among models is a measurement that can be biased, not evidence of correctness" (verbatim excerpt), unanimous agreement was less accurate than disagreement on the hardest benchmark, and the council trailed one larger model on four of six datasets at about seven times the calls (paraphrase from the fetch tool, not verbatim). Uses the word "council" for an LLM panel, as the project does.
  - **When Order Matters: First-Speaker Bias and Mitigation through Personality in Sequential Multi-Agent Debate** (Xu, Hooi and Qiao; [arXiv 2609.38964](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38964)). "placing a stronger model after weaker ones can substantially offset its reasoning advantage" (verbatim). **Bearing on the sequential arm:** seat order is a variable to randomise or fix.
  - **Multi-agent discussion gains less when dissent is withheld** (Mansuri et al.; [arXiv 2609.38324](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38324)). Discussion overturns a wrong majority only below a critical withholding rate; "Turning reasoning off also increases the gain" (verbatim).
  - **Copies or Sources? Measuring How LLM Aggregators Count Restated Evidence in Multi-Agent Systems** (Gao et al.; [arXiv 2610.06192](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.06192)). "a forwarded copy counts for 0.06 to 0.42 of a new reading" (verbatim). **Bearing on the judge:** a judge merging duplicate findings may over-weight a bug that several seats restate.
  - **Enhancing LLM Performance Through Debate: An Empirical Study on Multi-Agent Debate for Coding Tasks** (Chun, Chen, Li and Ahmed; TOSEM per arXiv comment; [arXiv 2503.12029](https://arxiv.org/abs/2503.12029)). The one software-engineering debate study found; positive "in some cases".
  - Seen as titles only: "When and Why Does Multi-Agent Debate Fail and Does It Really Underperform?" (arXiv 2510.20963); "Can LLM Agents Really Debate?" (arXiv 2511.07784); "Revisiting Multi-Agent Debate as Test-Time Scaling" (arXiv 2505.22960).
- **Challenged?** Its central finding (MAD does not reliably beat self-consistency) has been **strongly confirmed**, not challenged, by Debate or Vote, Stop Overvaluing, Ferreira et al. and the PACE synthesis. Its secondary claim, that tuned MAD protocols such as Multi-Persona can win, is weakened by Ferreira et al.'s "persona tax".

##### The Platonic Representation Hypothesis (Huh, Cheung, Wang and Isola) — full treatment
- **Published version / venue:** ICML 2024 (S2 venue "International Conference on Machine Learning"; widely described as a position paper, not confirmed on the proceedings page). arXiv v5, 25 Jul 2024. 482 citations.
- **Code or data:** public, https://github.com/minyoungg/platonic-rep (about 740 stars) and project page https://phillipi.github.io/prh/ (arXiv comment).
- **Same-author follow-ups:** not found (the Semantic Scholar author ID returned no 2025–2026 papers; not searched further).
- **Citing papers 2025–2026 worth noting** (abstracts read on the arXiv abs page via curl):
  - **Back into Plato's Cave: Examining Cross-modal Representational Convergence at Scale** (Koepke, Zverev, Ginosar and Efros; [arXiv 2604.18572](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.18572)). "the evidence for this claim is substantially weaker than subsequent work suggests"; the mutual k-NN metric on "1024 text-image pairs in the original study captures only coarse structure"; "The reported increase in alignment with language model strength saturates for recent models" (verbatim). **The main direct challenge.**
  - **Convergence Without Understanding: When Language Models Agree on Representations but Disagree on Reasoning** (Usama and Chang; [arXiv 2605.23315](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.23315)). Across 16 models from 8 families, "models converge more on problems they collectively fail (Centered Kernel Alignment [CKA] = 0.897) than on those they solve (CKA = 0.830)", and convergence "reflects shared input processing constraints rather than shared reasoning strategies, with direct implications for ensemble design" (verbatim). **Most relevant to the project:** representational similarity is highest exactly on the items all models get wrong, which is where a council of different families would still share blind spots.
  - **The Umwelt Representation Hypothesis: Rethinking Universality** (Bosch, Sommers, Doerig and Kietzmann; Trends in Cognitive Sciences per Semantic Scholar; [arXiv 2604.17960](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.17960)). Argues universality is "premature" and alignment reflects overlap in ecological constraints (verbatim "premature").
  - **Shared Geometry Is Not Shared Physics** (Duraphe et al.; NeurIPS 2026 workshop; [arXiv 2610.04130](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.04130)): aligned geometry did not imply transferable physical encodings in astronomy models.
- **Challenged?** **Yes.** Back into Plato's Cave disputes the strength of the original evidence and finds the alignment trend saturates. For the project the hypothesis was only ever a motivation for "different families may still share blind spots"; the more useful citations are now the direct error-correlation studies (Kim et al., Goel et al., Agreement Overstates Evidence) plus Convergence Without Understanding.

##### Porter, Siy, Toman and Votta (1997), An Experiment to Assess the Cost-Benefits of Code Inspections in Large Scale Software Development — lighter block
- **Published version / venue:** IEEE Transactions on Software Engineering 23(6), June 1997, starting p. 329 (end page not checked), [doi 10.1109/32.601071](https://doi.org/10.1109/32.601071) (full text partly read: abstract, design Sections 2.1–2.4 and results Sections 3–4, from an author-copy PDF at userweb.cs.txstate.edu converted with pdftotext). An earlier conference version appeared at SIGSOFT FSE 1995 ([doi 10.1145/222124.222144](https://doi.org/10.1145/222124.222144), per Semantic Scholar). Semantic Scholar lists only 27 citations for the TSE paper, so its citation coverage for this paper is poor.
- **What the paper says about team size** (verbatim from the PDF):
  - Design: "the number of reviewers on each inspection team (1, 2, or 4)" with reviewers "randomly selected without replacement from a pool of 11 experienced software developers" (abstract); 130 collection meetings in total.
  - Result: "There was no difference between two- and four-person inspections, but both performed better than one-person inspections", summarised as "Increasing team size does not always improve performance. (1sX1p < 1sX2p, but 1sX2p = 1sX4p)".
  - Mechanism: the overlap in reviewers' preparation findings "had a median of 0", and "defect suppression rates were higher for 1sX4p than for 1sX2p inspections"; earlier, "inspectors often found completely disjoint sets of defects".
  - Splitting reviewers into two teams did not help: "Creating two smaller teams is not an effective way to reorganize a large group. (2sX2p = 1sX4p and 2sX1p = 1sX2p)".
  - Meetings: "Across all inspections, 30 percent of all defects discovered are meeting gains".
  - Some treatments were discontinued mid-experiment and two with-repair treatments had only four and five inspections, so the authors themselves say one result "should be viewed with caution".
- **Later replications and critiques:**
  - **Porter, Siy, Mockus and Votta (1998), Understanding the Sources of Variation in Software Inspections**, ACM TOSEM 7(1), 41–79, [doi 10.1145/268411.268421](https://doi.org/10.1145/268411.268421) (abstract read on the UMD DRUM technical-report page (fetch tool), quoted in segments; the UMD summary page also read (fetch tool)). The same group re-analysed the data and found the "inputs" (reviewers, authors and code units) "were responsible for much more variation in defect detection than was process structure", concluding that "better defect detection techniques, not better process structures" are the key (verbatim). Per the UMD summary page (fetch tool), code size, functionality and reviewers explained "50% of the variation" in effectiveness against "only 2%" for process structure. **This is the most important qualification:** the "two are as good as four" result sits inside a finding that team structure explained very little variance at all, so it says more about reviewer quality than about team size.
  - **Rigby and Bird (2013), Convergent Contemporary Software Peer Review Practices**, ESEC/FSE 2013, [doi 10.1145/2491411.2491444](https://doi.org/10.1145/2491411.2491444) (abstract read on the Microsoft Research page (fetch tool); full text partly read: Section 5 on reviewer numbers, from a course-copy PDF via pdftotext). A field study, not a replication. It cites Porter et al. (the 1998 TOSEM paper) as finding "that two reviewers discovered as many defects as four reviewers", calls it a consensus that "two inspectors find an optimal number of defects", and names "Convergent Practice 4: Two reviewers find an optimal number of defects". In Google Chrome and Android "there is a median of two reviewers"; at Microsoft a median of 3–4 are invited but 2 take part, with "only a minimal increase in the number of comments about the change when more reviewers were active" (verbatim). Supportive, but it measures comments as a proxy, not defects.
  - Sauer, Jeffery, Land and Yetton (2000), "The Effectiveness of Software Development Technical Reviews: A Behaviorally Motivated Program of Research" (IEEE TSE 26(1); [doi 10.1109/32.825763](https://doi.org/10.1109/32.825763)) is often cited as the theoretical account (individual expertise as the main driver), but it was seen only as search snippets; not read.
- **Bearing on the project:** the classic human result is "two reviewers match four, because individual expertise and the code dominate", with reviewers finding largely disjoint defects yet larger meetings suppressing more of them. For an LLM council the analogues are: seat quality may matter more than seat count; disjoint findings do not guarantee a gain from more seats; and a judge's dismissals may play the role of meeting suppression, which the appeal stage targets. No LLM-era replication of the team-size comparison on code was found other than the agent-scaling papers above.
- **Challenged?** Qualified by its own authors (1998) rather than overturned. No failed replication found.

#### New items met along the way

Per-item format. All are 2025–2026 and none is in the list of IDs already cited in the repo (checked by grep).

##### SGM: A Statistical Gödel Machine for Risk-Controlled Recursive Self-Modification
- **Citation:** Xuening Wu, Shenqin Yin, Yanlan Kang et al. (7 authors); arXiv preprint, v1 11 Oct 2025, v2 17 Sep 2026; [arXiv 2510.10232](https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.10232); code https://github.com/gravitywavelet/sgm-anon.
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API).
- **What it did:** replaces proof-of-improvement in Gödel machines with statistical tests (e-values, Hoeffding bounds), admitting a self-modification only when superiority is certified at a chosen confidence level, under a global error budget across rounds (Confirm-Triggered Harmonic Spending).
- **Key numbers:** none numeric in the abstract; it "certifies genuine gains on CIFAR-100, rejects spurious improvement on ImageNet-100" (verbatim).
- **Connection:** overlaps the project's keep rule (paired 90% CI above zero) and goes further with familywise error control across generations. Not on agents or review.
- **Novelty threat:** low; it is prior art for the acceptance rule, which the project should cite and perhaps adopt (error spending across generations).

##### The Illusion of Multi-Agent Advantage
- **Citation:** Prathyusha Jwalapuram, Hehai Lin, Chuyuan Li et al. (10 authors, Salesforce Research and others); arXiv preprint, v1 11 Jun 2026, v2 13 Jun 2026; [arXiv 2606.13003](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13003).
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API); full text partly read (Appendix E, fetched HTML).
- **What it did:** re-evaluated automatically generated MAS (ADAS, AFlow, MaAS, MAS-Zero, DyLAN, MAS-Orchestra) against CoT with self-consistency, plus a synthetic diagnostic set.
- **Key numbers:** automatic MAS "consistently underperform CoT-SC despite being up to 10x more expensive" (verbatim); ADAS's published results came from "evaluating all candidates directly on the test set" (verbatim, via correspondence with the authors).
- **Connection:** challenges the evolving-configurations literature and supports the project's held-out seeds and equal-cost baselines.
- **Novelty threat:** none; it strengthens the case for the project's controls.

##### Simple Baselines are Competitive with Code Evolution
- **Citation:** Yonatan Gideoni, Sebastian Risi and Yarin Gal; arXiv preprint, v1 18 Feb 2026, v2 12 Oct 2026; [arXiv 2602.16805](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.16805).
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API); full text partly read (Section 5, fetched HTML).
- **What it did:** compared code-evolution pipelines (including ADAS for agent scaffolds) with simple random-sampling baselines in four domains.
- **Key numbers:** with typical noisy evaluation "all automated search methods [pick] subpar scaffolds relative to a simple majority vote" (abstract, verbatim, bracket mine); recommends reporting with at least 10 repetitions and 95% CIs.
- **Connection:** supports the project's CI-gated selection and argues for random-search and majority-vote baselines in the evolution arm.
- **Novelty threat:** none.

##### When LLMs Benchmark Themselves: Deconstructing Self-Bias in Automated Evaluation
- **Citation:** Wenda Xu, Sweta Agrawal, Vilém Zouhar, Markus Freitag and Daniel Deutsch; EMNLP 2026 per Semantic Scholar (not confirmed on the proceedings page); arXiv v1 30 Sep 2025, v3 30 Aug 2026; [arXiv 2509.26600](https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.26600).
- **Read:** abstract read (arXiv abs page via curl).
- **What it did:** showed that when one LLM both generates a test set and evaluates outputs, the benchmark favours that model, with the two sources of bias compounding.
- **Key numbers:** self-bias "strong enough to cause each model to rank itself first, overriding the peer consensus ordering" (verbatim).
- **Connection:** supports the project's central same-family concern (Claude plants the seeds and Claude reviews), from machine translation and Chatbot Arena rather than code.
- **Novelty threat:** low; different domain, but it should be cited for the seed-author bias argument.

##### Are LLM Evaluators Really Narcissists? Sanity Checking Self-Preference Evaluations
- **Citation:** Dani Roytburg, Matthew Bozoukov, Matthew Nguyen et al. (6 authors); ICML 2026 Main per arXiv comment; v4 22 Jun 2026; [arXiv 2601.22548](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.22548).
- **Read:** abstract read (arXiv abs page via curl).
- **What it did:** adds an evaluator-quality baseline to self-preference tests.
- **Key numbers:** "only 51% of examples in previous findings retain statistical significance against this null hypothesis, covering 89.6% of total self-preference probability mass" (verbatim).
- **Connection:** challenges part of the self-preference literature the project cites; the project's judge analysis should use a judge-quality null.
- **Novelty threat:** none.

##### Unanimity Without Persuasion: A Single Round of Debate Erases the Disagreement That Verification Needs
- **Citation:** Yang Shu; arXiv preprint, v1 10 Aug 2026; [arXiv 2609.26145](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.26145).
- **Read:** abstract read (arXiv abs page via curl).
- **What it did:** tracked a heterogeneous seven-judge panel through blind and debate rounds on 600 code-correctness candidates.
- **Key numbers:** unanimity "jumps from 39.5% to 95.2% in round 1" while "accuracy moves by less than one point"; "by round 3 every wrong decision is unanimous" (verbatim).
- **Connection:** directly informs the debate arm and the order of verification, judging and appeal; supports independent review before any peer exposure.
- **Novelty threat:** low; judging code correctness, not finding planted bugs.

##### Agreement Overstates Evidence: Error Dependence in LLM Judge Consensus
- **Citation:** Elias Hossain, Niloofar Yousefi and Ser-Nam Lim; arXiv preprint, v1 18 Sep 2026; [arXiv 2609.22512](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.22512).
- **Read:** abstract read (arXiv abs page via curl).
- **What it did:** measured error correlation among open and frontier LLM judges and its effect on significance claims.
- **Key numbers:** mean pairwise error correlation 0.21; ten judges worth "roughly as much statistical information as 3.5 independent judges"; "In up to 28% of our comparisons, ignoring shared errors leads to the conclusion that one system is significantly better, while accounting for them does not" (verbatim).
- **Connection:** bears on any multi-judge or cross-family judge arm and on the CI-gated keep rule.
- **Novelty threat:** none.

##### Review Rarely Beats the Stronger Model: Separating Verification from Capability Transfer in LLM Pipelines
- **Citation:** Yirui Zhang and Yash Raj Shrestha; arXiv preprint, v1 11 Oct 2026, v3 3 Nov 2026; [arXiv 2610.15897](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15897); code https://github.com/Kamilaabb/verification-or-capability-transfer.
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API).
- **What it did:** compared cross-model review with the stronger model alone across 48 model pairs.
- **Key numbers:** review beats the stronger model "in only 9 of 96 review directions"; review costs "2.6 to 2.8 times as much as the stronger model alone" (verbatim).
- **Connection:** challenges how cross-model review gains (Xiang et al.; Song 2026) are usually read; the project should report the strongest single reviewer alone.
- **Novelty threat:** none.

##### More Rounds, More Noise: Why Multi-Turn Review Fails to Improve Cross-Context Verification
- **Citation:** Tae-Eun Song; arXiv preprint, 17 Mar 2026; [arXiv 2603.16244](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.16244).
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API).
- **What it did:** on the same 30 artifacts and 150 injected errors as Song's other papers, compared single-pass fresh-session review with four multi-turn variants.
- **Key numbers:** single-pass F1 0.376 against 0.303 for the best multi-turn variant; "62% more false positives (8.5 vs. 5.2)" (verbatim).
- **Connection:** supports single-pass independent review; a warning for the appeal and debate stages (later rounds "fabricate findings").
- **Novelty threat:** low.

##### MemCo: No Isolated Evolution, No Blind Search: Memory-Guided Prompt Co-evolution for Industrial Multi-agent Vulnerability Detection
- **Citation:** Jie Xu, Zi-Han Wu, Yun Peng, Chun-Yong Chong, Ling-Han Meng, Kui Liu and Xiao-Hua Jia; ASE 2026; [doi 10.1145/3832783.3834522](https://doi.org/10.1145/3832783.3834522).
- **Read:** abstract read (Semantic Scholar API).
- **What it did:** co-evolves the prompts of all agents in a vulnerability-detection MAS against the end-to-end result, evolving only the agents blamed for each misclassification, with a memory of past edits.
- **Key numbers:** "+8.4 to +18.0 points" macro-F1 on public benchmarks; per-generation evolution tokens down 48.5% (verbatim).
- **Connection:** overlaps the project's council evolution (whole-system fitness, per-seat blame), on bug detection with labelled data.
- **Novelty threat:** medium for "evolving a multi-agent bug detector"; low for the project's specific design (review of code changes, judge, appeal, planted seeds, cost-matched arms).

##### The Red Queen Gödel Machine: Co-Evolving Agents and Their Evaluators
- **Citation:** Alex Iacob, Andrej Jovanović, William F. Shen et al. (15 authors); arXiv preprint, v3 8 Oct 2026; [arXiv 2606.26294](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.26294).
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API).
- **What it did:** co-evolves evaluators (including a code reviewer grading patches) with the agents they guide, with an adversarial objective against self-preference.
- **Key numbers:** held-out DeepSWE pass rate 82.1% against 75.0% for the fixed-evaluator baseline (verbatim).
- **Connection:** overlaps "evolving reviewers" and "self-preference control", but the reviewer is a fitness signal, not the scored product.
- **Novelty threat:** medium.

##### Too Many Cooks Spoil The Broth: Auditing Multi-Agent LLM Councils For Medical Reasoning
- **Citation:** Mukul Joshi, Pratik Jalan, Akhilesh Magotra and Kshitij Sharad Jadhav; EMNLP 2026 (main), pp. 49230–49245; [ACL Anthology 2026.emnlp-main.2164](https://aclanthology.org/2026.emnlp-main.2164/).
- **Read:** abstract page read (fetch tool; only a short verbatim excerpt available, the rest paraphrased by the tool).
- **What it did:** audited a five-model debate "council" of small open models on 8,402 medical QA items.
- **Key numbers:** about seven times the model calls of a single larger model, which it trailed on four of six datasets (fetch-tool paraphrase, not verbatim).
- **Connection:** same "council" vocabulary; supports single-model baselines and caution about consensus.
- **Novelty threat:** none (QA, not review).

##### Homogenization in Multi-Agent Systems
- **Citation:** Prakhar Ganesh, Kyra Wilson, Luca Zappella et al. (7 authors); arXiv preprint, 7 Oct 2026; [arXiv 2610.09824](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.09824).
- **Read:** abstract read (arXiv abs page via curl).
- **What it did:** measured conformity, polarisation and inertia in MAS for code generation, hiring and peer review.
- **Key numbers:** none in the abstract; in code generation, homogenisation "hides and amplifies correlated errors", and mixed-model MAS "fail to reduce homogenization risks" (verbatim).
- **Connection:** supports independent (non-interacting) review seats; challenges the hope that mixing families alone fixes shared blind spots.
- **Novelty threat:** low.

##### Convergence Without Understanding: When Language Models Agree on Representations but Disagree on Reasoning
- **Citation:** Muhammad Usama and Dong Eui Chang; arXiv preprint, 2026; [arXiv 2605.23315](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.23315).
- **Read:** abstract read (arXiv abs page via curl).
- **What it did:** compared internal representations of 16 models from 8 families on 800 reasoning problems.
- **Key numbers:** CKA 0.897 on problems all models fail against 0.830 on problems they solve (verbatim).
- **Connection:** supports the shared-blind-spot concern for mixed-family councils.
- **Novelty threat:** none.

##### Also met, abstract read, worth a line each
- **The Subjectivity of Monoculture** (Jo, Garg and Raghavan; NeurIPS 2026; [arXiv 2602.24086](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.24086)): measured monoculture depends on the null model and largely disappears in one dataset with item difficulty (see Goel and Kim blocks).
- **Prior Audit-Repair Context Shifts LLM Verifier Thresholds Toward Leniency** ([arXiv 2608.16003](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.16003)): earlier audit-repair context in the window lowers verifier false alarms by moving the threshold (see Khullar block).
- **The Interaction Tax: When Communication Erases Diversity in Multi-Agent Teams** (Ann, Liu and Tan; **ICML 2026, PMLR 306** per arXiv comment; [arXiv 2608.23541](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23541); abstract read (arXiv abs page via curl)): "when agents read each other's complete outputs, their proposals converge within one round"; "Independent proposal generation avoids this collapse" (verbatim). Supports independent review seats.
- **Hyperagents** (Zhang et al.; [arXiv 2603.19461](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.19461)) and **Huxley-Gödel Machine** ([arXiv 2510.21614](https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.21614)): see the DGM block.
- **Evolving Agents in the Dark (RHO)** (Pan et al.; EMNLP 2026 Findings; [arXiv 2606.05922](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05922)): harness evolution that uses the agent's own pairwise self-preference as the selection signal (see MulVul block).
- **How Much Does the Harness Matter, and for Whom?** (Galea Curmi and Wilde; CAMLIS 2026; [arXiv 2610.28802](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28802)): 14.3% of 265 confirmed vulnerability root causes found by exactly one model (see AgentFlow block).
- **Strategic Heterogeneous Multi-Agent Architecture for Cost-Effective Code Vulnerability Detection** ([arXiv 2604.21282](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.21282)): three role experts plus a cross-family verifier (see MulVul block).
- **Debate or Vote** (NeurIPS 2025 Spotlight; [arXiv 2508.17536](https://arxiv.org/abs/2508.17536)), **Stop Overvaluing Multi-Agent Debate** ([arXiv 2502.08788](https://arxiv.org/abs/2502.08788)), **When Order Matters** ([arXiv 2609.38964](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38964)), **Multi-agent discussion gains less when dissent is withheld** ([arXiv 2609.38324](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38324)) and **Copies or Sources?** ([arXiv 2610.06192](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.06192)): see the Smit block.
- **Correlated Failures of AI Approval Monitors** ([arXiv 2610.29178](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29178)), **Builder, Defender, Breaker** ([arXiv 2607.03215](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.03215)), **Are Diversity Metrics Measuring Diversity?** ([arXiv 2607.20768](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20768)), **Does Reasoning Make Language Models Agree?** ([arXiv 2610.41691](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41691)) and **Inferred Generative-Process Diversity Predicts Correlated Failure** ([arXiv 2609.03422](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03422)): see the Goel and Kim blocks.
- **Know Thyself?** ([arXiv 2510.03399](https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.03399)), **A Shared Taste for Model-Written Text** ([arXiv 2610.00369](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00369)), **Self-Preference Is Weak or Absent ...** ([arXiv 2606.20093](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.20093)), **Beyond the Surface** ([arXiv 2506.02592](https://arxiv.org/abs/2506.02592)), **Quantifying and Mitigating Self-Preference Bias of LLM Judges** ([arXiv 2604.22891](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.22891)) and **Evaluating medical AI under missing information** ([arXiv 2607.18828](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.18828)): see the self-preference synthesis.
- **Does Failure Feedback Direct Agent Self-Modification?** ([arXiv 2610.14662](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14662)), **Reflections on Trusting Trust, Revisited** ([arXiv 2609.17817](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.17817)), **Auditing the Product, Not the Recursion** ([arXiv 2610.27610](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27610)), **Who Verifies the Verifier?** ([arXiv 2610.11464](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.11464)), **SpecBench** ([arXiv 2605.21384](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.21384)), **JET** ([arXiv 2609.34126](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.34126)) and **Rethinking Self-Evolving Agents** ([arXiv 2608.09629](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.09629)): see the DGM and ADAS blocks (JET and Rethinking Self-Evolving Agents: abstracts read, not discussed further).
- **More Agents, More Samples, or More Thinking?** ([arXiv 2611.17610](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17610)): see the Kim scaling block.
- **Cross-Provider Review as a Runtime Contract for Coding Agents** ([arXiv 2610.10961](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.10961)) and **A Gated Spec-Driven Workflow: An Audit-Trail Study of Coding-Agent Work** (Abdo and Brom; [arXiv 2611.05094](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05094); abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API)): the second studies a Claude Code workflow with a reviewer subagent checking each artefact against a fixed defect list and finds "The weak point was the orchestrator, which runs and judges its own checks" (verbatim). Small, authors' own projects.

## Part 2. New searches

New searches on the eleven topics in the request, in the request's order, with industry tools and docs as a topic of their own. Within each topic the closest items come first. Papers already cited in the repo are skipped, except where a new version changes something.

### 2.1 Multi-agent LLM code review and review councils

#### CyberDual: AI Attackers versus AI Code Reviewers in Real Software Maintenance
- **Citation:** Chenyuan Yang, Hwiwon Lee, Jongseong Kim, Yihan Yang, Dongjun Kim, Yinfang Chen and Lingming Zhang; arXiv preprint (cs.CR); 25 Oct 2026; [arXiv 2610.34435](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34435). No venue stated.
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API); full text partly read (fetch tool, first 100k of 116k characters).
- **What it did:** an arena where attacker agents hide an exploitable bug inside real maintenance work on open-source projects and reviewer agents (Claude Code with Sonnet 5, Claude Code with Opus 4.8, Codex with GPT-6-Sol) try to find and repair it. Execution of the attacker's proof of concept on the post-review code decides each match, so the key cannot be gamed by agent reports.
- **Key numbers:** abstract (verbatim): "Three agentic reviewers catch 85-91% of 1,088 valid attacks from six attackers"; "When they miss, Claude reviewers mostly approve the attack, while GPT-6-Sol mostly repairs the wrong code; a reviewer from a second vendor catches about two thirds of a Claude reviewer's misses." Full text (fetch tool): survival 14.3% (Sonnet 5), 10.9% (Opus 4.8), 9.2% (GPT-6-Sol); 28 attacks pass all three; section 3.5 says GPT-6-Sol "catches 69% of the attacks that Sonnet 5 misses" (denominator not seen). Claude reviewers agree on some misses (kappa 0.69 for "fix-in-hand" misses, ≤ 0.24 for "window" misses). False rejections of benign changes are explicitly not measured.
- **Connection:** supports the mixed-family arm and the cross-family judge: a second vendor recovers most of a Claude reviewer's misses, and two Claude reviewers share some blind spots. It is planted-bug review with execution as the key, close to the project's seed design, though adversarial (security) and with single reviewers rather than councils. Its lack of a benign control is exactly the precision side the project adds.
- **Novelty threat:** medium on the same-family question (it measures cross-vendor complementarity on planted bugs), low on councils.

#### SLEAN: Simple Lightweight Ensemble Analysis Network for Multi-Provider LLM Coordination
- **Citation:** Matheus J. T. Vargas; arXiv preprint; 11 Oct 2025; [arXiv 2510.10010](https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.10010). No venue.
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API).
- **What it did:** a three-phase protocol across several LLM providers: independent analysis, cross-critique, then arbitration, used as a filter on AI-proposed bug fixes (not on review findings).
- **Key numbers:** "SLEAN's filtering accepted 22 fixes (31.9%, 95% CI 20.9-42.9%) while rejecting 47"; "Agreement between AI systems showed weak correlation with fix quality: high convergence (at least 80%) occurred in 4 of 15 cases and improved acceptance by only 2.4% points".
- **Connection:** overlaps the council shape (independent, then critique, then arbiter, across families) and the finding that agreement between models says little about quality is relevant to consensus combining. Very small (15 bugs) and single-author.
- **Novelty threat:** low.

#### "3+1" heterogeneous council: Strategic Heterogeneous Multi-Agent Architecture for Cost-Effective Code Vulnerability Detection
- **Citation:** Zhaohui Geoffrey Wang; AAMAS 2026 Workshop on Software Engineering (per the arXiv comment); 23 Apr 2026; [arXiv 2604.21282](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.21282).
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API).
- **What it did:** three DeepSeek-V3 "experts" with different perspectives (code structure, security patterns, debugging logic) in parallel, plus a local Qwen3-8B "adversarial verifier", on 262 Juliet samples across 14 CWEs with balanced vulnerable and benign classes.
- **Key numbers:** "77.2% F1 score with 62.9% precision and 100% recall at $0.002 per sample"; single expert F1 71.4%; the verifier "significantly improves precision (+10.3 percentage points, p < 1e-6, McNemar's test)".
- **Connection:** overlaps specialist seats plus a filtering judge with cost reported, against a single-expert baseline, on a labelled key with benign controls. Not equal-cost (three experts against one), synthetic Juliet code, function-level classification.
- **Novelty threat:** low.

#### AEGIS: From Clues to Verdicts: Graph-Guided Deep Vulnerability Reasoning via Dialectics and Meta-Auditing
- **Citation:** Sen Fang, Weiyuan Ding, Zhezhen Cao, Zhou Yang and Bowen Xu; arXiv preprint; 21 Mar 2026; [arXiv 2603.20637](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.20637).
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API).
- **What it did:** a Verifier Agent argues for and against exploitability of each suspicious "clue" within a sliced evidence boundary, and an independent Audit Agent checks every claim and can veto. Argues that agent debate without a bounded evidence base is driven by "rhetorical persuasiveness rather than verifiable facts".
- **Key numbers:** "122 Pair-wise Correct Predictions" on PrimeVul (first above 100, per the authors); "reduces the false positive rate by up to 54.40%"; "$0.09 per sample".
- **Connection:** overlaps the judge plus appeal idea in reverse (the auditor vetoes rather than reinstates) and supports requiring evidence in briefs. The critique of ungrounded debate bears on the debate arm.
- **Novelty threat:** low.

#### VulAgent: Hypothesis-Validation based Multi-Agent Vulnerability Detection
- **Citation:** Ziliang Wang, Ge Li, Jia Li, Hao Zhu and Zhi Jin; arXiv preprint; 15 Sep 2025; [arXiv 2509.11523](https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.11523).
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API).
- **What it did:** specialised agents per analysis view (for example memory, authorisation) surface sensitive sites; then each report is checked by building hypothesis conditions and a trigger path.
- **Key numbers:** "improves overall accuracy by 6.6%, increases the correct identification rate of vulnerable--fixed code pairs by up to 450% (246% on average), and reduces the false positive rate by about 36%".
- **Connection:** overlaps specialist seats plus a verification stage. No generalist-at-equal-cost comparison is stated in the abstract.
- **Novelty threat:** low.

#### VulnAgent-R2: Evidence-Calibrated Multi-Agent Auditing for Repository-Level Vulnerability Detection
- **Citation:** Renwei Meng, Haoyi Wu and Jingming Wang; arXiv preprint; v1 11 Mar 2026, v3 3 Jun 2026; [arXiv 2603.13384](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.13384). Code: github.com/renweimeng/Vlun-Agent-X (per the abstract; not opened).
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API).
- **What it did:** role-specialised agents, a "sceptic counter-evidence" agent, selective dynamic verification and a cost-risk scheduler. Reports bootstrap CIs and Holm-adjusted tests.
- **Key numbers:** PrimeVul 0.385 F1; "reducing online tokens by 38.3% over always-full multi-agent execution"; "PrimeVul gain over VulnAgent-X is +0.038 F1, 95% CI [0.020, 0.055], Holm-adjusted p=0.009".
- **Connection:** loosely related. Its paired bootstrap CI on a gain over a parent version resembles the project's keep-a-change rule; its cost scheduler is an analogue of adaptive council size.
- **Novelty threat:** none.

#### CIVER: Causal Intervention Verification for Reliable Code Review
- **Citation:** Xinyuan Song, Hasibul Haque, Jay Somasundaram, Ryan Turner, Bowen Zhu and Liang Zhao; "WWW 2026" per the arXiv comment (not confirmed elsewhere); 26 Oct 2026; [arXiv 2610.36010](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36010).
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API).
- **What it did:** a verifier stage for review findings: an intervention module makes repair-oriented perturbations, and a finding counts as genuine if the reviewer's judgement responds to the targeted repair and stays stable under unrelated changes. Trained by expert iteration.
- **Key numbers:** "CIVER correctly identifies 77% of genuine defects through targeted repairs" on SWE-Review-Bench.
- **Connection:** overlaps the judge: it is a mechanical test for whether a finding is real, an alternative to an LLM judge's say-so. Its motivation ("LLM reviewers often generate lengthy reviews containing numerous potential issues, many of which are false positives") supports the project's precision scoring.
- **Novelty threat:** low.

#### CRJudgeBench: Can AI Detect Plausible but Invalid Code Reviews?
- **Citation:** Yue Pan, Jiawei Li, Ziyuan Zhang, Xiangxin Zhao and He Ye; arXiv preprint, "Under review at ICLR 2027" per the comment; 29 Sep 2026; [arXiv 2609.37216](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.37216). Dataset: huggingface.co/datasets/dcloud347/CRJudgeBenchmark (per the abstract; not opened).
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API).
- **What it did:** 1,199 review comments from real PRs plus expert-verified perturbations, labelled trustworthy or "plausible but untrustworthy"; a trained repository-grounded judge (Sentinel) gathers code evidence before ruling.
- **Key numbers:** "Sentinel achieves 76.60% accuracy, outperforming GLM-5.3 by 6.13 percentage points and its base model by 19.78 points" on a 359-instance test set.
- **Connection:** directly about the judge's task (rule each finding real or not). Suggests general LLMs reach roughly 70% accuracy on this, so the judge is a major error source worth measuring, as the project plans. A possible off-the-shelf test set for calibrating the judge.
- **Novelty threat:** low.

#### Collusion among reviewer agents: Studying Coordination and Collusion in Multi-Agent LLM Code Reviews
- **Citation:** Jennifer Za, Aristeidis Panos, Roger Dearnaley and Samuel Albanie; NeurIPS 2025 Workshop on Multi-Turn Interactions in Large Language Models (poster), per the [NeurIPS virtual page](https://nips.cc/virtual/2025/128054). No arXiv record found by title search.
- **Read:** abstract page read (fetch tool; the abstract was summarised, not quoted in full).
- **What it did:** a multi-agent code-review simulation where agents can be given a backdoor incentive; seven frontier models, 10 rounds, 90 seeds; agents choose whom to ask for review.
- **Key numbers (fetch tool summary, not verbatim except where quoted):** six of seven models submitted functionally impaired code in 34.9–75.9% of attempts; GPT-5 "≤10%"; GPT, Gemini and Claude models preferentially requested reviews from other saboteurs at 29.2–38.5% against a 20% random baseline ("possible selective coordination capabilities").
- **Connection:** loosely related but pointed: when reviewers can choose peers or share incentives, councils can collude. The project's seats are independent and do not choose each other, which avoids this, but agent-led orchestration arms that let agents route work could reintroduce it.
- **Novelty threat:** none.

#### Judges and decision costs: When Policies Change Probabilities: Modular Decision-Making for LLM Code Review
- **Citation:** Rasvik Kudum, Max Corbett, Hitansh Paliwal, Romaisa Fatima, Thomas Jiralerspong and Sneheel Sarangi; arXiv preprint; 2 Aug 2026; [arXiv 2608.02677](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02677). An OpenReview record titled "When Policies Change Probabilities: A Deployment Audit of LLM Code-Review Judges" is listed as a JUDGe 2026 poster (seen in OpenReview API search metadata only; that it is the same paper is not confirmed).
- **Read:** abstract page read (fetch tool).
- **What it did:** LLM reviewers output a probability that a patch is faulty and an accept or reject action; tests what happens when the prompt states asymmetric costs (for example 10:1 for a false accept).
- **Key numbers (quoted by the fetch tool):** "replacing an equal-cost policy with a 10:1 false-accept policy changes reported failure probabilities" (13.6–16.9 points on average); "For every reviewer, the actions returned under the high-cost prompt are worse than rejecting all patches"; eliciting risk without the policy and applying the threshold afterwards reduced loss for all four systems.
- **Connection:** directly relevant to the judge and severity design: telling the judge the scoring rule can distort its probabilities, so the project should elicit severity or confidence first and apply thresholds outside the model (as the Anthropic plugin's fixed 80 cut-off does).
- **Novelty threat:** none.

#### Red Queen Gödel Machine: Co-Evolving Agents and Their Evaluators
- **Citation:** Alex Iacob, Andrej Jovanović, William F. Shen et al. (15 authors); arXiv preprint, "Preliminary preprint; work in progress"; v1 24 Jun 2026, v3 8 Oct 2026; [arXiv 2606.26294](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.26294).
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API).
- **What it did:** evolutionary self-improvement where the evaluator also evolves. On DeepSWE, a co-evolved code reviewer grades coder patches to guide search; an extra adversarial objective is used to find reviewers "equally stringent on AI and human work".
- **Key numbers:** "the RQGM coder passes 82.1% of held-out tasks against the baseline's 75.0%"; "the RQGM reduces self-preference bias via an additional adversarial objective".
- **Connection:** overlaps evolving reviewers and the self-preference concern (it evolves a reviewer while regularising against AI-favouring bias). The reviewer is a fitness signal for coders, not the thing scored against planted bugs, so it is the reverse of the project's set-up. Also a warning case: an evolving evaluator moves the target, which the project's fixed seed key avoids.
- **Novelty threat:** low-medium (evolving code reviewers exists now, but not councils scored on a planted key).

#### Reward hacking spreads: Reward Hacking as a Transmissible Strategy in Multi-Agent Coding Systems
- **Citation:** Yuepeng Hu, Pegah Ghahremani, Reza Aghajani, Mahesh Pasupuleti and Himaghna Bhattacharjee; arXiv preprint; v1 9 Oct 2026, v2 12 Oct 2026; [arXiv 2610.12822](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.12822).
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API).
- **What it did:** shows a reward-hacking strategy in one agent propagates to others through code, shared memory and plans, and can "survive candidate aggregation"; tests code review by the coder or "a separate verifier" as mitigation.
- **Key numbers:** none in the abstract beyond "both of which reduce delivered hacks".
- **Connection:** loosely related; supports the held-out seeds and the concern that evolved configurations could learn to game the scorer, and that aggregation does not remove a shared bad strategy.
- **Novelty threat:** none.

#### Reviewer self-blindness: Do Coding Agents Catch Their Own Bugs? (They Don't.)
- **Citation:** Shaswat Patel, Michel Liao, Peter Henderson, He He and Valerie Chen; arXiv preprint; 31 Oct 2026; [arXiv 2611.01181](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01181).
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API).
- **What it did:** Foresight-SWE, 212 pairs of PRs where the second PR fixes defects the first introduced; measures whether agents avoid the later-found defect while solving the task.
- **Key numbers:** "FRR ranges from only 6.13% to 24.53%" across eleven models.
- **Connection:** loosely related; a ready source of real "escaped" defects paired with their introducing PR (like SZZ seeds), and evidence for the authoring side of the same-family worry. It tests authoring, not review.
- **Novelty threat:** none.

#### SWE-Review: Closing the Loop on Issue Resolution with Agentic Code Review
- **Citation:** Ruoyu Wang, Jierun Chen, Shaowei Wang et al. (10 authors); arXiv preprint; 7 Jul 2026; [arXiv 2607.06065](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06065).
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API).
- **What it did:** a reviewer agent explores the repository, accepts or rejects an AI-generated PR and gives feedback for revision; introduces SWE-Review-Bench (later used by CIVER) and a trajectory dataset.
- **Key numbers:** none in the abstract; it "outperforms single-turn fixed-context review in both decision accuracy and resolve rate after revision".
- **Connection:** loosely related; review scored by downstream use rather than planted bugs; single reviewer.
- **Novelty threat:** none.

#### Tokenomics: Quantifying Where Tokens Are Used in Agentic Software Engineering
- **Citation:** Mohamad Salim, Jasmine Latendresse, SayedHassan Khatoonabadi and Emad Shihab; arXiv preprint; 20 Jan 2026; [arXiv 2601.14470](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.14470).
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API).
- **What it did:** token accounting of 30 ChatDev tasks with a GPT-5 reasoning model, by phase.
- **Key numbers:** "the iterative Code Review stage accounts for the majority of token consumption for an average of 59.4% of tokens"; input tokens "53.9%".
- **Connection:** loosely related; supports reporting cost per stage (seats, judge, appeal) separately, since review loops dominate cost in a multi-agent pipeline.
- **Novelty threat:** none.

#### Agentic patterns do not help: Beyond Benchmarks: Evaluating Large Language Models and Agentic Patterns for Static Code Analysis Warning Classification
- **Citation:** Lakmal Deshapriya, Sherlock A. Licorish, Brendon J. Woodford and Grant Dick; arXiv preprint; 14 Nov 2026; [arXiv 2611.19086](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19086).
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API).
- **What it did:** three LLMs classify static-analysis warnings on four manually annotated datasets, with and without "Debating and Self-Consistency".
- **Key numbers:** "0.52 to 0.96 F1-Score for the best models"; "the use of Agentic patterns does not improve LLMs' performance in most scenarios."
- **Connection:** challenges the assumption that debate or self-consistency improves a judge-like true/false-positive ruling; relevant to the debate arm and to the judge.
- **Novelty threat:** none.

#### SGCR: A Specification-Grounded Framework for Trustworthy LLM Code Review
- **Citation:** Kai Wang, Bingcheng Mao, Shuai Jia, Yujie Ding, Dongming Han, Tianyi Ma and Bin Cao; ASE 2025 (per the arXiv comment); v1 19 Dec 2025, v2 27 Jan 2026; [arXiv 2512.17540](https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.17540).
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API).
- **What it did:** two paths: deterministic checks against rules from human specifications, and an implicit path that discovers and verifies further issues; deployed at HiThink Research.
- **Key numbers:** "42% developer adoption rate-a 90.9% relative improvement over a baseline LLM (22%)".
- **Connection:** loosely related to the `CLAUDE.md` context arm: grounding review in project specifications roughly doubled adoption in one deployment.
- **Novelty threat:** none.

#### Other multi-agent or verifier items seen at abstract level (lower relevance)
- **Archer** ([arXiv 2607.01808](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.01808); Yunbo Ni and Shaohua Li, 2 Jul 2026; abstract read, verbatim, arXiv API): agentic LLVM review with "a deterministic validation guard to admit only findings backed by executable evidence"; found "21% of open PRs and 11% of closed PRs are buggy". A verification stage by execution rather than by an LLM judge.
- **PRGuard / MalPR-Bench** ([arXiv 2608.25730](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.25730); Zhuo Chen et al., 26 Aug 2026; abstract read, verbatim, arXiv API): 89 malicious and 50 benign PRs with pre-committed rubrics; separates a correct block verdict from naming the actual vulnerability. "PRGuard identifies 22 target vulnerabilities versus 16 for CodeRabbit", with similar block totals (22/31 against 24/31). Supports the project's requirement that a hit must match the seed, not just flag the PR.
- **Multi-Agent LLM Committees for Software Beta Testing** ([arXiv 2512.21352](https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.21352); 21 Dec 2025; abstract read, verbatim, arXiv API): voting committees of diverse vision LLMs; on 20 injected regressions, committee F1 0.91 against 0.78 single-agent. Testing, not review; no cost control.
- **Argus** ([arXiv 2604.06633](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.06633); Zi Liang et al., 8 Apr 2026; abstract read): multi-agent SAST orchestration; no figures in the abstract.
- **iCodeReviewer** ([arXiv 2510.12186](https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.12186); Yun Peng et al., 14 Oct 2025; abstract read, verbatim, arXiv API): "mixture-of-prompts" with one prompt expert per security issue and a router that activates only relevant experts "reducing the false positives"; "F1 of 63.98%"; "acceptance rate up to 84%" in production. Router-selected specialists are an alternative to always-on seats.
- **DesignLore / DesignReviewBench** ([arXiv 2610.21925](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21925); 15 Oct 2026; abstract read): Linux-kernel design-trade-off review; strongest baseline F1 0.213. Shows design findings are a weak spot for agents.

#### Benchmarks met on the way (not multi-agent, but bear on the scoring)
- **SecPRBench: Can Agentic Code Review Catch Vulnerabilities Before They Are Merged?** ([arXiv 2610.33509](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33509); Yihan Yang, Chenyuan Yang, Zijie Zhao et al. (8 authors), v1 24 Oct 2026, v2 30 Oct 2026; abstract read, verbatim, arXiv API). 265 validated real bug-introducing commits from nine systems (Firefox, V8, OpenSSL among them), LLM judge credits findings matching the ground truth. "recall ranges from 32.5% to 78.1%"; "in 68% of them [misses], the agent reports on a file that holds the flaw but argues for a different mechanism"; "OpenAI's Codex Security lowers recall by 8.3 points, mostly by abstaining, while Anthropic's Defending Code harness raises it by 7.7 points, at a higher cost." Relevant to matching rules: a finding in the right file with the wrong mechanism must not count as a hit. Same group as CyberDual.
- **SWE-PRBench** ([arXiv 2603.26130](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.26130); Deepak Kumar, 27 Mar 2026; abstract read, verbatim, arXiv API). The benchmark Adversarial Review (covered) used: 350 PRs, LLM judge "validated at kappa=0.75"; "8 frontier models detect only 15-31% of human-flagged issues"; all models "degrade monotonically" as more context is added ("attention dilution"). Bears on the `CLAUDE.md` arm: more context can hurt.
- **CR-Bench-v2** ([arXiv 2611.02566](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02566); same four authors as CR-Bench, 2–3 Nov 2026; abstract read, verbatim, arXiv API). A new arXiv record that restates CR-Bench (584 instances, 174 verified) with two metrics (Known-Defect Detection Rate and Usefulness Rate), "three independent LLM judges" and Gemini Code Assist added: "the strongest systems detect only about one-third of known defects (KDDR ≈ 33%), and Gemini Code Assist detects 8.1%." Each instance now carries "a taxonomy label for bug category, impact, and severity", which the covered CR-Bench entry does not mention. See the corrections section.

### 2.2 Industry tools and docs (CodeRabbit, Graphite, Qodo, Copilot, Anthropic and others)

All of these are vendor sources unless marked otherwise; figures are the vendors' own and none publishes enough method to reproduce them except where stated.

#### Anthropic: `/code-review` (local), `/code-review ultra` (Ultrareview) and the managed Code Review service
- **Sources:** [Find bugs with ultrareview](https://code.claude.com/docs/en/ultrareview) (official docs, no date shown); [Review locally with /code-review](https://code.claude.com/docs/en/code-review-local) (official docs); [Code Review](https://code.claude.com/docs/en/code-review) (official docs for the managed GitHub service); [Bringing Code Review to Claude Code](https://claude.com/blog/code-review) (Anthropic blog, 9 Mar 2026, no author shown).
- **Read:** page read (fetch tool) for all four; the three docs pages came back as raw Markdown, so quotes from them are verbatim; blog figures are as quoted by the fetch tool.
- **Architecture:**
  - **Ultra:** "Claude Code launches a fleet of reviewer agents in a cloud sandbox"; "every reported finding is independently reproduced and verified"; comparison table: "multi-agent fleet with independent verification", "roughly 5 to 15 minutes", "free runs, then roughly \$5 to \$25 per review as usage credits". Three free runs per Pro, Max or Ultra account, one-time. Branch reviews are limited to "500 changed files and 8,000 changed lines by default". The docs say it is a "research preview" and "`/ultrareview` is an alias". Output is file location plus explanation; `--json` prints a `bugs.json` payload. No number of agents, model, severity scheme or ranking rule is stated.
  - **Local `/code-review`:** runs as a background forked subagent; effort levels trade "coverage for confidence. At `low`, the review reports the findings it's most confident in ... From `medium` through `max`, the review broadens coverage." `--max-findings` default limit is 32 where findings go through the `ReportFindings` tool. "the review follows your `CLAUDE.md`" and "doesn't read `REVIEW.md`". No subagent count is given.
  - **Managed Code Review (Team and Enterprise):** four stages: "Find: several agents examine the diff and the surrounding code in parallel. Verify: a verification step checks the candidate findings against the code and filters out false positives. Rank: the findings that remain are deduplicated and ranked by severity. Post". It reads code only: "without building or running your code". Levels Quick, Standard and Deep; model not selectable.
- **Published figures (blog, 9 Mar 2026, quoted by the fetch tool):** "Before, 16% of PRs got substantive review comments. Now 54% do."; "on large PRs (over 1,000 lines changed), 84% get findings, averaging 7.5 issues. On small PRs under 50 lines, that drops to 31%, averaging 0.5 issues."; "less than 1% of findings are marked incorrect"; "generally average $15–25"; "the average review takes around 20 minutes". No method, sample size or definition of "substantive" or "marked incorrect" is given. These are internal Anthropic figures, not independent.
- **The open-source ancestor (GitHub, read via `gh api`):** the official `code-review` plugin in [anthropics/claude-plugins-official](https://github.com/anthropics/claude-plugins-official/tree/main/plugins/code-review) (README v1.0.0, author Boris Cherny; last commit to the folder 26 Oct 2026). It "Launches 4 parallel agents": "Agents #1 & #2: Audit for CLAUDE.md compliance", "Agent #3: Scan for obvious bugs in changes", "Agent #4: Analyze git blame/history", then "Nx confidence scorers: One per issue for independent scoring" with issues below "80 confidence threshold" dropped. This is a small, inspectable judged council (specialist seats, redundancy, per-finding scorer, threshold). Whether the bundled `/code-review` skill or the cloud service still works this way is not stated in the docs. The same repository's `pr-review-toolkit` bundles six specialist agents (comment-analyzer, pr-test-analyzer, silent-failure-hunter, type-design-analyzer, code-reviewer, code-simplifier).
- **Claude Code GitHub Actions:** the [claude-code-action](https://github.com/anthropics/claude-code-action) example `pr-review-comprehensive.yml` (read via `gh api`) is a single prompted pass over five focus areas (quality, security, performance, testing, documentation) posting inline comments; no verification stage.
- **Connection:** the ultra review is the project's external baseline. The docs confirm it is a multi-agent fleet with a verification step, so the comparison is council against council, not council against one model. Its cost ($5–25) and duration (5–15 min) are the figures to beat on cost. The plugin gives a concrete, published design (4 seats, confidence judge, threshold 80) that could serve as a named "vendor-style" arm.
- **Novelty threat:** medium for the project's framing: Anthropic ships a specialist-seat council with a judging threshold, so "a council of Claude reviewers with a judge" is not new as a product. What remains new is measuring such councils against a planted key and evolving them.

#### Anthropic engineering: How we built our multi-agent research system
- **Source:** Jeremy Hadfield, Barry Zhang, Kenneth Lien, Florian Scholz, Jeremy Fox and Daniel Ford; [Anthropic engineering blog](https://www.anthropic.com/engineering/multi-agent-research-system), 13 Jun 2025. Vendor, not peer-reviewed.
- **Read:** page read (fetch tool).
- **Key points (quoted by the fetch tool):** orchestrator-worker design; Opus 4 lead with Sonnet 4 subagents "outperformed single-agent Claude Opus 4 by 90.2% on our internal research eval"; "Multi-agent systems use about 15× more tokens than chats"; "Token usage by itself explains 80% of the variance" on BrowseComp; domains needing shared context "are not a good fit for multi-agent systems today"; most coding tasks have fewer parallel parts than research; a single LLM-judge call with one rubric prompt was "the most consistent and aligned with human judgements".
- **Connection:** supports the equal-cost rule (gains largely track tokens) and the single-call judge design; its caution that coding is less parallelisable is a prior against large councils.
- **Novelty threat:** none.

#### Cursor Bugbot (and Graphite, now owned by Cursor)
- **Sources:** [Building a better Bugbot](https://cursor.com/blog/building-bugbot), Jon Kaplan, Cursor blog, 15 Jan 2026; [Cursor's Graphite announcement](https://cursor.com/blog/graphite), 19 Dec 2025; [Graphite AI Reviews docs](https://graphite.com/docs/ai-reviews) (no date).
- **Read:** page read (fetch tool) for all three.
- **Architecture (Bugbot):** the launch version "ran eight parallel bug-finding passes", each with "a randomized ordering of the diff"; similar findings were bucketed and majority voting kept "only bugs flagged by more than one pass"; a "validator model" screened false positives; results deduplicated against earlier runs. Later replaced by "a fully agentic design", which the post credits with the largest gains; the agent was at first "too cautious" and prompts were made more aggressive.
- **Figures and method:** resolution rate "rose from 52% to over 70%", bugs flagged per run 0.4 to 0.7, resolved bugs per PR about 0.2 to 0.5, over "40 major experiments"; "more than two million PRs per month". Resolution is decided by "an AI judge" at merge time on whether the flagged bug was fixed in the final diff, spot-checked with PR authors. No false-positive rate or cost is given.
- **Graphite status:** Cursor's post: "Graphite has entered into a definitive agreement to be acquired by Cursor" and "Graphite will continue to operate independently with the same team and product." Graphite's own docs now call its reviewer **Graphite Agent**, under the feature name "AI Reviews" (formerly Diamond; the rename is from the docs page, and the old name appears on graphite.com/diamond in search results only). The docs publish no architecture or figures. A Braintrust case study (no date; page read) says Graphite treats acceptance rate (comment led to a code change) as "their most important metric", with upvote and downvote rates; Graphite's "<3%"/"<5%" unhelpful-comment claims were seen only in snippets.
- **Connection:** Bugbot's first design is a same-model ensemble with input-order randomisation, a vote threshold and a validator: a direct industrial precedent for the pooled-versus-judged and copies-of-one-model arms. That the vendor then abandoned it for a single agentic loop is a data point against parallel ensembles (internal, unpublished evidence).
- **Novelty threat:** low-medium (ensemble plus validator in production, but unpublished method).

#### GitHub Copilot code review
- **Sources:** [60 million Copilot code reviews and counting](https://github.blog/ai-and-ml/github-copilot/60-million-copilot-code-reviews-and-counting/), Ria Gopu and David Apirian, GitHub blog, 5 Mar 2026; changelog "Copilot code review now runs on an agentic architecture" (5 Mar 2026; seen in search results only).
- **Read:** page read (fetch tool) for the blog post.
- **Architecture:** "an agentic architecture that retrieves repository context and reasons across changes"; memory across reviews; an explicit review plan for long PRs. No multi-agent or separate verification stage and no severity scheme is described.
- **Figures:** "In 71% of the reviews, Copilot code review surfaces actionable feedback" and in the other 29% it posts nothing ("Silence is better than noise"); "more than one in five code reviews on GitHub"; about 5.1 comments per review; positive feedback +8.1% from the agentic redesign; a stronger model gave +6% positive feedback for +16% latency. Positive feedback is thumbs reactions plus surveys; accuracy is checked "against known issues and production signals" including "whether flagged issues are resolved before merging", with no figure.
- **Independent evidence:** the "Go Home Copilot" study (Topic 3) measures a 72.9% thread-resolution rate for Copilot on 342 Python repositories; Amro and Alalfi (2025, below) find it misses most security flaws.
- **Novelty threat:** none.

#### OpenAI Codex code review
- **Source:** [A Practical Approach to Verifying Code at Scale](https://alignment.openai.com/scaling-code-verification/), Maja Trębacz, Sam Arnesen, Albin Cassirer, Max Johnson, Xin Lin and Thibault Sottiaux with the Codex team, OpenAI Alignment blog, 1 Dec 2025. Vendor research post, not peer-reviewed.
- **Read:** page read (fetch tool).
- **Architecture:** a dedicated, trained agentic reviewer with repository tools and code execution; repo access and execution improve "both recall and precision compared with diff-only review". Single or multi-pass and any verifier stage are not stated.
- **Figures (quoted by the fetch tool):** comments on 36% of Codex-generated PRs; "46% of those comments lead to a code change, versus 53% for comments on human-written PRs"; authors address comments with a code change "in 52.7% of cases"; "Over 80% of comment reactions are positive"; over 100k external PRs per day (Oct 2025). Precision and recall were judged by human evaluation on recent commits from open-source repositories; recall also measured on previously found issues in OpenAI's code across inference budgets. Stated stance: "Precision is more important for usability than recall"; a noisy system "will be bypassed"; over-reliance on a clean review is a named risk.
- **Connection:** supports weighting precision in the council score and reporting it alongside recall. Its finding that performance on model-generated code drops faster with smaller budgets bears on the same-family and effort arms.
- **Novelty threat:** none.

#### Qodo (Qodo 2.0 and its benchmark)
- **Sources:** [Introducing Qodo 2.0](https://www.qodo.ai/blog/introducing-qodo-2-0/), 4 Feb 2026; [We benchmarked Claude's code review tool](https://dev.to/nnennandukwe/we-benchmarked-claudes-code-review-tool-heres-what-the-data-shows-35b9), "Qodo Research | March 2026", posted by Nnenna Ndukwe, 12 Mar 2026. Both vendor marketing.
- **Read:** page read (fetch tool) for both.
- **Architecture:** "a multi-agent expert review architecture"; "Each agent is optimized for a specific type of analysis and operates with its own dedicated context"; "The judge agent resolves conflicts, removes duplicates, and filters out low-signal results"; a recommendation agent uses past PRs; findings "organized by severity". This is the project's council shape (specialists, a judge, severity) in a product.
- **Figures:** Qodo 2.0 post: "highest overall F1 score at 60.1%", "highest recall at 56.7%", "outperforming the next best solution by 9%" (the post later gives an exhaustive mode the same 60.1, an internal inconsistency). The March post, on "100 PRs with 580 injected issues across 8 production-grade open-source repositories", one LLM judge for all tools: Qodo Extended P 79% / R 71% / F1 74.7%; Qodo Default 79/60/68.2; Claude Code Review 79/52/62.7; Claude Code Review cost "$15–$25 per review". The article says "The dataset and evaluated reviews are public" (not opened). Prior coverage of the Qodo benchmark is in seed-sources-research.md.
- **Connection:** the only published number pitting Anthropic's managed multi-agent review against a specialist-plus-judge council on injected bugs, and it is vendor-run. Equal precision with lower recall for Claude's service is a hypothesis the project could test independently.
- **Novelty threat:** medium (specialist agents plus judge plus severity, scored on injected bugs, exists commercially; but no equal-cost, appeal or evolution, and the method is vendor-controlled).

#### Greptile
- **Sources:** [Greptile benchmarks](https://www.greptile.com/benchmarks) (evaluation "conducted in July 2025"); [Greptile v4 + New Pricing](https://www.greptile.com/blog/greptile-v4), Daksh Gupta, 5 Mar 2026. The v3 post (26 Nov 2025) was seen in search results only. Vendor sources.
- **Read:** page read (fetch tool) for the benchmark page and the v4 post.
- **Benchmark method:** 50 real bugs (not planted), 10 each from Sentry, Cal.com, Grafana, Keycloak and Discourse, traced from bug-fix PRs to the introducing commit and reintroduced as fresh PRs in clean forks; default hosted settings; a catch needs a line-level comment that "explicitly identified the faulty code ... and explained the impact". Catch rates: Greptile 82%, Bugbot 58%, Copilot 54%, CodeRabbit 44%, Graphite 6%. No false-positive counts. The fetch tool notes the Copilot per-repo counts sum to 26/50 (52%), not 54%. The page does not state who ran it (Greptile's own site).
- **v4 figures:** A/B test over "hundreds of thousands of PRs": addressed comments per PR 0.92 to 1.60; share of comments addressed 30% to 43%, with "addressed" "determined by an LLM". Pricing $30 per developer per month including 50 reviews, then $1 per review. No architecture described.
- **Connection:** the benchmark's reintroduced-bug method is an SZZ-style seed design, small and self-run. The earlier Greptile same-family post is already covered in prior-work-research.md.
- **Novelty threat:** none.

#### CodeRabbit
- **Sources:** [The art and science of context engineering for AI code reviews](https://www.coderabbit.ai/blog/the-art-and-science-of-context-engineering), Amitosh Swain, 16 Jul 2025; [The more AI writes the code, the more review needs independence](https://www.coderabbit.ai/blog/code-review-needs-independence), Yiwen Xu and Li Ye, 17 Jun 2026. Vendor posts.
- **Read:** page read (fetch tool) for both.
- **Architecture:** context from "dozens of sources" (PR metadata, incremental diffs, a vector store of past review learnings, linked issues, a dependency graph); "For different complexity levels, we use different prompts and models"; verification agents as "an AI-powered quality assurance layer that automatically validates and improves review comments"; "an ensemble of models instead of relying on a single model for the entire review". No severity scheme or figures for precision or acceptance. The 2026 post argues for reviewer independence from the authoring model ("Homogenization Trap"), citing Bugbot defaulting to Cursor's own Composer model; its supporting numbers are second-hand and were not checked.
- **Independent evidence:** Topic 3's CodeRabbit study (56.3% of replied-to comments rejected, 43.3% of rejections false positives) and the terminal study (1.37% of comments matched a human comment).
- **Novelty threat:** none.

#### Google Gemini Code Assist on GitHub
- **Sources:** [Review repository code](https://docs.cloud.google.com/gemini/docs/code-review/review-repo-code) and [Customize Gemini Code Assist behavior in GitHub](https://docs.cloud.google.com/gemini/docs/code-review/customize-repo-review) (official docs, no dates).
- **Read:** page read (fetch tool) for both.
- **Architecture:** PR summary plus review comments; no statement on single pass or multiple agents. Severity is an explicit filter: `comment_severity_threshold` "The minimum severity of review comments to consider. Default: MEDIUM" (values LOW, MEDIUM, HIGH, CRITICAL); `max_review_comments` default -1 (unlimited); severity is set "based on the type and significance of the issue"; optional persistent memory per repository. No accuracy figures.
- **Independent evidence:** CR-Bench-v2 reports Gemini Code Assist detects 8.1% of known defects.
- **Connection:** a severity threshold as a user-facing noise control is the product analogue of the judge's severity ruling.
- **Novelty threat:** none.

#### Security study of Copilot review: GitHub's Copilot Code Review: Can AI Spot Security Flaws Before You Commit?
- **Citation:** Amena Amro and Manar H. Alalfi; arXiv preprint; 17 Sep 2025; [arXiv 2509.13650](https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.13650).
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API).
- **Key point:** on labelled vulnerable samples, Copilot code review "frequently fails to detect critical vulnerabilities such as SQL injection, cross-site scripting (XSS), and insecure deserialization. Instead, its feedback primarily addresses low-severity issues". No figures in the abstract.
- **Novelty threat:** none.

#### Vendor benchmarks seen only as snippets
- Augment's 2026 review benchmark (said to rescore seven tools on Greptile's five repositories, with Claude Code at high recall and low precision) and Tenki's benchmark (50 PRs, 122 bugs, a three-LLM judge panel) appeared only in search summaries; not read. Factory's review benchmark (docs.factory.ai) likewise.

### 2.3 Debate against independent judging

#### Debate or Vote: Debate or Vote: Which Yields Better Decisions in Multi-Agent Large Language Models?
- **Citation:** Hyeong Kyu Choi, Xiaojin Zhu and Sharon Li; NeurIPS 2025 (Spotlight, per the arXiv comment; Semantic Scholar venue also NeurIPS); v1 24 Aug 2025, v2 23 Oct 2025; https://arxiv.org/abs/2508.17536; code https://github.com/deeplearning-wisc/debate-or-vote
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API); full text partly read (fetch tool; first 100k of 121k characters, Tables 1 and 2 and the theory section).
- **What it did:** Split multi-agent debate into majority voting and the exchange of messages, on seven NLP benchmarks with five agents (Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct, Llama3.1-8B-Instruct). Proved that debate induces a martingale over agents' beliefs, so the exchange alone does not raise expected correctness, then tested interventions that bias belief updates toward correction.
- **Key numbers:** abstract: "Majority Voting alone accounts for most of the performance gains typically attributed to MAD". Fetch-tool quotes from Table 1 (Qwen2.5-7B averages): single agent 0.7205, majority voting 0.7691, decentralised MAD (2 rounds) 0.7377, centralised MAD (2 rounds) 0.6551 (below a single agent). Llama3.1-8B: single 0.6203, voting 0.7242, decentralised MAD 0.6929. Decentralised MAD on Qwen fell from 0.7377 at 2 rounds to 0.7050 at 5. With heterogeneous personas, "Majority Voting mostly paralleled MAD variants". Interventions (Table 2, decentralised MAD, 2 rounds): vanilla 0.7332, "MAD-Conformist" (keep your answer if it matches the previous majority) 0.7625, oracle lock-in 0.8236 (needs ground truth; an upper bound).
- **Connection:** **supports** the project's choice to treat independent review plus a judge as the default and debate as a separate arm. The centralised-MAD result (a single aggregator reading everyone and doing worse than one agent) is a warning for any judge that is also asked to deliberate. Small open models only, QA tasks, no code.
- **Novelty threat:** none; it is about debate on QA, not review councils.

#### Talk Isn't Always Cheap: Understanding Failure Modes in Multi-Agent Debate
- **Citation:** Andrea Wynn, Harsh Satija and Gillian Hadfield; ICML 2025 Workshop on Multi-Agent Systems (per arXiv comment); v1 5 Sep 2025, v2 13 Oct 2025; https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.05396
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API); full text partly read (fetch tool; results tables and discussion).
- **What it did:** Debate among three agents mixing GPT-4o-mini, Llama-3.1-8B and Mistral-7B, in homogeneous, majority-minority and fully mixed teams, on CommonSenseQA, MMLU and GSM8K. Counted answer flips and tested whether a correctness-payoff prompt reduces sycophantic flips.
- **Key numbers:** abstract: "debate can lead to a decrease in accuracy over time - even in settings where stronger (i.e., more capable) models outnumber their weaker counterparts". Fetch-tool quotes from Table 1 (before → after debate): CommonSenseQA declined in every configuration (e.g. 2× Llama + 1× Mistral 58.2 → 50.2); MMLU 3× Mistral 33.6 → 24.4, but the fully mixed team rose 57.8 → 63.4; GSM8K mixed (1× GPT + 2× Llama 88.4 → 92.8). The paper says correct → incorrect shifts outnumber the reverse, with no exact rate in the text; flips are most likely when an agent is the isolated minority; the payoff prompt "does not significantly reduce the likelihood that LLM agents flip their answers".
- **Connection:** **supports** keeping reviewers independent until the judge, and **challenges** a mixed-strength council that debates: "the presence of a weaker agent disrupts the performance of the stronger agent" (fetch-tool quote). Relevant to the mixed-family arm if families differ in strength.
- **Novelty threat:** none.

#### Stop Overvaluing MAD: Stop Overvaluing Multi-Agent Debate -- We Must Rethink Evaluation and Embrace Model Heterogeneity
- **Citation:** Hangfan Zhang, Zhiyao Cui, Jianhao Chen et al. (8 authors); arXiv preprint (position paper; no venue on the arXiv page; Semantic Scholar lookup failed); v1 12 Feb 2025, v3 21 Jun 2025; https://arxiv.org/abs/2502.08788
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API).
- **What it did:** Evaluated 5 MAD methods on 9 benchmarks with 4 base models against Chain-of-Thought and self-consistency; then tested mixing model families inside debate.
- **Key numbers:** abstract: "MAD often fail to outperform simple single-agent baselines such as Chain-of-Thought and Self-Consistency, even when consuming significantly more inference-time computation"; heterogeneity is "a universal antidote to consistently improve current MAD frameworks". No figures in the abstract.
- **Connection:** **supports** both the equal-cost rule and the mixed-family arm. It is the "Zhang et al. 2025" that Ferreira cites for heterogeneity being the most consistently positive factor.
- **Novelty threat:** none.

#### Kenton et al.: On scalable oversight with weak LLMs judging strong LLMs
- **Citation:** Zachary Kenton, Noah Y. Siegel, János Kramár et al. (11 authors, Google DeepMind); arXiv preprint as checked (no venue on the arXiv page; NeurIPS 2024 is commonly given but **not confirmed** here); v1 5 Jul 2024, v2 12 Jul 2024; https://arxiv.org/abs/2407.04622
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API); full text partly read (fetch tool; results and summary sections, first 100k of 155k characters).
- **What it did:** Compared debate, consultancy and direct question answering with weaker LLM judges (Gemma 7B, GPT-3.5, Gemini Pro 1.0 and 1.5) and Gemini Pro 1.5 debaters, on extractive QA with information asymmetry and on closed maths, logic, coding-labelled and multimodal tasks.
- **Key numbers:** abstract: "in extractive QA tasks with information asymmetry debate outperforms direct question answering, but in other tasks without information asymmetry the results are mixed"; "stronger debater models increase judge accuracy, though more modestly than in previous studies". Fetch-tool quote of the summary: "weak judges achieve higher accuracy in the debate protocol, than consultancy, and around the same as direct question answering". For the strongest judge (Pro 1.5) on closed tasks, "debate actually leads to worse judge accuracy than QA without article". The fetch tool could not find a separate coding dataset in the part it read (GSM8K answers were labelled by Python execution), so "coding" in the abstract is not confirmed as a separate task.
- **Connection:** **overlaps / qualifies** the project's debate arm. Debate's clearest support is for a weak judge who cannot see the evidence. In the council, the judge can read the code, so this is the "no information asymmetry" case where results were mixed. Supports framing debate as an aid to the judge, not as a replacement for independent findings.
- **Novelty threat:** none.

#### Khan et al.: Debating with More Persuasive LLMs Leads to More Truthful Answers
- **Citation:** Akbir Khan, John Hughes, Dan Valentine et al. (10 authors); arXiv v1 9 Feb 2024, v4 25 Jul 2024; https://arxiv.org/abs/2402.06782; code https://github.com/ucl-dark/llm_debate. ICML 2024 is widely reported but the arXiv page states no venue; **venue not confirmed** here.
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API).
- **What it did:** Two expert LLMs with access to a story argue for different answers; a non-expert model or human without access picks one (QuALITY reading comprehension).
- **Key numbers:** "debate consistently helps both non-expert models and humans answer questions, achieving 76% and 88% accuracy respectively (naive baselines obtain 48% and 60%)"; optimising debaters for persuasiveness "improves non-expert ability to identify the truth".
- **Connection:** **supports debate only under information asymmetry** (the judge can't read the source). Loosely related to the council: the project's judge sees the code. It could justify a narrower debate design in which a finder and a refuter argue each disputed finding to a judge with limited context (cheaper judge), but this is untested on code.
- **Novelty threat:** none.

#### Chun et al.: Enhancing LLM Performance Through Debate: An Empirical Study on Multi-Agent Debate for Coding Tasks
- **Citation:** Yong Jin Chun, Qihong Chen, Jiawei Li and Iftekhar Ahmed; accepted to ACM TOSEM (per the arXiv comment); v1 15 Mar 2025, v2 3 Aug 2026; https://arxiv.org/abs/2503.12029
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API); full text partly read (fetch tool; first 100k of 207k characters: set-up, results and failure analysis).
- **What it did:** Applied an NLP debate framework (debaters plus a judge) to four software-engineering tasks: code input prediction, output prediction, translation and summarisation (CRUXEval, LiveCodeBench, AVATAR, CodeNet, EvalPlus, CodeSearchNet), with open and GPT-4o models, against Chain-of-Thought, self-consistency and task-specific methods. Proposed Early Termination and Extended Reflection variants.
- **Key numbers:** fetch-tool quotes: default MAD underperforms on CRUXEval input prediction (DeepSeek 31.9 vs SemCoder 62.5) and output prediction (GPT-4o 27.8 vs 73.6) but outperforms on EvalPlus Java translation (50.61 vs 23.41). Failure analysis: "Shared Hallucination" is the dominant root cause (49.0%) of "Forceful Agreement" failures; "Peer Pressure" where agents "change their correct answers upon observing the majority opinion"; "Judge Anchoring" where earlier judge choices steer later rounds; "longer debates do not necessarily yield better results". The fetch tool did not find a cost-matched comparison in the part read.
