- **Connection:** **closest debate-on-code study found, and it challenges debate**: it names peer pressure, shared hallucination and judge anchoring as failure modes in code tasks. None of its tasks is bug finding or review, so it does not answer the project's debate-vs-independent question.
- **Novelty threat:** low; code tasks, but not review, no planted bugs and no independent-review arm.

#### Wisdom of Artificial Deliberative Crowds: The Wisdom of Artificial Deliberative Crowds
- **Citation:** Federico Barrera-Lemarchand, Mariano Sigman and Joaquin Navajas; arXiv preprint (no venue stated); v1 18 Sep 2026; https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.22497
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API); full text partly read (PDF downloaded and converted with pdftotext; introduction, procedure, Studies 2–3, "Model Diversity and Expert Dominance" and discussion read directly, so quotes below are verbatim from the PDF text).
- **What it did:** Groups of three small models from three families (Claude Haiku 4.5, GPT-5-nano, Gemini 3.1 Flash Lite) each answer independently (stage i1), deliberate in a short-message chat room until consensus (stage c) and then re-answer privately (stage i2). Four domains: jar-of-disks estimation, accept/reject of 100 NeurIPS 2024 submissions against the human verdict, suspicion ratings of 50 agent trajectories (25 with a hidden malicious goal, Monitor Red Teaming data) and World Cup forecasting against Polymarket. Same-model "clone" groups were run in Studies 1 and 2.
- **Key numbers:** "In every study, the aggregation of consensus estimates produced by small deliberating groups was more accurate than the classical wisdom of crowds obtained by pooling a much larger number of independent judgments from the same models." Study 3 (sabotage trajectories): each group's consensus beat the mean of its own three opening ratings (Wilcoxon z = 3.64, p = 2.8 × 10^-4). Clones: "when groups were composed of three copies of the same model, deliberation produces consensus values that fail to improve accuracy over pooling that model's independent estimates", with Bayes factors of 3.99–6.19 for the null in Study 1; Study 2 clones also showed no benefit (p = 0.098, 0.87 and 0.64) against heterogeneous groups at p = 0.023. "Expert dominance": the best single family alone beat the mixed crowd in three of four domains, and which family led changed by domain (Gemini in Studies 1 and 4, Claude in Study 2). Not cost-matched: deliberation adds up to ten short rounds, and no token comparison appears in the sections read.
- **Connection:** **supports** the mixed-family arm and **qualifies** the debate arm. Deliberation helped only across families, never among copies, which is the strongest direct evidence found tonight that debate among Claude copies should not be expected to beat independent review plus aggregation. The peer-review and sabotage-monitoring studies are the nearest non-code analogues to judging findings. "Expert dominance" warns that a mixed council can lose to its best member, and the best member is not knowable in advance; per-seat ablations in the project could test this.
- **Novelty threat:** low; judgement tasks, not code review and no judge or appeal.

#### Not All Flips Are Conformity: Decomposing Stance Convergence in Multi-Agent LLM Debate
- **Citation:** Xiqi Hao, Zengqing Wu, Yu-Xuan Qiu et al. (7 authors); arXiv preprint; v1 30 May 2026, v2 18 Oct 2026; https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.00820
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API).
- **What it did:** Five GPT-4o agents on MMLU-Pro questions where agents disagree, with matched self-reflection (no peer information), stance-only and full-reasoning conditions, to separate genuine peer influence from answer changes that happen anyway.
- **Key numbers:** "37% of agent-question pairs change even without peer information"; among stable pairs "29.3% adopt a peer answer after seeing peer stances"; "Full reasoning improves individual accuracy but provides no additional majority-vote gain over self-reflection while using 57% more tokens"; an answer-agnostic intervention "suppresses both harmful and beneficial peer adoption without a detectable accuracy gain".
- **Connection:** **supports** the debate-vs-independent arm and gives a control worth copying: a matched "re-think alone" condition, so that changes after debate are not all credited to debate. Convergence is "an ambiguous signal of value", which matters if the council uses agreement between seats as a confidence signal.
- **Novelty threat:** none.

#### When Debate Helps: Proposal Supply and Verification-Aware Readout in Multi-Agent Reasoning
- **Citation:** Zihao Zhao, Tunyu Zhang, Haizhou Shi et al. (6 authors); arXiv preprint; v1 3 Oct 2026; https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.04686; code https://github.com/Wang-ML-Lab/when-debate-helps
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API).
- **What it did:** Argues debate needs two things to beat voting: some agent must propose the right answer ("proposal supply", measured as "recoverable headroom", where a correct proposal exists but the majority is wrong) and the read-out must pick it when voting misses it. Builds agent societies chosen for coverage and tests them at matched budgets.
- **Key numbers:** qualitative only in the abstract: coverage-selected societies "increase complementary proposal supply and improve aggregate accuracy"; interaction "provides gains beyond applying the same finalizer directly to the initial proposals".
- **Connection:** **overlaps** with the council's logic. In a review council, "proposal supply" is the union of findings across seats and "readout" is the judge plus appeal; a finding raised by only one seat is the "correct minority proposal" case. It supports reporting recoverable headroom directly: seeds found by at least one seat but lost at the judge, and how many the appeal wins back.
- **Novelty threat:** low.

#### The Cost of Consensus: Isolated Self-Correction Prevails Over Unguided Homogeneous Multi-Agent Debate
- **Citation:** Blaž Bertalanič and Carolina Fortuna; ACM Conference on AI and Agentic Systems (per the arXiv comment; not otherwise confirmed); v1 29 Apr 2026; https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.00914
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API).
- **What it did:** Teams of 10 homogeneous 7–8B agents (Qwen2.5-7B, Llama-3.1-8B, Ministral-3-8B), 3 rounds, on GSM-Hard and MMLU-Hard, against isolated self-correction and a noise control that injects rationales from unrelated problems.
- **Key numbers:** "sycophantic conformity ... (modal adoption up to 85.5%)"; "contextual fragility ... (vulnerability rate up to 70.0%)"; "consensus collapse, where plurality voting discards correct answers already present in the generation pool (oracle gap up to 32.3 percentage points)"; conformity is high at minimal peer exposure (K = 2); "debate consumes 2.1-3.4× more tokens (up to 28,631 tokens per problem) than self-correction for equal or lower accuracy".
- **Connection:** **supports** independent review and is direct motivation for an appeal: plurality voting discarding correct answers already in the pool is the loss the appeal is meant to recover at the judge. Small homogeneous models only.
- **Novelty threat:** none.

#### Statistical Scouting: Statistical Scouting Finds Debate-Safe but Not Debate-Useful Cases: A Matched-Ceiling Study of Open-Weight LLM Reasoning Protocols
- **Citation:** Julia Hu, Alfred Shen and Kumar Lakshmipathi; arXiv preprint (technical report); v1 10 May 2026; https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.09618
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API).
- **What it did:** Greedy decoding, three-sample voting and a two-agent critique-revise debate under a matched cap of 960 generated tokens per example, on MuSiQue and GSM8K with Llama 3.1 8B and Ministral 3 8B; asks whether cheap signals can route each example to the right protocol.
- **Key numbers:** oracle routing gains "+14.0 and +13.7 pp over the best fixed one" on MuSiQue; "each (model, dataset) cell has a different winner"; vote-entropy routing +1.3 and +1.7 pp with CIs including zero; "66% of debate-helpful examples (31/47) occur when voting is unanimous but wrong".
- **Connection:** **loosely supports** caution about debate: under matched budgets no fixed protocol wins everywhere. "Unanimous but wrong" matters for councils: agreement among seats is not evidence that a finding (or a miss) is right, which argues for an appeal that is not triggered only by disagreement.
- **Novelty threat:** none.

#### Peacemaker or Troublemaker: How Sycophancy Shapes Multi-Agent Debate
- **Citation:** Binwei Yao, Chao Shang, Wanyu Du et al. (9 authors); arXiv preprint (Semantic Scholar venue: arXiv); v1 27 Sep 2025; https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.23055
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API).
- **What it did:** Defines and measures inter-agent sycophancy in debate, varying it separately for debaters and judges, in decentralised and centralised (judge-led) debate.
- **Key numbers:** none in the abstract; sycophancy "amplifies disagreement collapse before reaching a correct conclusion", "yields lower accuracy than single-agent baselines" and "arises from distinct debater-driven and judge-driven failure modes".
- **Connection:** **supports** treating a sycophantic judge as a separate risk. The council's judge reads all seats' findings; a judge that defers to the most confident or most numerous seats is the "judge-driven" mode. The project could log how often the judge sides with a majority of seats against the key.
- **Novelty threat:** none.

#### When Identity Skews Debate: Anonymization for Bias-Reduced Multi-Agent Reasoning
- **Citation:** Hyeong Kyu Choi, Xiaojin Zhu and Sharon Li; ACL 2026 Main (per the arXiv comment); v1 8 Oct 2025, v5 9 Apr 2026; https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.07517; code https://github.com/deeplearning-wisc/MAD-identity-bias
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API).
- **What it did:** Models debate as an identity-weighted Bayesian update, proposes removing identity markers so agents cannot tell their own past answer from a peer's and defines an Identity Bias Coefficient.
- **Key numbers:** none in the abstract; "identity bias is widespread, with sycophancy far more common than self-bias".
- **Connection:** **supports** a design detail: when the judge or appeal agent reads seat findings, strip seat names, roles and model family so it rules on content rather than on who raised it. Relevant to the cross-family judge arm and to self-preference.
- **Novelty threat:** none.

#### Silent Dissent: LLM Agents That Yield to the Majority Still Represent Their Original Premise
- **Citation:** Ziang Ni and Peng Zou; arXiv preprint; v1 2 Oct 2026, v2 5 Oct 2026; https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02702
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API).
- **What it did:** Asch-style scripted peers unanimously assert a wrong answer to two-hop factual questions; the authors read the agent's internal "bridge" entity from the residual stream with pre-registered probes.
- **Key numbers:** agents that gave in still represented their original premise (hit@100 0.85, 0.22 and 0.24 for three models); "Hiding the earlier answer also changed conformity: Qwen3.5-4B gave in on 89% of questions instead of 8%".
- **Connection:** **loosely related.** Stated consensus can overstate agreement, and the swing from 8% to 89% conformity when the agent's own earlier answer is hidden shows how sensitive conformity is to what an agent is shown. That bears on how the appeal agent is briefed (whether it sees the judge's reasons).
- **Novelty threat:** none.

#### ReasonVul: Three Heads Are Better Than One: A Multi-perspective Reasoning Framework for Enhanced Vulnerability Detection
- **Citation:** Xin Peng, Bo Lin, Jing Wang et al. (8 authors); arXiv preprint; v1 18 May 2026; https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.18153
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API).
- **What it did:** Three agents with different reasoning modes analyse code independently, then debate conflicts "through iterative rebuttal and revision" to a joint verdict, on PrimeVul and JITVUL function-level vulnerability detection.
- **Key numbers:** "PairAcc of 40.00% and an F1-score of 72.52%, surpassing the best baseline by 81.24% in PairAcc"; JITVUL PairAcc 28.67%; "we analyzed 542 conflict cases and found that 389 were correctly resolved".
- **Connection:** **overlaps** with an "independent first, then debate only the disagreements" design, on code. But it classifies functions (vulnerable or not) rather than finding bugs in a diff, and the abstract gives no budget-matched voting baseline, so it does not settle debate against independent voting.
- **Novelty threat:** low.

#### Deshapriya et al.: Beyond Benchmarks: Evaluating Large Language Models and Agentic Patterns for Static Code Analysis Warning Classification
- **Citation:** Lakmal Deshapriya, Sherlock A. Licorish, Brendon J. Woodford and Grant Dick; arXiv preprint; v1 14 Nov 2026; https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19086
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API); full text partly read (PDF via pdftotext; method section 5.3, RQ2 results, cost paragraph and discussion section 7.2).
- **What it did:** o4-mini, Gemini 2.5 Pro and OpenReasoning-Nemotron-32B classify PMD and SonarQube warnings as true or false positives on four manually annotated datasets (531–646 sampled warnings each). For the two hardest rules it compared one Structured-CoT prompt with a debate pattern (Prosecutor, Defender, Critic and Arbiter agents) and five-sample self-consistency.
- **Key numbers:** abstract: "the use of Agentic patterns does not improve LLMs' performance in most scenarios". PDF text: for Gemini (both rules) "both SCoT and Self-Consistency perform significantly better than Debating" (pairwise McNemar with Holm-Bonferroni); o4-mini cost per warning 0.009134 USD for one prompt, 0.045668 USD for self-consistency (5 calls) and 0.036535 USD for debate (4 calls); "investing more in Agentic patterns is not cost effective".
- **Connection:** **challenges debate on a code-adjacent judging task.** An adversarial prosecutor/defender/arbiter debate over whether a warning is real did no better, and sometimes worse, than one prompt. That is close to what the council's judge does when it rules a finding real or not. Small samples in RQ2 (two rules only).
- **Novelty threat:** none.

#### Unanimity Without Persuasion: A Single Round of Debate Erases the Disagreement That Verification Needs
- **Citation:** Yang Shu; arXiv preprint; v1 10 Aug 2026 (arXiv number 2609.26145); https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.26145
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API); full text partly read (fetch tool; set-up, results and recommendations).
- **What it did:** A panel of seven LLM judges from seven providers judged 600 code-correctness candidates (solutions to HumanEval+ and MBPP+ problems from seven solver models, labelled by running the full extended tests) in a blind round and then three debate rounds. Identical-cohort controls separated peer reasoning from peer labels and from reconsideration without peers; an execution-based "verification ballot" (pass/fail on a fixed 20% of tests) replaced one judge's vote.
- **Key numbers:** abstract: "unanimity on a fixed cohort jumps from 39.5% to 95.2% in round 1 (93.1% of the total collapse), while accuracy moves by less than one point and 96.3% of verdict flips follow the displayed peer majority"; the verification ballot "corrects 8 of 2,037 pre-debate candidate-substitution instances but changes zero in every later round; by round 3 every wrong decision is unanimous, erasing dissent that had flagged two-thirds of the panel's errors"; "no-peer reconsideration reproduces 79.6% of the collapse, real labels without reasoning reproduce 91.5%". Fetch-tool quotes: judges were GPT-4o-mini, Claude Haiku, DeepSeek-V4-Flash, Qwen3-max, GLM-5-Turbo, Kimi-K2.6 and Doubao-Seed-1.6; blind-round majority accuracy 82.2% on all 600; on the fixed 291-candidate cohort 91.8% at round 0 against 92.4% after debate (McNemar p = 0.50); 16 of 24 round-0 majority errors were on split votes.
- **Connection:** **the closest debate-on-code result found, and it challenges a debate arm placed before the judge or appeal.** On code-correctness judging, one round of peer exposure turned informative disagreement into unanimous agreement without improving accuracy, and destroyed the signal a later checker could use. Direct design advice for the project: "verify before any second-pass evaluation or peer exposure, and never treat post-debate unanimity as independent evidence of reliability". If the council has a debate round, the appeal (and any disagreement-based routing) should see the pre-debate findings, not the post-debate consensus. Single author, unrefereed.
- **Novelty threat:** low; judging given solutions, not finding bugs and no appeal.

---

### 2.4 Diversity of LLM judges, and correlated errors

Already covered and not repeated: Goel et al. (CAPA, 2502.04313), Kim et al. (Correlated Errors, 2506.07962), PoLL (2404.18796), Who Judges Matters (2609.17857), Song 2026 (2610.01471), Self-MoA (2502.00674). Many items below were found through Semantic Scholar's list of papers citing Goel et al. and Kim et al.

#### Agreement Overstates Evidence: Error Dependence in LLM Judge Consensus
- **Citation:** Elias Hossain, Niloofar Yousefi and Ser-Nam Lim; arXiv preprint; v1 18 Sep 2026; https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.22512
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API); full text partly read (fetch tool; first 100k of 222k characters).
- **What it did:** Measured pairwise correlation of judge errors (against trusted labels) in a bank of ten open-weight judges (five models × two prompt styles) and in frontier judges from different providers, mostly on RewardBench v2, UltraFeedback, PKU-SafeRLHF and LLM-AggreFact; converted correlation to an effective number of independent judges; tested voting methods that account for dependence.
- **Key numbers:** abstract: "the average pairwise correlation between judge errors is 0.21. As a result, the ten judges only provide roughly as much statistical information as 3.5 independent judges"; "The dependency is even stronger among the high-accuracy frontier judges we evaluate, including judges from different providers"; "In up to 28% of our comparisons, ignoring shared errors leads to the conclusion that one system is significantly better, while accounting for them does not." Fetch-tool quotes: n_eff = n / (1 + (n−1)ρ̄); three frontier judges from three providers on RewardBench had ρ̄ = 0.56, n_eff = 1.41 at mean accuracy 0.92 (PKU-SafeRLHF ρ̄ = 0.60, n_eff = 1.37); "Higher accuracy comes with stronger error correlation"; on code, "None of the 11 settings met all three criteria" for a filter that beats plain majority. Model names reported by the fetch tool (e.g. "Claude Opus 5") are not confirmed.
- **Connection:** **supports and sharpens** the project's same-family worry and its statistics. Three strong cross-provider judges were worth about 1.4 independent judges, so a cross-family judge or panel buys less independence than its count suggests, and less as models get stronger. The n_eff formula and a small trusted set (they suggest about 100 labels) are a cheap way for the project to report how independent its seats and judges really are. Not code review.
- **Novelty threat:** none.

#### Are Diversity Metrics Measuring Diversity? A Capability-Controlled Audit of Majority-Vote Gain in LLM Ensembles
- **Citation:** Donghwan Kim; arXiv preprint; v1 22 Jul 2026; https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20768
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API).
- **What it did:** Tested five diversity measures (disagreement, double-fault and others) as predictors of majority-vote gain over the best member across 31,900 subsets of 30 LLMs on MMLU-Pro and 29 on TruthfulQA, with capability controlled.
- **Key numbers:** "oracle gain is positive in 100% of subsets, yet simple voting beats the strongest member in only 9.98% of all canonical size-3 subsets (18.71% with held-out best selection)"; a joint-correctness diversity proxy "is nearly collinear with one minus mean accuracy (size-3 Spearman rho = +0.991 / +0.988)"; after control, only "a modest residual pairwise co-failure association in which more shared error corresponds to lower gain" survives.
- **Connection:** **challenges** an easy reading of the mixed-family arm. Complementarity always exists in principle (the union catches more) but simple voting rarely beats the best member, and most "diversity" scores mostly measure weakness. For a council, this argues for union-plus-judge rather than voting, for comparing a mixed council against its best single seat, and for using pairwise co-failure (double-fault on seeds) rather than generic diversity scores.
- **Novelty threat:** none.

#### Does Reasoning Make Language Models Agree?
- **Citation:** Mila Gorecki and Moritz Hardt; arXiv preprint; v1 30 Oct 2026; https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41691
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API).
- **What it did:** Compared between-model agreement under controlled changes in reasoning effort, and across observed reasoning lengths, for nine models from two developers on three classification tasks, against the agreement expected from accuracy alone.
- **Key numbers:** "enabling reasoning increases agreement between models evaluated on the same individuals. Yet the individuals who elicit longer reasoning traces receive less consistent predictions across models. Both patterns persist after accounting for differences in accuracy."
- **Connection:** **bears on the effort axis of the evolution.** Raising reviewer effort may make seats agree more (less diversity) beyond what accuracy explains, so higher effort and a mixed council may partly cancel. Items that trigger long reasoning are where seats disagree, which could guide where an appeal looks. Classification tasks about people, not code.
- **Novelty threat:** none.

#### Homogenization in Multi-Agent Systems
- **Citation:** Prakhar Ganesh, Kyra Wilson, Luca Zappella et al. (7 authors); arXiv preprint; v1 7 Oct 2026; https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.09824
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API); full text partly read (fetch tool; first 100k of 206k characters, code-generation section).
- **What it did:** Defined homogenisation in multi-agent systems by conformity to the majority, polarisation and growing inertia, and measured it in code generation, hiring and scientific peer review. The code task (fetch-tool quote) uses "the CodeLMSec dataset ... 280 Python and C++ prompts for security-relevant coding tasks", reviewer roles "Security, Optimality, Correctness, Readability", "T=5 rounds across M=10 independent trials", and every agent "an independent instance of the same underlying LLM"; a mixed-model test used "all six permutations of generator–reviewer pairings with three different LLMs".
- **Key numbers:** abstract: "in code generation, it hides and amplifies correlated errors which can create systemic vulnerabilities"; "simple approaches to increase diversity---leveraging sampling stochasticity and mixed-models MAS---fail to reduce homogenization risks". Fetch-tool quotes: overall vulnerability rates fall "by up to 50%" yet "Certain vulnerabilities grow over rounds" and "Many blind spots are shared across models (e.g., py/url-redirection)"; "Even in mixed setups, the failures of homogenization persist". Per-type numbers are only in figures, not read.
- **Connection:** **close in set-up** (role-briefed reviewer agents on security-relevant code, with mixed-model variants) and **challenges** the hope that mixing models alone removes shared blind spots when reviewers interact over rounds. It supports keeping seats independent and scoring by seed type, so that shared blind spots show up instead of hiding in the average.
- **Novelty threat:** low-medium; role-based reviewer agents on code with a mixed-model arm, but the reviewers steer generation over rounds, there is no planted key, judge or appeal.

#### LLMs as a Jury: Cross-Model Consensus Can Outperform Process Reward Models for LLM Reasoning
- **Citation:** Ning Liu; arXiv preprint; v1 11 Jul 2026, v3 17 Aug 2026; https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.10139
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API).
- **What it did:** Used agreement among independently trained models, each answering once, as a free selector of the correct answer across seven benchmarks, against self-consistency, self-scoring and four trained verifiers; derived a closed-form law predicting consensus accuracy from panel statistics.
- **Key numbers:** "on competition math it closes the entire gap to an oracle selector, while self-scoring closes almost none"; the law predicts consensus accuracy "to a mean absolute error of 0.03"; ceiling is "a shared-error floor where models share a misconception, near zero on math but non-trivial on science".
- **Connection:** **supports** the mixed-family arm on tasks with a single answer: "independently trained models err differently". The shared-error floor is the quantity the project should estimate for its seats on seeds. Bug finding is open-ended (many possible findings), so consensus is a weaker signal there.
- **Novelty threat:** none.

#### State-dependent error correlations shape voting thresholds in committees of AI agents
- **Citation:** Haifeng Li and Mo Hai; arXiv preprint; v1 27 Jul 2026; https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.23931
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API).
- **What it did:** Combined Sah-Stiglitz screening (how many approvals a committee needs) with error dependence that differs between good and bad cases; estimated it from 174,384 votes by 28 LLMs on four binary-screening benchmarks.
- **Key numbers:** "shared errors create a positive asymptotic error floor for majority voting and can change the approval threshold that minimizes expected loss"; dependence model raised R² "from 0.840 under independence to 0.967"; "The overall reduction from majority was 15.73% (95% bootstrap CI, 13.41-16.75%)".
- **Connection:** **supports** treating the council's aggregation rule (how many seats must agree, or what the judge needs) as something to tune against the cost of misses versus false alarms, not as a fixed majority. Relevant to the pooled and consensus combining modes.
- **Novelty threat:** none.

#### Inferred Generative-Process Diversity Predicts Correlated Failure Across Language Models
- **Citation:** Ross Tieman and Evan Markou; arXiv preprint; v1 3 Sep 2026; https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03422
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API).
- **What it did:** Measured diversity between 38 models by normalised compression distance between raw outputs (corrected against a permutation control) and tested whether it predicts chance-corrected correlated failure across ten benchmark families, beyond semantic similarity and capability.
- **Key numbers:** "The cross-benchmark partial rank association is −0.216 with a 95% interval of [−0.309,−0.122], and the estimate is negative on all ten benchmarks."
- **Connection:** **loosely related.** A cheap, label-free way to choose seats likely to fail differently, which the evolution could use as a prior on model mixes. Effect is modest.
- **Novelty threat:** none.

#### Shared Item-Level Error Limits the Aggregation of Small Language Models in Fermi Estimation
- **Citation:** Carolina Fortuna and Blaž Bertalanič; arXiv preprint; v1 12 Nov 2026; https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16315
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API).
- **What it did:** Split each answer's error into a model-on-item component shared across its samples and a residual, from 2.51M answers by 15 open models (3B–72B); tested teams, debate, mixing and temperature.
- **Key numbers:** "the item-level component accounts for a median 0.906 of the error variance"; "Teams of thirty therefore reduce a single agent's error by a median 1.7% (at most 5.4%)"; "Three rounds of debate reduce within-team dispersion by 93% and leave the item-level component unchanged; revision without peers attains the same small gain"; "Mixing five models of similar accuracy reduces error by 6.6% relative to their own homogeneous teams, but no mixture improves on the most accurate model".
- **Connection:** **supports** the expectation that copies of one model add little (shared item-level error dominates) and that debate removes disagreement but not shared error. It also **challenges** large gains from mixing: a mix helped against homogeneous teams but never beat the best model. Same authors as The Cost of Consensus.
- **Novelty threat:** none.

#### Correlated Failures of AI Approval Monitors
- **Citation:** Luca Zanolini; arXiv preprint; v1 21 Oct 2026; https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29178
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API).
- **What it did:** Borrowed "fail-prone systems" from distributed computing to model monitors that fail together because they share a base model or an input; measured twelve small open monitors (pre-registered) and several closed frontier models on payment approval under prompt-injection and persuasion attacks.
- **Key numbers:** "Every reliable fooling of a monitor into approving a fraud came through an evidence source and reached only that source's readers"; "a rule that counts sources instead of votes blocked 0.9% of correct payments, against 7.4% for the best counting rule, at equal safety"; "The pre-registered worst-case test failed"; "Adding a rule engine, which cannot be persuaded, restored the condition for a safe and live rule". Closed models are named in the abstract as Claude Opus 5.5, GPT-6 Astra and Gemini 4 Argon.
- **Connection:** **loosely supports** diversifying what seats read (context, tools, files) and not only which model they are. Directly relevant to the `CLAUDE.md` arm and to "When AI Agrees Too Much": if every seat reads the same biased PR description or `CLAUDE.md`, they fail together regardless of family.
- **Novelty threat:** none.

#### Vibe Coding on Trial: Operating Characteristics of Unanimous LLM Juries
- **Citation:** Muhammad Aziz Ullah and Abdul Serwadda; submitted to IEEE ICSC 2026 (per the arXiv comment; acceptance not confirmed); v1 12 Feb 2026, v2 22 Aug 2026; https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.18492
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API).
- **What it did:** Benchmarked 15 open models on 82 MySQL text-to-SQL tasks with execution-based ground truth, then built unanimous committees of 1–6 of the six best models that accept a query only if every member judges it correct.
- **Key numbers:** none in the abstract; "single model judges are uneven, that small unanimous committees of strong models can cut false accepts while still passing many good queries, and that the exact committee composition matters significantly".
- **Connection:** **overlaps** with the consensus combining mode, on code. A unanimous rule trades misses for fewer false accepts; for bug finding the direction flips (a unanimity rule on findings would drop real bugs), which is the case for an appeal. Small task set.
- **Novelty threat:** none.

#### DVDR-LLM: Diverse LLMs vs. Vulnerabilities: Who Detects and Fixes Them Better?
- **Citation:** Arastoo Zibaeirad and Marco Vieira; arXiv preprint; v1 14 Dec 2025; https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.12536; artefact https://github.com/Erroristotle/DVDR_LLM
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API).
- **What it did:** An ensemble that combines outputs of diverse LLMs for vulnerability detection and repair, with an agreement threshold.
- **Key numbers:** "10-12% higher detection accuracy compared to the average performance of individual models, with benefits increasing as code complexity grows"; for multi-file vulnerabilities "recall (+18%) and F1 score (+11.8%) over individual models"; trade-off: "reducing false positives in verification tasks while simultaneously increasing false negatives in detection tasks".
- **Connection:** **supports** mixed-model ensembles for bug finding, with the caveat that the comparison is against the average member, not the best one (compare Kim's audit above). The threshold trade-off between misses and false alarms is the same one the judge and appeal manage.
- **Novelty threat:** low.

#### Sun et al.: Ensembling Large Language Models for Code Vulnerability Detection: An Empirical Evaluation
- **Citation:** Zhihong Sun, Jia Li, Yao Wan et al. (10 authors); arXiv preprint; v1 16 Sep 2025, v2 18 Sep 2025; https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.12629
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API).
- **What it did:** Bagging, boosting and stacking over five small code LLMs (DeepSeek-Coder-6.7B, CodeLlama-7B/13B, CodeQwen1.5-7B, StarCoder2-15B) on Devign, ReVeal and BigVul; proposed Dynamic Gated Stacking.
- **Key numbers:** none in the abstract beyond "ensemble approaches can significantly improve detection performance"; it notes "notable discrepancies in detection results ... among architecturally distinct LLMs".
- **Connection:** **loosely supports** complementarity between code models; fine-tuned classifiers on function-level datasets, far from agentic review.
- **Novelty threat:** none.

#### Don't Always Pick the Highest-Performing Model: An Information Theoretic View of LLM Ensemble Selection
- **Citation:** Yigit Turkmen, Baturalp Buyukates and Melih Bastopcu; arXiv preprint; v1 8 Feb 2026; https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.08003
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API).
- **What it did:** Framed choosing ensemble members under a query budget as maximising mutual information with the true label; modelled correlated errors with a Gaussian copula and derived an error floor.
- **Key numbers:** none in the abstract; greedy mutual-information selection "consistently outperforms strong baselines under the same query budget" on MedMCQA, MMLU and IMDB.
- **Connection:** **loosely supports** choosing seats for what they add given the others, not for solo score, which the evolution's mutation step could use.
- **Novelty threat:** none.

#### Exposing Vulnerability Monoculture Through Systemic Adversarial Robustness
- **Citation:** Leo Elmecker-Plakolm, Lukasz Szpruch, Avishek Joey Bose and Matthew Wicker; arXiv preprint; v1 19 Oct 2026; https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27353
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API).
- **What it did:** Defined Systemic Adversarial Robustness (how many distinct attacks are needed to compromise a pool of models) as a partial set-cover problem and measured it for vision and language model pools.
- **Key numbers:** none in the abstract; "model pools distilled from DeepSeek-R1 and Claude Opus 4.6 exhibit lower estimated systemic robustness than their corresponding parent pools".
- **Connection:** **loosely related.** Distillation from shared parents creates monoculture even across nominally different models; a "mixed-family" council of models distilled from Claude may not be mixed.
- **Novelty threat:** none.

---

### 2.5 Planted-bug and mutation benchmarks, and their contamination

#### 1a. How planted bugs are made, and how realistic they are

#### BugPilot: Complex Bug Generation for Efficient Learning of SWE Skills
- **Citation:** Atharv Sonwane, Isadora White, Hyunji Lee et al. (11 authors, Microsoft Research per the project link); arXiv preprint; v1 22 Oct 2025, v2 28 Oct 2025; [arXiv 2510.19898](https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.19898). Venue not confirmed.
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API); full text partly read (fetch tool: method, Tables 1, 2 and 7, Figure 4 and Appendix B).
- **What it did:** compares two ways of having an agent (SWE-agent with Claude Sonnet 4, on the 128 SWE-smith repositories) make bugs: **BugInstruct**, told to inject bugs on purpose, and **FeatAdd**, told to add a feature without breaking behaviour, where any run that breaks a test is kept as a bug. Argues that intentional bug injection "induce[s] an out-of-distribution effect" and that unintentional bugs look more like human edits.
- **Key numbers:** (fetch tool, quoted) Claude Sonnet 4's solve rate "drops from 63.5% to 41.4%" from R2E-Gym bugs to FeatAdd bugs; Table 1 gives 65.9% on SWE-smith bugs and 54.6% on BugInstruct. FeatAdd patches touch 4.2 files on average against 1.2 for SWE-smith, with 206.5 against 8.8 lines of code; about half of FeatAdd's edited files are new and about half its edited lines are documentation. "SWE-Smith bugs shows a particular skew towards logic and conditional bugs", while FeatAdd bugs have "a more even distribution of bugs across various categories". The fetch tool noted the text says ten categories but Appendix B lists nine (A to I). Dataset licence not stated on the page.
- **Connection:** **challenges and offers an alternative to** Claude-written seeds. It is the clearest evidence found that "write a bug on purpose" gives a different, easier and narrower bug population than bugs an agent makes by accident. For the project, a FeatAdd-style protocol (Claude implements a feature in one of the apps; regressions caught by tests become seeds) would give seeds that are still Claude-made but not deliberately planted, and would weaken the "the planter knows where it hid them" route of same-family bias. Same-family risk remains, and arguably grows: these are exactly the bugs Claude makes.
- **Novelty threat:** low. It is about training data for repair agents, not review.

#### SWE-smith: Scaling Data for Software Engineering Agents
- **Citation:** John Yang, Kilian Lieret, Carlos E. Jimenez et al. (10 authors); arXiv preprint; v1 30 Apr 2025, v2 21 May 2025; [arXiv 2504.21798](https://arxiv.org/abs/2504.21798); assets at swesmith.com. Venue not confirmed (the arXiv comment gives none). This is the paper seed-sources-research.md cites for SWE-bench Multilingual.
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API). Bug-type details below come only from BugPilot's description of it.
- **What it did:** a pipeline that, for any Python repository, builds an environment and "automatically synthesizes 100s to 1,000s of task instances that break existing test(s)"; 50k instances from 128 repositories.
- **Key numbers:** "50k instances sourced from 128 GitHub repositories"; SWE-agent-LM-32B trained on it reaches "40.2% Pass@1" on SWE-bench Verified.
- **Connection:** **overlaps** with the project's mutation and Claude-written seed arms: it is the largest public generator of test-breaking synthetic bugs, but BugPilot's analysis says its bugs are small (about 1.2 files, 8.8 lines) and skewed to logic and conditional faults, which is the same weakness the seed-sources doc attributes to mutation tools.
- **Novelty threat:** none.

#### SMART: Boosting LLMs for Mutation Generation
- **Citation:** Bo Wang, Ming Deng, Mingda Chen, Chengran Yang, Youfang Lin, Mark Harman, Mike Papadakis and Jie M. Zhang; arXiv preprint, v1 25 Mar 2026; [arXiv 2603.24560](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.24560). The arXiv comment says "to be published in the collection of FSE 2026".
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API).
- **What it did:** LLM mutation generation with retrieval over a database of real bugs, focused code chunking and fine-tuning on mutations "coupled with real-world bugs"; evaluated on 1,991 real Java bugs from Defects4J and ConDefects against LLMut and LLMorpheus.
- **Key numbers:** validity "from 42.89% to 65.6%"; non-duplicate rate "from 87.38% to 95.62%"; compilable rate "from 88.85% to 90.21%"; "real bug detection rate of 92.61% (vs. 57.86% for LLMut)".
- **Connection:** **supports** using LLM mutants as seeds only if they are steered towards real bugs. Its baseline numbers show that plain LLM mutants are often invalid (under half valid) or duplicated, which bears on the project's Claude-written seeds and on decision 6's advice to drop near-duplicate seeds. Note: "real bug detection rate" here is a coupling measure (tests that kill the mutants also detect the real bug), not review recall.
- **Novelty threat:** none.

#### Test-aware mutant generation: Beyond Rule-Based Mutation Testing: Test-Aware Mutant Generation Using Large Language Models
- **Citation:** Nils Kiele, Zainab Saad, Zirui Wang, Steve Drew and Samira Ebrahimi Kahou; arXiv preprint, 24 Sep 2026; [arXiv 2609.35841](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.35841). The arXiv comment says "Accepted to ESEM 2026".
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API).
- **What it did:** gives the LLM the problem, the correct solution and the base tests, and asks for a non-trivial mutant that still passes the base tests; EvalPlus's extended tests act as the oracle for whether a surviving mutant is a real bug.
- **Key numbers:** "verified fault rates of 87.7% (HumanEval) and 79.1% (MBPP)", against "12.2% and 23.0%" for test-blind prompting and "4.4% and 5.7%" for mutmut. "Fault subtlety ... remains comparable across all three methods."
- **Connection:** **supports** a cheap validity check for Claude-written seeds: requiring a seed to pass the app's existing tests but fail a stronger oracle makes it a genuine, test-escaping bug, which is the kind a reviewer is needed for. It also quantifies how few mutmut mutants are real escaped faults (about 5%). Toy benchmarks only (HumanEval, MBPP).
- **Novelty threat:** none.

#### MegaBugFix: Diff-Based Code Corruption using LLMs for Large-Scale Bugfix Benchmarking
- **Citation:** Balázs Szalontai, Ábel Szauter, Balázs Márton, Péter Verebics, Balázs Pintér and Tibor Gregorics; arXiv preprint; v1 27 Jun 2026, v3 1 Jul 2026; [arXiv 2606.29088](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.29088); data at hf.co/collections/szalontaib/megabugfix. Venue not confirmed.
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API).
- **What it did:** a fine-tuned LLM writes bug injections as diffs into correct Python programs, giving 12,629 buggy programs; 13 open-weight models do worse on it than on existing bug-fix benchmarks.
- **Key numbers:** "12,629 buggy Python programs"; diffs "avoid common pitfalls of LLM-based mutation techniques like injecting overly simplistic bugs or failing to modify the input program".
- **Connection:** **overlaps**: another LLM bug-injection generator, with the explicit claim that naive LLM injection gives "overly simplistic bugs" or no change at all. Its motivation (small benchmarks weaken "statistical reliability"; similar buggy programs "distort" results) matches decision 6's points on seed counts and near-duplicates. Repair, not review; small programs.
- **Novelty threat:** none.

#### BugFarm: Challenging Bug Prediction and Repair Models with Synthetic Bugs
- **Citation:** Ali Reza Ibrahimzada, Yang Chen, Ryan Rong and Reyhaneh Jabbarvand; v1 3 Oct 2023, v3 9 Sep 2025; [arXiv 2310.02407](https://arxiv.org/abs/2310.02407). The arXiv comment says "Published in SCAM 2025".
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API).
- **What it did:** uses an LLM to mutate code in several places, steering changes to the locations a learned bug-predictor attends to least, so that bugs are hard to detect and hard to repair; 435k+ bugs from 1.9M mutants.
- **Connection:** **loosely related but a warning**: it shows planted bugs can be deliberately made adversarial to a specific detector. A Claude planter that knows (or is) the reviewer could, even without intent, place bugs where Claude reviewers look least, or most. That is the same-family risk in a measurable form, and it argues for seeds whose placement Claude does not choose (mutation tools at random sites, SZZ bugs).
- **Novelty threat:** none.

#### Mutant realism in deep learning: An Empirical Study of the Realism of Mutants in Deep Learning
- **Citation:** Zaheed Ahmed, Philip Makedonski and Jens Grabowski; arXiv preprint; v1 18 Dec 2025, v2 12 Oct 2026; [arXiv 2512.16741](https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.16741). Venue not confirmed.
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API).
- **What it did:** a statistical framework scoring mutant realism by "coupling strength and behavioral similarity to real faults", applied to deep-learning mutation tools on four real-bug datasets.
- **Connection:** **loosely related**: a recent, reusable way to measure "are these seeds like real bugs" (coupling and behavioural similarity), which the project could borrow to compare its Claude-written seeds, mutants and SZZ bugs. The domain (DL models) is not the project's.
- **Novelty threat:** none.

#### Meta ACH: Mutation-Guided LLM-based Test Generation at Meta
- **Citation:** Christopher Foster, Abhishek Gulati, Mark Harman, Inna Harper, Ke Mao, Jillian Ritchey, Hervé Robert and Shubho Sengupta; arXiv preprint, 22 Jan 2025; [arXiv 2501.12862](https://arxiv.org/abs/2501.12862). The arXiv comment says "Submitted to FSE 2025 Industry Track" (acceptance not confirmed).
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API).
- **What it did:** an LLM generates a few targeted, "currently undetected" faults about a concern (privacy), then tests that kill them; includes an LLM equivalent-mutant detector.
- **Key numbers:** 10,795 Kotlin classes, "9,095 mutants and 571 privacy-hardening test cases"; equivalent-mutant detector "precision of 0.79 and a recall of 0.47 (rising to 0.95 and 0.96 with simple pre-processing)"; engineers "accepted 73% of its tests".
- **Connection:** **supports** concern-targeted planted faults (here privacy), which is close to the project's role-specific seeds (security, performance and so on). Its equivalent-mutant detector addresses a seed-validity problem the project will have: a planted "bug" that does not change behaviour is a free false miss.
- **Novelty threat:** none.

#### 1b. Contamination and memorisation of bug benchmarks: detection methods

#### Learned or Memorized: Learned or Memorized ? Quantifying Memorization Advantage in Code LLMs
- **Citation:** Djiré Albérick Euraste, Kaboré Abdoul Kader, Jordan Samhi, Earl T. Barr, Jacques Klein and Tegawendé F. Bissyandé; arXiv preprint, 15 Apr 2026; [arXiv 2604.13997](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.13997). The arXiv comment says "Accepted at ICSE 2026 (to appear)".
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API).
- **What it did:** a perturbation-based measure of "memorization advantage" (performance gap between likely-seen and unseen inputs) across 8 open code LLMs and 19 benchmarks, including bug fixing and vulnerability detection.
- **Key numbers:** StarCoder "up to 0.8" sensitivity on some benchmarks, QwenCoder "mostly below 0.4"; "CVEFixes remains below 0.1, and Defects4J is lower than other program repair benchmarks."
- **Connection:** **challenges** the seed-sources doc's flat "contamination: high" for Defects4J (which rests on Ramos et al.'s finding that Defects4J shows the highest *potential* memorisation). Here the measured *advantage* from memorisation on Defects4J and CVEfixes is low, at least for open 2024–2025 models. Exposure and score inflation are different things; the project's contamination section should keep them apart. Open models only, so not directly about Claude.
- **Novelty threat:** none.

#### BenchChecker: Assessing the Credibility of Bug-Fixing Benchmarks for LLMs
- **Citation:** Di Wu, Xu He, Shu Wang and Kun Sun; USENIX Security 2026, pp. 5077–5096 (arXiv `journal_ref`); arXiv 14 Oct 2026; [arXiv 2610.19447](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19447).
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API).
- **What it did:** a black-box, two-stage contamination detector for bug-fix benchmarks: a "repository presence test" (probe with code prefixes) then a "patch presence test" (does the fix persist in recent snapshots, checked by similarity and tests). Validated on StarCoder, StarCoder2 and CodeGen2, whose training data (The Stack v2) is known.
- **Key numbers:** "AUC scores around 0.9 across six benchmarks"; filtering contaminated samples "reduces the reported resolution rates of most LLMs by more than 20% relative to the original rates on medium-difficulty tasks" on SWE-bench Verified and Multi-SWE-bench.
- **Connection:** **supports** the contamination check, and offers a usable signal for SZZ-traced seeds in particular: a seed whose fix has persisted in the public repository for a long time is more likely to have been trained on. A cheap filter for SZZ seeds is "fix commit after the reviewing model's cutoff", which this paper's logic endorses. Validation is on open models only.
- **Novelty threat:** none.

#### SWE-Verified memory: Does SWE-Bench-Verified Test Agent Ability or Model Memory?
- **Citation:** Thanosan Prathifkumar, Noble Saji Mathews and Meiyappan Nagappan; arXiv preprint; v1 11 Dec 2025, v2 22 Dec 2025; [arXiv 2512.10218](https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.10218). The HTML page lists the "International Workshop on Agentic Engineering", April 2026, Rio de Janeiro (fetch tool); acceptance not confirmed.
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API); full text partly read (fetch tool: Tables 3 and 4).
- **What it did:** asks **Claude** models to name the files to edit from issue text alone on SWE-bench Verified, BeetleBox and SWE-rebench, where the task "should be logically impossible to solve".
- **Key numbers:** abstract: "models performed 3 times better on SWE-Bench-Verified" and "6 times better at finding edited files". Table 3 (fetch tool), all ground-truth files from issue text only: Claude 3.5 Sonnet 65% on Verified against 12.2% on BeetleBox and 12% on SWE-rebench (09/2025); Claude 3.7 Sonnet 63.2% against 12% and 8%. No significance tests.
- **Connection:** **supports** the project's concern about Claude specifically: the same-family risk includes Claude having memorised public bug benchmarks. It is direct evidence for using post-cutoff SWE-bench-Live tasks rather than Verified, and a cheap probe the project could run on its own candidate seeds (can Claude name the buggy file or line from the PR title alone?).
- **Novelty threat:** none.

#### Measurement-first audit: Measurement-First Auditing of Agentic Leaderboards: Contamination Susceptibility, Matched-Control Re-evaluation, and Scorer Validation
- **Citation:** Dishu Yang, Qi Su, Hongbo Qin and Hansong Zhang; NeurIPS 2026 Workshop LCFM (arXiv `journal_ref`); arXiv 5 Oct 2026; [arXiv 2610.05830](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.05830).
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API).
- **What it did:** separates three contamination channels (training-time exposure, evaluation-time retrieval, pipeline or scaffold leakage), codes each as open, partial, closed or unknown, and re-tests the reported SWE-bench Verified file-localisation gap with a same-repository matched-control design, paired and repository-aware intervals and a validated scorer.
- **Key numbers:** "none of the 27 channel assessments was coded closed, but incidents were confirmed in four configurations"; on 100 retained pairs GPT-4.1 showed "a +10.0-point pair-weighted Top-3 benchmark-associated gap, but the 95% intervals ... included zero"; "The reproduction scorer did not pass its validation gate".
- **Connection:** **challenges** strong readings of the localisation-gap evidence (including the Claude paper above and the covered SWE-Bench Illusion paper): with matched controls and paired intervals the gap was inconclusive. Directly useful as a template for the project's own contamination check: name the channel (for a Claude Code council, evaluation-time retrieval and scaffold leakage, such as web search or git history in the repo, are live channels alongside training exposure), use matched controls and validate the scorer before trusting it.
- **Novelty threat:** none.

#### Cross-Context Verification (CCV): Hierarchical Detection of Benchmark Contamination through Session-Isolated Analysis
- **Citation:** Tae-Eun Song; arXiv preprint; v1 23 Mar 2026, v2 1 Apr 2026; [arXiv 2603.21454](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.21454). Venue not confirmed. (Same author name as the "Song 2026" cross-model review paper in prior-work-research.md; whether it is the same person is not confirmed.)
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API).
- **What it did:** solves the same benchmark problem in N isolated sessions and uses solution diversity to separate recall from reasoning; adds a multi-agent analysis structure that restricts information between roles to avoid confirmation bias.
- **Key numbers:** "On 9 SWE-bench Verified problems (45 trials, Claude Opus 4.6, temperature 0), CCV achieves perfect separation ... (Mann-Whitney U=0, p approx 0.012, r = 1.0)"; "33% of prior contamination labels are false positives"; a Worker to Verifier to Director pilot "yields a negative result--100% sycophantic confirmation". Also: "multi-turn review generates false positives faster than it discovers true errors".
- **Connection:** **loosely related, two ways.** (1) A cheap Claude-specific contamination probe the project could reuse on seeds: low diversity across independent sessions suggests recall. (2) Its side findings bear on councils: a sequential verifier chain confirmed sycophantically, and repeated review added false positives faster than true catches, which is relevant to the sequential-vs-parallel and appeal arms. Very small n (9 problems), single author, so weak evidence.
- **Novelty threat:** low.

#### SchrodingerRepo: Schrödinger's Code Repository: Have LLMs Learned SWE-bench or Memorized It?
- **Citation:** Silin Chen, Yufei Yang, Xiaodong Gu, Yuling Shi, Chengcheng Wan and Haibing Guan; arXiv preprint, 21 Aug 2026; [arXiv 2609.27891](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.27891); code and data at github.com/cslsolow/Schrodinger-Repo. Venue not confirmed.
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API).
- **What it did:** rewrites the test repository at evaluation time (problem statement, namespace remapping, layout reordering, behaviour-preserving rewriting) to strip familiar cues; performance falls and cost rises.
- **Key numbers:** none given in the abstract beyond "consistently degrades agent performance and substantially increases interaction costs".
- **Connection:** **supports** a decontamination option for public seeds: if the project uses real bugs from public repositories (SZZ or ReviewBench), renaming identifiers and reordering files before review reduces memorised-cue help. It also warns that such rewriting changes difficulty, so rewritten and unrewritten seeds should not be pooled.
- **Novelty threat:** none.

#### SWE-Bench+: Enhanced Coding Benchmark for LLMs
- **Citation:** Reem Aleithan, Haoran Xue, Mohammad Mahdi Mohajer, Elijah Nnorom, Gias Uddin and Song Wang; arXiv preprint, 9 Oct 2024 (v2 10 Oct 2024); [arXiv 2410.06992](https://arxiv.org/abs/2410.06992). Venue not confirmed.
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API).
- **Key numbers:** "32.67% of the successful patches involve cheating as the solutions were directly provided in the issue report or the comments"; "31.08% of the passed patches are suspicious patches due to weak test cases"; filtered resolution "dropped from 12.47% to 3.97%"; "over 94% of the issues were created before LLM's knowledge cutoff dates".
- **Connection:** **supports** a point the project should carry over to review seeds: **solution leakage through the PR or issue text**. If a seed's PR description or linked issue describes the bug, reviewers get the answer. Seed PRs should carry neutral descriptions. This complements the covered "When AI Agrees Too Much" result (biased descriptions hide bugs): descriptions can either leak or hide.
- **Novelty threat:** none.

#### Refreshed collections, briefly
- **SWE-rebench V2** (Ibragim Badertdinov, Maksim Nekrashevich, Anton Shevtsov and Alexander Golubev; arXiv comment "ICML 2026"; v1 27 Feb 2026, v2 1 Jun 2026; [arXiv 2602.23866](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.23866); abstract read, verbatim, arXiv API): "32,079 tasks spanning 20 languages and 3,617 repositories", plus "120,000+ tasks"; filters with "an ensemble of LLM judges, validated against human-verified SWE-bench annotations" and flags confounders. **Connection:** answers the seed-sources doc's open note ("SWE-rebench v2 is promoted, but I didn't read it"): v2 is language-agnostic, so C#, JavaScript and TypeScript tasks probably exist; per-language counts were not in the abstract (not confirmed). It is a training collection, not a moving-window leaderboard.
- **SWE-MERA** (Pavel Adamenko, Mikhail Ivanov, Aidar Valeev et al.; arXiv comment "EMNLP 2025"; v1 15 Jul 2025, v3 11 Jul 2026; [arXiv 2507.11059](https://arxiv.org/abs/2507.11059); abstract read, verbatim, arXiv API): a "dynamic, continuously updated benchmark" with "approximately 10,000 potential tasks with 728 samples currently available", tasks "collected between September 2024 and June 2025". **Connection:** another refreshed issue collection; Python, issue resolution. Low relevance beyond SWE-bench-Live.
- **Saving SWE-Bench** (Spandan Garg, Benjamin Steenhoek and Yufan Huang; arXiv comment "Accepted at CAIN 2026"; [arXiv 2510.08996](https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.08996); abstract read, verbatim, arXiv API): rewriting formal issues into realistic chat-style queries shows benchmarks "overestimate agent capabilities for some models by >50%" on public benchmarks and "~10-16%" on a private C# benchmark. **Connection:** loosely related; input framing moves scores a lot, which bears on how much context (PR text, `CLAUDE.md`) a seed is presented with.

### 2.6 SZZ and escaped-bug datasets

**Headline for the plan.** The plan names R-SZZ and V-SZZ. Every 2026 evaluation found here puts classic SZZ variants well below LLM-agent tracers, and V-SZZ in particular scores 33–40% F1 on a new verified benchmark. Three 2026 papers also find that roughly one bug-inducing commit in five to one in four cannot be reached by `git blame` at all, and one finds about a fifth of a widely used developer-informed oracle's labels wrong or ambiguous. Claude Code itself is now a strong SZZ tool (F1 0.771, or 0.938 on corrected labels), which raises a same-family point the plan should state: if Claude traces the seeds, Claude has chosen them.

#### 2a. SZZ variants and evaluations, 2023–2026

#### Claude Code as SZZ: Rethinking SZZ Search Strategies: Lessons from Claude Code
- **Citation:** Inase Kondo, Masanari Kondo, Daniel M. German, Olivier Nourry, Yasutaka Kamei and Yoshiki Higo; arXiv preprint, 10 Oct 2026; [arXiv 2610.14462](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14462). Venue not confirmed.
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API); full text partly read (fetch tool: dataset, RQ3 oracle errors, results, threats).
- **What it did:** lets Claude Code (2.1.232, Claude Sonnet 5 at high effort, Bash only, no internet) investigate a repository freely to find the bug-inducing commit for 79 Java cases from 70 repositories in Rosa et al.'s Developer-Informed Oracle dataset, against ten SZZ baselines.
- **Key numbers:** abstract: "In 79.7% of runs, Claude Code reviewed commit histories before inspecting individual code diffs"; "F1 score of 0.771, significantly outperforming all ten evaluated SZZ baselines"; after rebuilding the dataset, "F1 score of 0.938". Fetch tool: precision 0.767, recall 0.776 on the original 79; strongest baseline "Simple-SZZ-Agent ... F1 score of 0.696" (0.826 on the corrected set); 7 cases with wrong oracle labels and 9 ambiguous ones (introducing versus triggering, what counts as a bug, equivalent commits on branches, several fixes in one commit); "three times" per case, Wilcoxon on case-level means, no run-to-run spread reported; "mean API cost of USD 0.166 and a mean running time of 50.14 seconds per run". On contamination: "we cannot rule out the use of bug information learned during model training". R-SZZ and V-SZZ were not among the baselines (fetch tool).
- **Connection:** **supports and complicates** the SZZ seed source. It is cheap (about $0.17 a case) and accurate, so it could replace R-SZZ and V-SZZ for tracing. But a Claude tracer picking seeds that Claude councils then review is a same-family selection step; a non-Claude tracer, or agreement between Claude and a classic SZZ, would keep seed choice independent of the reviewer family. Its oracle-error finding (16 of 79 cases wrong or ambiguous) is direct evidence of label noise in SZZ ground truth.
- **Novelty threat:** none.

#### Agentic SZZ on the Linux kernel: LLM-Based Agents for Identifying Bug-Introducing Commits
- **Citation:** Niklas Risse and Marcel Böhme; arXiv v1 31 Mar 2026, v2 23 Sep 2026; [arXiv 2603.29378](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.29378). The arXiv comment says "Accepted at ... ASE '26. Camera-Ready Version".
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API).
- **What it did:** a simple agent workflow that searches a candidate set of commits, evaluated on the developer-labelled Linux kernel dataset.
- **Key numbers:** "raises the F1-score from 0.64 to 0.81 on the most popular Linux kernel dataset, a bigger jump than between the original 2005 method (0.54) and the previous SOTA (0.64)". Agents "derive short greppable patterns from the fix commit diff and message".
- **Connection:** **challenges** the choice of R-SZZ and V-SZZ as the tracing tools: an agentic tracer is a bigger step up than two decades of SZZ variants. Also notes that agents rely on the fix's commit message, so the fix text matters for tracing even though it must be hidden from reviewers.
- **Novelty threat:** none.

#### AgentSZZ: Teaching the LLM Agent to Play Detective with Bug-Inducing Commits
- **Citation:** Yunbo Lyu, Jieke Shi, Hong Jin Kang et al. (11 authors, including Julia Lawall and David Lo); arXiv preprint, 3 Apr 2026; [arXiv 2604.02665](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.02665). Venue not confirmed.
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API).
- **What it did:** a ReAct-style agent with SZZ-specific tools and context compression.
- **Key numbers:** prior methods have "recall of 0.552 on the Linux kernel"; reliance on `git blame` makes "nearly one-quarter of bug-inducing commits inherently untraceable"; "F1-score gains of up to 27.2% over prior LLM-based approaches"; recall gains "up to 300% and 60%" for cross-file and ghost cases; compression "reduce[s] token consumption by over 30%".
- **Connection:** **supports** the point that blame-based SZZ (R-SZZ included) systematically misses cross-file and "ghost" bug introductions, so SZZ-derived seeds are biased towards bugs that are local to the lines later fixed. Those are the easiest bugs to spot by reading, which would make SZZ seeds look easier than escaped bugs really are.
- **Novelty threat:** none.

#### AgenticSZZ: Temporal Knowledge Graph-Guided Agentic Bug-Inducing Commit Identification
- **Citation:** Yu Shi, Hao Li, Bram Adams and Ahmed E. Hassan; arXiv v1 3 Feb 2026, v2 7 May 2026; [arXiv 2602.02934](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.02934). Venue not confirmed.
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API).
- **Key numbers:** on "2,102 validated bug-fixing commits", "28% of BICs require traversing commit history beyond blame results and 14% are blameless"; "F1-scores of 0.47 to 0.79" across three datasets, "up to 34%" better than the state of the art; "60 additional true positives" from context expansion.
- **Connection:** **supports** the same bias point with an independent count (28% beyond blame, 14% blameless). Same group as the AIDev dataset (Hao Li, Ahmed E. Hassan), which the seed-sources doc lists as a lead for agent-written bugs; the two could be combined to trace bugs introduced by Claude Code PRs.
- **Novelty threat:** none.

#### LLM4SZZ: Enhancing SZZ Algorithm with Context-Enhanced Assessment on Large Language Models
- **Citation:** Lingxiao Tang, Jiakun Liu, Zhongxin Liu, Xiaohu Yang and Lingfeng Bao; arXiv preprint, 2 Apr 2025; [arXiv 2504.01404](https://arxiv.org/abs/2504.01404). Venue not confirmed (the arXiv record gives none).
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API).
- **Key numbers:** "improving F1-score by 6.9% to 16.0% without significantly sacrificing recall" across three datasets.
- **Connection:** **overlaps**: the first LLM-assisted SZZ; now the baseline that 2026 agentic tools beat. Lingfeng Bao is also first author of V-SZZ (Bao, Xia, Hassan and Yang, ICSE 2022; author list seen only in a search snippet), so this is in effect the V-SZZ group's own successor.
- **Novelty threat:** none.

#### VICBench: A Multi-Language Benchmark for Code Vulnerability Detection
- **Citation:** Jin Lu, Xuening Han, Yang Zhong, Lin Tan, Kevin Luo, Andrew Gacek and Neha Rungta; arXiv preprint, 12 Aug 2026; [arXiv 2608.12246](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12246). Venue not confirmed.
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API).
- **What it did:** 100 verified vulnerability-inducing commits for 100 CVEs across 88 projects in Python, Java and C++, annotated by both human experts and an agentic workflow.
- **Key numbers:** fixes "averaging 38.6 lines and corresponding VICs of 252.5 lines"; "V-SZZ and LLM4SZZ achieve only 33.3%-40.1% F1".
- **Connection:** **challenges** V-SZZ as a seed tracer for security seeds: on harder, larger fixes it gets about a third of the answers right, so most V-SZZ-traced seeds would need hand checking. It is also a small, verified, Python-including security seed source in its own right (licence not checked).
- **Novelty threat:** none.

#### MAS-SZZ: Multi-Agentic SZZ Algorithm for Vulnerability-Inducing Commit Identification
- **Citation:** Sicong Cao, Jinxuan Xu, Le Yu, Jing Yang, Xingwei Lin, Linlin Zhu and Fu Xiao; arXiv preprint, 27 Apr 2026; [arXiv 2604.24398](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.24398). Venue not confirmed.
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API).
- **Key numbers:** "neither the customized V-SZZ nor state-of-the-art LLM4SZZ perform satisfactorily due to the incorrect anchor selection and inadequate backtracking"; "F1-score gains of up to 65.22% over the best-performing SZZ algorithm".
- **Connection:** **challenges** V-SZZ, as above; a multi-agent tracer is the current best for vulnerability-introducing commits.
- **Novelty threat:** none.

#### SZZBench: Controlled Git Histories with Explicit Oracles for Diagnosing SZZ Implementations
- **Citation:** Yoshiki Higo, Inase Kondo, Masanari Kondo and Yasutaka Kamei; arXiv preprint, 13 Nov 2026; [arXiv 2611.17669](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17669); tool at github.com/higolab/SZZBench. Venue not confirmed.
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API).
- **What it did:** generates synthetic Git repositories from declarative scenarios with known answers, scores any SZZ tool per scenario and compares it with 26 naive baselines.
- **Key numbers:** "279 scenarios in 17 categories"; "PyDriller passes 161 scenarios and a rule that always answers the second commit passes 160, yet only 97 scenarios are passed by both".
- **Connection:** **supports** checking the project's chosen tracer before trusting its seeds. It is also a methodological parallel to the project: aggregate scores hide which items each system succeeds on, and per-item comparison against trivial baselines is what reveals it (the same argument as decision 6's paired, seed-by-seed analysis).
- **Novelty threat:** none.

#### Linux kernel SZZ evaluation: Evaluating SZZ Implementations: An Empirical Study on the Linux Kernel
- **Citation:** Yunbo Lyu, Hong Jin Kang, Ratnadira Widyasari, Julia Lawall and David Lo; arXiv v1 9 Aug 2023, v2 7 Jun 2024; [arXiv 2308.05060](https://arxiv.org/abs/2308.05060). The arXiv comment says "accepted for publication in IEEE Transactions on Software Engineering".
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API).
- **What it did:** uses the Linux kernel's developer-written `Fixes:` tags as a researcher-independent oracle for six SZZ algorithms.
- **Key numbers:** "76,046 pairs of bug-fixing patches and bug-inducing commits"; SZZ "experience[s] a more significant decline in recall on our dataset (13.8%)"; "17.47% of bug-fixing commits are ghost commits".
- **Connection:** **supports** a cleaner escaped-bug source than SZZ: projects whose fix commits name the inducing commit (Linux's `Fixes:` tag; some other projects follow the same convention) give developer-labelled bug-introducing changes that passed review, with no SZZ step. None of the project's languages, but the convention exists elsewhere and is worth searching for in C#, Python and JavaScript projects (not checked).
- **Novelty threat:** none.

#### Labelling strategies, briefly
- **WIA-SZZ** (Salomé Perez-Rosero, Robert Dyer, Samuel W. Flint, Shane McIntosh and Witawas Srisa-an; arXiv preprint, 19 Nov 2024; [arXiv 2411.12740](https://arxiv.org/abs/2411.12740); abstract read, verbatim, arXiv API): treats a bug-inducing commit and its fixes as one "work item"; heuristic "64% accurate in finding work items"; "improvement in F1 scores ranging from 2% to 9%" over six variants on 821 repositories. Low relevance.
- **One Strategy Does Not Fit All** (Quan Tran, Duong Nguyen, Thanh Le-Cong et al.; arXiv preprint, 3 Nov 2026; [arXiv 2611.05013](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05013); abstract read, verbatim, arXiv API): 28 datasets from four fix-labelling and four introducing-commit labelling strategies on OpenSSL and Linux; "labeling effectiveness is strongly task-dependent". **Connection:** supports reporting which labelling strategy produced each SZZ seed, since the strategy changes the data.
- **ReDef** (Doha Nam, Taehyoun Kim, Duksan Ryu and Jongmoon Baik; arXiv comment "Accepted to FSE 2026"; [arXiv 2509.09192](https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.09192); abstract read, verbatim, arXiv API): defects anchored by **revert commits** rather than SZZ, with "GPT-assisted triage"; "3,164 defective and 10,268 clean modifications" from 22 C/C++ projects, because existing datasets "suffer from noisy labels and low precision in identifying bug-inducing commits". **Connection:** reverts are another SZZ-free route to escaped bugs: a reverted merged change is a reviewed change that was then judged bad. Useful for the project's own repos too.

#### 2b. Benchmarks of bug-introducing changes that passed review

#### SecPRBench: Can Agentic Code Review Catch Vulnerabilities Before They Are Merged?
- **Citation:** Yihan Yang, Chenyuan Yang, Zijie Zhao, Anh Nguyen Hoang, Ziqi Zhang, Weidong Wang, Zhiheng Lyu and Lingming Zhang; arXiv preprint; v1 24 Oct 2026, v2 30 Oct 2026; [arXiv 2610.33509](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33509); benchmark at github.com/secprbench/secprbench. Venue not confirmed.
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API); full text partly read (fetch tool: the first 100,000 of 121,633 characters, so the later appendices were not read).
- **What it did:** an agentic pipeline harvests real vulnerability-introducing commits from nine large systems (Firefox, V8, OpenSSL and others), validates each mainly by proof-of-concept replay on the parent, introducing and fixed versions, and asks review agents to review the introducing change. An LLM judge credits findings that match the ground-truth vulnerability.
- **Key numbers:** abstract: "265 tasks from nine critical systems"; "recall ranges from 32.5% to 78.1%" across eight models; "in 68% of [misses], the agent reports on a file that holds the flaw but argues for a different mechanism"; "OpenAI's Codex Security lowers recall by 8.3 points, mostly by abstaining, while Anthropic's Defending Code harness raises it by 7.7 points, at a higher cost". Fetch tool: 217 of 265 cases pass mechanical checks and 48 went to human adjudication; the judge is "Claude Opus 5.5 (high), with tool use and web access disabled"; "Opus 5.5 and Sonnet 4.6 agree on 96.9% of verdicts" over 2,143 reports; both judges matched the authors on "all 25 randomly sampled agreement controls", and on 25 disagreements adjudication "supported Opus in 21 cases and Sonnet in four"; "SecPRBench does not measure report precision"; "Each agent has one run per task"; paired McNemar tests separate no top-four pair; Opus 5.5 recall 74.0% (196 of 265), Sonnet 5 59.2%; mean nominal cost per scan $0.44 for Opus 5.5 and $3.20 for Grok 4.6; the Defending Code harness costs "about ten times the median nominal cost". Contamination: post-cutoff recall is lower for four of five testable models, but no difference is statistically clear. Licence not stated.
- **Connection:** **overlaps closely** with the escaped-bug seed arm and the scoring: real introducing changes that passed review, review by reading, an LLM matching judge with a small human check, paired McNemar comparisons and costs. Differences: security only, C/C++ and JavaScript engines rather than the project's languages, single reviewers and harnesses rather than councils, no precision, one run per task. Two findings matter directly. (1) The 68% "right file, wrong mechanism" misses are a judge-matching question: whether a council finding that names the right place but a different cause counts as a catch must be fixed in the scoring rules in advance, and the appeal agent could be allowed to contest such rulings. (2) A Claude judge scoring Claude reviewers, here and in the project, is exactly the same-family judging risk.
- **Novelty threat:** medium on the escaped-bug benchmark and scoring parts; none on councils, evolution or appeals.

#### VIC-RAGENT: Detecting Vulnerability-Inducing Commits via Multi-Stage Reasoning with LLM-Based Agents
- **Citation:** Liyou Chen, Hailong Sun, Xiang Gao and Yue Pan; arXiv preprint, 7 Jul 2026; [arXiv 2607.05772](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.05772). Venue not confirmed.
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API); full text read (fetch tool: method, Tables 3, 4 and 6, threats).
- **What it did:** reviews commits for introduced vulnerabilities with role agents (code analyst, target or intent analyst, a three-stage vulnerability inspector, a documentation specialist and an audit supervisor); a commit is flagged if "at least one fragment is confirmed as vulnerable". Data: 241 commits (106 inducing, 135 fixing) from the V-SZZ dataset.
- **Key numbers:** (fetch tool, Table 3) F1 57% against 33% for CodeAgent with DeepSeek-V3.2, 60% against 49% with GPT-4o-mini and 66% against 55% with Qwen-Plus. Ablation (DeepSeek-V3.2 only): removing the final verification stage raised F1 from 57% to 62% (recall 48% to 57%, precision 70% to 67%). No repeated runs or intervals; the knowledge base is cleaned with ground-truth labels, which may leak labels.
- **Connection:** **overlaps** in structure (role specialists plus a verifying filter on commits that later proved vulnerable) and **supports** the appeal idea: its own ablation shows the one-way verification stage cost more recall than it bought in precision, which is the failure an appeal against dismissals is meant to catch. Same-model roles only, no family mixing, no evolution.
- **Novelty threat:** low to medium (another role-split review system on SZZ-type data; weak evaluation).

#### 2c. Studies of bugs that escape code review
- **Paul, Turzo and Bosu, "Why Security Defects Go Unnoticed during Code Reviews? A Case-Control Study of the Chromium OS Project"** (ICSE 2021, per the arXiv `journal_ref`; [arXiv 2102.06909](https://arxiv.org/abs/2102.06909); abstract read, verbatim, arXiv API): "516 code reviews that successfully identified security defects and 374 code reviews where security defects escaped"; "significant differences between the categories of security defects that are identified and that are missed"; a logistic model reached "AUC score of 0.91". Review time and mutual reviews helped; "the number of directories under review" hurt. **Connection:** supports weighting escaped-bug seeds towards the categories human review misses, and treating change spread (directories touched) as a difficulty covariate.
- **Khoshnoud, Rezaei Nasab, Toudeji and Sami, "Which bugs are missed in code reviews: An empirical study on SmartSHARK dataset"** (arXiv comment: accepted at MSR 2022; [arXiv 2205.09428](https://arxiv.org/abs/2205.09428); abstract read, verbatim, arXiv API): "187 bugs missed in 173 pull requests" from 77 projects; distribution "semantic (51.34%), build (15.5%), analysis checks (9.09%), compatibility (7.49%), concurrency (4.28%), configuration (4.28%), GUI (2.14%), API (2.14%), security (2.14%), and memory (1.6%)". **Connection:** a base-rate mix for escaped bugs, useful when setting the seed category mix; note that build, configuration and compatibility faults (about 27% here) are rarely planted.
- **Morshed and Carver, "Use of Peer Code Review to Identify Potential Security Vulnerabilities in Open Source Software: A Systematic Literature Review"** (arXiv preprint, 20 Jul 2026; [arXiv 2611.18568](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18568); abstract read, verbatim, arXiv API): 20 included studies; review "effectively detect[s] localized, well-known vulnerabilities ... but often miss[es] subtle, complex, context-dependent issues (e.g., input validation weaknesses, access control weaknesses, resource lifecycle weaknesses)". **Connection:** supports a severity or difficulty stratification for security seeds; planted security seeds tend to be the localised, well-known kind that review already catches.

### 2.7 Precision, noise and ranking measures for AI review

#### 3a. How AI review is scored beyond recall

#### CR-Bench-v2: Evaluating the Real-World Utility of AI Code Review Agents
- **Citation:** Kristen Pereira, Neelabh Sinha, Rajat Ghosh and Debojyoti Dutta; arXiv preprint; v1 2 Nov 2026, v2 3 Nov 2026; [arXiv 2611.02566](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02566). Venue not confirmed. A new arXiv record for the covered CR-Bench ([arXiv 2603.11078](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.11078)), not a new version of the same record.
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API); full text partly read (fetch tool: the first 100,000 of 132,171 characters; Appendices E and F and Tables 7, 9 and 10 were not reached).
- **What it did:** same 584 instances (174 in the Verified subset), now scored by two named metrics: **Known-Defect Detection Rate** (KDDR: at least one comment identifies the labelled defect) and **Usefulness Rate**, U = (H + S) / R, Bug Hits plus Valid Suggestions over all review comments. Judged by three LLM judges.
- **Key numbers:** abstract: "Even the strongest systems detect only about one-third of known defects (KDDR ≈ 33%), and Gemini Code Assist detects 8.1%." Fetch tool: judges GPT-5.4, Claude Sonnet 4.5 and DeepSeek V4 Flash, combined as a "three-judge mean"; human check: three experts labelled 60 reviews from one system, Claude Sonnet 4.5 matched the human majority on 71.7%, and pairwise expert agreement ranged 0.48 to 0.75 (statistic not named in the fetch summary); confidence intervals from 1,000 instance-level bootstrap resamples keeping each instance's comments together; paired bootstrap and two-sided permutation tests for strategy comparisons; Gemini Code Assist Usefulness 78.0%. No Claude-based review agent was evaluated, though Claude Opus 4.5 built the dataset. The fetch tool noted the abstract says "eight code review agents" and the body "nine systems".
- **Connection:** **supports and updates** the prior-work entry, which says CR-Bench's judge "isn't checked against human labels". v2 adds a small human check (71.7% on 60 reviews) and two more judge families. The Usefulness Rate is close to the project's precision-like measure (true plus valid unplanted findings over all findings), and the bootstrap that resamples instances while keeping their comments together is the right clustering for council findings within seeds.
- **Novelty threat:** low.

#### Vendor benchmarks on the same 50 PRs: Greptile, Augment and Luxor (grey literature)
Three vendor posts score AI reviewers on one shared set: 50 PRs whose bugs were traced to the introducing change, in Sentry (Python), Cal.com (TypeScript), Grafana (Go), Keycloak (Java) and Discourse (Ruby). These are the same five projects and PR count as Martian's offline set in seed-sources-research.md, so it is probably the same set (not confirmed); if so, it has been public since July 2025 and scored by at least four parties.
