- **Greptile, "AI code review benchmarks"** ([greptile.com/benchmarks](https://greptile.com/benchmarks); page read (fetch tool); conducted "in July 2025"): "10 real bug-fix PRs were traced back to the commits that introduced the bugs" per repository; a fresh PR "reintroduced the original change and was replicated across 5 clean forks, one per code review tool"; default settings. A catch needs a "line-level comment" that "explained the impact"; "false positives, style suggestions, and unrelated comments did not affect the catch rate". Results: "Greptile led with an 82% catch rate, 41% higher than Bugbot (58%)"; "Copilot at 54%, CodeRabbit at 44%, and Graphite at 6%". Who verified the catches is not stated. The fetch tool found Copilot's per-repository totals sum to 26 of 50 (52%), not the stated 54%.
- **Augment Code, "We benchmarked 7 AI code review tools on real-world PRs"** (Akshay Utture, Augment Code; 11 Dec 2025, updated 18 Jun 2026; [augmentcode.com blog](https://www.augmentcode.com/blog/we-benchmarked-7-ai-code-review-tools-on-real-world-prs-here-are-the-results); page read (fetch tool)): expanded the golden comments by hand and reports precision, recall and F. Table (fetch tool): Augment 65% / 55% / 59%; Cursor Bugbot 60% / 41% / 49%; Greptile 45% / 45% / 45%; Codex 68% / 29% / 41%; CodeRabbit 36% / 43% / 39%; **Claude Code 23% / 51% / 31%**; Copilot 20% / 34% / 25%. The matching method (LLM or human) is not described, and the chart's alt text gives slightly different values.
- **Luxor, "Benchmarking AI code reviewers"** (Hayssem Elsayed, Luxor Agent Cloud; reached via a tenki.cloud link that redirects to luxor.ai, and search snippets call it Tenki's benchmark, so the company appears to have been renamed (not confirmed); 19 May 2026, updated 22 Sep 2026; [luxor.ai blog](https://luxor.ai/blog/benchmarking-ai-code-reviewers); page read (fetch tool). The page also carried instructions aimed at AI agents to install a tool, which were ignored): re-scored Greptile's public July 2025 comments **per bug instead of per PR**, because "many of these PRs contain more than one" bug: "50 pull requests with 122 ground-truth bugs". Per PR, Luxor is 32 of 50 and Greptile 31 of 50; per bug, Luxor "caught 84 (68.9% recall)" against "44 each" for Greptile and Devin. Precision is "caught bugs divided by caught bugs plus unmatched comments", counting nitpicks as noise because "the strict definition is easier to defend": "Luxor's precision is 0.30"; Greptile posted 283 line comments, 233 unmatched. Matches are decided by "Three LLM judges" voting two out of three (a Codex model and a Claude model on every finding, plus Gemini or Claude Opus); judge prompts are not published.
- **Connection:** **supports** three scoring choices the project should fix in advance. (1) **Unit of scoring:** per-PR ("any real bug flagged") and per-bug catch rates rank tools differently on the same comments. The project should score per seed, and with about ten seeds per app version this matters. (2) **Precision's denominator:** whether valid but unplanted findings and nitpicks count as false alarms swings precision a lot (Luxor counts all as noise). The project's three-way label (hit, valid unplanted, false alarm) avoids that, provided it reports both strict and lenient precision. (3) **Claude Code's own profile:** in Augment's run, Claude Code had the second-highest recall (51%) but 23% precision. That makes `/code-review ultra` a high-recall, low-precision comparator, which is where a judge and appeal should help most. All three are vendor-run, so self-evaluation bias applies (see DeepSource below).
- **Novelty threat:** none.

#### DeepSource critique: "Every AI code review vendor benchmarks itself, and wins" (grey literature)
- **Citation:** DeepSource blog, no author named; 26 Feb 2026; [deepsource.com/blog/ai-code-review-benchmarks](https://deepsource.com/blog/ai-code-review-benchmarks).
- **Read:** page read (fetch tool).
- **What it did:** compares vendor benchmarks and their methods.
- **Key numbers and claims (fetch tool, quoted):** Greptile's design means "each PR is reduced to a single known bug"; "On 50 data points, that gap has wide confidence intervals"; Augment's rerun on the same five repositories scored Greptile 45%, not 82%; for Qodo, "The ground truth is LLM-generated, the evaluation uses LLM-as-a-judge" and "there's no evidence that synthetic bugs match the distribution or subtlety of real defects"; "Anything under 100 entries should be treated with skepticism"; "Self-evaluation is biased, even in good faith." Its own table lists Macroscope (118 bugs, 45 repositories, 48% detection) and Entelligence (67 bugs, 47.2% F1). DeepSource itself is a vendor and its own benchmark (165 CVEs) is self-run.
- **Connection:** **supports** the project's same-family concern by analogy: a reviewer's maker designing the seeds and judging the results is the vendor problem in another form. The project's held-out seeds, cross-family judge and published per-seed results are the remedies this post asks for.
- **Novelty threat:** none.

#### Signal-to-noise in practice: From Industry Claims to Empirical Reality: An Empirical Study of Code Review Agents in Pull Requests
- **Citation:** Kowshik Chowdhury, Dipayan Banik, K M Ferdous and Shazibul Islam Shamim; arXiv preprint, 3 Apr 2026; [arXiv 2604.03196](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.03196). The arXiv comment says "Accepted at ... MSR 2026".
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API).
- **What it did:** uses the AIDev dataset to compare PRs reviewed only by code review agents with human-only reviews, and hand-scores signal-to-noise in 98 closed agent-only PRs.
- **Key numbers:** "CRA-only PRs achieve a 45.20% merge rate, 23.17 percentage points lower than human-only PRs (68.37%)"; "60.2% of closed CRA-only PRs fall into the 0-30% signal range, and 12 of 13 CRAs exhibit average signal ratios below 60%".
- **Connection:** **supports** treating noise as a first-class cost in the project's score: low-signal review is associated with abandoned PRs. Observational and small (98 PRs) for the signal analysis.
- **Novelty threat:** none.

#### CodeRabbit in the wild: Is Agentic Code Review Helpful? Mining Developers' Feedback to CodeRabbit Reviews in the Wild
- **Citation:** Hong Yi Lin, Mingzhao Liang, Patanamon Thongtanunam and Kla Tantithamthavorn; arXiv preprint; v1 3 Jul 2026, v2 23 Jul 2026; [arXiv 2607.03316](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.03316). Venue not confirmed.
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API).
- **Key numbers:** "31,073 pairs of code reviews and developer feedback from 10,191 pull requests across 239 GitHub repositories"; "36.4% were accepted and 7.3% triggered discussion, while 56.3% were rejected"; rejections mainly "invalid suggestions that were false positives, redundant, or out of scope"; functional comments "were more likely to be invalid"; lightweight models predict rejection at "up to 76% F1".
- **Connection:** **supports** the project's false-alarm and "redundant" categories as the main cost of AI review; also suggests the judge's job (predicting which findings developers would reject) is learnable. Field acceptance is a noisy proxy for validity (see the Beko study below).
- **Novelty threat:** none.

#### Limits of automated evaluation: Understanding the Limits of Automated Evaluation for Code Review Bots in Practice
- **Citation:** Veli Karakaya, Utku Boran Torun, Baykal Mehmet Uçar and Eray Tüzün; arXiv preprint, 27 Apr 2026; [arXiv 2604.24525](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.24525). The arXiv comment says "Accepted to EASE 2026".
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API).
- **What it did:** compares G-Eval and an LLM-as-a-judge pipeline (binary and 0–4 Likert) with engineers' fixed or won't-fix labels on 2,604 industrial bot comments at Beko.
- **Key numbers:** "Agreement ratios range from approximately 0.44 to 0.62, with noticeable variation across models and between binary and Likert-scale formulations" (Gemini-2.5-pro, GPT-4.1-mini, GPT-5.2).
- **Connection:** **challenges** using developer actions (fixed, resolved) as ground truth for precision, and shows that judge agreement depends on judge model and on prompt format (binary against Likert). For the project this argues for planted seeds as the precision anchor for hits, and for fixing the judge's model and format before the search, since changing either moves scores.
- **Novelty threat:** none.

#### Does AI Code Review Lead to Code Changes? A Case Study of GitHub Actions
- **Citation:** Kexin Sun, Hongyu Kuang, Sebastian Baltes, Xin Zhou, He Zhang, Xiaoxing Ma, Guoping Rong, Dong Shao and Christoph Treude; arXiv v1 26 Aug 2025, v2 25 Apr 2026; [arXiv 2508.18771](https://arxiv.org/abs/2508.18771). Venue not confirmed.
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API).
- **Key numbers:** "16 popular AI-based code review actions", "more than 22,000 review comments in 178 repositories"; comments that are "concise, contain code snippets, and are manually triggered, particularly those from hunk-level review tools, are more likely to result in code changes".
- **Connection:** **loosely related**: "addressed rate" as an actionability measure, measured by an LLM-assisted classifier. It suggests finding format (concise, with code) affects uptake, which matters if the project ever reports actionability, not only correctness.
- **Novelty threat:** none.

#### RovoDev Code Reviewer: A Large-Scale Online Evaluation of LLM-based Code Review Automation at Atlassian
- **Citation:** Kla Tantithamthavorn, Yaotian Zou, Andy Wong et al. (11 authors); arXiv v1 3 Jan 2026, v2 20 Jan 2026; [arXiv 2601.01129](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.01129). The arXiv comment says "Accepted at ... ICSE'26, SEIP Track".
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API).
- **Key numbers:** comments "that could lead to code resolution for 38.70%"; "decreasing the PR cycle time by 30.8%"; "reducing the number of human-written comments by 35.6%".
- **Connection:** **loosely related**: a production "code resolution rate" of about 39% is a useful real-world reference for what fraction of AI review comments are acted on; it is in line with CodeRabbit's 36.4% acceptance above.
- **Novelty threat:** none.

#### CIVER: Causal Intervention Verification for Reliable Code Review
- **Citation:** Xinyuan Song, Hasibul Haque, Jay Somasundaram, Ryan Turner, Bowen Zhu and Liang Zhao; arXiv preprint, 26 Oct 2026; [arXiv 2610.36010](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36010). The arXiv comment says "WWW 2026".
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API).
- **What it did:** separates genuine findings from false positives by generating targeted repairs for each finding and checking whether the review responds to the repair but stays stable under unrelated changes.
- **Key numbers:** "correctly identifies 77% of genuine defects through targeted repairs" on SWE-Review-Bench.
- **Connection:** **overlaps** with the judge's real-or-not ruling: an intervention test ("does a fix for this finding change the verdict?") is an alternative or supplement to an LLM judge reading the finding. It could be a cheap evidence check for the appeal agent.
- **Novelty threat:** low.

#### Agentic reviewers in the terminal: Agentic Code Review in the Terminal: A Trajectory-Level Analysis of Behavior, Cost, and Human-Alignment
- **Citation:** Wachiraphan Charoenwet, Kla Tantithamthavorn, Patanamon Thongtanunam, Hong Yi Lin, Minwoo Jeong and Ming Wu; arXiv preprint, 18 Jul 2026; [arXiv 2607.16740](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.16740). Venue not confirmed.
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API); full text partly read (fetch tool: setup, Table 1, cost and threats).
- **What it did:** runs terminal agents (Rovo Dev, **Claude Code 2.1.72 on Claude Sonnet 4.6**, Gemini CLI, CodeRabbit CLI) and static LLM baselines on 362 verified review tasks (AgenticCR-Verified) with developer review comments as ground truth; a comment counts if it is within ±5 lines of a human comment (localised) and an LLM judge rules it aligned.
- **Key numbers:** abstract: "agentic reviewers achieve higher review precision but incur substantial exploration and validation overhead". Table 1 (fetch tool): Claude Code posted 947 comments, 22.39% localised, 14.37% human-aligned, 5.60% both; static Claude Sonnet 4.6 posted 4,823 comments, 7.26% localised, 1.87% both; Claude Sonnet 4.5 posted 9,060 comments. Temperature 0, no repeated runs or intervals; the judge model is not named in the text read.
- **Connection:** **supports** the project's reading of precision as the main gain from agentic review: the same Claude model posts about a fifth as many comments inside Claude Code as when prompted statically, with about three times the overall hit share. It also shows the volume problem (thousands of comments over 362 tasks) that makes ranking and top-k scoring necessary. Ground truth is human comments, not planted bugs.
- **Novelty threat:** none.

#### SWE-PRBench: Benchmarking AI Code Review Quality Against Pull Request Feedback
- **Citation:** Deepak Kumar; arXiv preprint, 27 Mar 2026; [arXiv 2603.26130](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.26130). No venue listed. It appears in prior-work-research.md only inside the Adversarial Review entry, and its ID is not on the list of IDs already cited in the repo.
- **Read:** abstract page read (fetch tool; quotes below are short verbatim fragments).
- **Key numbers:** "350 pull requests with human-annotated ground truth"; judge "validated at kappa=0.75"; "8 frontier models detect only 15-31% of human-flagged issues on the diff-only configuration"; "All 8 models degrade monotonically from config_A to config_C" as context grows; a "structured 2,000-token diff-with-summary prompt" beat a "2,500-token full-context prompt" for all eight; the top four are "statistically indistinguishable (mean score 0.147-0.153)".
- **Connection:** **supports** the `CLAUDE.md` arm's "hurt" hypothesis in a general form: more context made every model worse, attributed by the author to attention dilution. It also shows ties among top models are common at this scale, which is what decision 6 predicts for the council comparisons.
- **Novelty threat:** none.

#### ReviewBench's metric details (covered in seed-sources-research.md; new detail only)
- **Read:** page read (fetch tool), [GitHub blog, 5 Oct 2026](https://github.blog/ai-and-ml/reviewbench-an-open-benchmark-for-ai-code-review/).
- **New detail:** two metric families: "Grounded precision, recall, and F1 score use only the existing gold-set labels", while "Augmented precision, recall and F1 score also evaluate findings that do not match anything in the golden set", with the judge (Claude Sonnet 5) ruling unmatched findings true or false. Users can set β in Fβ. Severity (Critical, Medium, Low) is used for slicing, not weighting (per the fetch tool). The final run covers all 219 PRs "over three rounds", with no intervals reported. In GitHub's online A/B test for Copilot, "addressed rate" is treated as precision.
- **Connection:** **overlaps** with the project's scoring: grounded precision corresponds to hits against seeds only, and augmented precision to the judge's ruling on unplanted findings. Reporting both, as ReviewBench does, separates "found the seeds" from "did not cry wolf". The project's severity weighting would go beyond ReviewBench, which does not weight.

#### 3b. Judge reliability and statistics for comparing reviewers on small item sets

#### The Coin Flip Judge? Reliability and Bias in LLM-as-a-Judge Evaluation
- **Citation:** Abel Yagubyan; arXiv preprint, 23 Apr 2026; [arXiv 2606.13685](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13685). No venue listed.
- **Read:** abstract page read (fetch tool; the tool paraphrased the abstract and gave one verbatim fragment).
- **What it did:** repeats the same judgement 50 times per item (pairwise and pointwise) with GPT-4o-mini and GPT-4.1-mini on 29 tasks.
- **Key numbers:** verbatim: "pairwise preferences flip on average 13.6% of the time". Paraphrased by the fetch tool, not verbatim: significant first-position bias for GPT-4o-mini; cross-judge agreement 76% (κ = 0.51); semantically equivalent prompt templates changed the majority outcome in 25% of tested cases; about 11 trials were needed for a majority vote to match the 50-trial reference with 95% probability (15 for high-variance items).
- **Connection:** **supports** treating the judge as a source of run-to-run noise in its own right, separate from reviewer noise. For the project's CI-based advance rule, judge flips on borderline findings add variance to "variant minus parent"; options are a fixed judge prompt, randomised finding order and several judge samples per contested finding. Small models only and a single author, so the sizes are indicative.
- **Novelty threat:** none.

#### Coding Agents Have Converged: Why the SWE-bench Leaderboard Can No Longer Order Its Top Entries, and What to Measure Instead
- **Citation:** Fengshuo Liu, Ying Liu, Ruize Sun, Lie Luo and Siyuan Guo; arXiv preprint, 15 Sep 2026; [arXiv 2609.17394](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.17394). The arXiv comment says "Accepted at ADMA 2026 ... Special Session on Responsible Data Intelligence".
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API).
- **What it did:** audits 254 SWE-bench submissions using their published per-instance verdicts, without rerunning anything.
- **Key numbers:** "the leading two entries each resolve 396 of 500 instances"; "The top ten share 285 successes and 51 failures, leaving 164 instances that distinguish their outcomes"; "within-model scaffold ranges reach 29.8 percentage points, compared with the 8.8-point spread of the top thirty"; "Exact paired McNemar tests separate none of the 29 adjacent Verified top-thirty pairs at alpha=0.05, while the larger Test split separates 14 of 23"; "non-rejection does not establish equivalence".
- **Connection:** **supports** decision 6's paired, discordant-items analysis with a large real example: only the items on which systems disagree carry information, and at 500 items adjacent leaders cannot be separated. Also relevant to the scripted-versus-agent-led arm: scaffold choice moved scores more (29.8 points) than the spread among the top thirty systems.
- **Novelty threat:** none.

#### AgentAssay: Token-Efficient Regression Testing for Non-Deterministic AI Agent Workflows
- **Citation:** Varun Pratap Bhardwaj; technical report on arXiv, 3 Mar 2026; [arXiv 2603.02601](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.02601); Zenodo DOI 10.5281/zenodo.18842011. No peer-reviewed venue.
- **Read:** abstract page read (fetch tool; short verbatim fragments).
- **Key numbers:** "stochastic three-valued verdicts" "(PASS/FAIL/INCONCLUSIVE) grounded in hypothesis testing"; SPRT (a sequential test) "reduces trials by 78%"; "7,605 trials" over five models including Claude Sonnet 4.6.
- **Connection:** **loosely related, possibly useful**: the three-valued verdict with sequential testing maps onto the project's advance rule. A generation could end "advance", "reject" or "inconclusive, run more seeds or repeats", stopping early when the evidence is already clear. Decision 6 considered adaptive resampling (Cantú-Paz) but not a sequential probability ratio test. Single-author, unreviewed, so the savings figures need checking.
- **Novelty threat:** none.

### 2.8 Appeal and re-check steps

Already covered and not repeated: Refute-or-Promote (2604.19049), Evident (2606.15122), HalluJudge, Du et al. (2601.18844), ZeroFalse, QASecClaw, RepoAudit, PoLL, D3, the Meta-Judges paper (2504.17087), SE-Jury and OpenCodeReview. Each item says whether its second step **can reinstate** items the first step dismissed (like the project's appeal) or **only removes** them.

#### TinyV: Reducing False Negatives in Verification Improves RL for LLM Reasoning
- **Citation:** Zhangchen Xu, Yuetai Li, Fengqing Jiang et al. (7 authors); arXiv preprint (no venue on the arXiv page); v1 20 May 2025, v2 22 May 2025; https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14625; code https://github.com/uw-nsl/TinyV
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API).
- **What it did:** Measured how often a rule-based answer checker wrongly rejects correct maths answers in RL training data, and added a small LLM verifier that re-examines rejected answers and recovers the valid ones.
- **Key numbers:** "over 38% of model-generated responses suffer from false negatives, where the verifier fails to recognize correct answers"; adding TinyV "boosts pass rates by up to 10% and accelerates convergence".
- **Reinstates or removes?** **Reinstates.** The second checker only looks at items the first rejected and can overturn the rejection, which is the same shape as the project's appeal (with the first checker a rule, not an LLM judge).
- **Connection:** **supports** the appeal's premise with a clean number: a first-stage checker's false-negative rate can be large, and a cheap second look at the rejected set alone recovers much of it. Maths answers, not code findings.
- **Novelty threat:** low; the pattern exists but not for review findings or LLM judges.

#### Klieber et al.: Using LLMs to Adjudicate Static-Analysis Alerts with Error Reduction Techniques
- **Citation:** William Klieber, David Svoboda, Lori Flynn and Ruben Martins; arXiv preprint; v1 10 Jul 2026; https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.09979
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API); full text partly read (fetch tool; first 100k of 134k characters: method, results tables and trigger study).
- **What it did:** LLMs classify static-analysis alerts as real or false on Juliet, FormAI and SV-COMP, with two mistake-mitigation steps: a consistency check (run several times; if no verdict reaches a threshold, return "uncertain") and an LLM reasoning evaluation that reads all runs' reasoning when they disagree and picks a verdict. Also asked the LLM to write a program that triggers each flaw at runtime.
- **Key numbers:** abstract: with mitigation, mid-tier reasoning models "reach at least 98% recall and at least 94.8% specificity on every suite". Fetch-tool quotes: an uncertain verdict is "leaving the alert for manual attention" and "Every true, dependent, or uncertain alert is returned for human attention"; CC raised gpt-oss-20b recall on FormAI from 96.9% to 99.6% (specificity 93.6% → 92.2%), and LRE+CC brought specificity back to 97.0%; on Juliet LRE+CC lowered recall for o4-mini (99.0% → 97.0%). Trigger drivers: 18 of 30 true positives confirmed and 0 of 27 false alarms (the validity check rejected all 9 drivers aimed at false alarms), so "a valid trigger is therefore strong evidence of a real flaw" but an absent one is weak evidence of a false alarm. Juliet memorisation was found (o4-mini could reconstruct test identities).
- **Reinstates or removes?** **Neither, strictly:** it protects recall before dismissal rather than after. Disagreement among runs blocks dismissal (the alert goes to a human), and LRE can side with a minority. It never revisits an alert once dismissed.
- **Connection:** **supports** two cheap design options for the judge: treat inconsistent judge verdicts as "keep" rather than "dismiss", and use an executable trigger as one-way evidence (a passing trigger confirms a finding; no trigger proves nothing). Static-analysis alerts on synthetic suites, not review findings.
- **Novelty threat:** low.

#### VulnSAGE: Adjudicating SAST Reports by Connecting LLM Reasoning to Program Structure
- **Citation:** Jinbao Chen, Zixiang Zhou, Yu Zhang and Yihong Wu; arXiv preprint; v1 16 Oct 2026; https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23035
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API).
- **What it did:** An LLM adjudicator for static-analysis reports whose interpretations are recorded as facts citing program relations; program analysis checks the premises and the verdict follows only from facts whose premises hold. Positives then go to proof-of-concept generation.
- **Key numbers:** "Across 187 labeled reports from four corpora covering five vulnerability families, VulnSAGE rejects 89 of 97 false alarms and retains 85 of 90 real defects"; 25 of its positive verdicts reproduce at runtime against 3 for LLM4SA; on 45,440 alerts from the 200 most-starred C/C++ projects it "reproduces 104 defects at runtime"; "Maintainers have confirmed 24 of the 26 defects they have responded to".
- **Reinstates or removes?** **Removes only** (a filter), but with the dismissal grounded in checked premises, so 5 of 90 real defects were lost (about 5.6%).
- **Connection:** **supports** requiring a judge's dismissal to cite something checkable; an appeal could target dismissals whose stated premise fails a check. Same family of evidence as Evident.
- **Novelty threat:** none.

#### Multi-Agent Screening: What Agreement and Acceptance Cannot Tell You
- **Citation:** Benjamin Chang and Dell Zhang; arXiv preprint; v1 9 Nov 2026; https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12705
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API).
- **What it did:** A theory paper on what can be learnt about the errors of AI agents that screen items for a costly assessor (agentic search, e-discovery, AI paper reviewing), when only passed items get judged.
- **Key numbers:** "Agreement shows that agents concur, not that they are right, however many there are. Acceptance by the assessor gives precision but only a lower bound on recall"; "recall can be pinned down only by judging items they did not pass: a random sample of these estimates how many relevant items were missed ... and the sample needed does not grow with the number of agents".
- **Reinstates or removes?** Not a pipeline; it says how to **measure** what a removal step loses.
- **Connection:** **supports** a measurement the project should add outside seeded runs. With planted seeds the key gives recall directly, but on real code (or for the judge's dismissals of non-seed findings) the only unbiased way to know what the judge threw away is to label a random sample of dismissed findings. That sample is also the natural test set for the appeal agent.
- **Novelty threat:** none.

#### Harnessing Disagreement: Detecting Correlated Agreement Blindness in Multi-Agent Triage
- **Citation:** Shay Seiya McDonnell, Avantika Singh, Quoc-Viet Pham, Vratislav Havlik and Gregory M. P. O'Hare; PAAMS 2026, LNAI vol. 17174, Springer, pp. 266–278 (per the arXiv journal reference; the arXiv text is the pre-review version); v1 22 Jul 2026; https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.19899
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API).
- **What it did:** Studied escalation triggered by disagreement between base classifiers (random forest and k-NN agents plus a meta-model) on network intrusion alarms, and showed the blind spot when agents agree and are wrong.
- **Key numbers:** "57.2% of errors occur under agreement and 90.6% of dangerous under-predictions evade disagreement-based monitoring even after conservative override"; "strengthening base learners increases error correlation while reducing disagreement"; under-prediction cut "from 4.80% to 1.70%" by a conservative override and a safety-flag gate.
- **Reinstates or removes?** **Reinstates in effect:** the conservative override raises predictions the ensemble had set too low, but only where a gate fires.
- **Connection:** **challenges** an appeal triggered only by seat disagreement. As seats get stronger and more alike, the misses that matter happen when everyone agrees. The project's appeal re-examines all judge dismissals, which avoids this blind spot; if it is ever narrowed to "disputed" dismissals to save tokens, this is the risk. Not LLMs (classical learners), so the strength-correlation link is indirect.
- **Novelty threat:** none.

#### Trust or Escalate: LLM Judges with Provable Guarantees for Human Agreement
- **Citation:** Jaehun Jung, Faeze Brahman and Yejin Choi; arXiv v1 25 Jul 2024; https://arxiv.org/abs/2407.18370. ICLR 2025 is widely reported, but the arXiv page shows no venue and it was not checked elsewhere: **venue not confirmed**.
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API).
- **What it did:** Selective evaluation: a judge's verdict is used only when its estimated confidence is high enough to guarantee a chosen human-agreement level; otherwise the item is escalated, first to a stronger judge model ("Cascaded Selective Evaluation"), then to humans.
- **Key numbers:** "on a subset of Chatbot Arena where GPT-4 almost never achieves 80% human agreement, our method, even while employing substantially cost-effective models such as Mistral-7B, guarantees over 80% human agreement with almost 80% test coverage".
- **Reinstates or removes?** **Escalates before deciding**, so neither: low-confidence verdicts are never final. It does not revisit confident verdicts.
- **Connection:** **overlaps** with an escalation design for the judge: send only low-confidence dismissals to the appeal (cheaper), with a guarantee tied to a calibration set. The confidence-only trigger has the agreement-blindness risk above.
- **Novelty threat:** none.

#### Reviewing the Reviewer: Graph-Enhanced LLMs for E-commerce Appeal Adjudication
- **Citation:** Yuchen Du, Ashley Li and Zixi Huang; KDD 2026 Applied Data Science Track (per the arXiv comment); v1 27 Feb 2026; https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.19267
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API). (prior-work-research.md lists this as snippet only; the abstract is now read.)
- **What it did:** Learns from maker-checker review, where a second-tier checker corrects first-tier decisions, for seller appeals on an e-commerce platform; builds graphs of past maker-checker disagreements and adds a "Request More Information" outcome when evidence is insufficient.
- **Key numbers:** "a standard LLM-only baseline achieves only 70.8% alignment with human experts, incorporating action modeling with RMI improves alignment to 87.5%. Augmenting this with the retrieval-based knowledge graph yields the best offline performance of 95.8%"; "96.3% alignment rate in production".
- **Reinstates or removes?** **Can reinstate:** a seller appeal can overturn a first-tier decision, and the checker corrects makers in either direction.
- **Connection:** **overlaps** with the appeal tier as a production two-tier review, and adds a third outcome ("ask for more information") that the project's appeal lacks. Not code; an industrial deployment with human checkers as ground truth.
- **Novelty threat:** low.

#### Figalová et al.: Evaluating human and LLM screening workflows in a conceptually complex scoping review: Recall--workload trade-offs and run-to-run consistency
- **Citation:** Nikol Figalová, Lynn Huestegge and Anne Böckler-Raettig; arXiv preprint (preregistered study); v1 27 Aug 2026; https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26885
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API).
- **What it did:** Compared human and LLM title-and-abstract screening (seven LLM runs, models and configurations varied, including a repeat) on 1,131 records against 316 verified eligible ones.
- **Key numbers:** "No workflow recovered all verified eligible records"; humans and two GPT-5.4 runs retained 42.2–45.0% of records at 82.3–82.9% recall; "Two nominally identical GPT-5.4 file-batch runs agreed on 91.7% of records but differed on 94 records, including 29 verified eligible records retained by only one run"; "LLMs are better suited to validated, auditable, human-supervised workflows than autonomous exclusion".
- **Reinstates or removes?** The paper compares exclusion workflows; it shows the **case for** a reinstating step (a second run would have kept 29 eligible records the first excluded).
- **Connection:** **supports** the appeal from outside software: run-to-run variation alone means one exclusion pass loses real items that a second independent pass keeps. Also a reminder that a repeat of the same judge is itself a cheap appeal baseline the project could include.
- **Novelty threat:** none.

#### Kodus: We ran 20 experiments to improve AI code review recall (vendor blog)
- **Citation:** Gabriel Malinosqui, Kodus; blog post, 18 Aug 2026; https://kodus.io/en/ai-code-review-recall/ (vendor, not peer reviewed).
- **Read:** page read (fetch tool).
- **What it did:** About 20 experiments on a diff → finder → verification → anchoring/severity-filter pipeline, against a golden set of "136 labeled bugs from five real repositories".
- **Key numbers (fetch-tool quotes):** in one run verification "discarded seven serious findings, including an SSRF, a race condition, a FIPS bypass"; in a voting experiment true positives fell "from 83 to 52 after verification" while false positives fell "from 294 to 159"; changing the verifier so a finding is discarded only with "a concrete reason to refute it" roughly doubled delivered recall in that comparison; baseline 72 of 136 (about 53%) with 170 false positives against "Heavy" 84 of 136 (about 62%) with 328 false positives; Gemini Flash about $0.29 per PR.
- **Reinstates or removes?** **Removes only**; no re-check of dropped findings is described. The fix was changing the burden of proof at the verifier.
- **Connection:** **supports** the appeal's motivation with practitioner data from a code-review product: a verification stage cut true positives by 37% (83 → 52). The project's appeal is one answer; Kodus's alternative ("dismiss only with a concrete refutation") is a cheaper judge-brief change the project could test as a rival arm.
- **Novelty threat:** none (vendor practice note).

#### Unblocked: Recall first: how we built Unblocked's Deep code review (vendor blog)
- **Citation:** Kaihao Zhao, Unblocked; blog post, 9 Nov 2026; https://getunblocked.com/blog/recall-first-deep-code-review/ (vendor, not peer reviewed).
- **Read:** page read (fetch tool).
- **What it did:** Describes a production review pipeline: understand the change, parallel reviewer agents (one security-focused) in a sandbox, a per-file coverage check, a separate judge that verifies each candidate against the code, then merging by root cause.
- **Key numbers (fetch-tool quotes):** on Martian's Code Review Bench (50 PRs) it "caught 108 of 158 known bugs" (self-run); removing the coverage check "cost about 15 points of recall", recovered by "a single correction turn"; "Every filter removed real bugs along with the comments people didn't want".
- **Reinstates or removes?** **Removes only**, but dropping "requires an explicit reason". Not stated whether dropped findings are ever reconsidered.
- **Connection:** **overlaps** strongly with the council design (parallel role reviewers plus a verifying judge) in industry, and supports requiring reasons for dismissals so an appeal has something to examine. Vendor self-report on a benchmark it did not build.
- **Novelty threat:** low for the research question (no controlled comparison), but it shows the council-plus-judge shape is already shipped.

#### Detection Without Correction: A Two-Parameter Decomposition of Multi-Stage LLM Pipelines
- **Citation:** Prashanti Nilayam, Kiran Ramanna and Prashil Tumbade; arXiv preprint; v1 26 May 2026; https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.27559
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API).
- **What it did:** Splits a downstream agent's response to upstream content (in debate, self-correction or verification) into detection (whether it treats the upstream content as authoritative) and conditional generation (what it produces if not); fits this on a nine-cell grid over four model families and four maths and science benchmarks.
- **Key numbers:** "the conditional miscorrection rate is consistently dominant (53-94% across cohorts) while detection rate varies contextually by more than an order of magnitude".
- **Reinstates or removes?** Not a pipeline; a model of why second stages fail.
- **Connection:** **challenges** an appeal agent in a specific way: even when a later stage detects that the earlier one is wrong, it often replaces it with another wrong answer. For the appeal this suggests measuring two rates separately: how often it flags a wrong dismissal, and how often its reinstated finding (and severity) is then right.
- **Novelty threat:** none.

#### Sifting the Noise: A Comparative Study of LLM Agents in Vulnerability False Positive Filtering
- **Citation:** Yunpeng Xiong and Ting Zhang; to appear at ISSTA 2026 (per the arXiv comment); v1 30 Jan 2026, v3 23 Jul 2026; https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.22952
- **Read:** abstract page read (fetch tool; the tool summarised rather than quoted the abstract); full text partly read (fetch tool; first 100k of 136k characters, results tables).
- **What it did:** Compared three agent frameworks (Aider, OpenHands, SWE-agent) and vanilla prompting, with Claude Sonnet 4, DeepSeek Chat and GPT-5 backbones, for filtering false positives from SAST output on the OWASP Benchmark, Vul4J projects with CodeQL alerts and a post-cutoff C/C++ OSS-Fuzz study.
- **Key numbers (fetch-tool quotes):** abstract: "reducing an initial FP detection rate of over 92% on the OWASP Benchmark to as low as 6.3% in the best configuration". For Claude Sonnet 4 with SWE-agent on OWASP, 314 of 1,411 completed runs on vulnerable cases were labelled false positive (77.7% of real vulnerabilities kept, 22.25% suppressed). Miss rates were strongly CWE-dependent: 0.41% for CWE-79 but 77.17% (CWE-327), 84.50% (CWE-328) and 77.11% (CWE-501). Vul4J mis-identified-as-FP rates ranged from 0.0% to 25.0% across agent-model pairs.
- **Reinstates or removes?** **Removes only**; no recovery step. The authors advise against "unconditional, automatic suppression" and list human-in-the-loop auditing as future work.
- **Connection:** **supports** the appeal with the strongest numbers found tonight for LLM filters dropping real bugs: a filter that removes most false alarms also removed about a fifth of real vulnerabilities, concentrated in particular bug classes. It suggests the appeal (and the judge's score) be reported per bug class, since the losses are not uniform.
- **Novelty threat:** none.

#### Verification Blindness: Execution-Grounded Measurement of Verification Blindness in Multi-Agent LLM Systems
- **Citation:** Atharv Santosh, Rohan Sashank Babbellapati and Phil Mui; NeurIPS 2026 workshop poster ("AI for Verifiable Coding" workshop, per the NeurIPS virtual page); OpenReview https://openreview.net/forum?id=S4OY486OKb; page https://neurips.cc/virtual/2026/171478; no arXiv link found.
- **Read:** page read (fetch tool; the tool paraphrased the abstract, so only the quoted figures below are verbatim).
- **What it did:** DEFECTBED, an execution-grounded benchmark of LLM-generated code with machine-checkable faults graded by how visible the fault is in the text, plus matched clean controls; 14,713 episodes over eight verifier configurations.
- **Key numbers:** detection Youden's J ≥ 0.90 for visible faults (tiers T0–T2); localisation falls from 0.764 at T0 to 0.218 at T2; on clean controls verifiers "report defects on 59.3% of correct artifacts" at the cross-artifact tier.
- **Reinstates or removes?** Not a pipeline; measures verifier agents.
- **Connection:** **supports** using matched clean controls (unseeded code) in the project's scoring, so that a seat or appeal that flags everything is not credited for sensitivity alone. The 59.3% false-alarm rate on correct code is the cost side an over-eager appeal would add.
- **Novelty threat:** low.

#### VulnHunter (Capital One, open-source Claude Code skill)
- **Citation:** Capital One Tech, "Announcing VulnHunter", 16 Jul 2026; https://capitalone.com/tech/open-source/announcing-vulnhunter; code reported at github.com/capitalone/vulnhunter (not opened).
- **Read:** page read (fetch tool).
- **What it did:** An agentic security-review workflow that, after surfacing a finding, "runs a structured reasoning workflow specifically designed to disprove its own argument" and is "designed to immediately discard findings that rely on unsupported assumptions". It requires "access to Claude Opus 4.8 and access to a working Claude Code environment".
- **Key numbers:** none published on the page.
- **Reinstates or removes?** **Removes only**, as described.
- **Connection:** **overlaps** with the project's subject (a Claude Code review workflow with a refutation stage) and is a same-family case: Claude finds and Claude disproves. Its one-way design has the recall risk the appeal targets. No evaluation to compare against.
- **Novelty threat:** none (no study).

#### Looping Is Not Reliability: State-Bound Evidence and Typed Revision Contracts for Agentic Code Repair
- **Citation:** Xueping Gao, Jianwei Yang and Qiang Yang; AgenticDev 2026 (per the arXiv comment); v1 27 Jul 2026, v3 4 Oct 2026; https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.24604
- **Read:** abstract page read (fetch tool; summarised, not verbatim).
- **What it did:** Multi-seed study of generate-test-revise loops on HumanEval repairs and 24 repository bugs.
- **Key numbers:** none verbatim; the fetch summary says a correct patch can be lost or harmed by later revisions, stale verifier traces can mislead a model that started correct and repository-level differences were not significant after correction.
- **Reinstates or removes?** Not applicable; about repeated revision rounds.
- **Connection:** **loosely related.** More re-check rounds can undo correct states; supports keeping the appeal a single bounded step that cannot itself dismiss items the judge kept.
- **Novelty threat:** none.

#### AgentReview: Exploring Peer Review Dynamics with LLM Agents
- **Citation:** Yiqiao Jin, Qinlin Zhao, Yiyang Wang et al. (7 authors); EMNLP 2024 Main (Oral) (per the arXiv comment); v1 18 Jun 2024, v3 10 May 2026; https://arxiv.org/abs/2406.12708
- **Read:** abstract page read (fetch tool).
- **What it did:** Simulates the full peer-review pipeline with LLM reviewers, authors (rebuttal) and an area chair, varying reviewer and chair traits.
- **Key numbers:** "a notable 37.1% variation in paper decisions due to reviewers' biases". The rebuttal result seen in a search summary ("less significant effect") was not on the abstract page and is not reported here.
- **Reinstates or removes?** The rebuttal-plus-area-chair stage can overturn reviewer recommendations in either direction (a human-process analogue of an appeal), but the abstract gives no figure for it.
- **Connection:** **loosely related** human-process analogue for the appeal and meta-review tiers. Papers, not code.
- **Novelty threat:** none.

---

#### Also noted (abstract read, not summarised further)

- **CR-Bench-v2** (https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02566; Pereira, Sinha, Ghosh and Dutta; v1 2 Nov 2026; abstract read, arXiv API). The abstract gives 584 instances (174 Verified), "Even the strongest systems detect only about one-third of known defects (KDDR ≈ 33%), and Gemini Code Assist detects 8.1%". prior-work-research.md covers CR-Bench under another ID; worth checking whether 2611.02566 is a new version or a separate paper.
- **SecPRBench: Can Agentic Code Review Catch Vulnerabilities Before They Are Merged?** (https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33509; Yang et al.; v2 30 Oct 2026; abstract read, arXiv API). 265 real bug-introducing commits; recall 32.5–78.1% across eight frontier models; "in 68% of them, the agent reports on a file that holds the flaw but argues for a different mechanism"; "Anthropic's Defending Code harness raises it by 7.7 points". Relevant to seeds and to how the judge matches findings to seeds (near misses), not to this agent's topics.
- **CIVER: Causal Intervention Verification for Reliable Code Review** (https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36010; WWW 2026 per arXiv comment; abstract read, arXiv API). A verifier that tests code-review findings by generating repair perturbations; "correctly identifies 77% of genuine defects". A one-way filter on review findings.
- **Strategic Heterogeneous Multi-Agent Architecture for Cost-Effective Code Vulnerability Detection** (https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.21282; AAMAS 2026 SE workshop per arXiv comment; abstract read, arXiv API). Three DeepSeek-V3 experts plus a local Qwen3-8B adversarial verifier on 262 Juliet samples: "77.2% F1 score with 62.9% precision and 100% recall"; the verifier adds "+10.3 percentage points" precision. A one-way filter.
- **AEGIS** (https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.20637; abstract read, arXiv API). Verifier agent argues for and against exploitability within sliced evidence and an "independent Audit Agent ... exercising veto power"; one-way.
- **Memoir** (https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.09181) and **SastBench** (https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.02941): abstracts read (arXiv API); SAST false-positive filtering and a triage benchmark, both one-way.
- **The Wisdom of Deliberating AI Crowds: Does Deliberation Improve LLM-Based Forecasting?** (https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.22625; Schneider and Schramm; abstract read, arXiv API). GPT-5, Claude Sonnet 4.5 and Gemini Pro 2.5 on 202 Metaculus questions: deliberation helped diverse models with shared information ("reducing Log Loss by 0.020 or about 4 percent", p = 0.017) but "when homogeneous groups (three instances of the same model) engaged in the same process, no benefit was observed". Independently replicates the clone result of Barrera-Lemarchand et al.
- **Knowledge Divergence and the Value of Debate for Scalable Oversight** (https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.05293; Robin Young; abstract read, arXiv API). Theory: "When models share identical training corpora, debate reduces to RLAIF-like where a single-agent method recovers the same optimum". Formal support for "debate among copies adds nothing".
- **Multi-LLM / homogenisation and monoculture background, abstracts read (arXiv API) but loosely related:** Epistemic diversity across language models mitigates knowledge collapse (https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.15011); When LLMs Agree, Are They Right? (https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08065; agreement ≥0.8 on 77% of GPQA entries for the most consistent frontier model, "48% of those wrong"); RoPoLL (https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.30931; robust aggregation for judge panels); Decomposing Wrong-Consensus Agreement (https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18795); Sixteen models, fewer than two voices (https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.00285); When More Sampling Hurts (https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.28661); Choi et al., An Empirical Study of Group Conformity in Multi-Agent Systems (Findings of ACL 2025 per arXiv journal ref; https://arxiv.org/abs/2506.01332); Demystifying Multi-Agent Debate (https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.19921); a three-stage escalation cascade for LLM guard models (https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.19011), whose most expensive stage recovers the out-of-distribution failures (title not quoted here because it trips the repo's hardware-term privacy pattern); Large Language Models in the Abuse Detection Pipeline (https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.00323; a survey with a "Review & Appeals" stage, no results).

### 2.9 Scripted workflows against agent-led orchestration

#### Anthropic guidance and Claude Code documentation (primary sources for the scripted-vs-agent-led arm)

#### BEA: Building effective agents (Anthropic engineering blog)
- **Citation:** Erik S. and Barry Zhang ("Written by Erik S. and Barry Zhang"); Anthropic engineering blog; published 19 Dec 2024 (page now carries a note that "Much of the tooling landscape described in this post has changed since December 2024"); https://www.anthropic.com/engineering/building-effective-agents
- **Read:** full text read (downloaded with curl and stripped to text; WebFetch refused the domain).
- **What it did:** defines the workflow/agent split and five workflow patterns (prompt chaining, routing, parallelisation, orchestrator-workers, evaluator-optimiser) plus autonomous agents, with advice on when to use each.
- **Key numbers / quotes (verbatim):**
  - "Workflows are systems where LLMs and tools are orchestrated through predefined code paths. Agents, on the other hand, are systems where LLMs dynamically direct their own processes and tool usage, maintaining control over how they accomplish tasks."
  - "Agentic systems often trade latency and cost for better task performance, and you should consider when this tradeoff makes sense."
  - "workflows offer predictability and consistency for well-defined tasks, whereas agents are the better option when flexibility and model-driven decision-making are needed at scale."
  - Under parallelisation/voting, the post's own example is "Reviewing a piece of code for vulnerabilities, where several different prompts review and flag the code if they find a problem."
  - Orchestrator-workers is classed as a *workflow*: "a central LLM dynamically breaks down tasks, delegates them to worker LLMs, and synthesizes their results ... subtasks aren't pre-defined, but determined by the orchestrator based on the specific input."
  - "The autonomous nature of agents means higher costs, and the potential for compounding errors."
  - "you should consider adding complexity only when it demonstrably improves outcomes."
  - No measured numbers; it is practitioner guidance.
- **Connection:** supports the arm's framing directly. A review council with a fixed role set is the "parallelisation: voting" workflow the post names for vulnerability review; an LLM lead that decides how many reviewers to spawn is "orchestrator-workers". The post predicts the trade the arm measures (predictability and lower cost against flexibility) but offers no data, so the arm would supply the first measured comparison for review that this sweep found.
- **Novelty threat:** none (guidance, no experiment).

#### MARS: How we built our multi-agent research system (Anthropic engineering blog)
- **Citation:** Jeremy Hadfield, Barry Zhang, Kenneth Lien, Florian Scholz, Jeremy Fox and Daniel Ford; Anthropic engineering blog; published 13 Jun 2025; https://www.anthropic.com/engineering/multi-agent-research-system
- **Read:** full text read (curl, stripped to text).
- **What it did:** describes the orchestrator-worker Research feature (lead agent spawns subagents, a CitationAgent at the end), its prompt-engineering lessons, LLM-judge evaluation and production reliability problems.
- **Key numbers (verbatim):**
  - "a multi-agent system with Claude Opus 4 as the lead agent and Claude Sonnet 4 subagents outperformed single-agent Claude Opus 4 by 90.2% on our internal research eval."
  - "three factors explained 95% of the performance variance in the BrowseComp evaluation ... token usage by itself explains 80% of the variance, with the number of tool calls and the model choice as the two other explanatory factors."
  - "agents typically use about 4× more tokens than chat interactions, and multi-agent systems use about 15× more tokens than chats."
  - Orchestrator failure modes: "Early agents made errors like spawning 50 subagents for simple queries, scouring the web endlessly for nonexistent sources, and distracting each other with excessive updates." Vague delegation meant "subagents misinterpreted the task or performed the exact same searches as other agents"; "Agents struggle to judge appropriate effort for different tasks, so we embedded scaling rules in the prompts" (1 agent with 3–10 tool calls for simple queries, 2–4 subagents for comparisons, "more than 10 subagents" for complex research).
  - "most coding tasks involve fewer truly parallelizable tasks than research, and LLM agents are not yet great at coordinating and delegating to other agents in real time."
  - Evaluation: "a single LLM call with a single prompt outputting scores from 0.0-1.0 and a pass-fail grade was the most consistent and aligned with human judgements" (they tried multiple judges per component). Started with "a set of about 20 queries"; "A prompt tweak might boost success rates from 30% to 80%."
  - Reliability: "Agents make dynamic decisions and are non-deterministic between runs, even with identical prompts. This makes debugging harder." "The compound nature of errors in agentic systems means that minor issues for traditional software can derail agents entirely." Lead agents "execute subagents synchronously".
  - Tool-description self-improvement: a tool-testing agent rewrote tool descriptions, giving "a 40% decrease in task completion time".
- **Connection:** strongly relevant to three parts of the project. (1) The "token usage explains 80% of the variance" finding is the strongest argument for the project's equal-cost comparisons: a council that wins only by spending more tokens has not shown that councils help. (2) The listed orchestrator errors (over-spawning, duplicated work, misjudged effort) are exactly the failure modes the agent-led arm should log, and they suggest the agent-led orchestrator needs an effort rule to be a fair arm. (3) The single-judge-call finding is a practitioner data point against multi-judge set-ups, relevant to cross-family judge arms. The 90.2% is a single internal eval with no CI and no cost-matched baseline.
- **Novelty threat:** none (practitioner report, different task).

#### CC-WF: Orchestrate subagents at scale with dynamic workflows (Claude Code docs)
- **Citation:** Anthropic, Claude Code documentation, https://code.claude.com/docs/en/workflows (markdown copy at /docs/en/workflows.md); undated page, retrieved 17 Nov 2026; mentions versions up to v2.1.319.
- **Read:** page read (downloaded markdown with curl; sections "When to use a workflow", "How a workflow runs", "Behavior and limits", "Cost", "Set a size guideline", "Resume after a pause" read in full).
- **What it did:** documents dynamic workflows: a JavaScript script Claude writes that a runtime executes, orchestrating many subagents, resumable, with fan-out prompt caching.
- **Key quotes (verbatim):**
  - Table "who decides what runs next": subagents "Claude, turn by turn"; agent teams "The lead agent, turn by turn"; workflows "The script". "Where intermediate results live": workflows "Script variables". "What's repeatable": workflows "The orchestration itself". Scale: workflows "Dozens to hundreds of agents per run".
  - "A workflow moves the plan into code. With subagents, skills, and agent teams, Claude is the orchestrator: it decides turn by turn what to spawn or assign next, and every result goes into a context window."
  - "it can have independent agents adversarially review each other's findings before they're reported".
  - Determinism for resume: "Claude Code makes `Date.now()`, `Math.random()`, and a no-argument `new Date()` throw inside the script, so that a relaunched run repeats the same `agent()` calls."
  - Cost: "a single run can use meaningfully more tokens than working through the same task in conversation." A `Large workflow` warning appears when "a workflow schedules more than 25 agents, or its projected token total passes 1.5 million"; the warning "is advisory". Caps: "Up to 16 concurrent agents by default", "1,000 agents total per run".
  - Size guideline is "advice, not a cap": small "Fewer than 5 agents", medium "Fewer than 10", large "Fewer than 50"; default medium (small on Pro from v2.1.271).
  - Prompt caching: agents with the same "model, effort level, agent type, tools, output schema, and working directory" share a cache prefix; matching agents are held up to `CLAUDE_CODE_WORKFLOW_PREFIX_STAGGER_MS` (5000 ms default) so later ones read the first agent's cache.
  - Ultracode: "each request uses more tokens and takes longer than the same request without a workflow."
  - Verification honesty: when verifiers cannot check a claim "after a rate limit or API error, the report lists that claim as unverified instead of counting it as refuted."
- **Connection:** this is the platform the scripted arm runs on, so these are design facts the method should record: (a) the script, not Claude, fixes the council's size and order, which removes orchestrator variance but makes the script itself a confound (Claude writes it unless the researcher writes it); (b) the shared-prefix cache means a workflow council with identical reviewer settings is cheaper per reviewer than a heterogeneous one, so cost comparisons between specialist and generalist councils must account for caching (the page says the shared part is the "tools-and-system-prompt prefix", so whether role briefs break sharing depends on whether they sit in a custom agent type's system prompt or in the task prompt; this inference is not stated on the page and should be checked from token logs); (c) the size guideline silently steers how many agents a Claude-written script spawns; (d) "unverified" vs "refuted" is a useful precedent for how the judge should treat an appeal that errors out.
- **Novelty threat:** none.

#### CC-TEAMS and CC-SUB: Agent teams and custom subagents pages; CC-COSTS (Claude Code docs)
- **Citation:** Anthropic, Claude Code documentation: https://code.claude.com/docs/en/agent-teams , https://code.claude.com/docs/en/sub-agents , https://code.claude.com/docs/en/agents , https://code.claude.com/docs/en/costs ; retrieved 17 Nov 2026.
- **Read:** page read (markdown via curl; agent-teams "When to use", "Compare with subagents", "Token usage", "Run a parallel code review", "Limitations"; sub-agents "what loads at startup" and frontmatter table; costs "Agent team token costs").
- **Key quotes (verbatim):**
  - Agent teams are "experimental and disabled by default". "Agent teams add coordination overhead and use significantly more tokens than a single session. ... For sequential tasks, same-file edits, or work with many dependencies, a single session or subagents are more effective."
  - Costs page: "Agent teams use approximately 7x more tokens than standard sessions when teammates run in plan mode". "Token costs scale linearly"; "Start with 3-5 teammates for most workflows."
  - The docs' own review example: "A single reviewer tends to gravitate toward one type of issue at a time. Splitting review criteria into independent domains means security, performance, and test coverage all get thorough attention simultaneously." (No evidence is given for this.)
  - Limitations include "Task status can lag: teammates sometimes fail to mark tasks as completed, which blocks dependent tasks" and no session resumption for in-process teammates.
  - **CLAUDE.md reaches subagents by default:** "Explore and Plan start without your CLAUDE.md files ... Other built-in and custom subagents load both, unless you set `omitClaudeMd`" (requires v2.1.271+). Even with `omitClaudeMd`, "a subdirectory's `CLAUDE.md` and matching path-scoped rules still load when the subagent works with files there", and "CLAUDE.md files and project memory still load through the normal message flow, even when the agent's definition sets `omitClaudeMd`" (the second sentence is about a custom agent's system prompt; the two passages are worth testing rather than trusting). Teammates "load CLAUDE.md, MCP servers, and skills automatically".
- **Connection:** (1) The specialist-reviewer claim ("a single reviewer tends to gravitate toward one type of issue") is an untested vendor assertion that the generalist-vs-specialist arm tests. (2) The ~7x team token multiplier and the linear scaling inform equal-cost budgets. (3) The CLAUDE.md loading rules matter for the CLAUDE.md arm: the "without CLAUDE.md" condition must set `omitClaudeMd` *and* keep reviewed files out of directories with nested CLAUDE.md files, or the condition leaks. The method should verify with `/context` or a canary string that the no-CLAUDE.md reviewers really did not see it.
- **Novelty threat:** none.

#### CC-MEM and CC-CRC: How Claude remembers your project; Customize Code Review (Claude Code docs)
- **Citation:** Anthropic, https://code.claude.com/docs/en/memory and https://code.claude.com/docs/en/code-review-customize ; retrieved 17 Nov 2026.
- **Read:** page read (markdown via curl; memory sections on CLAUDE.md writing, size, consistency, audit; code-review-customize "Use CLAUDE.md for project-wide rules" and the note on which files each tool reads).
- **Key quotes (verbatim):**
  - "Claude treats them as context, not enforced configuration." "The more specific and concise your instructions, the more consistently Claude follows them."
  - "target under 200 lines per CLAUDE.md file. Longer files consume more context and reduce adherence."
  - "if two instructions contradict each other, Claude may pick one arbitrarily."
  - Code Review "reads your repository's `CLAUDE.md` files as project context. A change that violates one of their rules becomes a finding". "The Code Review service and the local `/code-review` command are two separate tools, and they read different files. The service reads both `CLAUDE.md` and `REVIEW.md`. The `/code-review` command ... reads your `CLAUDE.md` files and not `REVIEW.md`."
- **Connection:** directly relevant to the CLAUDE.md arm and to the `/code-review ultra` baseline: Anthropic's own reviewers treat CLAUDE.md as a source of findings, so a reviewed project's CLAUDE.md changes what the baseline flags. The baseline comparison is fair only if councils and `/code-review` see the same CLAUDE.md. Also: whether `/code-review ultra` (cloud) reads `REVIEW.md` is not stated on this page; the researcher should check the ultrareview page before relying on it.
- **Novelty threat:** none.

Note: the ultrareview page (https://code.claude.com/docs/en/ultrareview, page read via curl) says nothing about CLAUDE.md or REVIEW.md; it states "every reported finding is independently reproduced and verified", "typically $5 to $25 in usage credits" after free runs and "A review typically takes 5 to 15 minutes". Whether the cloud review reads CLAUDE.md is therefore not confirmed from the docs.

#### Academic work: controlled comparisons of fixed vs LLM-led orchestration

#### COBOL-Orch: Deterministic vs. LLM-Controlled Orchestration for COBOL-to-Python Modernization
- **Citation:** Naing Oo Lwin and Rajesh Kumar; AIware 2026 (Semantic Scholar venue "AIware", DOI 10.1145/3805760.3814891); arXiv v1 11 May 2026 (only version); https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.09894
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API); full text partly read (sections 3–5 and limitations, arxiv.org/html via curl).
- **What it did:** in one framework (ATLAS) it held models, prompts, tools, configuration and programs fixed and varied only who controls execution (a fixed stage pipeline vs the LLM choosing tools, order, retries and termination), on the NIST COBOL85 suite with Claude Sonnet 4.5, GPT-5.1-Codex-Max and Grok-Code-Fast-1, over repeated seeded runs.
- **Key numbers:** abstract: "deterministic orchestration achieves comparable computational accuracy to LLM-controlled orchestration while improving worst-case robustness and reducing performance variability across runs. Deterministic execution also reduces token consumption by up to 3.5x". Table 1 (full text) for Claude Sonnet 4.5: CA 0.966 deterministic vs 0.964 LLM-controlled; success rate 0.902 vs 0.918; CVaR0.1 0.953 vs 0.949. The paper itself says "LLM-controlled orchestration yields higher SR across all models". Tokens: LLM-controlled "often requires between 1.75 million and 2.25 million tokens for complex modules"; deterministic "approximately 400,000 to 700,000 tokens". Cost per successful translation in one category: "exceeds $140" vs "approximately $40". No confidence intervals or significance tests in the sections read; the number of runs per program was not found in the sections read.
- **Connection:** the closest controlled design to the project's scripted-vs-agent-led arm: same models, prompts and tools, only control varies. Its result shape (quality roughly equal, variance and tokens lower for the fixed pipeline, but the agent-led version wins a different metric) is a useful prior and warns that the arm should report several metrics, since the winner can differ by metric. Its accuracy gaps are tiny and untested, so the project's paired-CI design would be a methodological step up. Different task (translation, with executable oracles).
- **Novelty threat:** low (different task; no review, judge or evolution).

#### SwarmBench: Can Large Language Models Act as Agent Swarm Orchestrators?
- **Citation:** Jinshan Gao, Zhuoran Jin, Tianyi Men, Kang Liu and Jun Zhao; Findings of EMNLP 2026 (arXiv comment "EMNLP 2026 Findings"; not checked in the ACL Anthology); v1 31 Aug 2026; https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.30661 ; code https://github.com/ying1973/SwarmBench
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API); full text partly read (sections 2–3, appendices C.3, D.1 and the repeated-run appendix).
- **What it did:** 8 tasks, 400 samples, in which an orchestrator model must spawn, delegate to and aggregate subagents from a shared pool; ten orchestrator models compared on accuracy, parallelism gain, dollar cost and a process-quality score (decomposition, delegation, aggregation).
- **Key numbers / findings (full text):** "GPT-5.4 is the strongest and most balanced orchestrator ... Claude-Sonnet-4-6 is the closest proprietary competitor." "cost is not a linear function of performance"; "weaker orchestrators often require more interactions and redundant execution without necessarily obtaining better results". "aggregation is consistently lower than the other two dimensions ... current models are comparatively better at initiating and assigning subtasks than at reliably integrating subagent outputs". Cost-matched single-agent baseline (Claude Haiku 4.5 as both): the swarm "still outperforms it on MTU, LTG, and WS under the same budget" (four tasks were tested, so it did not win the fourth). Five repeated runs gave standard deviations "roughly from 0.7 to 1.6".
- **Connection:** supports the project's concern that an agent-led orchestrator adds its own error source, and specifically that the weakest step is aggregation, which in a council is the judge. It also models the cost-matched single-agent baseline the project uses. It is a benchmark of orchestrators, not a scripted-vs-agent comparison.
- **Novelty threat:** low.

#### Blueprint First: Blueprint First, Model Second: A Framework for Deterministic LLM Workflow
- **Citation:** Libin Qiu, Yuhang Ye, Zhirong Gao et al. (10 authors); ICSME (Semantic Scholar venue "IEEE International Conference on Software Maintenance and Evolution", DOI 10.1109/ICSME67971.2026.00018, so ICSME 2026); arXiv v1 1 Aug 2025, v2 16 Jun 2026; https://arxiv.org/abs/2508.02721
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API).
- **What it did:** codifies an expert procedure as a source-code "Execution Blueprint" run by a deterministic engine; the LLM is called only for bounded sub-tasks, "never to decide the workflow's path".
- **Key numbers (abstract):** on TravelPlanner with Claude Sonnet 4, "35.56% final pass rate, a 97.6% improvement over the state-of-the-art ATLAS baseline (18.00%)"; "reduces constraint violations by 96.0% (11 vs 275)"; "10.2±0.7 steps vs 14.0".
- **Connection:** supports the scripted arm's premise (code holds the plan, the model does bounded work), matching the dynamic-workflow design. The baseline is a different system, not the same system under agent control, so it does not isolate orchestration as COBOL-Orch does.
- **Novelty threat:** none.

#### Progressive Crystallization: Turning Agent Exploration into Deterministic, Lower-Cost Workflows in Production
- **Citation:** Arun Malik; arXiv preprint; v1 8 Jul 2026; https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07052
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API).
- **What it did:** a lifecycle that promotes repeatedly validated agent behaviours into deterministic workflows (and demotes ones that regress), in a production AIOps system.
- **Key numbers (abstract):** "increased deterministic execution from 0% to 45% over eight months, reduced per-incident agent costs by more than 70% despite doubling incident volume".
- **Connection:** loosely related but suggestive: it is the inverse of the project's arm (agent-led exploration first, then freezing it into a script), and its promotion and demotion on evidence resembles the project's keep-if-CI-above-zero rule. Single-author industrial report; no controlled comparison in the abstract.
- **Novelty threat:** none.

#### MAFBench: Architectural Design, Not Only Model Intelligence, Governs Multi-Agent LLM Performance
- **Citation:** Abdelghny Orogat, Ana Rostam and Essam Mansour; SIGMOD 2027 per the arXiv comment ("This paper is accepted to SIGMOD 2027"); v1 3 Feb 2026, v2 17 Sep 2026; https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.03128
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API).
- **What it did:** a controlled comparison of nine multi-agent frameworks with the LLM fixed and only architecture varied, along five dimensions including orchestration and topology.
- **Key numbers (abstract):** "Orchestration alone increases latency by over 60x, and a minimal implementation of the same paradigm isolates that cost as implementation rather than paradigm." "Schema-constrained planning interfaces reduce accuracy by up to 32 points through formatting failures, not reasoning errors." "Communication topology drops coordination success from above 90% to below 30% under mismatched structure."
- **Connection:** supports treating the harness as a variable: the project's scripted and agent-led arms differ in implementation as well as in who decides, so overheads like the 60x latency could be implementation artefacts. The 32-point formatting loss is a warning for structured judge outputs.
- **Novelty threat:** none.

#### MAST: Why Do Multi-Agent LLM Systems Fail?
- **Citation:** Mert Cemri, Melissa Z. Pan, Shuyi Yang et al. (13 authors); NeurIPS (Semantic Scholar metadata gives venue "Neural Information Processing Systems"; year and track not confirmed); v1 17 Mar 2025, v3 26 Oct 2025; https://arxiv.org/abs/2503.13657
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API).
- **What it did:** a failure taxonomy from expert annotation of multi-agent traces across 7 frameworks, plus an LLM-judge annotator.
- **Key numbers (abstract):** "1600+ annotated traces collected across 7 popular MAS frameworks"; "14 unique modes, clustered into 3 categories: (i) system design issues, (ii) inter-agent misalignment, and (iii) task verification"; "kappa = 0.88"; opening line: "their performance gains on popular benchmarks are often minimal".
- **Connection:** supports logging council failures by category; "task verification" failures map onto judge errors, "inter-agent misalignment" onto lead or orchestrator errors in the agent-led arm. A ready-made coding scheme for the project's failure log.
- **Novelty threat:** none.

#### SAS-or-MAS: Single-agent or Multi-agent Systems? Why Not Both?
- **Citation:** Mingyan Gao, Yanzi Li, Banruo Liu et al. (7 authors); arXiv preprint; v1 23 May 2025; https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.18286
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API).
- **What it did:** compares multi-agent and single-agent systems across agentic applications and proposes cascading between them.
- **Key numbers (abstract):** "the benefits of MAS over SAS diminish as LLM capabilities improve"; the cascade "improves accuracy by 1.1-12% while reducing deployment costs by up to 20%".
- **Connection:** supports the strong single-reviewer baseline, alongside the already-cited Rethinking the Value of Multi-Agent Workflow (2601.12307).
- **Novelty threat:** none.

#### Who&When: Which Agent Causes Task Failures and When? On Automated Failure Attribution of LLM Multi-Agent Systems
- **Citation:** Shaokun Zhang, Ming Yin, Jieyu Zhang et al. (11 authors); ICML 2025 (Semantic Scholar venue "International Conference on Machine Learning", year 2025; the arXiv comment says only "camera-ready"); v1 30 Apr 2025, v3 2 Jun 2025; https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.00212
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API).
- **Key numbers (abstract):** "failure logs from 127 LLM multi-agent systems"; "The best method achieves 53.5% accuracy in identifying failure-responsible agents but only 14.2% in pinpointing failure steps, with some methods performing below random." The follow-up AgenTracer (Guibin Zhang et al., https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.03312, abstract read, arXiv API) says current reasoning LLMs reach "accuracy generally below 10%" on attribution.
- **Connection:** relevant to the evolution loop: if an LLM is asked to diagnose which council member caused a miss and propose a mutation, these results say its diagnosis is unreliable, which argues for the project's outcome-only selection (CI on score) rather than trace-based credit assignment.
- **Novelty threat:** none.

#### SpecGate: A Gated Spec-Driven Workflow: An Audit-Trail Study of Coding-Agent Work
- **Citation:** Rodrigo Figueiredo Abdo and Pedro Carvalho Brom; arXiv preprint; v1 3 Nov 2026; https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05094
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API).
- **What it did:** audit-trail study of a Claude Code spec-driven workflow in which "almost every control is an instruction carried out by the same model that it constrains", with a reviewer subagent checking each artefact "against a fixed, stage-specific defect list".
- **Key numbers (abstract):** nine features in seven projects, all run by the authors; "288 of the 581 planned tasks were completed block by block"; "The weak point was the orchestrator, which runs and judges its own checks. It recorded undeclared changes in 11 of 247 block checks ... and stopped work in only four."
- **Connection:** supports the project's separation of judge from orchestrator and the preference for code-enforced checks over instruction-enforced ones; small, self-run sample.
- **Novelty threat:** none.

#### Other topic-1 items (abstract read, verbatim, arXiv API; one line each)
- **Agentless: Demystifying LLM-based Software Engineering Agents** (Chunqiu Steven Xia, Yinlin Deng, Soren Dunn and Lingming Zhang; arXiv 2407.01489, v1 1 Jul 2024, v2 29 Oct 2024; Semantic Scholar venue shows only "arXiv.org", so no venue confirmed; 582 citations): a fixed three-phase pipeline "without letting the LLM decide future actions" got "32.00%, 96 correct fixes" on SWE-bench Lite at "$0.70", then the best open-source result. The classic software-engineering case for fixed pipelines; supports the scripted arm.
- **Puppeteer: Multi-Agent Collaboration via Evolving Orchestration** (Yufan Dang, Chen Qian, Xueheng Luo et al.; NeurIPS 2025 per the arXiv comment; arXiv 2505.19591): an RL-trained orchestrator "dynamically directs agents"; reports "superior performance with reduced computational costs" and "more compact, cyclic reasoning structures". The strongest pro-agent-led-orchestration result found, but the orchestrator is trained, unlike a prompted Claude lead.
- **AOrchestra: Automating Sub-Agent Creation for Agentic Orchestration** (Jianhao Ruan et al.; arXiv 2602.03786, Feb 2026): an orchestrator creates sub-agents on the fly as (Instruction, Context, Tools, Model) tuples; "16.28% relative improvement against the strongest baseline when paired with Gemini-3-Flash" on GAIA, SWE-Bench and Terminal-Bench.
- **AWO: Optimizing Agentic Workflows using Meta-tools** (Sami Abuzakuk et al.; arXiv 2601.22037): turning recurring tool-call sequences into deterministic meta-tools cut LLM calls "up to 11.9%" and raised success "by up to 4.2 percent points". Small-scale support for moving recurring steps into code.
- **AgentSpawn** (Igor Costa; arXiv 2602.07072, single author): dynamic spawning gives "34% higher completion rates than static baselines on benchmarks like SWE-bench". A pro-dynamic claim; baseline and benchmark details are vague in the abstract.
- **Token Budgets: An Empirical Catalog of 63 LLM-Agent Budget-Overrun Incidents** (Sajjad Khan; arXiv 2606.04056): "63 confirmed production incidents from 21 orchestration frameworks", with "the delegation-fanout race documented in 11 incidents". Evidence that agent-led fan-out needs hard caps (dynamic workflows have a 1,000-agent cap and an advisory warning only).
- **OpenCodeReview** (2608.09290) is already cited in prior-work-research.md. Its subtitle, "Determinism over Non-Determinism for Cost-Effective Agent-Based Code Review", and its claim of "5-15x fewer tokens" than Claude Code and Codex make it the most direct review-domain evidence for the scripted arm, worth cross-referencing in that arm's motivation.
- **Towards a Science of Scaling Agent Systems** (2512.08296) is on the list of IDs already cited in the repo; not re-read.
- **Dive into Claude Code: The Design Space of Today's and Future AI Agent Systems** (Jiacheng Liu, Xiaohan Zhao, Xinyi Shang and Zhiqiang Shen; tech report, arXiv 2604.14228, v3 10 Oct 2026; abstract read, verbatim, arXiv API): an architecture analysis of Claude Code from "the publicly available source code", describing "a simple while-loop that calls the model, runs tools, and repeats" plus "a subagent delegation and orchestration mechanism". Background for describing the platform; no measurements of orchestration reliability in the abstract.

---

### 2.10 Instruction files like CLAUDE.md, as help or bias

Summary of the topic: by late 2026 there is a dense, fast-growing literature (almost all 2026 preprints) on AGENTS.md and CLAUDE.md. Controlled studies mostly find that context files do **not** reliably raise task success, do raise cost, are followed closely (including when wrong or stale) and may help through generic "priming" rather than content. None of the studies found tests the effect of a reviewed project's CLAUDE.md on **reviewers** finding planted bugs, so the project's CLAUDE.md arm remains open. Several of them give design lessons for that arm (placebo controls, stale-file conditions, token-cost reporting).

#### Controlled evaluations of effect on agent performance

#### AGENTbench/CTXbench: Evaluating AGENTS.md: Are Repository-Level Context Files Helpful for Coding Agents?
- **Citation:** Thibaud Gloaguen, Niels Mündler-Sasahara, Mark Niklas Müller, Veselin Raychev and Martin Vechev; arXiv preprint (Semantic Scholar venue "arXiv.org"; no peer-reviewed venue found); v1 12 Feb 2026, v3 29 Sep 2026; https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.11988 ; 45 citations on Semantic Scholar.
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API); full text partly read (sections 3–4.3, via arxiv.org/html v3).
- **What it did:** SWE-bench Lite (300 tasks, LLM-generated context files) and a new CTXbench (138 instances from 12 niche repositories with developer-committed context files), three conditions (none, LLM-generated, developer-written), four agent/model pairs including Claude Code with Sonnet 4.5 (context file written to CLAUDE.md), with Cochran-Mantel-Haenszel tests.
- **Key numbers (verbatim):** "providing context files does not generally improve task success rates, while increasing inference cost by over 20% on average". LLM-generated files: resolution "reduced by 0.5% and 2% on average on SWE-bench and CTXbench"; p-values 0.87 and 0.37; cost "increase of 20% and 23%". Developer-written files: "improve agent performance by 2.4% on average (p=21%)", and "they improve performance for all agents but Claude Code". Agents "run more tests", read and grep more. "instructions in context files are well followed"; "context files are not effective at providing a repository overview". Claude Code Sonnet 4.5 cost per SWE-bench instance: \$1.30 without vs \$1.51 with LLM-generated files (Table 2).
- **Connection:** the main prior for the CLAUDE.md arm: expect small or null effects on outcome and a measurable token cost, so the arm needs enough seeds to detect a few points and must report tokens. It concerns solving, not reviewing; whether a CLAUDE.md that states conventions helps a reviewer spot convention-violating planted bugs is untested.
- **Novelty threat:** low (task is solving, not review).

#### Guardrails Beat Guidance: A Large-Scale Study of Rules, Skills, and Persistent Configuration for Coding Agents
- **Citation:** Xing Zhang, Guanghui Wang, Yanwei Cui et al. (7 authors); arXiv preprint (no venue on Semantic Scholar); v1 13 Apr 2026, v2 28 May 2026; https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.11088
