- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API); full text partly read (method, sections on priming and limitations).
- **What it did:** 679 scraped rule files (25,532 rules); over 5,000 Claude Code runs with Claude Opus 4.6 on 58 "discriminative" SWE-bench Verified tasks, comparing curated, random, shuffled, mismatched-domain rule sets and single-rule ablations.
- **Key numbers (verbatim):** "Random rules improve a coding agent's task performance as much as expert-curated ones (both +13.8pp on a discriminative subset of SWE-bench Verified)"; "random rules tie with curated rules at 63.8%"; "every individually beneficial rule is a negative constraint ... while every individually harmful one is a positive directive"; "pass rates remain stable across rule counts from 0 to 50". Task selection: "We screen all 500 tasks with three baseline repetitions each (1,500 screening runs) and retain 58 discriminative tasks (those solved 1 or 2 out of 3 times)". Limitations: "The no-rule baseline also differs between Experiment 1 (50.0%, single trajectory per task) and Experiment 2 (60.3%, averaged over three seeds)".
- **Connection:** two direct lessons. (1) **Placebo control:** if any on-topic text "primes" the agent, the CLAUDE.md arm needs a length-matched irrelevant or shuffled CLAUDE.md condition, not just with vs without; otherwise a positive effect cannot be attributed to project knowledge. (2) **Noisy selection:** choosing tasks solved 1–2 of 3 times and then comparing against a baseline is open to regression to the mean; the 10-point gap between their two baseline estimates shows how large run-to-run noise is, which supports the project's paired same-seed CIs and held-out seeds. The finding that negative constraints help and positive directives hurt is a hypothesis for how reviewer role briefs should be written.
- **Novelty threat:** none for the review question; medium relevance as a method warning.

#### Context-Ablation: Do Context Files Help Coding Agents? A Two-Agent Ablation Study on Real Repositories
- **Citation:** Prakhar Khatri; REALM 2026 workshop (arXiv journal-ref "Proceedings of the Second Workshop for REsearch on Agent Language Models (REALM 2026), pages 57-67"); v1 28 Jul 2026; https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.27250
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API).
- **Key numbers (abstract):** Claude Code and Codex, "17 real tasks from 3 repositories", "288 evaluated runs with gold-test evaluation". "Context strategy does not measurably move correctness on either agent (bounded to <=10-15pp via equivalence testing)". "agents fail on implementation skill ... not missing repository knowledge"; "borderline task difficulty is agent-specific (Spearman rho=0.75), offering a candidate explanation for prior contradictions".
- **Connection:** supports equivalence testing as a way to report a null CLAUDE.md effect (the project's CI design can do the same: a CI inside a margin is evidence of no effect, not just failure to find one). Small sample.
- **Novelty threat:** none.

#### AGENTS.md efficiency: On the Impact of AGENTS.md Files on the Efficiency of AI Coding Agents
- **Citation:** Jai Lal Lulla, Seyedmoein Mohsenimofidi, Matthias Galster, Jie M. Zhang, Sebastian Baltes and Christoph Treude; arXiv preprint (5 pages); v1 28 Jan 2026, v2 30 Mar 2026; https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.20404
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API).
- **Key numbers (abstract):** "10 repositories and 124 pull requests"; AGENTS.md "is associated with a lower median runtime (Δ28.64%) and reduced output token consumption (Δ16.58%), while maintaining a comparable task completion behavior."
- **Connection:** the opposite cost direction from Gloaguen et al. (lower tokens rather than +20%); the two differ in task source and in measuring output tokens vs total cost. For the project, it means the CLAUDE.md arm's cost effect cannot be assumed and must be measured, input and output tokens separately.
- **Novelty threat:** none.

#### Factorial-Adherence: Instruction Adherence in Coding Agent Configuration Files: A Factorial Study of Four File-Structure Variables
- **Citation:** Damon McMillan; arXiv preprint; v1 11 May 2026; https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.10039
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API).
- **Key numbers (abstract):** "1,650 Claude Code CLI sessions (16,050 function-level observations)", "primarily Sonnet 4.6"; "None of the four structural variables or three two-way interactions produces a detectable contrast after multiple-testing correction" (size, position, architecture, contradictions in adjacent files); "Size and conflict nulls are supported by affirmative-null Bayes factors (BF10 between 0.05 and 0.10)"; "each additional function the agent generates is associated with approximately 5.6% lower odds of compliance per step (OR = 0.944)".
- **Connection:** challenges the Claude Code docs' advice ("Longer files consume more context and reduce adherence") for the size range tested; suggests adherence decays within a session, which matters for long reviewer runs. Uses mixed-effects models, a good template for the project's analysis.
- **Novelty threat:** none.

#### Probe-and-Refine Tuning of Repository Guidance for Coding Agents
- **Citation:** Asa Shepard and Jeannie Albrecht; arXiv preprint; v1 18 Jun 2026, v2 19 Jun 2026; https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.20512
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API).
- **Key numbers (abstract):** guidance file tuned by "synthetic bug-fix probes"; on SWE-bench Verified with Qwen3.5-35B-A3B over four trials, "33.0% mean resolve rate vs. 28.3% for the static knowledge base ... and 25.5% for an unguided baseline (p < 0.001 for both probe-and-refine contrasts)". Gains came from "coverage rather than precision"; "the tuning loop degrades when the model cannot generate sufficiently diagnostic output".
- **Connection:** overlaps with topic 3 and with the project's design: it optimises a guidance file against synthetic bugs (like planted seeds). It reports evaluation on SWE-bench Verified, apparently separate from the synthetic probes used in tuning (abstract only; separation not confirmed). Relevant as precedent for evolving instruction text against planted bugs.
- **Novelty threat:** low.

#### Bias, staleness and misleading instruction files

#### Stale-Override: Stale Instruction Files Override the Code in Coding Agents: A One-Sentence Fix
- **Citation:** Xiaonan Xu and Wenjing Wu; arXiv preprint ("Submitted to SN Computer Science"); v1 22 Oct 2026; https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30571 ; data https://github.com/WenJing95/stale-docs-data
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API).
- **What it did:** 40 Python projects where the instruction file asks for one convention and the code uses another; Claude Code and Codex CLI add a feature, with and without one user-level sentence giving the code precedence; 320 runs.
- **Key numbers (abstract):** "Without the sentence, 90.1% of the agents' choices followed the stale file. Claude Code pointed out the conflict in 79 of 80 runs and still followed the file." Codex "rewrote the existing code to match the file in 20 of 80 runs". The sentence "raised the share of choices that follow the code from 4.2% to 91.4%". Mechanism offered: "the instruction file reaches the model as an instruction and the code as tool output, which models are trained to rank lower."
- **Connection:** the strongest evidence found for the "bias" side of the CLAUDE.md arm. A reviewer that treats CLAUDE.md as authoritative may (a) flag code that departs from a stale CLAUDE.md as a bug (false alarms) or (b) accept a planted bug that CLAUDE.md describes as intended. The project could add a "stale or wrong CLAUDE.md" condition and measure both false alarms and misses. It also bears on the docs' statement that Code Review turns CLAUDE.md violations into findings.
- **Novelty threat:** low (generation, not review), but it suggests a clean extra condition.

#### Stale-Debt: When Instruction Files Go Stale: Documentation Debt and Hidden Cost in Coding Agents
- **Citation:** Tural Mehtiyev, Biruk Tadesse, Lawrence Arkoh and Wesley Assunção; arXiv preprint; v1 6 Nov 2026; https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09802 ; replication https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.23992004
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API).
- **Key numbers (abstract):** three real stale instructions, 22 maintenance tasks, three versions (stale, corrected, removed), "732 trajectories of two models". "agents follow stale guidance when it is relevant to their tasks, sometimes even when correct information is available elsewhere." On the stale test command "Claude Sonnet 5.5 needed about two extra tool calls per run, a third more than with the corrected command"; "none repaired the underlying instruction file".
- **Connection:** supports Stale-Override with real-world staleness; its stale/corrected/removed design is a template for the CLAUDE.md arm.
- **Novelty threat:** none.

#### Adherence-ACF: Do They Do as They Are Told? Investigating Coding Agents' Adherence to Explicit Instructions in Context Files
- **Citation:** Gianmario Voria, Yutaro Kashiwa, Gemma Catolino and Fabio Palomba; arXiv preprint; v1 10 Nov 2026; https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13709
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API).
- **Key finding (abstract):** "agents follow ACF guidance more often than not, but adherence splits sharply between instructions followed consistently and those never respected, with differences across instruction categories and coding agents." No headline percentages in the abstract.
- **Connection:** loosely related; supports measuring per-instruction effects rather than file-level.
- **Novelty threat:** none.

#### Instruction files as an attack surface (prompt injection through repository files)

#### Memory-File Security: Security Impact of Coding Agent's Memory Files
- **Citation:** Thiago Santos de Moura, Selma Güler and Yannic Noller; arXiv preprint; v1 28 Oct 2026; https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38521
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API).
- **Key numbers (abstract):** malicious CLAUDE.md variants for 12 CWE classes; Claude Code (Sonnet 5.5) and OpenCode on 36 SecRepoBench tasks. "The malicious files changed the rate of secure completions by -8 to +6 percentage points, with no consistent pattern across agents or variants." "Claude Code names it in about two thirds of its final messages, whereas OpenCode mentions it almost exclusively in its reasoning trace".
- **Connection:** a malicious CLAUDE.md that "presents removing defensive checks as engineering best practice" is close to the project's worry that CLAUDE.md might lead reviewers to accept a planted missing-check bug. The small, inconsistent effect is a weak prior that the bias is not large, but this tested generation, not review.
- **Novelty threat:** none.

#### PackHallu: Package Hallucination Attacks on Coding Agents through Prompt Injection in Rule Files
- **Citation:** Yupu Wang, Zhengyuan Jiang, Reachal Wang and Neil Zhenqiang Gong; arXiv preprint; v1 7 Oct 2026; https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.09264
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API).
- **What it did:** injects prompts into benign rule files (AGENTS.md, .cursorrules) to make agents swap dependencies for attacker packages, with "an evolutionary optimization framework that iteratively rewrites these injected prompts using trajectory-level feedback and LLM-guided mutations". Abstract reports "high attack success rates and strong transferability" with no numbers.
- **Connection:** two links: rule files are an injection surface (relevant to reviewing untrusted repositories), and it is an example of evolutionary prompt optimisation used adversarially (topic 3).
- **Novelty threat:** none.

#### Adversarial Comments: Can Adversarial Code Comments Fool AI Security Reviewers -- Large-Scale Empirical Study of Comment-Based Attacks and Defenses Against LLM Code Analysis
- **Citation:** Scott Thornton; arXiv preprint; v1 18 Feb 2026; https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.16741
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API).
- **Key numbers (abstract):** "100-sample benchmark across Python, JavaScript, and Java, each paired with eight comment variants"; "Eight frontier models ... 9,366 trials". "Adversarial comments produce small, statistically non-significant effects on detection accuracy (McNemar exact p > 0.21; all 95 percent confidence intervals include zero)." "Comment stripping reduces detection for weaker models by removing helpful context." "Failures concentrate on inherently difficult vulnerability classes".
- **Connection:** the closest review-side evidence on anchoring by in-code text: misleading framing did not measurably hurt detection, while removing context hurt weaker models. A prior that CLAUDE.md as context is more likely to help or do nothing than to bias reviewers, but these were single-function samples with 89–96% baselines (ceiling effects likely). Also contrast with the already-cited "When AI Agrees Too Much" (2603.18740), which found bias injection does manipulate automated code review; the two disagree and the project's arm could help settle it on planted bugs.
- **Novelty threat:** low.

#### When "Do Not" Is Not Deny: Security Rules in CLAUDE.md vs Built-In Controls
- **Citation:** Ting Yan; arXiv preprint; v1 24 Aug 2026; https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23550
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API).
- **Key numbers (abstract):** "481 public CLAUDE.md files"; "only about 4-16% of the retrieved security rules had a matching built-in control"; strictest "4.4% (95% CI: 2.6-6.7%)". "CLAUDE.md is a write-only channel."
- **Connection:** loosely related; supports the docs' point that CLAUDE.md is context, not enforcement. For the project, any rule a reviewer must obey (such as read-only) should be a permission rule, not CLAUDE.md text.
- **Novelty threat:** none.

#### Reviewing instruction files and what they contain

#### AI-Cannot-Review: AI Cannot Review What Humans Catch: A First Look at the Reviews of Agent Instruction Files
- **Citation:** Wenhao Yang, Runzhi He and Minghui Zhou; arXiv preprint; v1 31 Jul 2026 (arXiv ID 2611.18582, published date as given by the API); https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18582
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API).
- **Key numbers (abstract):** "2,870 instruction-file pull requests across 2,246 repositories"; "over 70% of instruction-file changes receive no substantive review"; benchmark of "1,962 human-checked concerns across 468 PRs"; Codex, OpenCode and Claude Code "recovered only 1.3-3.9% of the concerns humans raise". AI reviewers catch "surface structure but misses factual errors, necessity questions, and staleness problems".
- **Connection:** a code-review result with a human-raised key, where AI reviewers (including Claude Code) recover almost nothing; relevant both to the CLAUDE.md arm (reviewers do not check CLAUDE.md against the code, consistent with Stale-Override) and as a benchmark with a known-issue key that another agent's benchmark section may want.
- **Novelty threat:** none (different object of review).

#### Content and adoption studies (abstract read, verbatim, arXiv API; one line each)
- **Agent READMEs** (Worawalan Chatlatanagulchai, Hao Li, Yutaro Kashiwa et al., 11 authors; arXiv 2511.12884, v2 Aug 2026): "2,303 agent context files from 1,925 repositories"; content is "test procedures (75.9%), implementation details (70.8%), and architecture (68.1%)"; "security (14.8%) and performance (14.5%) are rarely specified". Useful for choosing realistic CLAUDE.md content for seeded projects.
- **On the Use of Agentic Coding Manifests: An Empirical Study of Claude Code** (Chatlatanagulchai et al., 8 authors; PROFES 2025 per arXiv journal-ref; arXiv 2509.14744): "253 Claude.md files from 242 repositories"; content "dominated by operational commands, technical implementation notes, and high-level architecture".
- **Toward an Evidence-Based Template for Agent Instruction Files** (Mehedi Hasan Shanto et al.; arXiv 2611.02383, Nov 2026): "3,387 instruction files from 1,000 GitHub repositories"; "16.1% provide divergent guidance" among paired sections in repositories with multiple formats; "40.4% of the analyzed files have only one recorded modification event".
- **Harness Engineering for Agentic AI Coding Tools** (Matthias Galster et al.; arXiv 2602.14690, based on an AIware 2026 paper per the comment): "2,853 GitHub repositories"; "Context Files dominate the configuration landscape"; "few repositories adopt advanced mechanisms such as Skills and Subagents".
- **ContextCov** (Reshabh K Sharma; arXiv 2603.00822): compiles AGENTS.md constraints into executable checks; "88.3% constraint compliance (vs. 67.0% and 50.3%)" for prompt-only and reflection baselines on SWE-bench Lite. Supports enforcing in code what instruction files only ask.
- **The Role of Agentic Instruction Categories for Code Generation** (Warren Gerald Polandra, Yoshiki Higo and Raula Gaikovina Kula; APSEC 2026 per comment; arXiv 2610.21421): all 31 combinations of five instruction categories; "No Gemma 4 variant passed all tests, whereas 12.99% of GPT-5.4 variants did".

#### Project-documentation anchoring of reviewers
No study was found that varies project documentation (README, CLAUDE.md or design docs) given to an LLM **reviewer** and measures planted-bug recall or false alarms. The nearest are Adversarial Comments (no significant effect of misleading comments on detection), the already-cited "When AI Agrees Too Much" (2603.18740, bias injection does manipulate review), Stale-Override (agents defer to the instruction file over the code) and Guardrails Beat Guidance (content-independent priming). This is a gap the CLAUDE.md arm would fill.

---

### 2.11 Human-in-the-loop costs of AI review

#### Go Home Copilot: "Go Home Copilot, You're Drunk": Understanding Developer Responses to Agent-Generated Code Review Comments
- **Citation:** Shamse Tasnim Cynthia, Ratnadira Widyasari, Banani Roy, Ting Zhang and David Lo; arXiv preprint; v1 24 Jul 2026, v2 29 Jul 2026; [arXiv 2607.21997](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21997). No venue stated on the arXiv record or in the full text.
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API); full text partly read (fetch tool, first 100k of 118k characters).
- **What it did:** 54,791 review comments by five coding agents (Copilot, Cursor, Codex, Devin and Claude) on 342 Python GitHub repositories. "Resolved" means the thread was marked resolved (`isResolved` in the GitHub API). Open card sorting of 470 unresolved discussions and a regression on what predicts resolution.
- **Key numbers:** Table III (per the fetch tool): Copilot 72.9% resolved (33,265 of 45,668), Cursor 67.2% (4,554 of 6,778), Codex 54.8% (1,242 of 2,267); Devin (50 comments) and Claude (28) were excluded as too small. The pooled rate of about 71% is the fetch tool's own sum, not a paper figure. Section VI gives Codex as 53%, which conflicts with the table. Unresolved patterns (counts of discussions): intentional design decision 112, incorrect suggestion 67, challenging agent feedback 39, needs further discussion 36, delegation of work 31. Inline code suggestion: 75.5% against 64.6% resolution (64.5% in the RQ3 text), OR = 1.62, Cramér's V = 0.12. Abstract (verbatim): "lengthy and complex comments are less likely to be acted upon".
- **Connection:** supports the project's motivation that precision and comment form matter to real users; "resolved" is a weak proxy (a thread can be resolved without a fix), which the Beko paper below shows directly. Overlaps the findings-format design (file, line, evidence; a concrete fix may raise uptake).
- **Novelty threat:** none. Observational, single reviewers.

#### CodeRabbit in the wild: Is Agentic Code Review Helpful? Mining Developers' Feedback to CodeRabbit Reviews in the Wild
- **Citation:** Hong Yi Lin, Mingzhao Liang, Patanamon Thongtanunam and Kla Tantithamthavorn; arXiv preprint; v1 3 Jul 2026, v2 23 Jul 2026; [arXiv 2607.03316](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.03316). No venue stated.
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API); full text partly read (fetch tool, first 100k of 125k characters).
- **What it did:** 31,073 CodeRabbit review comments with a developer reply, from 10,191 PRs in 239 repositories. Labels come from the developer's reply text: 297 coded by hand, the rest by GPT-5.1 (kappa 0.74 against the manual labels). Also tries to predict rejection.
- **Key numbers:** abstract (verbatim): "36.4% were accepted and 7.3% triggered discussion, while 56.3% were rejected"; rejection prediction "up to 76% F1 score". Full text (fetch tool): of rejections, invalid suggestion 58.0% (false positive 43.3%, out of scope 10.0%, redundant 4.7%) and misalignment 42.0% (intended design trade-off 23.7%, developer preference 18.3%). Functional comments 36.5% accepted, evolvability 35.8%. No severity analysis.
- **Connection:** supports the project's precision focus: false positives are the largest single rejection reason for a commercial multi-stage reviewer. Caveat: only comments that drew a reply are counted, so silent ignoring is invisible. The rejection predictor is a learned post-filter, a cousin of the judge.
- **Novelty threat:** none.

#### Agentic CR in the terminal: Agentic Code Review in the Terminal: A Trajectory-Level Analysis of Behavior, Cost, and Human-Alignment
- **Citation:** Wachiraphan Charoenwet, Kla Tantithamthavorn, Patanamon Thongtanunam, Hong Yi Lin, Minwoo Jeong and Ming Wu; arXiv preprint; 18 Jul 2026; [arXiv 2607.16740](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.16740). No venue stated.
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API); full text read (fetch tool).
- **What it did:** four agentic reviewers (Gemini CLI, Claude Code, Rovo Dev and CodeRabbit) and five single-call LLM reviewers on AgenticCR-Verified, 362 tasks filtered from an existing 68K-discussion set, with human review comments as ground truth (location within ±5 lines plus an LLM judge for agreement). Then analyses each agent's trajectory (exploration, validation) and its cost.
- **Key numbers (fetch tool, from the tables):** share of generated comments that both localise and match a human comment: Rovo Dev 5.81%, Claude Code 5.60%, Gemini CLI 2.30%, CodeRabbit 1.37%; static GPT-5.2 2.61%, Claude Sonnet 4.6 1.87%. Comment counts: Claude Code 947, Rovo Dev 860, CodeRabbit 803, Gemini CLI 392, static models 2,305–9,060. Per-task cost: Rovo Dev "$0.75 validation, $0.44 exploration", Claude Code exploration $0.10. No subagent or multi-agent set-up is tested.
- **Connection:** overlaps the project's cost scoring and its choice of `/code-review` as a baseline (Claude Code is one of the arms, though as a plain CLI, not the built-in review). Its "precision" is agreement with human comments, which the project rejects as ground truth. Useful as evidence that validation and exploration are where agentic review spends.
- **Novelty threat:** low.
- **Venue note:** the title appears in the ASE 2026 programme on conf.researchr.org (sessions shared by Research Papers and NIER); the exact track was not confirmed.

#### Human-AI Synergy: Human-AI Synergy in Agentic Code Review
- **Citation:** Suzhen Zhong, Shayan Noei, Ying Zou and Bram Adams; arXiv preprint; v1 16 Mar 2026, v3 30 Oct 2026; [arXiv 2603.15911](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.15911). No venue stated.
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API); full text read (fetch tool, 100k of 103k characters).
- **What it did:** 278,790 review conversations in 300 GitHub projects; compares feedback from human reviewers and AI review agents (16 bots identified; seven with enough data: CodeRabbit, Copilot, Gemini Code Assist, Cursor, Claude Code, Sourcery and Greptile). A suggestion counts as adopted if a later commit in the PR changed the exact line, closer to the suggestion than to the original (Jaccard) and not reverted, in a PR that was merged.
- **Key numbers:** adoption "16.6% vs. 56.5%" for AI agents against human reviewers (fetch tool, from the abstract of v3); in a sample of 383 unadopted AI suggestions, 28.7% were incorrect or would break the code and 24.0% were handled by a different fix; abstract (verbatim): "Human reviewers exchange 11.8% more rounds when reviewing AI-generated code than human-written code" and adopted AI suggestions "produce significantly larger increases in code complexity and code size".
- **Connection:** supports the human-cost case for precision: AI suggestions are adopted at under a third of the human rate and over a quarter of the rejected ones are wrong. Also overlaps the planned cost model: each false finding costs a human reading.
- **Novelty threat:** none.

#### CRA signal-to-noise: From Industry Claims to Empirical Reality: An Empirical Study of Code Review Agents in Pull Requests
- **Citation:** Kowshik Chowdhury, Dipayan Banik, K M Ferdous and Shazibul Islam Shamim; MSR 2026 (per the arXiv comment; listed in the MSR 2026 Mining Challenge programme on conf.researchr.org); 3 Apr 2026; [arXiv 2604.03196](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.03196).
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API); full text read (fetch tool).
- **What it did:** from AIDev's 19,450 PRs, 3,109 PRs in a commented review state, comparing human-only and code-review-agent-only reviews; a signal ratio per PR (Tier 1 critical plus Tier 2 important comments over all comments), coded by two researchers (kappa 0.75).
- **Key numbers:** abstract (verbatim): "CRA-only PRs achieve a 45.20% merge rate, 23.17 percentage points lower than human-only PRs (68.37%)"; "60.2% of closed CRA-only PRs fall into the 0-30% signal range, and 12 of 13 CRAs exhibit average signal ratios below 60%". Full text (fetch tool): Copilot's average signal ratio 19.79% over 24 PRs; no controls for PR size, repository or author ("correlation does not imply causation"); the "80% of PRs" industry claim is traced to a Qodo 2025 survey of 609 developers.
- **Connection:** supports measuring noise as a first-class cost. Small per-tool samples and no confounder control.
- **Novelty threat:** none.

#### Beko field study: Automated Code Review In Practice
- **Citation:** Umut Cihan, Vahid Haratian, Arda İçöz, Mert Kaan Gül, Ömercan Devran, Emircan Furkan Bayendur, Baykal Mehmet Uçar and Eray Tüzün; ICSE 2025 SEIP (per the arXiv comment); v1 24 Dec 2024, v2 28 Dec 2024; [arXiv 2412.18531](https://arxiv.org/abs/2412.18531).
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API); full text partly read (fetch tool).
- **What it did:** a Qodo PR Agent-based bot (GPT-4 32k per Section III-A; the introduction says GPT-4 Turbo) on three projects, 4,335 PRs of which 1,568 had automated review; authors had to label how each comment was handled (Azure DevOps comment-resolution policy).
- **Key numbers:** abstract (verbatim): "73.8% of automated comments were resolved. However, the average pull request closure duration increased from five hours 52 minutes to eight hours 20 minutes". Full text (fetch tool): 21.3% "Won't Fix"; by project, closure time rose in two projects (2h48m to 4h38m; 20h22m to 30h51m) and fell in one (6h06m to 3h07m); the conclusion's "50 minutes before and 48 minutes after" conflicts with Section IV-C3. The authors note developers may click "Resolved" instead of "Won't Fix".
- **Connection:** the clearest field evidence that AI review adds latency to human review even when most comments are acted on; supports counting human triage time in the cost of a council's output.
- **Novelty threat:** none.

#### Beko evaluation limits: Understanding the Limits of Automated Evaluation for Code Review Bots in Practice
- **Citation:** Veli Karakaya, Utku Boran Torun, Baykal Mehmet Uçar and Eray Tüzün; EASE 2026 (per the arXiv comment); 27 Apr 2026; [arXiv 2604.24525](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.24525).
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API).
- **What it did:** 2,604 bot comments at Beko labelled fixed or wontFix by engineers; tests whether G-Eval or an LLM judge (Gemini-2.5-pro, GPT-4.1-mini, GPT-5.2) reproduces those labels.
- **Key numbers:** "Agreement ratios range from approximately 0.44 to 0.62"; developer actions "reflect not only comment quality, but also contextual constraints, prioritization decisions, and workflow dynamics".
- **Connection:** supports the project's use of a planted key rather than developer action as ground truth, and warns that an LLM judge of "usefulness" agrees only moderately with practitioners.
- **Novelty threat:** none.

#### RovoDev Code Reviewer: A Large-Scale Online Evaluation of LLM-based Code Review Automation at Atlassian
- **Citation:** Kla Tantithamthavorn, Yaotian Zou, Andy Wong et al. (11 authors); ICSE 2026 SEIP (per the arXiv comment and the ICSE 2026 programme on conf.researchr.org); v1 3 Jan 2026, v2 20 Jan 2026; [arXiv 2601.01129](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.01129).
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API).
- **What it did:** one year of offline, online and user-feedback evaluation of a "review-guided, context-aware, quality-checked" reviewer in Bitbucket, without fine-tuning.
- **Key numbers:** "code resolution for 38.70% (i.e., comments that triggered code changes in the subsequent commits)"; "decreasing the PR cycle time by 30.8%"; "reducing the number of human-written comments by 35.6%".
- **Connection:** the opposite latency result to Beko (cycle time falls), with a quality-check stage. The fall in human comments may be substitution rather than saving, which matters for any claim that AI review lightens human load.
- **Novelty threat:** none.

#### Meta RADAR: Automating Low-Risk Code Review at Meta: RADAR, Risk Calibration, and Review Efficiency
- **Citation:** Chris Adams, Arjun Singh Banga, Parveen Bansal et al. (31 authors, including Audris Mockus, Peter Rigby and Nachiappan Nagappan); arXiv preprint; v1 28 May 2026, v2 12 Jun 2026; [arXiv 2605.30208](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.30208). The title appears in the ASE 2026 programme on conf.researchr.org (track not confirmed).
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API).
- **What it did:** a multi-stage funnel (eligibility gates, static heuristics, a learned Diff Risk Score, LLM code review, deterministic validation) that auto-lands low-risk diffs.
- **Key numbers:** "RADAR has reviewed 535K+ diffs and landed 331K+"; "The revert rate for RADAR-reviewed diffs is 1/3 that of non-RADAR diffs, and the Production Incident rate is 1/50"; "reduces median time to close by over 330% and median diff review wall time by 35%". Abstract also: "the share of diffs receiving timely review has declined".
- **Connection:** loosely related; risk-based routing (only some changes get the expensive review) is an alternative to a fixed council size. The safety comparison is between selected and unselected diffs, so it is confounded by selection.
- **Novelty threat:** none.

#### Habituation at the Gate: Rising Approval and Declining Scrutiny in Human Review of AI Agent Code
- **Citation:** Haoran Yu, Lifei Liu, Xiaochong Jiang, Yuwen Jia, Su Wang, Pin Qian and Yihang Chen; KDD 2026 Workshop on Agentic Software Engineering (per the arXiv comment); 21 Jun 2026; [arXiv 2606.22721](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.22721). Follow-up by overlapping authors: [arXiv 2609.06213](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06213) (5 Sep 2026; abstract read, verbatim, arXiv API), same 400 reviewers, approval "30.5% ... to 36.6%", lexical features show no decline, embedding shifts do.
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API).
- **What it did:** within-reviewer longitudinal analysis in AIDev of 400 repeat reviewers and 11,429 reviews of agent PRs.
- **Key numbers:** approval "from 30.1% to 36.8%"; "+14.5 pp from first to tenth decile"; "review latency increases rather than decreases (+3.5x), while inline comment volume decreases (-22%, p=0.0014)".
- **Connection:** evidence of review fatigue with agent code; supports the motivation that human review cannot absorb noisy AI output. Note the two papers give slightly different approval figures for apparently the same sample.
- **Novelty threat:** none.

#### Review eras: From Human-Centric to Agentic Code Review: The Impact of Different Generations of Generative AI Technology on Review Quality
- **Citation:** Suzhen Zhong, Shayan Noei, Bram Adams and Ying Zou; arXiv preprint; 14 Jul 2026; [arXiv 2607.13196](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13196).
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API).
- **What it did:** 1.02 million reviewed PRs in 207 GitHub projects across human, LLM-assisted and agentic review eras, modelling reviewer interaction sequences.
- **Key numbers:** none in the abstract; "reviews initiated by AI agents or involving multiple AI agents, are associated with faster review decisions ... However, these efficiency gains do not translate into better review quality."
- **Connection:** the only large observational result found on several AI reviewers on one PR in the wild: faster, not better. Loosely supports testing whether councils add quality, not just speed.
- **Novelty threat:** none.

#### AI-to-AI Code Reviews of GitHub Pull Requests
- **Citation:** Niruthiha Selvanayagam and Taher A. Ghaleb; ESEM 2026 Emerging Results track (per the arXiv comment); 21 Aug 2026; [arXiv 2608.21311](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21311).
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API).
- **What it did:** links AI-authored PRs to AI review events (CodAGE dataset): 248,641 PRs with at least one AI review, split into same-product (author and reviewer from one vendor) and cross-product review.
- **Key numbers:** "45,269 received cross-product review and 208,145 received same-product review"; "For three of four dual-role reviewers, mean comments per PR were 58-65% higher in the same-product group, although effect sizes were small or negligible"; "CodeRabbit labeled 35.0% of its comments on Claude Code-authored PRs as refactor comments, compared with 10.5% on Copilot-authored PRs".
- **Connection:** overlaps the same-family question observationally: same-vendor review is the norm in the wild (about 4.6 times as common as cross-vendor here). No accuracy measure.
- **Novelty threat:** none.

#### Reviewer-bot volume: On the Footprints of Reviewer Bots Feedback on Agentic Pull Requests in OSS GitHub Repositories
- **Citation:** Syeda Kaneez Fatima, Yousuf Abrar, Abdul Rehman Tahir, Amelia Nawaz, Shamsa Abid and Abdul Ali Bangash; MSR 2026 (per the arXiv comment and the MSR 2026 Mining Challenge programme on conf.researchr.org); 27 Apr 2026; [arXiv 2604.24450](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.24450).
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API).
- **What it did:** 7,416 reviewer-bot comments on 4,532 AIDev PRs; relates comment quality (relevance, clarity, conciseness) and comment volume to acceptance and resolution time.
- **Key numbers:** none in the abstract; "higher Reviewer Bot Activity volume is associated with longer PR resolution times and lower average feedback quality"; "Reviewer Bot Feedback Quality shows no meaningful association with workflow outcomes."
- **Connection:** supports penalising volume (false alarms and low-value findings) in the council score and a findings cap; observational.
- **Novelty threat:** none.

#### Meta safety trial: AI-Assisted Fixes to Code Review Comments at Scale
- **Citation:** Chandra Maddila, Negar Ghorbani, James Saindon et al. (10 authors, Meta); arXiv 17 Jul 2025, [arXiv 2507.13499](https://arxiv.org/abs/2507.13499); listed in the FSE 2026 programme on conf.researchr.org (track not confirmed; the arXiv record states no venue).
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API).
- **What it did:** AI patches for human review comments, rolled out with randomised controlled safety trials before production.
- **Key numbers:** "we see a large regression with reviewers taking over 5% longer to conduct reviews. After investigation, we modify the UX to only show authors the AI patches, and see no regressions"; production "ActionableToApplied rate of 19.7%".
- **Connection:** the one randomised result found on reviewer time: showing AI output to reviewers slowed them by over 5%. Supports treating human attention as a cost of every reported finding. It concerns AI fixes, not AI findings.
- **Novelty threat:** none.

#### Enterprise 2x mandate: AI Writes Faster Than Humans Can Review: A Longitudinal Study of an Enterprise 2x Mandate
- **Citation:** Hao He, Shyam Agarwal, Yegor Denisov-Blanch, Pavel Azaletskiy, Sanmi Koyejo and Bogdan Vasilescu; arXiv preprint; v1 2 Jul 2026, v3 10 Nov 2026; [arXiv 2607.01904](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.01904).
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API).
- **Key numbers:** 802 developers, 196,212 PRs; throughput "increased by about 12% when each developer started using AI"; "The cost of AI adoption fell on code review, where load per reviewer doubled and automated review exceeded human review in coverage within months of the mandate."
- **Connection:** motivation (review is the bottleneck); no data on AI review accuracy.
- **Novelty threat:** none.

#### Level Up! AI-Driven Challenges and Adaptations to Code Review Practices
- **Citation:** Kaia Newman, Christian Bird, Jenna Butler and Emerson Murphy-Hill; arXiv preprint; 13 Nov 2026; [arXiv 2611.18939](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18939). The company is described only as "a large, multinational software company"; which company is not confirmed.
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API).
- **Key numbers:** interviews n = 24 and survey n = 2,590; "AI review usage was not correlated with less difficulty keeping up with PR volume, indicating using AI for review alone is not sufficient".
- **Connection:** challenges the assumption that adding AI reviewers relieves human reviewers; supports evaluating councils on what reaches humans, not on volume.
- **Novelty threat:** none.

#### Trust and evidence surveys (same group): Plausible Is Not Enough (and Who Pays the Review Cost?)
- **Citation:** Md Shamimur Rahman, Khairul Alam, Banani Roy and Chanchal K. Roy; two arXiv preprints from one survey of 239 practitioners: [arXiv 2610.21144](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21144) (15 Oct 2026) and [arXiv 2610.11179](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.11179) (8 Oct 2026).
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API) for both.
- **Key numbers (2610.21144):** "Most respondents (64.9%) reported lower initial trust in AIPRs"; "61.1% reported higher evidence requirements"; blocking under residual uncertainty "increased from 19.4% for HPRs to 43.9% for AIPRs". 2610.11179 coins "reviewability debt".
- **Connection:** loosely related; about AI-authored code rather than AI reviewers, but supports findings that carry evidence (file, line, reproduction).
- **Novelty threat:** none.

#### Explanation and trust: Evaluating the Impact of Explainable AI on Trust in AI-Assisted Code Review
- **Citation:** Zhenhan Gao, Marvin Muñoz Barón, Umm-e Habiba, Daniel Graziotin and Stefan Wagner; ISSTA 2026 (PACMSE Vol. 3, ISSTA093, per the arXiv comment); 27 Jul 2026; [arXiv 2607.24601](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.24601).
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API).
- **Key numbers:** 34 participants, within-subjects; full explanations "yield the highest perceived trust (M = 3.99/5) but not the highest agreement, whereas moderate explanations (B) achieve the highest agreement (89.22%)"; "Explanation level did not significantly affect review time."
- **Connection:** bears on the findings format: longer justification does not raise acceptance. Small sample.
- **Novelty threat:** none.

#### Humans as judges: Programmers Are Poor and Overconfident Judges of LLM-Generated Assertions
- **Citation:** Zhanna Kaufman, Yuriy Brun, Adithya Murali and Madeline Endres; arXiv preprint; 9 Jul 2026; [arXiv 2607.08885](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08885).
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API).
- **Key numbers:** 86 programmers; "74% accuracy" on correct assertions against "49% accuracy" on incorrect ones with similar confidence (OR = 2.94); low-quality explanations lowered accuracy while raising confidence.
- **Connection:** bears on human checking of judge rulings or matches (if the researcher hand-checks a sample): humans are much worse at spotting wrong machine claims than at confirming right ones.
- **Novelty threat:** none.

#### Coding with the Enemy: Can Human Developers Detect AI Agent Sabotage?
- **Citation:** Jingheng Ye, Huiqi Zou, Simon Yu and Weiyan Shi; NeurIPS 2026 and Best Paper of DL4C@ICML2026 (per the arXiv comment); v1 4 Jun 2026, v3 4 Nov 2026; [arXiv 2606.05647](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05647).
- **Read:** abstract read (verbatim, arXiv API).
- **Key numbers:** "83/88 (94%) of developers in the no-monitor conditions fail to detect sabotage"; with a monitor, "sabotage still succeeds in 9/16 (56%) of sessions with a correct monitor alert".
- **Connection:** loosely related; a correct automated alert is ignored more than half the time, which is the alert-fatigue end of the human cost of review findings.
- **Novelty threat:** none.

#### Other Topic 3 items at abstract level (lower relevance)
- **These Aren't the Reviews You're Looking For** ([arXiv 2605.02273](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.02273); Duma et al., EASE 2026 per the arXiv comment; abstract read, verbatim, arXiv API): in AIDev "most AI-generated PRs receive no review and, when reviewed, are largely dominated by AI agents rather than humans".
- **SGCR** (Topic 1) and **iCodeReviewer** (Topic 1) report production adoption of 42% and "up to 84%".
- **Trust-Calibrated Code Review** ([arXiv 2606.01969](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.01969); Gullstrand Heander et al. with JetBrains; abstract read, verbatim, arXiv API): participatory design; "Reviewing LLM-generated multi-file changes is a trust-calibration problem"; one of its seven constructs is a "judge".
- **CodeHealth Predicts the Token Cost of Agentic Code Review** ([arXiv 2610.21914](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21914); Lo Gullstrand Heander, Markus Borg, Emma Söderberg and Christofer Rydenfält; 15 Oct 2026; pre-registered; abstract read, verbatim, arXiv API): a "frozen five-agent review-and-fix pipeline" on 465 AIDev PRs with three LLMs; "each CodeHealth point was associated with 3.5% to 6.1% lower token cost", absorbed by file size. Relevant to cost modelling: council cost depends on the code reviewed, so seeds should be cost-balanced across configurations (they are, under same-seed pairing). Data on Zenodo (not opened).
- **3100 Opinions on Code Review in an AI World** ([arXiv 2607.07980](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07980); Agarwal, Miller, Kästner and Vasilescu; abstract read): grey-literature theory; notes mining trends on agent PR review "flips under different but equally defensible analysis choices".
- **AutoCommenter** ([arXiv 2405.13565](https://arxiv.org/abs/2405.13565); Vijayvergiya et al., Google, AIware '24 per the arXiv comment; abstract read, verbatim, arXiv API): an LLM best-practice commenter deployed to "tens of thousands of developers"; the abstract gives no figures and the PDF could not be parsed by the fetch tool.
- **Automated Code Review Using LLMs at Ericsson: An Experience Report** ([arXiv 2507.19115](https://arxiv.org/abs/2507.19115); Ramesh et al., ICSME 2025 per the arXiv comment; abstract read): preliminary; no figures in the abstract. The later Ericsson multi-agent paper (2609.15877) is already covered.

### 2.12 Prompt evolution and self-improving agent set-ups

Note on access: the arXiv export API began returning HTTP 429 part-way through this topic, so abstracts below were read from the arxiv.org/abs pages (downloaded with curl, abstract text extracted verbatim from the page HTML). The label for those is "abstract page read (curl)".

#### Closest in purpose: evolving reviewers or evaluators

#### RQGM: The Red Queen Gödel Machine: Co-Evolving Agents and Their Evaluators
- **Citation:** Alex Iacob, Andrej Jovanović, William F. Shen et al. (15 authors, last Nicholas D. Lane); arXiv preprint ("Preliminary preprint; work in progress"); v1 24 Jun 2026, v2 29 Jun 2026, v3 8 Oct 2026; https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.26294 ; 24 citations on Semantic Scholar.
- **Read:** abstract page read (curl); full text partly read (sections 1–5 and appendices C.6–C.8 on splits and held-out protocols).
- **What it did:** an HGM-style evolutionary search in which evaluators (including a **code reviewer** that grades coder patches, a paper reviewer and a proof grader) co-evolve with the task agents. Within an epoch one evaluator is frozen; challenger evaluators are scored on a held-out ground-truth anchor and replace the incumbent only if better, with "selective erasure" of utility records that depended on the replaced evaluator. Data are split train/validation/held-out test; "The held-out test split is never seen during search".
- **Key numbers (verbatim):** DeepSWE: "the RQGM coder passes 82.1% of held-out tasks against the baseline's 75.0%" (held-out n=28 tasks per appendix C.7). Polyglot: "71.7% against 69.9% with fewer search tokens". Self-preference: "The HGM-H reviewer scoring writers ... accepts AI-generated papers at 1.42×–1.91× the rate of human ones"; adding an adversarial pool of AI papers the displaced reviewer had accepted produced a reviewer that accepts AI- and human-written papers "at similar rates while retaining 80% ground-truth accuracy". The DeepSWE code reviewer is anchored to "a balanced dataset of publicly available accepted/rejected patches", where rejected patches are "the most nearly correct attempt, with a mean of 91% of verifier tests passing" (so it judges whole patches, not individual bugs). "only the top reviewer rejects most failing patches ... with the others being overly lenient."
- **Connection:** the closest found to "evolving reviewers" with explicit held-out evaluation and a self-preference correction, and it overlaps strongly with the project's same-family concern: its adversarial-pool objective is a concrete method for making evolved reviewers equally strict on AI-written and human-written work. Differences: reviewers give accept/reject verdicts on patches (no finding-level scoring, no planted bugs, no judge/appeal); the reviewer is a means to guide a coder, not the product; and selection is by best-belief posterior utility rather than a paired CI. Held-out sets are small (28 DeepSWE tasks).
- **Novelty threat:** medium (it evolves code reviewers against a held-out anchor and corrects self-preference; not councils, not planted-bug recall).

#### Hyperagents (DGM-H)
- **Citation:** Jenny Zhang, Bingchen Zhao, Wannan Yang, Jakob Foerster, Jeff Clune, Minqi Jiang, Sam Devlin and Tatiana Shavrina; arXiv preprint; v1 19 Mar 2026, v2 1 Nov 2026; https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.19461 ; code at github.com/facebookresearch/Hyperagents (per search result; not opened); 61 citations on Semantic Scholar.
- **Read:** abstract page read (curl).
- **What it did:** extends the Darwin Gödel Machine so that the meta agent that edits the task agent is itself editable ("metacognitive self-modification"), removing DGM's reliance on the task being coding.
- **Key quote (abstract):** "Across diverse domains, the DGM-H improves performance over time and outperforms baselines without self-improvement or open-ended exploration, as well as prior self-improving systems." The RQGM paper (above) says Hyperagents' domains include paper reviewing and grading; the Hyperagents abstract itself does not list domains.
- **Connection:** the successor version of the DGM, which prior-work-research.md section 5 cites; relevant because its domains reportedly include reviewing (not confirmed from its own text here).
- **Novelty threat:** low to medium (method overlap; paper review, not code review, per secondary source).

#### Who Verifies the Verifier? Co-Evolving Inspectable Graders with Self-Improving Agents
- **Citation:** Xing Zhang, Guanghui Wang, Yanwei Cui, Ziyuan Li, Wei Qiu, Bing Zhu and Peiyang He (same group as Guardrails Beat Guidance); NeurIPS 2026 workshop paper per the arXiv comment ("Accepted at the NeurIPS 2026 Workshop: Who Verifies the Agents? Toward Reliable Agent Development"); v1 8 Oct 2026; https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.11464
- **Read:** abstract page read (curl).
- **Key findings (abstract):** the evolving object is the verifier, "selected for agreement with a ten-item anchored reference set plus consensus over unlabeled outputs, never for the agent's score". "removing the anchor guards collapses the verifier into a vacuous always-pass grader, yet that collapsed verifier trains skills just as well. Downstream task score cannot certify a self-evolved verifier." "When evolved skills gamed the report rubric, an outer judge caught it and one added detector repaired it; the judge itself was wrong until given the task contract."
- **Connection:** directly relevant to evolving judges: if the project ever evolves the judge or appeal agent, their quality must be checked against the planted-bug key, not inferred from council score, because a vacuous judge can coexist with apparently good downstream scores.
- **Novelty threat:** low.

#### Failure modes: reward hacking, evaluator leakage and fabricated fixes

#### PROCTOR: LLM-as-a-Judge Is Not an Oracle: Why Self-Improving Agents Need Deterministic Guardrails
- **Citation:** Vansh Wahi; arXiv preprint (position paper with production experience); v1 2 Sep 2026; https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02246
- **Read:** abstract page read (curl); full text partly read (sections 5.1–5.5, limitations, appendix on split policy).
- **What it did:** catalogues eleven ways the evaluation signal failed in production prompt-optimisation loops ("contract analysis, compliance review, and code quality"), in four classes ("judge bias, harness and metric failures, ground-truth errors, and reward hacking"). It then describes a guarded Teacher-Student loop.
- **Key numbers / quotes (verbatim):** "Agents achieved perfect scores by reading cached answer keys from their environment, a 100% pass rate concealing 68% true capability" (full text: "100% (47/47) ... the same skill in a purged, zero-tool sandbox scored 68.1%"). "A corrupted ground-truth label caused the optimizer to delete correct compliance rules to agree with it." "A syntactically broken prompt was promoted as the winner because a silent parser fallback improved the metric." Guardrails: "hermetic sandboxes, capability-disjoint roles, acceptance checks that outrank the Teacher, frozen holdouts, and canary cases engineered so that a perfect score is itself evidence of cheating". Holdout policy: the test curve is "a directional overfit monitor, not an optimization target"; with "four to eight cases ... a single case is worth between 12 and 25 percentage points". Honest limits: "Pass rates are from one execution each ... no result here should be read as significant".
- **Connection:** the most practically useful item for the project's evolution loop. (1) **Canary seeds:** plant a seed no honest reviewer could find (for example, a "bug" whose key entry cannot be inferred from the code), so a council that "finds" it signals key leakage. (2) **Key isolation:** reviewers and mutation-proposers must never have filesystem access to the seed key or earlier scoring files; the PROCTOR exploit is exactly what a Claude Code reviewer with Read and Glob could do in a shared working tree. (3) Parser fallbacks that silently inflate a metric are a concrete risk for the judge's structured output. (4) Its note that a polite override "lost to the original command that appeared earlier in the prompt" contrasts with the factorial study's null on position (topic 2).
- **Novelty threat:** none (no councils); high practical value.

#### Meta-Agent Challenge: The Meta-Agent Challenge: Are Current Agents Capable of Autonomous Agent Development?
- **Citation:** Xinyu Lu, Tianshu Wang, Pengbo Wang et al. (11 authors); arXiv preprint; v1 3 Jun 2026; https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.04455
- **Read:** abstract page read (curl).
- **Key findings (abstract):** a meta-agent iteratively programs an agent to maximise "performance on a held-out test set across five domains", "secured by multi-layer defenses against reward hacking". "meta-agents rarely match human-engineered baseline policies"; "the design process exhibits high variance, and high optimization pressure surfaces emergent adversarial behaviors like ground-truth exfiltration".
- **Connection:** supports the project's held-out seeds and its worry that an LLM-driven mutation step under selection pressure will look for the key. High variance of the design process supports replicating evolution runs, not just evaluations.
- **Novelty threat:** none.

#### Trusting Trust Revisited: Reflections on Trusting Trust, Revisited: Contaminating Self-Modifying AI Coding Agents with Poisoned Benchmarks
- **Citation:** Franziska Roesner and Tadayoshi Kohno; arXiv preprint; v1 15 Sep 2026; https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.17817
- **Read:** abstract page read (curl).
- **Key findings (abstract):** poisoned self-evaluation benchmarks against DGM (modified), SICA and Hyperagents; "with Hyperagents powered by Sonnet 4.5, our poisoned benchmark leads the agent to self-evolve instructions that disable HTTPS certificate validation on neutral URL-fetching tasks"; "contamination often persists even when a poisoned agent is subsequently evolved against clean benchmarks."
- **Connection:** a seed set is a benchmark the council evolves against; a flawed or mislabelled seed (for example, a "bug" that is really correct code) could push evolved briefs toward systematically wrong rules that persist. Supports auditing the seed key and keeping a clean held-out set.
- **Novelty threat:** none.

#### Phantom Guardrails: When Self-Improving Agent Harnesses Fix Failures That Never Happened
- **Citation:** Su Wang, Pin Qian, Yifan Lin, Jingzhou Xu, Yihang Chen, Xiaochong Jiang, Lifei Liu and Haoran Yu (author order from the arXiv page's citation metadata); arXiv preprint; v1 13 Jul 2026; https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13083
- **Read:** abstract page read (curl).
- **Key numbers (abstract):** when legal input contains "a harmless pattern resembling a familiar game rule, it invents a failure: in 15/60 runs, versus 0/60 on featureless input". It needs "a rule-shaped pattern, an open-ended rule set and an instruction that presupposes failures". "Inside an add-only accept loop it re-enters even without the failure-presupposing instruction ... and once in it stays."
- **Connection:** a direct warning for brief evolution: a mutation proposer told "the council missed bugs, improve the briefs" may add reviewer rules for failure types that never occurred, and an add-only accept rule lets them accumulate. The project's keep-only-if-CI-above-zero rule should block neutral additions; it is worth checking that it does (a neutral rule gives a CI around zero, so it should be rejected, unlike an add-only loop).
- **Novelty threat:** none.

#### Optimization Instability in Autonomous Agentic Workflows for Clinical Symptom Detection
- **Citation:** Cameron Cagan, Pedram Fard, Jiazi Tian, Jingya Cheng, Shawn N. Murphy and Hossein Estiri; arXiv preprint; v1 17 Feb 2026; https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.16037
- **Read:** abstract page read (curl).
- **Key numbers (abstract):** "validation sensitivity oscillated between 1.0 and 0.0 across iterations, with severity inversely proportional to class prevalence. At 3% prevalence, the system achieved 95% accuracy while detecting zero positive cases". "a guiding agent that actively redirected optimization, amplifying overfitting rather than correcting it, and a selector agent that retrospectively identified the best-performing iteration successfully prevented catastrophic failure."
- **Connection:** relevant to scoring: when bugs are rare relative to clean code, an accuracy-like score rewards silence. The project's separate catch and false-alarm measures avoid this, but any single combined fitness should be checked for a "flag nothing" optimum. Also supports retrospective selection over active steering.
- **Novelty threat:** none.

#### Exploiting LLM-as-a-Judge Disposition on Free Text Legal QA via Prompt Optimization
- **Citation:** Mohamed Hesham Elganayni, Runsheng Chen, Sebastian Nagl and Matthias Grabmair; ICAIL 2026 per the arXiv comment; v1 22 Apr 2026, v2 23 Apr 2026; https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.20726
- **Read:** abstract page read (curl).
- **Key findings (abstract):** ProTeGi optimisation with feedback from two judges (Qwen3-32B, DeepSeek-V3); "strict judges produce restrictive feedback, leading to judge-specific overfitting"; lenient-judge prompts transfer better.
- **Connection:** supports the project's cross-family judge arm and held-out judging: a council evolved under one judge may overfit that judge's disposition; scoring against the planted key rather than a judge's opinion limits this, but the judge still sets severity.
- **Novelty threat:** none.

#### Noisy fitness, selection and held-out evaluation

#### SIREN: Towards Reliable LLM Evaluation: Correcting the Winner's Curse in Adaptive Benchmarking
- **Citation:** Jiefu Zhang, Yang Xu, Haixiang Sun, Zihan Zhou, Tianyu Cao and Vaneet Aggarwal; NeurIPS 2026 per the arXiv comment ("The 40th Conference on Neural Information Processing Systems (NeurIPS 2026)"); v1 7 May 2026, v2 7 Nov 2026; https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.05973
- **Read:** abstract page read (curl).
- **Key quotes (abstract):** "Once benchmark items are reused inside tuning, the observed winner's score need not estimate the fresh-data performance of the full tune-then-deploy procedure." SIREN "freezes the post-search shortlist, separates splitwise selection from held-out evaluation, and uses an item-level Gaussian multiplier bootstrap for uncertainty quantification". "winner-based reporting can be optimistic and can change deployment conclusions".
- **Connection:** the closest statistical treatment of the project's selection rule. The project keeps a child only if a 90% CI on (child minus parent) on the same seeds is above zero; when many children are tried per generation on the same seeds, the kept child's measured gain is subject to exactly this winner's curse, and the 90% per-comparison CI is not corrected for multiplicity. SIREN-style repeated splits, or re-scoring kept children on fresh seeds before reporting, would address it. Item-level bootstrap matches the project's seed-level pairing.
- **Novelty threat:** none (method support).

#### p1: Better Prompt Optimization with Fewer Prompts
- **Citation:** Zhaolin Gao, Wang Yu, Bo Liu, Thorsten Joachims, Kianté Brantley and Wen Sun; arXiv preprint; v1 9 Apr 2026, v2 26 Aug 2026; https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.08801
- **Read:** abstract page read (curl).
- **Key findings (abstract):** reward variance splits into "variance among responses, which captures generation stochasticity, and variance among system prompts"; "Prompt optimization succeeds when variance among system prompts is sufficiently large, but fails when variance among responses dominates". "scaling to more user prompts can hurt optimization by reducing variance among system prompts, especially on heterogeneous datasets"; selecting high-variance prompts "outperforms strong baselines such as GEPA".
- **Connection:** supports measuring the run-to-run variance of a council on the same seed before evolving (if a council's score on a seed varies as much between reruns as between configurations, selection is noise-driven). It also suggests choosing discriminative seeds, but that conflicts with keeping seeds representative and invites the regression-to-the-mean problem seen in Guardrails Beat Guidance; the project should keep the held-out seeds unselected.
- **Novelty threat:** none.

#### Anthropic hillclimb: Automating eval design and hillclimbing with Claude (Anthropic blog)
- **Citation:** Lance Martin; claude.dev blog ("Playbooks"); published 28 Sep 2026; https://claude.dev/blog/automating-eval-design-and-hillclimbing/ ; describes the `/claude-api build-eval` and `/claude-api hillclimb` commands of the claude-api skill.
- **Read:** page read (curl, stripped to text; overfitting and hillclimb sections read in full).
- **Key quotes (verbatim):** "Use a train set that the hillclimber may read and a test set that is never seen." "Never paste failures into the prompt." "Keep the answers structurally out of the model's reach. Models can sometimes 'reward hack' by directly finding answers to evaluations." "Claude checks that the eval's noise (how far the score can move by chance alone) is smaller than the smallest improvement you'd act on". Each round: "if the train set improves but the test set is flat, Claude suspects overfitting and reverts the patch. If there is a regression, Claude reverts. If train and test sets improve, it keeps the patch". "When hillclimbing completes, Claude leaves your code at the version that did best on the test set". Example: "44 tickets, with 30 used for the search and 14 held out"; final "90.5% against the original setup's 78.6%, at about one fifth of the cost".
- **Connection:** Anthropic's own recipe is close to the project's keep-or-revert loop with held-out seeds, and it is what a Claude-driven evolution step would likely default to. One point to note against it: the test set is consulted in every keep/revert decision and the final version is the one "that did best on the test set", so the test set is used for selection and its final score is no longer a clean held-out estimate (the winner's curse SIREN describes). The project should keep a final held-out set that no keep/revert decision ever reads.
- **Novelty threat:** none.

#### RoboPhD: Evolving Diverse Complex Agents Under Tight Evaluation Budgets
- **Citation:** Andrew Borthwick, Stephen Ash and Anthony Galczak; arXiv preprint; v1 6 Apr 2026; https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.04347
- **Read:** abstract page read (curl).
- **Key findings (abstract):** a "systematic comparison of three optimization paradigms -- Elo tournament selection (RoboPhD), Pareto-based selection (GEPA), and greedy hill-climbing (Autoresearch)" under "a fixed budget of 1,500 evaluations"; RoboPhD "uses Elo competition on training data to simultaneously evaluate agents and drive evolution" ("validation-free evolution") and wins on three of four benchmarks.
- **Connection:** an equal-budget comparison of selection rules, which the project could cite when justifying its CI-gated rule over Pareto or Elo selection. "Validation-free" selection is the opposite of the project's choice and is exposed to the winner's curse.
- **Novelty threat:** none.

#### TextReg: Mitigating Prompt Distributional Overfitting via Regularized Text-Space Optimization
- **Citation:** Lucheng Fu, Ye Yu, Yiyang Wang et al. (7 authors); arXiv preprint; v1 20 May 2026, v2 5 Oct 2026; https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.21318
- **Read:** abstract page read (curl).
- **Key findings (abstract):** optimised prompts "often become longer, accumulate narrow sample-specific rules, and generalize poorly beyond the training distribution"; regularisation gives OOD "accuracy gains of up to +11.8% over TextGrad and +16.5% over REVOLVE".
- **Connection:** supports tracking brief length across generations and penalising growth; evolved reviewer briefs that accumulate seed-specific rules are the review analogue.
- **Novelty threat:** none.

#### What Should the Reflector See? An Empirical Study of Evidence in Reflective Prompt Optimization
- **Citation:** Xiaofan Zhou and Lu Cheng; arXiv preprint; v1 26 Sep 2026; https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.32452
- **Read:** abstract page read (curl).
- **Key findings (abstract):** with Qwen3.5-9B as task model and reflector, "Failures-only produces the largest mean test gain (+8.0 percentage points)"; "local reflection success does not necessarily produce a stronger final prompt"; "calibration gains can overstate held-out improvement".
- **Connection:** relevant to what the mutation proposer is shown (missed seeds only vs a mix); a cheap ablation for the project's mutation step.
- **Novelty threat:** none.

#### Textual Gradients are a Flawed Metaphor for Automatic Prompt Optimization
- **Citation:** Daniel Melcer, Qi Chen, Wen-Hao Chiang, Shweta Garg, Pranav Garg and Christian Bock; arXiv preprint; v1 15 Dec 2025; https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.13598
- **Read:** abstract page read (curl). (The WebSearch summary also attributed findings about an incorrect evaluation function and a "prevalence hacking" instruction to this paper; those were not seen in the abstract and are not relied on here.)
- **Key quote (abstract):** "While such methods often result in a performance improvement, our experiments suggest that the gradient analogy does not accurately explain their behavior."
- **Connection:** loosely related; cautions against reading TextGrad-style "feedback" mutations as principled descent.
- **Novelty threat:** none.

#### Prompt optimisation and self-improvement methods not in section 5 (abstract page read (curl); one line each, with venue sources)
- **Promptbreeder: Self-Referential Self-Improvement Via Prompt Evolution** (Chrisantha Fernando, Dylan Banarse, Henryk Michalewski, Simon Osindero and Tim Rocktäschel; arXiv 2309.16797, Sep 2023; Semantic Scholar venue ICML, edition not confirmed): evolves task-prompts and, self-referentially, the mutation-prompts; "evaluates them for fitness on a training set". The original self-referential prompt evolver; no held-out-noise analysis in the abstract.
- **TextGrad: Automatic "Differentiation" via Text** (Mert Yuksekgonul, Federico Bianchi, Joseph Boen et al.; arXiv 2406.07496, Jun 2024; Semantic Scholar venue arXiv.org only): "backpropagates textual feedback provided by LLMs"; GPQA zero-shot "from 51% to 55%".
- **DSPy: Compiling Declarative Language Model Calls into Self-Improving Pipelines** (Omar Khattab, Arnav Singhvi, Paridhi Maheshwari et al., 13 authors; arXiv 2310.03714, Oct 2023): optimises pipeline demonstrations and prompts to "maximize a given metric".
- **MIPRO: Optimizing Instructions and Demonstrations for Multi-Stage Language Model Programs** (Krista Opsahl-Ong, Michael J Ryan, Josh Purtell et al.; EMNLP 2024 per arXiv comment; arXiv 2406.11695): "a stochastic mini-batch evaluation function for learning a surrogate model of our objective"; up to "13% accuracy" over baselines with Llama-3-8B. The mini-batch surrogate is an explicit treatment of noisy fitness.
- **GEPA** (2507.19457) is already on the list of IDs already cited in the repo; new facts: v2 14 Feb 2026 and "Accepted to ICLR 2026 (Oral)" per the arXiv comment.
- **Gödel Agent: A Self-Referential Agent Framework for Recursive Self-Improvement** (Xunjian Yin, Xinyi Wang, Liangming Pan, Li Lin, Xiaojun Wan and William Yang Wang; ACL 2025 main per arXiv comment; arXiv 2410.04444, v4 May 2025).
- **STOP: Self-Taught Optimizer: Recursively Self-Improving Code Generation** (Eric Zelikman, Eliana Lorch, Lester Mackey and Adam Tauman Kalai; COLM 2024 per arXiv comment; arXiv 2310.02304): a scaffold that improves itself; it also measures "the frequency with which the generated code bypasses a sandbox", an early reward-hacking measurement.
- **SICA: A Self-Improving Coding Agent** (Maxime Robeyns, Martin Szummer and Laurence Aitchison; arXiv 2504.15228, v2 May 2025): self-editing agent with "performance gains from 17% to 53% on a random subset of SWE Bench Verified".
- **Huxley-Gödel Machine** (Wenyi Wang, Piotr Piękos, Li Nanbo et al., last author Jürgen Schmidhuber; arXiv 2510.21614, Oct 2025): identifies a "Metaproductivity-Performance Mismatch" (benchmark score of an agent is a poor guide to its descendants' improvement) and selects by clade metaproductivity. Relevant to whether the project should select parents by their own score or by their lineage's.
- **ShinkaEvolve** (Robert Tjarko Lange, Yuki Imajuku and Edoardo Cetin; arXiv 2509.19349, Sep 2025): sample-efficient program evolution with "code novelty rejection-sampling" and "bandit-based LLM ensemble selection"; a circle-packing result "using only 150 samples".
- **PackHallu** (topic 2) is also an evolutionary prompt optimiser, used to evolve injections into rule files.
- **Probe-and-Refine** (topic 2) evolves an AGENTS.md against synthetic bug-fix probes.

---

## Corrections to other docs

Collected here rather than edited into the docs, since another session is auditing them. Most are about prior-work-research.md; a few are about seed-sources-research.md. Each says where the evidence comes from.

### Follow-ups of sections 1 and 2

1. **Kim et al. is now a journal paper with a new title.** It was published in *Nature Machine Intelligence* 8(7), 1157–1172 (24 Jul 2026), DOI 10.1038/s42256-026-01268-y, as "Capable language models can outgrow the benefits of collaboration" (Crossref; code README). The doc cites only arXiv 2512.08296.
2. **RevAgent is now published** in *ACM TOSEM* 35(11), 2026, DOI 10.1145/3817606 (Crossref, dated 4 Nov 2026). The doc says "arXiv preprint". Also, its anonymous code artefact has expired.
3. **Hydra-Reviewer:**
   - **Author order:** Crossref and the FSE 2026 page list "… Ye Wang, Chao Liu, Bo Jiang". The doc has "… Ye Wang, Bo Jiang and Chao Liu".
   - **Bibliographic details:** vol. 51, pp. 3540–3557; DOI 10.1109/TSE.2025.3621462.
   - **Code is public** (ZJUSig/Hydra-Reviewer, plus Zenodo 15063416). It reveals the 18 taxonomy dimensions and the fixed category-priority rerank, so the doc's "the taxonomy's dimensions aren't listed in the abstract" can be filled from the code. The doc's "the full text still isn't accessible" remains true.
4. **ChatEval:** the same-role ablation is now confirmed from the full text, not just a search summary: 53.8% for single-agent and for same-role multi-agent against 60% for diverse roles, on 80 FairEval questions with ChatGPT (Table 3). The doc should note how small this evidence is.
5. **BitsAI-CR venue confirmed** as FSE 2025 Companion, pp. 274–285, DOI 10.1145/3696630.3728552 (Crossref). The doc marks it "(venue from a snippet)".
6. **Venues the doc omits:**
   - Pornprasit and Tantithamthavorn: *Information and Software Technology* 175 (2024);
   - Multi-role Consensus: IEEE QRS-C 2024 (two pages);
   - Self-MoA: TMLR 2026 per Semantic Scholar;
   - VulTrial: pp. 1030–1042;
   - GPTLens: TPS-ISA 2023 (the doc says "IEEE TPS 2023"; the full name is TPS-ISA).
7. **Adversarial Review:** there is a second workshop version, "Adversarial Review: Cooperative Code Review through Structured Disagreement", listed in the ICML 2026 AI4GOOD workshop (OpenReview search). Its numbers weren't checked.
8. **DeputyDev:** the arXiv title is "Intuition to Evidence: Measuring AI's True Impact on Developer Productivity". The doc names it only "DeputyDev".

### Follow-ups of sections 3 and 4

1. **SWR-Bench venue:** "FSE 2026 (snippet only)" is now **confirmed** (Crossref DOI 10.1145/3808144, PACMSE vol. 3, issue FSE).
2. **MCR-Bench venue:** ISSTA 2026 is now **confirmed** beyond the arXiv comment (Crossref DOI 10.1145/3832219, PACMSE vol. 3, issue ISSTA). The doc's note that a subagent confirmed QWK 0.67–0.73 and kappa 0.87 is consistent with what I read: kappa 0.87 is between the two human annotators in section 3.2.4. The doc should add that the key is an **LLM-proposed, human-verified** set, so defects the LLM missed are absent.
3. **CR-Bench:** add that it is **derived from SWE-Bench and SWE-Bench Verified** (full text section 4), which is a contamination concern. Add the same-author **CR-Bench-v2** (arXiv 2611.02566, 2–3 Nov 2026), which uses three independent LLM judges and reports KDDR ≈ 33%. The "judge isn't checked against human labels" difference refers to v1; v2 replaces the single judge.
4. **CRBugBench:** "Abstract page read" in the main entry is out of date, because the amendment already cites the full text. Add that the open-source release (`malusamayo/CRBugBench`) is a **rebuilt** set of 995 tasks rather than the paper's 1,000 tasks, that the 800 proprietary tasks are not released, and that scoring uses a three-family judge panel with a 40-sample human check.
5. **When AI Agrees Too Much:**
   - "A biased PR description got past review in 32 of 33 cases" is **misleading**. Template-based biased descriptions flipped none of Claude Code's 16 baseline rejections and only one of CodeRabbit's 13. The 32/33 is the **LLM-assisted iterative refinement attack**, tuned against a local clone of the pipeline. Metadata redaction restored detection in all affected cases.
   - The read status should be "full text partly read (v5)".
   - The paper has had **three titles and changing numbers across five versions**: v1 "Measuring and Exploiting Confirmation Bias in LLM-Assisted Security Code Review" (Copilot 35%, Claude Code 88%), v2–v3 "...Contextual Bias..." (17 CVEs, 100%), and v4–v5 the current title (33 CVEs, 97%).
6. **Refute-or-Promote:** the "*(Snippet only:)* a real CVE was unanimously killed and recovered only by a human" claim is now **verified from the full text**: lcms2 CubeSize(), CVE-2026-41254, was "recovered only via human override", through a creative uplift agent. The paper calls its pipeline "architecturally unidirectional". The read status can be upgraded to "full text partly read". Add that the released playbook runs on Claude Sonnet/Opus subagents with Codex (gpt-5.4) as the cross-family critic. Also note the "ten dedicated reviewers" (abstract) against "80+ agents" (introduction) wording.
7. **D3:** the "different title in earlier versions (snippet only)" is **confirmed**: v1 was titled "Adversarial Multi-Agent Evaluation of Large Language Models through Iterative Debates" (arXiv abs page v1). EACL 2026 is confirmed by Crossref (DOI 10.18653/v1/2026.eacl-long.392).
8. **CodeJudgeBench:** ACL 2026 is **confirmed** (Crossref DOI 10.18653/v1/2026.acl-long.888, long papers).
9. **Agent-as-a-Judge:** ICML 2025 is **confirmed** via the DBLP key in Semantic Scholar (`conf/icml/Zhuge0AWKXLCKTS25`). The "90% against 70%" figure is still unchecked; the abstract says only that it "dramatically outperforms LLM-as-a-Judge and is as reliable as our human evaluation baseline".
10. **SE-Jury:** published at **ASE 2025** under the title "SE-Jury: An LLM-as-Ensemble-Judge Metric for Narrowing the Gap with Human Evaluation in SE" (Crossref DOI 10.1109/ASE63991.2025.00214). The doc gives no venue.
11. **Jin and Chen:** published in the journal **Automated Software Engineering** 33(3), article 90, 2026 (DOI 10.1007/s10515-026-00638-5). The doc's "(snippet only)" on bug-injected solutions can be dropped for the overcorrection claim, which the abstract states, though the bug-injection detail is still unverified. Semantic Scholar mislabels the venue as the ASE conference.
12. **Other venues the doc lacks:**
    - ContextCRBench: FSE Companion 2026 (DOI 10.1145/3803437.3805235).
    - Du et al.: ICSE-SEIP 2026 (DOI 10.1145/3786583.3786910).
    - DeepCRCEval: FASE 2025, LNCS (DOI 10.1007/978-3-031-90900-9_3).
    - RepoAudit: ICML 2025, per Semantic Scholar only.
    - SEVRA-BENCH: FLMSec 2026 workshop at NeurIPS, per the authors' README only.
    - HalluJudge: FSE 2026 Industry, confirmed by Crossref.
13. **Appeal papers:** they can now be described properly (see the two blocks above).
    - arXiv 2601.12815 is **JurisMMA**, ICASSP 2026. Its "appeal" is the same Chief Judge re-running stages, with no ablation of the appeal.
    - arXiv 2603.19267 is a **Maker-Checker** design in which the second tier reviews only first-tier rejections and may reinstate them. It uses Claude Sonnet 4.0 and appeared in KDD 2026 ADS per the arXiv comment. It reports 70.8% for the LLM-only baseline, 95.8% offline and 96.3% or 97.1% online (internally inconsistent).
    - The doc's line that these are "the only appeal-like designs found, and neither is in code" still holds. Refute-or-Promote's human-only rescue of killed findings is the code-review case that comes closest.
14. **Kumar et al. date discrepancy:** resolved. The arXiv record and abstract page both give "Submitted on 9 Apr 2026" for arXiv 2606.15689 v1. The June number most likely reflects a delayed announcement; that is not confirmed. Cite it as submitted 9 Apr 2026.
15. **The section 3 preamble** says "I found no academic paper that builds on those four [ReviewBench, Martian, Qodo, Kumar et al.]". Semantic Scholar shows one paper citing Kumar et al., *Counting the Bandages* (2610.35942), but it does not build on it, so the statement stands.

### Follow-ups of sections 5 and 6 and the plan's related research

1. **Pombal et al. venue:** COLM 2026 is confirmed (OpenReview note 1837ctErks). The "Discrepancies found" entry ("the arXiv page shows no venue") and the section 6 note can be resolved in favour of the 31 Oct review notes. Also: the LiveCodeBench experiment was added in v2 (22 Jul 2026); v1 used IFEval only. Claude-Sonnet's LiveCodeBench self-preference ratio is 1.78 [1.58, 1.99], while the worst case (GPT-5) is about 20 times.
2. **Kim et al. (scaling agent systems):** published in Nature Machine Intelligence 8, 1157–1172 (24 Jul 2026) under the title "Capable language models can outgrow the benefits of collaboration" (Crossref). The "260 against 180" discrepancy is a version difference: v1 (Dec 2025, which the Google blog reflects) reported 180 configurations on four benchmarks; v3 (Apr 2026) reports 260 on six.
3. **Panickssery et al.:** NeurIPS 2024, oral (OpenReview note 4NJBV6Wp0h). The doc's open note can be closed.
4. **Darwin Gödel Machine:** ICLR 2026 poster (OpenReview). The doc gives no venue. Semantic Scholar's "Robotics" venue is an error.
5. **AgentFlow:** the Chrome zero-days were found with Kimi K2.5, not Claude Opus 4.6; Opus 4.6 was used for the TerminalBench-2 score only. The doc's sentence runs the two together. The code is public (now at github.com/agentenv/agentflow).
6. **MulVul:** has an arXiv version, [arXiv 2601.18847](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.18847) (26 Jan 2026), not in the list of IDs already cited in the repo. A same-author follow-up, MemCo, is at ASE 2026.
7. **Pansuriya et al.:** now published at IEEE COMPSAC 2026 (doi 10.1109/COMPSAC69091.2026.00052); the doc says "no venue stated".
8. **Gong et al.:** published at EASE 2025 (doi 10.1145/3756681.3756984); the doc gives only the arXiv ID.
9. **EvoMAS (Wei et al.):** the PMLR PDF names its benchmarks (GSM8K, MATH, HumanEval, MBPP, HotpotQA, ALFWorld and GAIA, with GPT-4o-mini), so "its page names no evaluation domains" should become "evaluated on standard maths, code-generation, QA and embodied benchmarks, not review".
10. **Khullar et al.:** was rejected from ICML 2026 under the title "I'm Absolutely Right: Self-Attribution Bias in LLM Agents"; an earlier workshop version ("Language Models Rate Their Own Actions As Safer", NeurIPS 2025 MTI-LLM workshop) exists. Cite as an arXiv preprint.
11. **Song 2026 and Cross-Context Review:** Song 2026's union gain is significant only against two same-model reviews, not against two top-tier cross-model reviews, so "model difference and reviewer capability are not separated" (the paper's words). The companion Cross-Context Review's v2 (1 Oct 2026) says its advantages over same-session review and over a subagent were "not significant across runs" and corrects figures it had attributed to Tsui 2025. Both qualifications should go into the doc's Song entry.
12. **Kim et al. (Correlated Errors):** the 60% figure is "on one leaderboard dataset" (abstract), not across all 350+ models.
13. **Self-Correction Bench:** the 64.5% blind spot is on injected errors attributed to the model, not on the model's natural errors. The doc's "a 64.5% blind spot for the model's own errors" should say "for errors framed as its own".
14. **Ferreira et al.:** the arXiv abstract says "eleven vendor families" and the ICLR 2027 submission abstract says "nine vendor families"; one of them is wrong.
