15. **Greptile:** a later Greptile post (9 Oct 2026) claims "bug catch rates go up ~10%" from model inversion, against the 2–3 recall points the doc read off the original chart.
16. **ADAS and AFlow** are listed in the doc without challenges; both now have published re-evaluations that cut against their headline results (The Illusion of Multi-Agent Advantage; Simple Baselines are Competitive with Code Evolution). The ADAS one reports, from the authors, that the published numbers came from picking the best candidate on the test set.
17. **ARTEMIS:** the arXiv title is "Evolving Excellence: Automated Optimization of LLM-based Agents"; cite it by that title.

### New searches: multi-agent review, industry tools and human costs

- **CR-Bench (2603.11078):** the doc gives it as an arXiv preprint. OpenReview API search metadata lists "CR-Bench: Evaluating the Real-World Utility of AI Code Review Agents" with venue "ICLR 2026 AIWILD" (a workshop). Not checked on the OpenReview page itself (blocked). Separately, the same authors posted **CR-Bench-v2** as a new arXiv record ([2611.02566](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02566), 2–3 Nov 2026), which adds a Usefulness Rate metric, three independent LLM judges, Gemini Code Assist (8.1% KDDR) and per-instance labels for "bug category, impact, and severity". The doc's entry ("the judge isn't checked against human labels") may be out of date for v2 and should be rechecked against its full text; the severity labels also matter for the doc's claim that only MCR-Bench has severity in its key.
- **"Checking the judge's severity calls against human labels: looks new. None found. MCR-Bench has severity in its key":** CR-Bench-v2 now also carries severity labels, and Greptile's vendor benchmark reports catch rates by severity (Critical, High, Medium/Low). Neither checks a judge's severity calls, so the "looks new" verdict stands, but the list of keys with severity should grow.
- **RevAgent (2511.00517):** listed as an arXiv preprint; its title ("Issue-Oriented Agent-Based Framework for Automated Review Comment Generation") appears in the ASE 2026 programme on conf.researchr.org (track not confirmed). Worth checking for a venue.
- **SWR-Bench (2509.01494):** the doc marks FSE 2026 as snippet only; the title is in the FSE 2026 programme on conf.researchr.org, which supports the venue (track not confirmed).
- **HalluJudge (2601.19072):** its title is in the FSE 2026 programme, consistent with the doc.
- **"An appeal that can reinstate the judge's dismissals: looks new. Every filter found is one-way."** Still true after this sweep: Bugbot's validator, Anthropic's Code Review verification step, Qodo's judge agent, the Anthropic plugin's confidence threshold, AEGIS's veto auditor, the "3+1" verifier and CIVER are all one-way filters. None restores a dismissed finding.
- **"Several LLM reviewer agents in role seats, with a judge or critic: yes, plenty"** should now cite industry too: Anthropic's own managed Code Review ("several agents ... in parallel", a verification step, severity ranking), its open `code-review` plugin (4 seats plus per-finding confidence scorers, threshold 80), Qodo 2.0 (specialist agents plus a judge agent plus severity) and Bugbot's 2025 design (8 shuffled passes, majority vote, validator). The external baseline `/code-review ultra` is itself a "multi-agent fleet with independent verification" per the docs, so the paper should describe the comparison as council against council.
- **Same-family row:** add CyberDual (2610.34435): a second vendor's reviewer "catches about two thirds of a Claude reviewer's misses" on planted, execution-checked bugs, and Claude reviewers' misses are partly shared (kappa up to 0.69). This is closer to the project's set-up than Xiang et al. and should be in the "strongly relevant" list.

### New searches: debate, judge diversity and appeals

- **Section 4, "Snippet only, not read" (Reviewing the Reviewer, arXiv 2603.19267):** the abstract is now read. It is a KDD 2026 Applied Data Science Track paper (per the arXiv comment) on maker-checker review of e-commerce seller appeals, with LLM-human alignment rising from 70.8% (LLM-only) to 95.8% offline and 96.3% in production. It is a genuine two-tier design where the second tier corrects the first in either direction.
- **Section 4, same line (legal-judgment prediction, arXiv 2601.12815):** the abstract (now read, arXiv API) is JurisMMA, accepted to ICASSP 2026 per the arXiv comment. The abstract says the framework "decomposes trial tasks, standardizes processes, and organizes them into distinct stages" but does not mention an appeal stage. The "appeal stage" claim rests only on a snippet and should not be cited without checking the full text.
- **"How new is this?" table, appeal row ("Every filter found is one-way"):** true within code review and static-analysis filtering as far as this sweep found too, but reinstating second checks do exist elsewhere and should be cited so the claim is not overstated: TinyV (2505.14625) re-examines answers a rule-based verifier rejected and recovers false negatives (over 38% of responses were false negatives); Reviewing the Reviewer (2603.19267) is a production maker-checker appeal; Klieber et al. (2607.09979) block dismissal when repeated judge runs disagree. Suggested wording: "no code-review or SAST pipeline found reinstates dismissed findings; reinstating second checks exist for RL answer verification and in human-supervised appeal workflows".
- **Section 6, "Correlated errors" (Goel et al.):** not a correction but a strengthening: Hossain, Yousefi and Lim (2609.22512) measure error correlation directly in judges and find it higher among strong frontier judges from different providers (ρ̄ ≈ 0.56, about 1.4 effective judges out of three, fetch-tool figures), consistent with Goel et al.'s "mistakes grow more similar as models get stronger".

### New searches: planted bugs, SZZ and measures

- **CR-Bench entry ("the judge isn't checked against human labels"):** out of date. CR-Bench-v2 ([arXiv 2611.02566](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02566), 2 Nov 2026, a new arXiv record) reports a human check: three experts labelled 60 reviews and Claude Sonnet 4.5 matched the human majority on 71.7% (fetch tool), and it now uses three judges (GPT-5.4, Claude Sonnet 4.5, DeepSeek V4 Flash) with instance-level bootstrap intervals. The headline "best recall was 32.8%" is now reported as "KDDR ≈ 33%".
- **SWE-PRBench:** prior-work-research.md cites it only inside the Adversarial Review entry ("100 real PRs ... kappa 0.75"). Its own record ([arXiv 2603.26130](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.26130)) describes "350 pull requests" with the judge "validated at kappa=0.75". The 100-PR figure may be the subset Adversarial Review used; worth checking which is meant.
- **Not a correction, but a caution on seed-sources-research.md** (outside this doc's scope but relevant): its "Contamination: high" for Defects4J rests on potential memorisation; Euraste et al. ([arXiv 2604.13997](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.13997), ICSE 2026 per the arXiv comment) measure low memorisation *advantage* on Defects4J and CVEfixes for open models. Its open note on SWE-rebench v2 can now be answered: [arXiv 2602.23866](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.23866) (ICML 2026 per the arXiv comment) is a 20-language, 32,079-task collection. Its Martian entry should note that Martian's offline set has the same five projects and the same PR count (50) as Greptile's July 2025 benchmark, later re-scored by Augment and Luxor; it is probably the same PR set (not confirmed), in which case it has been public and heavily used for over a year.
- I found nothing in sections 3 or 6 of prior-work-research.md that the new items contradict on councils, judges or appeals.

### New searches: workflows, instruction files and evolution

Nothing found to be wrong; the following are out of date or incomplete in section 5:
- **ADAS** (2408.08435) and **AFlow** (2410.10762): the table gives "(2024–2025)" with no venue; Semantic Scholar lists both under "International Conference on Learning Representations" (record year 2024; the edition is not confirmed here).
- **MaAS** (2502.04180): no venue in the table; Semantic Scholar lists "International Conference on Machine Learning", 2025.
- **Darwin Gödel Machine** (2505.22954): now at v3 (12 Mar 2026), per the arXiv abstract page. Its successor **Hyperagents** (arXiv 2603.19461, v2 1 Nov 2026) makes the meta agent editable and, per the RQGM paper, includes paper reviewing and grading among its domains; it is not cited in section 5.
- **Missing from section 5 and arguably close:** the **Red Queen Gödel Machine** (arXiv 2606.26294), which co-evolves a code reviewer with a coder against held-out anchors and corrects reviewer self-preference with an adversarial pool (novelty threat medium, see topic 3); **Who Verifies the Verifier?** (2610.11464) on evolving graders.
- **GEPA** (2507.19457, already covered elsewhere in the repo): now "Accepted to ICLR 2026 (Oral)" with v2 14 Feb 2026, per the arXiv comment.
- **Rethinking the Value of Multi-Agent Workflow** (2601.12307) is cited in section 5 as support for a strong single-reviewer baseline; Single-agent or Multi-agent Systems? Why Not Both? (2505.18286) makes the same point and could sit beside it.

- **arXiv API** (sorted by submission date, descending; script that skipped IDs already cited in the repo): `abs:"code review" AND (abs:"multi-agent" OR abs:multiagent)`; `abs:"code review" AND (abs:ensemble OR abs:panel OR abs:council OR abs:critic OR abs:verifier)`; `abs:"code review" AND (abs:agent OR abs:agents OR abs:agentic)` (100); `abs:"code review" AND (abs:debate OR abs:"self-consistency" OR abs:voting OR abs:aggregation OR abs:judge)`; `(abs:"bug detection" OR abs:"defect detection" OR abs:"vulnerability detection") AND (abs:"multi-agent" OR ...) AND abs:LLM`; `abs:review AND abs:"pull request" AND (abs:"multiple LLMs" OR abs:heterogeneous OR abs:"cross-model" OR abs:"mixture of agents")`; `(abs:"review comments" OR abs:"code review") AND (abs:Copilot OR abs:CodeRabbit OR abs:bot OR abs:bots)`; `abs:AIDev AND (abs:review OR abs:"pull request")`; `(abs:"automated code review" OR abs:"AI code review" OR abs:"LLM-based code review") AND (abs:industrial OR abs:deployment OR abs:production OR abs:developers)`; `(abs:Mozilla OR abs:Google OR abs:Microsoft OR abs:Ericsson OR abs:Meta OR abs:Tencent OR abs:Uber) AND abs:"code review" AND (abs:LLM ...)`; `ti:"code review" AND (abs:industrial OR abs:"in practice" OR abs:deployed ...)`; title searches for conference-listed papers; id_list lookups for all items. The API returned HTTP 429 for about 20 minutes mid-session (shared rate limit); a Mozilla-specific and an "alert fatigue" query did not run as a result, and no Mozilla LLM-review study was found by the queries that did run.
- **Semantic Scholar Graph API:** search failed throughout (HTTP 429 on every attempt, including retries with 10–35 s back-off and a later retry). No results used.
- **OpenReview API:** `notes/search?term=` for "multi-agent code review", "code review agents" and "code review judge" worked once (titles and venues only); later note and forum requests returned a "ChallengeRequiredError" (403), and the web forum page showed a browser check. So OpenReview reviews could not be read.
- **ACL Anthology:** no direct search interface usable; a web search found no aclanthology.org results for 2026 multi-agent code review beyond MulVul (already covered).
- **conf.researchr.org:** downloaded track pages (curl) for ICSE 2026 research and SEIP, FSE 2026 research and industry, ASE 2025 papers and industry showcase, ASE 2026 papers and industry showcase, MSR 2026 technical and mining challenge, ICSE 2027 research; matched titles by keyword. Each page embeds the whole conference programme, so a title's own track could not be told reliably from these pages; venues are given as "in the X programme" only. The ASE 2026 "papers" and ICSE 2027 pages had no parsable titles (ICSE 2027 acceptances may not be posted yet).
- **NeurIPS virtual site:** one page (collusion paper).
- **Web search and fetch (vendor sources):** Claude Code docs (ultrareview, code-review-local, code-review); Anthropic blog (Code Review launch) and engineering blog (multi-agent research system); GitHub `gh api` on anthropics/claude-plugins-official and anthropics/claude-code-action; Cursor blog (Bugbot, Graphite); Graphite docs; Braintrust case study; GitHub blog (Copilot review); OpenAI Alignment blog; Qodo blog and its dev.to benchmark post; Greptile benchmarks and v4 post; CodeRabbit blog (two posts); Google Cloud Gemini Code Assist docs (two pages). The Gemini docs URL on developers.google.com redirects to docs.cloud.google.com.
- **Fetch failures:** the AutoCommenter PDF (arXiv 2405.13565v1) came back as unparsed binary.

## Top 10 must-reads

In order of how much each should change the plan or the write-up. Each is entered in full above. **(R)** marks items I rechecked myself.

1. **The More the Better? A Comparison of Single- and Multi-Agent LLM Setups on a Financial Review Task.**
   - **Source:** Pei Liu, Oct 2026, [arXiv 2610.27488](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27488). (R)
   - **Why:** the closest controlled design to the project's specialist-against-generalist question. It has:
     - known defects, with misses and false alarms scored separately;
     - token cost;
     - Claude models, with a same-family labelling caveat;
     - Bonferroni-corrected primary predictions.
   - **Its result is the project's null hypothesis, stated with care:** split-scope seats lower the flagging threshold without improving discrimination, and three draws of one agent beat the team. Read the full text for its "misfiled" false alarms, which are true defects flagged under another seat's scope.
2. **Refute-or-Promote, full text and the `strat.md` playbook.**
   - **Source:** Agarwal, 2026, [arXiv 2604.19049](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.19049). (R)
   - **Why:** a Claude Sonnet and Opus council with a Codex cross-family critic. Its authors call the pipeline "architecturally unidirectional" and leave rescuing wrongly killed findings to a human. It is the clearest published statement of the gap the appeal fills, and a design to cite as the nearest precedent for the cross-family judge.
3. **CyberDual: AI Attackers versus AI Code Reviewers in Real Software Maintenance.**
   - **Source:** Yang et al., Oct 2026, [arXiv 2610.34435](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34435). (R)
   - **Why:** on hidden bugs scored by running the exploit, a second vendor's reviewer catches about two thirds of a Claude reviewer's misses, and Claude reviewers share some misses. It is the strongest cross-vendor complementarity result on planted bugs. It has no benign controls, so it says nothing about precision; that is the part the project adds.
4. **SecPRBench: Can Agentic Code Review Catch Vulnerabilities Before They Are Merged?**
   - **Source:** Yang et al., Oct 2026, [arXiv 2610.33509](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33509). (R)
   - **Why:** real vulnerability-introducing commits that passed review, a Claude Opus 5.5 matching judge with a small human check, and paired McNemar tests. That is the project's escaped-bug arm and scoring method, for single agents.
   - **Its matching problem:** 68% of misses name the right file but the wrong mechanism, so the project has to fix its own rule for these in advance.
5. **Anthropic's own review products, documented.**
   - **Sources:** the [ultrareview](https://code.claude.com/docs/en/ultrareview) and [Code Review](https://code.claude.com/docs/en/code-review) docs pages, the [Code Review launch post](https://claude.com/blog/code-review) and the official [`code-review` plugin](https://github.com/anthropics/claude-plugins-official/tree/main/plugins/code-review). (R, docs pages)
   - **Why:** the baseline is a council with a verification step. The plugin is a small, inspectable, judged council (4 seats and confidence scorers at threshold 80) that could be run as a named vendor-style arm. Read alongside Qodo's vendor benchmark, which puts Anthropic's managed review at 79% precision and 52% recall on 580 injected issues.
6. **The Red Queen Gödel Machine: Co-Evolving Agents and Their Evaluators.**
   - **Source:** Iacob et al., Jun–Oct 2026, [arXiv 2606.26294](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.26294). (R)
   - **Why:** it evolves a *code reviewer* against a held-out ground-truth anchor, and corrects reviewer self-preference with an adversarial pool. It is the closest evolutionary work to the project's method, though its reviewer judges whole patches to guide a coder rather than finding planted bugs. Novelty threat: medium.
7. **Unanimity Without Persuasion: A Single Round of Debate Erases the Disagreement That Verification Needs.**
   - **Source:** Shu, 2026, [arXiv 2609.26145](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.26145). (R)
   - **Why:** on code-correctness judging, one debate round produced near-unanimity with no accuracy gain. Afterwards, an execution check changed nothing. Its operational advice ("verify before any second-pass evaluation or peer exposure") bears directly on where the debate arm sits relative to the judge and appeal.
8. **Agreement Overstates Evidence: Error Dependence in LLM Judge Consensus.**
   - **Source:** Hossain, Yousefi and Lim, Sep 2026, [arXiv 2609.22512](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.22512). (R)
   - **Why:** it gives a formula for the effective number of independent judges, n_eff = n / (1 + (n − 1)ρ̄), with evidence that error correlation rises with accuracy, even across providers. It is a cheap way for the project to report how independent its seats and judges really are, and to qualify what a cross-family judge buys.
9. **Trusting the Tool: Asymmetric Deference of LLM Vulnerability Auditors to Static-Analysis Evidence.**
   - **Source:** Liu and Gao, Oct 2026, [arXiv 2610.32876](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32876). (R)
   - **Why:** false "this is safe" notes cut detection of real vulnerabilities by up to 19 points, while false alarms never significantly hurt accuracy. Verifying each claim in a separate call recovered 12 points in a pre-specified test.
   - **Read with:**
     - the corrected reading of *When AI Agrees Too Much*;
     - *Grounded but Not Immune* ([arXiv 2611.05662](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05662)), in which a single false authority note overturned up to 40% of tool-using judges' correct verdicts, mostly by over-trust in the two Claude judges.
   - **Together:** they explain the mechanism behind wrong dismissals, which is the appeal's target.
10. **The evolution loop's keep rule and its failure modes: SGM, read with SIREN and PROCTOR.**
    - **Sources:**
      - SGM: A Statistical Gödel Machine for Risk-Controlled Recursive Self-Modification (Wu et al., [arXiv 2510.10232](https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.10232));
      - SIREN (Zhang et al., NeurIPS 2026, [arXiv 2605.05973](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.05973)) (R);
      - LLM-as-a-Judge Is Not an Oracle (PROCTOR; Wahi, [arXiv 2609.02246](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02246)) (R).
    - **Why:**
      - **SGM** is the closest prior art for the project's "keep only if a 90% CI on the paired difference is above zero" rule. It adds what that rule lacks: an error budget across all generations.
      - **SIREN** covers the winner's curse when several children are selected on the same seeds.
      - **PROCTOR** is the practical failure catalogue: answer-key reading (100% against 68% true capability), parser fallbacks that inflate a metric, canary cases and frozen holdouts.
    - **Read beside them:** two critiques of automatically designed agents. *The Illusion of Multi-Agent Advantage* ([arXiv 2606.13003](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13003)) reports, from the ADAS authors, that ADAS's published results came from evaluating every candidate on the test set. *Simple Baselines are Competitive with Code Evolution* ([arXiv 2602.16805](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.16805)) finds that noisy evaluation makes search pick scaffolds that lose to a majority vote.

**Close behind:**
- Deterministic vs. LLM-Controlled Orchestration (2605.09894), the controlled design for the scripted-against-agent-led arm;
- Stale Instruction Files Override the Code (2610.30571) and Guardrails Beat Guidance (2604.11088), for the `CLAUDE.md` arm;
- Rethinking SZZ Search Strategies: Lessons from Claude Code (2610.14462) and AgentSZZ (2604.02665), for the seed pipeline;
- BugPilot (2510.19898), for Claude-written seeds;
- The Wisdom of Artificial Deliberative Crowds (2609.22497), for clones against mixed families;
- Sifting the Noise (2601.22952), for what filters lose;
- TeamBench (2605.07073), where verifiers approved 49% of failing submissions;
- What Survives the Next Model? (2609.00468), which re-tests VulTrial against a single prompt;
- MemCo (ASE 2026), which co-evolves a whole vulnerability-detection council and blames individual seats;
- When LLMs Benchmark Themselves (2509.26600), where a model that writes the test set and judges it ranks itself first;
- Review Rarely Beats the Stronger Model (2610.15897), where cross-model review beat the stronger model alone in only 9 of 96 directions.

## Close overlaps with the project's design

The project's parts, as listed at the top of prior-work-research.md, against what this sweep found.

| Part of the project | Closest new or newly read work | How close | What is still the project's own |
|---|---|---|---|
| Several reviewer agents in role seats, with a judge | Anthropic's managed Code Review and `code-review` plugin; Qodo 2.0 (specialist agents, a judge agent and severity); Bugbot's first design (8 shuffled passes, a vote and a validator); Unblocked (parallel reviewers, a verifying judge, "dropping requires an explicit reason"); VIC-RAGENT (role agents plus a verifying supervisor on SZZ data) | **High on shape.** It is shipped in industry, with no controlled study published | A controlled, equal-cost comparison against a planted key |
| Specialist seats against one generalist at equal cost | *The More the Better?* (2610.27488) | **High on design**, but document review rather than code, with no judge or appeal and one task | The same question on code, with seed-level pairing, a judge, an appeal and role sets A, B and C |
| Scoring against planted or escaped bugs, with an LLM matching judge | SecPRBench (2610.33509); CR-Bench-v2 (2611.02566); *Auditing AI peer reviewers* (2611.17052; planted errors at three severities, clean copies and Claude Code configurations, but on papers) | **Medium-high** | Councils, the same-family analysis and severity calibration of the judge |
| A judge whose severity calls are checked | CR-Bench-v2 now has severity labels per instance; Gemini Code Assist exposes a severity threshold; ReviewBench slices by severity | **Low.** None checks a judge's severity rulings against a key | The severity-calibration check still looks new |
| An appeal that reinstates dismissed findings | Refute-or-Promote (human rescue only, "unidirectional"); Reviewing the Reviewer (2603.19267, a production maker-checker appeal, not code); TinyV (2505.14625, re-examines rejected answers in RL); Klieber et al. (2607.09979, blocks dismissal when runs disagree) | **Medium outside code, low within it** | Still the newest-looking part, within code review |
| Evolving or racing council configurations | MemCo (ASE 2026, doi 10.1145/3832783.3834522; co-evolves every agent's prompt in an industrial vulnerability-detection system against the end-to-end result, evolving only the agents blamed for each miss; a MulVul follow-up); Red Queen Gödel Machine (2606.26294, a co-evolved code reviewer with held-out anchors); MetaVul (2610.40721, evolves one detector's harness on labelled pairs with a held-out set, by a VulTrial co-author); EvoTeam (2610.39813, evolves team organisation on general tasks); Probe-and-Refine (2606.20512, evolves a guidance file against synthetic bug probes) | **Medium-high for MemCo** (whole-system evolution with seat blame, on bug detection); **medium** for the rest | Evolving review councils over size, roles, models and effort, scored on planted bugs in code changes, with a judge, an appeal and equal-cost arms |
| The keep rule (a paired 90% CI above zero, with held-out seeds) | SGM (2510.10232; admits a self-modification only when a statistical test certifies it, under an error budget across rounds); SIREN (2605.05973); Simple Baselines are Competitive with Code Evolution (2602.16805; recommends at least 10 repetitions and 95% CIs) | **Medium-high on the rule itself**, though SGM is tested on vision, RL and black-box optimisation rather than agents | Applying it to councils; SGM's error budget across generations is worth adopting |
| Same-family blind spots (Claude writes, plants and reviews) | CyberDual (2610.34435); Agreement Overstates Evidence (2609.22512); When LLMs Benchmark Themselves (2509.26600, where a model that writes the test set and also judges ranks itself first, in translation and Chatbot Arena); AI-to-AI Code Reviews (2608.21311, same-product review in the field); The Specification as Quality Gate (2603.25773, small same-family planted-bug experiments); Song 2026 (already cited; its union gain is significant only against two same-model reviews) | **High.** The question has prior answers | Councils, a judge and an appeal; a larger planted set; non-Claude seeds |
| Debate against independent review | Unanimity Without Persuasion (2609.26145, on code); Chun et al. (2503.12029, TOSEM, code tasks other than review); Homogenization in MAS (2610.09824, role-briefed reviewers with mixed models on security code) | **Medium** | Debate as an arm inside a judged council scored on seeds |
| Scripted against agent-led orchestration | Deterministic vs. LLM-Controlled Orchestration (2605.09894) | **Medium on design**, different task | The same comparison for review councils, on Claude Code workflows |
| A project's `CLAUDE.md` as help or bias for reviewers | Evaluating AGENTS.md (2602.11988), Stale Instruction Files (2610.30571), Guardrails Beat Guidance (2604.11088) and Memory-File Security (2610.38521), all on solving rather than reviewing; AI Cannot Review What Humans Catch (2611.18582) | **Low for review** | Still open: no study varies a reviewed project's `CLAUDE.md` and measures planted-bug recall |

**Bottom line on novelty.**
- **Unchanged since 12 Nov:** the newest-looking pieces are the appeal of dismissals within code review, the severity-calibration check, the equal-cost role-set comparison on code and racing whole review councils.
- **What tonight's sweep changes:**
  1. *The More the Better?* now pre-empts the bare "do split seats beat one generalist at equal cost?" question in another domain, so the project should cite it and frame its own result as the code-review test of it.
  2. The council-plus-judge shape is standard in products (Anthropic's included), so the contribution is the measurement, not the architecture.
  3. MemCo, the Red Queen Gödel Machine and MetaVul bring evolving multi-agent bug detectors and reviewers close enough that the write-up should say plainly what is different: review of code changes, a judge and appeal, planted keys, equal-cost arms and a paired-CI keep rule.
  4. SGM is prior art for the keep rule, so cite it as such.

## Experiment ideas the literature suggests (suggestions only)

**These are suggestions from the literature, not decisions.** None has been added to the plan. Each names the work it comes from.

**The appeal and the judge**
1. **A "burden of proof" judge arm as a rival to the appeal.** The judge may dismiss a finding only with a concrete refutation, not for uncertainty. Compare it with the current judge plus appeal on recall, precision and tokens. (Kodus, where this change "roughly doubled delivered recall"; Unblocked; VulnSAGE.)
2. **Claim-by-claim verification.** The judge checks each finding in a separate call and is shown which claims were refuted, compared with one call over all findings. (Trusting the Tool, +12 points under false assurances.)
3. **Blind the appeal to the judge's reasons.** One arm gives the appeal the judge's dismissal reasons, as now; the other gives only the dismissed findings and the code. (Grounded but Not Immune, where one authority note overturned up to 40% of correct verdicts; Silent Dissent.)
4. **A cheap appeal baseline: rerun the same judge.** Use a second independent run of the same judge on the dismissed set, or "keep if the judge's runs disagree". Compare it with the appeal agent. (Figalová et al., where two identical runs differed on 29 eligible records; Klieber et al.'s consistency check.)
5. **Report "recoverable headroom" directly:**
   - seeds raised by at least one seat but lost at the judge;
   - the share the appeal wins back;
   - separately, how often a reinstated finding's severity is then right. (When Debate Helps, on proposal supply and read-out; Detection Without Correction, on detecting an error against fixing it.)
6. **Re-file instead of dismiss.** Let the judge move a finding to the seat whose scope it fits, rather than ruling it a false alarm. (*The More the Better?*, where many team false alarms were true defects under another seat's requirement.)
7. **A matching rule for near misses, fixed in advance.** Decide whether a finding in the right file with the wrong mechanism is a hit, a partial hit or a miss, and let the appeal contest such rulings. (SecPRBench's 68%.)
8. **A judge-swap robustness check on the keep rule.** Re-score a sample of parent-against-child comparisons with a second judge model, to see whether keep decisions survive. (Swapping the Judge Changes the Ranking, where rankings didn't survive a change of judge.)
9. **Per-bug-class reporting of what the judge drops.** (Sifting the Noise: losses ranged from 0.41% to 84.50% depending on CWE.)

**Seats, diversity and debate**

10. **An effective number of independent seats.** Estimate pairwise error correlation (double-fault on seeds) between seats and judges, and report n_eff alongside council size. (Agreement Overstates Evidence; the capability-controlled diversity audit, 2607.20768.)
11. **A "sample the generalist three times, flag on any draw" arm** at equal tokens, alongside the existing rerun-one-reviewer baseline. (*The More the Better?*, where it beat the split team.)
12. **For the debate arm:**
    - give the judge and appeal the pre-debate findings as well as the post-debate ones;
    - add a matched "re-think alone" control, so that changes after debate aren't all credited to debate. (Unanimity Without Persuasion; Not All Flips Are Conformity.)
13. **Effort and agreement.** Measure whether raising seat effort makes seats agree more beyond what accuracy explains, since that would cancel some of a mixed council's diversity. (Does Reasoning Make Language Models Agree?)
14. **Check what "mixed family" means.** Before treating a non-Claude model as independent, check whether it is known or reported to be distilled from Claude. (Exposing Vulnerability Monoculture, on lower systemic robustness in pools distilled from Claude Opus 4.6.)
15. **Report the strongest single reviewer alone,** not only the gain over the author or over same-model pairs, since cross-model "review" gains can be capability transfer from the stronger model. (Review Rarely Beats the Stronger Model; the Song 2026 qualification.)
16. **A judge-quality null for the self-preference analysis:** compare a judge's rulings on its own family's findings with its rulings on others', rather than with a fixed standard. (Are LLM Evaluators Really Narcissists?, where only 51% of earlier significant cases survived this control.)
17. **Randomise or fix seat order in the sequential arm,** and log it. (When Order Matters, where a stronger model placed after weaker ones lost much of its advantage.)
18. **Check how the judge counts restated findings.** Test whether a bug that several seats restate gets more weight than one independent reading would justify. (Copies or Sources?, where a forwarded copy counted for 0.06 to 0.42 of a new reading.)
19. **Run the official `code-review` plugin and OpenCodeReview's Claude Code plugin as named external arms** next to `/code-review ultra`, since both run on Claude and are inspectable. (The plugin's README; OpenCodeReview's README.)

**Seeds and contamination**

20. **FeatAdd-style Claude seeds.** Claude adds a feature to a test app, and regressions caught by tests become seeds, so the planter isn't choosing where bugs go. Tag them separately from deliberately planted seeds. (BugPilot.)
21. **A seed validity check:**
    - a seed must pass the app's existing tests but fail a stronger oracle;
    - drop seeds that an equivalent-mutant check says change nothing. (Test-aware mutant generation, 2609.35841; Meta ACH.)
22. **Random-site against Claude-chosen-site seeds,** to measure whether placement by a Claude planter makes seeds easier or harder for Claude reviewers. (BugFarm, which steers bugs to where a detector looks least.)
23. **Escaped bugs:**
    - trace them with an agentic SZZ, and record agreement with a classic SZZ or a non-Claude tracer, so seed choice doesn't depend only on Claude;
    - add `Fixes:`-tag and revert-based escaped bugs where the projects use those conventions;
    - record the labelling strategy for each seed. (Kondo et al.; Risse and Böhme; AgentSZZ; ReDef; One Strategy Does Not Fit All.)
24. **Cheap contamination probes on candidate seeds:**
    - can Claude name the buggy file from the PR title alone?
    - do independent sessions give suspiciously uniform answers?
    - in each case, compare against matched controls from the same repository. (SWE-Bench-Verified memory study, 2512.10218; Cross-Context Verification, 2603.21454; the measurement-first audit, 2610.05830.)
25. **Neutral seed PR descriptions, plus a metadata-redaction arm.** Descriptions can leak the bug (SWE-Bench+) or hide it (When AI Agrees Too Much, where redacting PR metadata restored detection in all affected cases).

**The evolution loop**

26. **Canary seeds and key isolation.** Plant a seed whose key entry can't be inferred from the code, so "finding" it signals leakage. Keep the key off any path a reviewer or mutation proposer can read. (PROCTOR; the Meta-Agent Challenge's "ground-truth exfiltration".)
27. **A final held-out set that no keep decision ever reads,** plus a winner's-curse correction or a fresh-seed re-score for kept children when several compete on the same seeds. (SIREN; Anthropic's hillclimb recipe, which consults its test set in every keep decision.)
28. **Track brief length across generations, and check for "phantom" rules** added for failures that never occurred. (TextReg; Phantom Guardrails.)
29. **Replicate whole evolution runs,** not just evaluations, since the design process itself has high variance. (Meta-Agent Challenge; *Auditing the Product, Not the Recursion*, 2610.27610, where only 22% of self-improvement studies audited two or more reruns of the full loop.)
30. **An error budget across generations** for the keep rule, so that the chance of keeping at least one spurious change over the whole run is bounded, not just per comparison. (SGM.)
31. **A majority-vote and random-search baseline in every generation,** plus a check that evolved councils haven't degenerated into "the same generalist three times". (The Illusion of Multi-Agent Advantage, on AFlow's "Degeneration to Trivial Ensembles"; Simple Baselines are Competitive with Code Evolution.)
32. **A three-way outcome for each generation:** advance, reject, or "inconclusive, run more seeds", with a sequential stopping test. (AgentAssay. The evidence is weak, as it is single-author and unreviewed.)

**`CLAUDE.md`, orchestration and human cost**

33. **`CLAUDE.md` conditions:**
    - none;
    - real;
    - a length-matched placebo (irrelevant or shuffled);
    - stale or wrong.
    - In each, measure false alarms and misses separately, and check with a canary string that the "none" condition really excludes the file. (Guardrails Beat Guidance; Stale Instruction Files; the Claude Code sub-agents page on `omitClaudeMd` and nested files.)
34. **Scripted against agent-led, done as COBOL-Orch did.**
    - Hold models, briefs and tools fixed and vary only who controls execution.
    - Report the mean score, run-to-run variance, worst case and tokens.
    - Log over-spawning and duplicated work in the agent-led arm.
    - Account for workflow prompt-cache sharing when comparing costs. (Deterministic vs. LLM-Controlled Orchestration; Anthropic's multi-agent research post; the workflows docs.)
35. **A human-cost proxy in the final report:** findings shown to a person per run, and an estimated triage time per finding, so volume counts against a council. (Beko, where PR closure time rose from 5h52m to 8h20m; Meta's randomised trial, where reviewers were over 5% slower; Habituation at the Gate.)
